SpO₂ 89% on air, HR 118, BP 104/68. Before reading on, decide: is the priority to measure how much blood he has lost?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Doctors now prefer the term life-threatening haemoptysis over "massive haemoptysis" — patients are unreliable at estimating how much blood they've lost, some blood is swallowed or stays in the lung, and even a small bleed can block the airway or overwhelm a weak chest. Don't wait for a measured volume before acting if the airway or breathing is under threat. The real danger here is choking and low oxygen, not blood loss — protecting the airway comes before finishing the history.

Step 2. History and red flags

First confirm this is true haemoptysis, and not haematemesis (preceded by nausea/vomiting, dark or coffee-ground blood, may contain food) or blood coming from the nose, mouth or throat (pseudohaemoptysis). Then characterise it: onset, an estimate of the volume (streaking, clots, or gross blood), whether it's ongoing or settling, any position that worsens it, anticoagulant or antiplatelet use, and known bronchiectasis, TB, cancer or vasculitis. Separately, classify any accompanying cough by duration — acute (under 3 weeks), subacute (3–8 weeks), chronic (over 8 weeks) — since this drives the differential.

In plain terms: a man with known bronchiectasis and ongoing life-threatening haemoptysis with low oxygen and a fast heart rate.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Look for site-specific chest findings to work out which side is bleeding; clubbing or cachexia; enlarged lymph nodes; telangiectasia; a vasculitic rash; leg swelling suggesting a clot; signs of heart failure; examine the mouth and nose to exclude a non-lung source of the blood.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CauseCharacteristic clues
Bronchogenic carcinomaCurrent or previous smoker, new or altered cough, weight loss
TuberculosisChronic cough, fever, night sweats, weight loss, relevant exposure history
BronchiectasisChronic productive cough, purulent sputum, recurrent infection, previous haemoptysis
Pulmonary embolismPleuritic pain, sudden breathlessness, risk factors for a blood clot
Pulmonary oedemaPink frothy sputum, raised JVP, peripheral oedema
Vasculitis / pulmonary-renal syndromeGranulomatosis with polyangiitis, anti-GBM disease, SLE — look for blood in the urine and red-cell casts
Must not miss
  • Airway-threatening haemoptysis — inability to clear blood, falling SpO₂, altered consciousness
  • Bronchogenic carcinoma — a new or altered cough in a smoker with haemoptysis, weight loss or hoarseness is never a "routine cough"
  • Pulmonary-renal vasculitis — haemoptysis together with blood in the urine or impaired kidney function

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideFBC, clotting, renal function, group and cross-match; ECG; ABG if SpO₂ is under 92% or the patient is significantly breathlessQuantifies how severe the bleed is and prepares for transfusion or reversal of anticoagulation
LaboratoryANA, ANCA, anti-GBM antibodies, urinalysis with microscopy when vasculitis is suspectedIdentifies a pulmonary-renal syndrome that needs immunosuppression
ImagingChest X-ray firstMay localise the bleeding, or show a mass, collapse or consolidation
SpecialCT chest ± bronchoscopy; bronchial artery angiographyLocalises the source and identifies the target for embolisation if bleeding continues

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: life-threatening haemoptysis in known bronchiectasis. Alternative kept alive: an unrecognised second pathology (malignancy, vasculitis) — known bronchiectasis does not exempt a patient from a full work-up for the cause. Severity is defined by the threat to the airway, gas exchange, breathing effort or blood pressure, not by a specific volume of blood.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: call for help early — senior physician, anaesthetics/critical care, respiratory medicine, interventional radiology, cardiothoracic surgery, and haematology if the patient is anticoagulated. Airway: sit the patient upright if this helps them clear blood; if the bleeding side is known, position them with the bleeding lung down to protect the good lung; get anaesthetic help if GCS is under 8 or the airway cannot be protected. Breathing: oxygen targeting SpO₂ above 94% (unless the patient has known chronic type II respiratory failure); ABG; consider ventilation if oxygenation or airway protection fails. Circulation: two large-bore cannulae (16G or larger), send FBC/clotting/group-and-cross-match, give fluids, and transfuse if haemoglobin is under 70 g/L or ongoing bleeding demands it.

Definitive: correct any clotting abnormality; give IV tranexamic acid 1 g three times daily (avoid in significant kidney failure); consider nebulised adrenaline (1 mL of 1:1000 adrenaline with 4 mL saline) or IV terlipressin (2 mg initially, then 1 mg every 4–6 hours) under senior supervision; proceed to bronchial artery embolisation for definitive control if bleeding continues.

Monitoring and failure: monitor SpO₂, respiratory rate, conscious level and serial haemoglobin continuously; recurrent or worsening bleeding despite first-line measures is the trigger for embolisation rather than repeating conservative measures.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

HDU or ICU for any life-threatening haemoptysis; inform interventional radiology and respiratory teams before deterioration, not after; the SBAR handover should state the estimated ongoing rate of bleeding, the side if known, and the escalation plan already agreed.

Two common student errors
  • Waiting to "measure" the volume of haemoptysis before escalating — volume estimates are unreliable, and the true threat is airway compromise.
  • Prescribing antibiotics for a cough purely because the sputum is coloured, without interpreting the colour alongside fever, inflammatory markers and imaging.

Check your understanding

Q1Why has "life-threatening haemoptysis" replaced "massive haemoptysis" as the preferred term?

Answer. Patients cannot reliably estimate how much blood they've lost, some blood is swallowed or stays within the lung, and even a small volume of blood can be fatal if it blocks the airway or overwhelms a patient with poor respiratory reserve. Severity should be judged by the threat to airway, gas exchange, breathing and circulation, not by an arbitrary volume cut-off.

Q2How do you distinguish true haemoptysis from haematemesis?

Answer. Haematemesis is usually preceded by nausea or vomiting, the blood is often dark or coffee-ground in appearance, and it may contain food. True haemoptysis is coughed up, often bright red and frothy, and may be mixed with sputum.

Q3In a patient with haemoptysis, what clinical clues should make you actively look for a pulmonary-renal vasculitic syndrome?

Answer. Haemoptysis occurring together with blood in the urine or impaired kidney function should prompt testing for ANA, ANCA and anti-GBM antibodies, and urinalysis with microscopy looking for red-cell casts, to identify conditions such as granulomatosis with polyangiitis, anti-GBM disease or SLE.

Q4What is the definitive treatment for ongoing life-threatening haemoptysis that does not respond to first-line medical measures?

Answer. Bronchial artery embolisation, once the source has been localised (typically by CT chest ± bronchoscopy).

Case scenario

A 64-year-old man, a lifelong smoker, presents with a new cough of 6 weeks' duration and two episodes of blood-streaked sputum. He has lost 5 kg in weight over 2 months and reports no fever or purulent sputum. Observations are normal and he is not in respiratory distress. What is the most important next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Urgent chest X-ray and referral on a suspected-cancer pathway. A new or altered cough with haemoptysis and weight loss in a smoker must be treated as suspicious for bronchogenic carcinoma until proven otherwise, even though he is haemodynamically stable and not acutely unwell.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A patient with known bronchiectasis has ongoing haemoptysis, is struggling to clear blood from his airway, and his SpO₂ is falling. What is the most appropriate immediate priority?

  1. Estimate the exact volume of blood lost before deciding on further action
  2. Start oral tranexamic acid and observe
  3. Protect the airway, positioning the bleeding lung down if known, and call for senior/anaesthetic help
  4. Wait for a chest X-ray to confirm the bleeding side before acting
  5. Arrange an urgent CT chest before any other intervention
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. Airway protection is the immediate priority in life-threatening haemoptysis; positioning the bleeding lung down (if known) protects the good lung, and senior/anaesthetic input should be sought early. A is wrong because volume estimation is unreliable and delays action. E and D are wrong because imaging should not delay immediate airway and breathing management. B is wrong because tranexamic acid should be given IV, not oral, in this setting, and is not a substitute for airway management.

MCQ 2. A patient with life-threatening haemoptysis has a haemoglobin of 65 g/L with ongoing bleeding. Renal function is normal. What is the most appropriate combination of immediate measures?

  1. Immediate bronchial artery embolisation without any medical therapy first
  2. Oral tranexamic acid and outpatient haematology referral
  3. IV terlipressin alone, with no transfusion
  4. IV tranexamic acid, blood transfusion, and correction of any clotting abnormality
  5. Withhold transfusion until haemoglobin falls below 50 g/L
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. With a haemoglobin below 70 g/L and ongoing bleeding, transfusion is indicated alongside IV tranexamic acid and correction of any clotting abnormality. B is wrong because tranexamic acid should be given IV, and outpatient referral is inappropriate for a life-threatening bleed. A skips first-line resuscitation and medical measures, which should generally be tried or at least initiated in parallel. E uses an incorrect, unsafely low transfusion threshold. C omits necessary transfusion and clotting correction.

Presentation 6Unilateral and bilateral leg swelling

Swelling (oedema) is not a diagnosis — it's a mechanism made visible. Four questions structure the whole assessment: is the patient unstable, is the swelling in one place or all over, is the blood volume high, normal or low, and which mechanism explains it — raised pressure in the veins, low blood protein, leaky vessels, or a blocked lymph system?

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 45-year-old woman, ten days after abdominal surgery, presents with a swollen, tender left calf and sudden pleuritic chest pain with breathlessness. HR 118, SpO₂ 92% on air, BP 118/72. Before reading on, decide: is a D-dimer the next test?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Stabilise first: check for fluid on the lungs, sepsis, a blood clot with possible clot in the lung, blocked blood flow to the limb or compartment syndrome, severe pre-eclampsia, or airway-threatening swelling. This patient has a fast heart rate and low oxygen with a swollen calf — treat the possibility of a lung clot at the same time as confirming the leg clot, not one after the other.

Step 2. History and red flags

Establish onset (sudden or gradual), distribution (one leg or both), pain, and provoking factors: immobility, recent surgery, active cancer, previous DVT, pregnancy, oestrogen exposure. Acute painful swelling in one leg is DVT until proven otherwise; clinical examination alone cannot safely exclude it.

In plain terms: a post-surgical woman with sudden painful swelling in one leg, together with pleuritic chest pain and low oxygen.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Check for pitting versus non-pitting oedema; one leg versus both; warmth, redness, tenderness, and a difference in calf circumference; JVP (raised in right heart failure or fluid overload, not in isolated venous disease); signs of chronic liver or kidney disease; the skin changes of chronic venous insufficiency versus the firm, non-pitting texture and Stemmer sign of lymphoedema.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

PatternDiagnoses
Acute, one leg, painfulDVT; cellulitis; ruptured Baker's cyst; acute compartment syndrome; muscle or joint injury
Chronic, one legChronic venous insufficiency; lymphoedema; post-thrombotic syndrome; obstruction of a pelvic or proximal vein
Bilateral, generalisedHeart failure; nephrotic syndrome; cirrhosis with low albumin; venous insufficiency; drug-induced (calcium-channel blockers); an underactive thyroid
Must not miss
  • DVT with concurrent or impending PE — treat as this patient shows, chest symptoms plus leg swelling is one presentation, not two
  • Compartment syndrome / limb-threatening ischaemia — a surgical emergency masquerading as swelling
  • Nephrotic-range proteinuria or decompensated cirrhosis presenting first as swelling in both legs

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideWells pre-test probability scoreDetermines whether D-dimer or imaging is the correct next step
LaboratoryD-dimer (only after estimating pre-test probability); FBC, U&E, albumin, LFTs, urinalysis with quantified proteinA negative, sufficiently sensitive D-dimer in a low pre-test probability patient can exclude VTE; in this high-probability patient, don't wait for it
ImagingCompression duplex ultrasound of the legNeeded for moderate/high probability, or whenever the cause of acute one-sided swelling is not otherwise obvious
SpecialCTPA if PE is suspected at the same time; echocardiography if the swelling is bilateral and a cardiac cause is suspectedChoose based on the hypothesis generated in step 4, not reflexively

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Wells score for this patient (using the PE version of the score, since PE is now suspected alongside the leg swelling): clinical signs and symptoms of DVT (3) + PE is the most likely diagnosis, or equally likely as an alternative (3) + heart rate over 100 (1.5) + immobilisation or surgery within the last 4 weeks (1.5) \= 9 — a high-probability score for PE. D-dimer is not needed to decide whether imaging is required; proceed directly to CTPA (and duplex ultrasound for the leg) and start anticoagulation according to timing, bleeding risk and local protocol.

Teaching moment D-dimer is a rule-out test in appropriately selected low-probability patients, not a universal screening test. Note that the Wells score used to assess PE and the separate Wells score used to assess isolated DVT are related but distinct tools with different criteria — here, PE is being scored, using clinical signs of DVT as one of its component questions.

Wells score for pulmonary embolism

Estimates the pre-test probability of PE and determines whether D-dimer testing is appropriate or whether the patient should go straight to imaging.

CriterionPoints
Clinical signs and symptoms of DVT (leg swelling and pain with palpation of the deep veins)3
PE is the most likely diagnosis, or at least as likely as an alternative3
Heart rate >100/min1.5
Immobilisation for ≥3 days, or surgery in the previous 4 weeks1.5
Previous objectively diagnosed DVT or PE1.5
Haemoptysis1
Malignancy (treated within 6 months, or palliative)1

Interpretation: two-tier model — a score of 4 or less means PE is "unlikely" (proceed to D-dimer); a score above 4 means PE is "likely" (proceed to imaging). Three-tier model — 0–1 low, 2–6 moderate, above 6 high probability. Use whichever version your local pathway specifies.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: oxygen if hypoxaemic; start anticoagulation based on clinical probability, without waiting for imaging confirmation, in a high-probability patient with no contraindication.

Definitive: for confirmed DVT/PE — a direct oral anticoagulant (for example apixaban or rivaroxaban) in suitable low-risk patients; risk-stratify any confirmed PE using the PESI or sPESI score, together with RV imaging and troponin; escalate to thrombolysis, catheter-directed therapy or surgical embolectomy if the patient becomes haemodynamically unstable (persistent systolic BP under 90, a sustained fall of 40 mmHg or more, or cardiac arrest).

Monitoring and failure: reassess oxygenation and haemodynamics after starting treatment; escalate immediately if instability develops despite anticoagulation.

Simplified PESI (sPESI) score

Used once PE is confirmed, to identify patients at low risk of early death who may be suitable for outpatient or early-discharge management, versus those needing closer monitoring.

CriterionPoints
Age over 801
History of cancer1
Chronic heart failure or chronic lung disease1
Heart rate 110/min or more1
Systolic BP under 100 mmHg1
SpO₂ under 90%1

Interpretation: a score of 0 identifies a low-risk patient (low predicted 30-day mortality), who may be suitable for early discharge or outpatient treatment if otherwise well and stable. A score of 1 or more identifies a higher-risk patient who generally needs admission and closer monitoring. This patient's PE would score at least 2 (heart rate ≥110 and SpO₂ <90%), so admission is appropriate.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit for anticoagulation and monitoring given the concurrent hypoxaemia; document the Wells score, the sPESI score and the imaging plan in the notes; give any future low-risk ambulatory DVT/PE patient explicit safety-net advice (worsening breathlessness, chest pain, haemoptysis) and a follow-up date.

Two common student errors
  • Ordering a D-dimer before estimating pre-test probability — a positive result in a low-probability patient is misleading, and in a high-probability patient it is simply unnecessary.
  • Treating bilateral leg swelling as automatically cardiac without checking albumin, urinalysis and liver function.

Check your understanding

Q1Why should you calculate a Wells score before ordering a D-dimer?

Answer. D-dimer has good sensitivity but poor specificity. In a low pre-test probability patient, a negative D-dimer can safely exclude PE/DVT, avoiding unnecessary imaging. In a high pre-test probability patient, D-dimer adds little because imaging is required regardless of the result, and a positive result in this context does not add useful information.

Q2What is the difference between the Wells score for PE and the Wells score for DVT?

Answer. They are related but distinct scoring tools. The PE Wells score assesses the probability of pulmonary embolism using criteria that include clinical signs of DVT, heart rate, immobilisation/surgery, previous VTE, haemoptysis and malignancy. The DVT Wells score is a separate tool focused specifically on the leg (localised tenderness, whole-leg swelling, calf circumference difference, pitting oedema, collateral veins, and so on) to estimate the probability of DVT itself.

Q3In a patient with confirmed PE, what is the purpose of the sPESI score?

Answer. It identifies patients at low risk of early (30-day) death from PE, who may be suitable for outpatient management or early discharge, versus higher-risk patients who need admission and closer monitoring.

Q4Why should you not wait for imaging confirmation before starting anticoagulation in a high pre-test probability patient?

Answer. In a high pre-test probability patient without a contraindication, the risk of a missed or delayed diagnosis of a potentially fatal PE outweighs the small risk of anticoagulating briefly before confirmation. Anticoagulation should be started on clinical grounds while imaging is arranged.

Case scenario

A 34-year-old woman on the combined oral contraceptive pill presents with gradual-onset swelling of both legs over several weeks, worse in the evenings, with no pain, redness or warmth. She has no breathlessness or chest pain. Urinalysis shows heavy proteinuria. What is the most likely category of cause, and what should you check next?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Bilateral, gradual, painless leg swelling with heavy proteinuria points towards a low-albumin state, most likely nephrotic syndrome, rather than DVT (which is typically unilateral, acute and painful) or a simple drug effect. Next steps include serum albumin, renal function, a full urine protein quantification (protein:creatinine ratio or 24-hour collection), and renal referral; the oral contraceptive pill is a relevant background risk factor for VTE but does not explain bilateral, painless, gradual swelling with heavy proteinuria.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 50-year-old man presents with sudden-onset unilateral calf swelling and pleuritic chest pain 12 days after a hip replacement. HR is 112, SpO₂ 91% on air. His Wells score for PE is calculated at 7.5. What is the most appropriate next step?

  1. Give high-dose aspirin only
  2. Proceed directly to CTPA and duplex ultrasound, and start anticoagulation without waiting for imaging
  3. Discharge with safety-net advice as the presentation is likely musculoskeletal
  4. Arrange outpatient duplex ultrasound within one week
  5. Send a D-dimer and wait for the result before further action
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. A Wells score of 7.5 is high probability for PE. In high-probability patients, D-dimer is not useful (E is wrong; it doesn't change management and delays care) — the correct step is to proceed straight to imaging and start anticoagulation empirically. C ignores clear red flags (hypoxia, tachycardia, risk factors). D is far too slow for a high-probability, hypoxic patient. A does not provide therapeutic anticoagulation for VTE.

MCQ 2. A patient with a confirmed PE has an sPESI score of 0, is haemodynamically stable, has good social support and no bleeding risk factors. What does this sPESI result suggest?

  1. She is a potential candidate for outpatient management or early discharge on anticoagulation
  2. An sPESI of 0 means no anticoagulation is needed
  3. She requires thrombolysis regardless of haemodynamic status
  4. She has a very high risk of death and needs ICU admission
  5. The sPESI score is only used before, not after, PE is confirmed
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. An sPESI of 0 identifies a low risk of early death from PE, and such patients (if otherwise clinically stable and with adequate support) are potential candidates for outpatient management or early discharge, per current guidance. D is the opposite of what a score of 0 indicates. E is wrong — PESI/sPESI is specifically used after PE is confirmed, to guide disposition. C is wrong; thrombolysis is reserved for haemodynamically unstable (high-risk) PE, not low-risk PE. B is wrong — anticoagulation is still required for confirmed PE regardless of the sPESI score; the score guides the level of monitoring/setting, not whether to anticoagulate.

Block 2 · Week 3 of the module
Neurological presentations

Presentation 1Headache

The key question is not which painkiller to give. It is whether this headache is a condition in its own right or a sign of something more serious. How fast the pain builds matters more than where it hurts: a true "thunderclap" headache reaches full intensity almost instantly, and that single fact should shape the first sixty seconds of the consultation.

Figure 2.1. Approach to acute headache using red flags and localising signs to distinguish urgent secondary causes from common primary headache syndromes and guide investigation.

Clinical vignette

Presentation. A 44-year-old woman presents with the worst headache of her life, reaching maximal intensity within seconds while lifting weights. She has vomited twice and complains of neck stiffness. GCS 15, no focal deficit. Before reading on, decide: how quickly did the pain peak, and why does that single answer matter more than how severe the pain is?

Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)

DomainScore
Eye opening4 spontaneous / 3 to speech / 2 to pain / 1 none
Verbal response5 orientated / 4 confused / 3 words / 2 sounds / 1 none
Motor response6 obeys commands / 5 localises pain / 4 withdraws / 3 abnormal flexion / 2 extension / 1 none

Interpretation: total score 3–15 (sum of the three domains). 13–15 is normal/mild impairment, 9–12 moderate, and 8 or below is severe and generally means the airway needs active protection.

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Check airway, breathing, circulation, temperature, conscious level and glucose. Look for neck stiffness, a rash that does not fade under pressure, or a rapidly falling conscious level. A thunderclap onset, altered consciousness, a focal deficit, seizure or neck stiffness puts this patient on an emergency pathway, not routine outpatient assessment.

Step 2. History and the SNOOP4 red flags

Establish the time course first. Instantaneous-or-seconds onset points to subarachnoid haemorrhage, other intracranial haemorrhage, reversible cerebral vasoconstriction syndrome, arterial dissection, cerebral venous sinus thrombosis or pituitary apoplexy. Then screen systematically:

FlagMeaning
SSystemic symptoms — fever, weight loss, cancer, HIV, immunosuppression
NNeurological abnormality — confusion, reduced consciousness, seizure, focal deficit
OOnset sudden — thunderclap
OOlder age at onset — new headache after 50
PPapilloedema
PPositional — worse standing, or worse lying flat
PPrecipitated by exercise, cough, Valsalva or sexual activity
PProgressive, or a change from the patient's usual pattern

In plain terms: a woman with an exertional, instantaneous-onset thunderclap headache with vomiting and meningism.

Step 3. Discriminating examination — including the eye exam most students skip

Check temperature, blood pressure, GCS, meningeal signs and full cranial nerves. Do not omit visual acuity, visual fields, pupils, eye movements and fundoscopy for papilloedema — one of the most frequently missed steps in headache assessment. Papilloedema changes the order of tests: image before you do a lumbar puncture.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

PrimaryImportant secondary causes
Migraine with or without auraSubarachnoid, intracerebral or subdural haemorrhage
Tension-type headacheMeningitis, encephalitis, cerebral abscess
Cluster / trigeminal autonomic cephalalgiasCerebral venous sinus thrombosis; raised ICP, tumour, hydrocephalus
Exertional, cough or coital headacheGiant cell arteritis; acute angle-closure glaucoma; low-CSF-pressure headache; medication overuse
Must not miss
  • Subarachnoid haemorrhage — thunderclap onset; apply the Ottawa SAH rule (age ≥40, neck pain/stiffness, witnessed LOC, onset during exertion, instantaneous peak, limited neck flexion)
  • Bacterial meningitis — fever, meningism, altered mental state; the classic triad may be incomplete
  • Giant cell arteritis — new headache after 50 with jaw claudication or visual symptoms; treat on suspicion, do not wait for biopsy
  • Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis — especially in pregnancy/puerperium, contraceptive pill use, dehydration
  • Acute angle-closure glaucoma — painful red eye, halos, mid-dilated pupil, hard globe

Ottawa SAH Rule

Feature (any one present → investigate for SAH)
Age 40 years or older
Neck pain or stiffness
Witnessed loss of consciousness
Onset during exertion
Thunderclap headache — instantly peaking pain
Limited neck flexion on examination

Interpretation: applies to alert, neurologically normal adults with acute non-traumatic headache reaching maximum intensity within an hour. If one or more features are present, investigate for SAH; the rule is a sensitive trigger for further workup, not a points-based score.

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideTemperature, BP, glucose, fundoscopyPapilloedema means image before LP; fever redirects to a septic-source workup
LaboratoryFBC, ESR/CRP if age >50 or GCA suspectedESR is often markedly raised in GCA, though a normal value does not exclude it
ImagingUrgent non-contrast CT headFirst step in any thunderclap headache; very high negative predictive value within 6 hours of onset under appropriate protocols
CSFLumbar puncture for xanthochromia if CT negative, generally at least 12 hours after onset for best yieldDetects SAH missed by CT beyond the early high-sensitivity window
SpecialCT/MR venographyWhenever CVST is a differential — do not stop at a normal non-contrast CT

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Case continued

She meets four Ottawa SAH criteria (age, neck stiffness, exertional onset, instantaneous peak). Non-contrast CT performed within 4 hours shows no haemorrhage.

Working diagnosis: possible SAH, CT-negative. Proceed to lumbar puncture for xanthochromia rather than discharging on a negative early scan alone, because her pre-test probability is high. Alternative kept alive: reversible cerebral vasoconstriction syndrome, which can also present as recurrent thunderclap headache and needs vascular imaging if LP is negative but suspicion remains high.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: analgesia, antiemetic, strict bed rest pending diagnosis. If SAH is confirmed, arrange urgent vascular imaging and neurosurgical/neurointerventional referral without delay.

Definitive: aneurysm securing (coiling or clipping) per neurosurgical assessment if SAH confirmed. For suspected bacterial meningitis, do not delay empirical antibiotics while waiting for CT or LP in a clinically convincing case. For suspected GCA, start corticosteroids immediately on clinical suspicion, because visual loss is often permanent once it happens.

Monitoring and failure: serial neurological observations and GCS. Deterioration or a new focal deficit triggers repeat imaging and neurosurgical review.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit under neurosurgery or acute medicine pending LP/vascular imaging results. Document which Ottawa SAH criteria were met and the imaging timeline. SBAR handover states the working diagnosis and the trigger for escalation (new deficit, falling GCS, rebleed symptoms).

Two common student errors
  • Ordering a routine CT for a classic, stable, recurrent migraine with a normal examination — investigations rarely establish a primary headache diagnosis.
  • Skipping fundoscopy, missing papilloedema, and going straight to lumbar puncture without imaging first.

Check your understanding

Q1Why does the speed of onset of a headache matter more than its severity?

Answer. A headache that reaches maximum intensity within seconds ("thunderclap") points to a small group of dangerous causes — SAH, other intracranial haemorrhage, RCVS, arterial dissection, CVST, pituitary apoplexy — regardless of how severe it feels. A headache can be very severe (e.g. migraine) but build up over minutes to hours and still be benign. Onset speed, not pain intensity, is what triggers the emergency pathway.

Q2A patient with suspected SAH has a normal non-contrast CT head performed at 3 hours. What should happen next, and why?

Answer. If clinical suspicion remains high, proceed to lumbar puncture for xanthochromia, ideally at least 12 hours after symptom onset — a CT within 6 hours has very high negative predictive value under good conditions, but it does not reach 100%, and LP detects SAH that CT can miss, particularly if performed after 6 hours or when doubt remains.

Q3Why should fundoscopy never be skipped in headache assessment?

Answer. Papilloedema signals raised intracranial pressure. If present, imaging must be done before any lumbar puncture, because LP in the presence of raised ICP and a mass lesion risks brain herniation.

Q4A 68-year-old man has a new headache, scalp tenderness and pain on chewing. What must you do before waiting for ESR or biopsy results?

Answer. Start corticosteroids immediately on clinical suspicion of giant cell arteritis. Visual loss from GCA is often permanent once it occurs, so treatment must not wait for confirmatory tests.

Case scenario

A 29-year-old woman, six days postpartum, presents with a headache that has progressively worsened over three days, worse on standing and easing when lying flat. She has no fever and no neck stiffness. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what investigation would you prioritise?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. A postural headache in the puerperium raises two possibilities that both need active exclusion: cerebral venous sinus thrombosis (pregnancy/puerperium is a major risk factor, though CVST headache is not typically classically postural) and low-CSF-pressure headache (e.g. post-dural-puncture if she had an epidural, or spontaneous intracranial hypotension) — a headache that is clearly worse standing and better lying flat is the hallmark of low-CSF-pressure headache. Given the puerperium and progressive course, first prioritise CT or MR venography to exclude CVST before attributing the headache to a benign postural cause.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 52-year-old man reports a headache that reached maximal severity within seconds during sexual intercourse, with vomiting but no neck stiffness and a normal neurological exam. Non-contrast CT head at 2 hours is normal. What is the most appropriate next step?

  1. Discharge with simple analgesia, as the CT excludes SAH
  2. MRI brain with contrast
  3. Lumbar puncture for xanthochromia, timed at least 12 hours from symptom onset if not already met
  4. Reassure and arrange routine neurology outpatient follow-up
  5. Repeat CT head in 24 hours
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. An exertional thunderclap headache meets Ottawa SAH criteria; even with a normal early CT, SAH is not fully excluded, so LP for xanthochromia is indicated, timed appropriately after onset. A: wrong — CT sensitivity is very high but not absolute, especially outside a validated 6-hour high-quality-scan protocol. E: repeating CT adds radiation without excluding SAH as reliably as LP. B: MRI is not the standard next test for suspected SAH after a negative CT. D: unsafe — this presentation meets criteria for urgent SAH workup, not routine follow-up.

MCQ 2. A 71-year-old woman has a two-week history of headache, scalp tenderness, and jaw pain on chewing that resolves with rest. ESR is pending. What is the most appropriate immediate action?

  1. Start empirical antibiotics for presumed meningitis
  2. Wait for ESR before starting any treatment
  3. Arrange temporal artery biopsy and start treatment only if positive
  4. Reassure and review in two weeks
  5. Start high-dose corticosteroids now, on clinical suspicion
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. Jaw claudication and new headache with scalp tenderness in an older patient is classic for GCA. Treatment must start on clinical suspicion because visual loss is often irreversible once it happens. B and C: both delay treatment while risking permanent visual loss. A: there is no fever or meningism to suggest meningitis. D: unsafe delay in a time-critical diagnosis.

Presentation 2Acute limb weakness

Neurological diagnosis rests on three questions: where is the problem, what disease process could cause it, and does a recognisable condition fit the pattern. The history gives you the timing and likely cause; the exam tells you where the lesion sits; tests then confirm or refine that idea — always in that order, never the reverse.

Figure 2.2. Approach to acute limb weakness by localisation pattern, highlighting stroke, brainstem disease, spinal cord or cauda equina lesions, Guillain–Barré syndrome, myasthenia, myopathy and metabolic causes.

Clinical vignette

Presentation. A 34-year-old man reports tingling that began in both feet two days ago, followed yesterday by difficulty climbing stairs, and this morning his hands feel weak. He had a diarrhoeal illness ten days ago. Before reading on, decide: what single bedside test must be done now, before any blood test is sent?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Check whether the airway is protected, how the swallowing muscles are working, the breathing rate, oxygen level, and how far the patient can count aloud in one breath. In suspected Guillain–Barré syndrome, or any acute nerve/muscle weakness, do not wait for the oxygen level to drop — measure lung function at the bedside repeatedly. A forced vital capacity below about 20 mL/kg, or a fall of more than 30% from baseline, means urgent critical-care review is needed.

Step 2. History and red flags

Define onset and tempo precisely — ask what happened first and how symptoms spread, not just "weak for three days". Ask about distribution (one limb, one side, both legs, all four limbs), sensory quality (positive versus negative symptoms), and localising features: aphasia/neglect/visual field loss (hemisphere), diplopia/vertigo/dysarthria (brainstem), back pain/sphincter symptoms/saddle numbness (cord or cauda equina), fatigability that is worse later in the day (neuromuscular junction).

FindingMajor concern
Sudden unilateral weaknessAcute stroke or intracranial haemorrhage
Rapidly ascending weaknessGuillain–Barré syndrome
Back/neck pain with weakness, sensory level, saddle loss, sphincter dysfunctionSpinal cord or cauda equina compression
Rapidly progressive deficit with known cancerMetastatic cord compression
Weak cough, dysphagia, breathlessnessRespiratory/bulbar neuromuscular failure

In plain terms: a young man with a symmetrical, ascending sensorimotor deficit evolving over days after a diarrhoeal illness.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Check for wasting and fasciculation, tone, power in individual movements, reflexes, plantars, coordination (interpreted in light of the weakness), and sensation for a level. Check rectal tone and perianal sensation whenever cauda equina syndrome is possible. Here: look for symmetrical, distal-then-proximal weakness with reduced or absent reflexes — this does not fit a single hemisphere or cord lesion, but does fit an acute polyradiculoneuropathy.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

LocalisationExamples
Brain (cerebral hemisphere)Stroke, TIA, tumour, abscess — usually hemiparesis with cortical signs
BrainstemStroke, demyelination — crossed signs, diplopia, dysarthria, dysphagia
Spinal cord / cauda equinaCompression (tumour, abscess, disc), transverse myelitis, vascular cord lesion
Peripheral nerve / rootGuillain–Barré syndrome, CIDP, compressive mononeuropathy, radiculopathy
Neuromuscular junctionMyasthenia gravis, Lambert-Eaton, botulism
MuscleInflammatory myopathy, toxic/drug myopathy, periodic paralysis
Must not miss
  • Guillain–Barré syndrome with respiratory failure — measure FVC serially; do not wait for desaturation
  • Metastatic or infective cord compression — urgent whole-spine MRI, not lumbar-only, because thoracic and cervical lesions are missed by a lumbar-only scan
  • Cauda equina syndrome — saddle sensory loss, new urinary retention, bilateral leg weakness — a surgical emergency
  • Acute stroke — sudden unilateral weakness with a clear time-of-onset

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideSerial FVC; bladder scan/post-void residual if cord or cauda equina suspectedDetects impending respiratory failure or sphincter involvement before it becomes obvious clinically
LaboratoryGlucose, U&E, calcium, magnesium, CK, TFTsMetabolic and toxic mimics of weakness
CSFLumbar puncture for albuminocytological dissociationClassic GBS pattern is raised protein with a normal cell count — but early CSF or nerve-conduction studies can be normal in about a third of patients and do not exclude the diagnosis
ImagingMRI brain for hemisphere/brainstem localisation; whole-spine MRI for cord/cauda equina localisationImaging is chosen by the localisation from step 4, not ordered as a generic "neurology screen"

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: Guillain–Barré syndrome, post-infective. Alternative kept alive: early cord lesion — excluded here by the absence of a sensory level and by areflexia rather than hyperreflexia. Risk stratification is about respiratory trend, not a numerical score: track FVC, bulbar function and autonomic instability (labile BP, arrhythmia).

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: continuous monitoring of respiratory function and cardiac rhythm; escalate to critical care if FVC falls below threshold or bulbar function deteriorates.

Definitive: IV immunoglobulin or plasma exchange per local neurology protocol; supportive care for autonomic dysfunction; DVT prophylaxis given immobility.

Monitoring and failure: FVC at least every 4–6 hours in the acute phase. Failure to stabilise, or continued ascent of weakness, is the trigger for ICU transfer before ventilatory failure occurs, not after.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Neurology or ICU/HDU depending on respiratory trend. Document explicit escalation triggers (FVC threshold, bulbar signs, autonomic instability). SBAR handover includes the FVC trend, not just the last value.

Two common student errors
  • Ordering only a lumbar MRI for suspected cord compression when the level is unclear — thoracic and cervical lesions are missed.
  • Excluding Guillain–Barré syndrome because an early CSF or nerve-conduction study is normal — both may take days to become abnormal.

Check your understanding

Q1Why is serial FVC more useful than pulse oximetry for monitoring a patient with suspected GBS?

Answer. Oxygen saturation is often preserved until respiratory muscle failure is advanced, because the lungs themselves are normal — it is the muscles that fail. FVC directly tracks respiratory muscle strength and falls before oxygen levels drop, giving earlier warning of impending respiratory failure.

Q2A patient with suspected cord compression has an unclear neurological level. Why is a lumbar-spine-only MRI inadequate?

Answer. Clinical localisation of a spinal level can be inaccurate, and a compressive lesion may sit in the thoracic or cervical cord above where symptoms suggest. A lumbar-only scan will miss it, so whole-spine MRI is required when the level is uncertain.

Q3A patient with two days of ascending weakness has a normal nerve conduction study and normal CSF protein. Can Guillain–Barré syndrome be excluded?

Answer. No. Early CSF protein is normal in roughly a third of patients, and neurophysiology has higher diagnostic yield after the first week. A normal early test does not rule out GBS if the clinical pattern fits.

Q4What clinical feature makes cauda equina syndrome a surgical emergency rather than a routine referral?

Answer. Saddle sensory loss, new urinary retention, and bilateral leg weakness together indicate acute compression threatening permanent bladder, bowel and sexual function, and lower-limb function — this requires same-day imaging and surgical decompression, not routine outpatient referral.

Case scenario

A 58-year-old woman with known breast cancer presents with two days of worsening back pain and now finds her legs "heavy". She has not been incontinent. Examination shows mild bilateral leg weakness and reduced sensation below the umbilicus. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what is the next best step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Metastatic spinal cord compression is the most likely diagnosis, given known malignancy, back pain, bilateral leg weakness and a sensory level. The next best step is urgent whole-spine MRI (not lumbar-only, since the level is at the umbilicus/thoracic region) to confirm the site and extent of compression, with corticosteroids and urgent oncology/spinal surgery input arranged without waiting for the scan to be reported if compression is strongly suspected clinically.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 40-year-old man develops ascending weakness over three days after a respiratory infection. FVC is 18 mL/kg, having been 24 mL/kg six hours earlier. Oxygen saturation is 98% on air. What is the most appropriate next action?

  1. Reassure and repeat FVC in 24 hours
  2. Await nerve conduction study results before escalating
  3. Urgent critical care review for probable evolving respiratory failure
  4. Start supplemental oxygen only
  5. Discharge with outpatient neurology follow-up
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. FVC below approximately 20 mL/kg with a fall of more than 30% from baseline meets the threshold for urgent critical care review, regardless of normal oxygen saturation — saturation is preserved until late in neuromuscular respiratory failure. A, E: unsafe delay. D: oxygen does not address the underlying muscle weakness or impending need for ventilatory support. B: nerve conduction results take time and do not change the urgency of the current respiratory threat.

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old man has sudden-onset left arm and leg weakness with facial droop, one hour after symptom onset. Which localisation and most likely diagnosis best fits this pattern?

  1. Neuromuscular junction — myasthenia gravis
  2. Peripheral nerve — Guillain–Barré syndrome
  3. Spinal cord — transverse myelitis
  4. Cerebral hemisphere — acute stroke
  5. Muscle — inflammatory myopathy
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. Sudden unilateral weakness with facial involvement and a clear time of onset is the classic pattern of a cerebral hemisphere stroke. B: GBS is typically symmetrical and ascending over days, not sudden and unilateral. A: myasthenia causes fatigable, often bulbar/ocular weakness, not sudden hemiparesis. C: cord lesions typically cause bilateral signs below a level, not unilateral facial and limb weakness together. E: myopathy causes proximal, symmetrical, gradual weakness.

Presentation 3Seizure

A seizure is mainly a clinical diagnosis. A witness account is often more useful than any test, and a normal EEG does not rule out epilepsy. Six questions structure the whole assessment: is the patient still seizing; was this really an epileptic seizure; what type was it; was it provoked; is there an underlying cause; and does the patient need admission, medication or urgent follow-up?

Figure 2.3. Approach to suspected seizure: immediate stabilisation, witness history, clinical classification, initial investigations, neuroimaging, EEG, differential diagnosis, diagnostic synthesis and ongoing management.

Clinical vignette

Presentation. A 27-year-old man is having generalised tonic–clonic movements when the team arrives; a witness says it began six minutes ago and he has not stopped. Before reading on, decide: at what point does this become status epilepticus, and what is your first drug?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — and is this status epilepticus?

Note the start time, protect the patient from injury, support airway/breathing/circulation, give oxygen, get IV access, and check glucose, temperature and blood pressure straight away. A seizure lasting 5 minutes, or repeated seizures without waking up in between, counts as status epilepticus — this patient qualifies. Do not wait for test results before starting treatment.

First-line IV lorazepam if access is available (or buccal/IM midazolam, or rectal diazepam if not). If seizures continue, give an IV loading antiseizure medication per the local status protocol (levetiracetam, fosphenytoin/phenytoin or valproate — valproate avoided if pregnancy is possible). Refractory status needs early anaesthetic and intensive-care involvement.

Step 2. History and red flags — before, during, after

Interview the witness directly and ask what they saw, not "was it a seizure" — and ask specifically whether anyone recorded it on a phone. Before: activity/position, presyncopal symptoms, focal aura (epigastric rising sensation, déjà vu, abnormal smell/taste, unilateral jerking), missed antiseizure medication, sleep deprivation, alcohol binge or withdrawal. During: sudden or gradual onset, first movement and which side, head/eye deviation, symmetry, automatisms, colour, duration. After: duration of confusion, first thing remembered, prolonged sleepiness, transient focal weakness (Todd's paresis).

In plain terms: a previously well young man with a generalised convulsive seizure now persisting beyond five minutes, meeting the definition of status epilepticus.

Step 3. Discriminating examination and seizure versus mimics

Once stable: check temperature, SpO₂, HR, BP, serial GCS, signs of intoxication or withdrawal, injury survey (scalp, lateral tongue laceration, shoulder dislocation), and evidence of infection or systemic illness.

FeatureEpileptic seizureSyncopeDissociative attack
ProdromeFocal aura may occurNausea, warmth, sweating, dim visionVariable
ColourCyanosis may occurPallor typicalVariable
EyesOften openOften openTightly closed eyes may occur
RecoveryProlonged confusion/sleepinessUsually rapidOften relatively rapid

Urinary incontinence and brief jerks occur in both seizure and syncope and are not reliable ways to tell them apart on their own.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
Acute symptomatic (provoked)Hypoglycaemia, hyponatraemia, hypocalcaemia, uraemia, hepatic/hypoxic encephalopathy, alcohol withdrawal, drug intoxication, stroke/haemorrhage, meningitis/encephalitis, eclampsia
Epilepsy (unprovoked)Two unprovoked seizures ≥24 h apart, or one with ≥\~60% ten-year recurrence risk, or a recognised syndrome
Not epilepticSyncope; dissociative (functional) seizure-like attack
Must not miss
  • Status epilepticus — ≥5 minutes continuous, or recurrent without recovery
  • Non-convulsive status epilepticus — suspect when the patient fails to return to baseline, has subtle eye/facial/finger movements, or fluctuating responsiveness; needs urgent EEG
  • Reversible metabolic causes — always check glucose immediately
  • Eclampsia — any seizure in pregnancy or the puerperium

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideCapillary glucose, temperature, ECGChecks immediate reversible causes; ECG screens for a cardiac cause of collapse mistaken for seizure
LaboratoryU&E, calcium, magnesium, LFTs, toxicology where indicatedIdentifies an acute symptomatic cause needing correction rather than antiseizure medication
ImagingCT or MRI brainFever/mass concern, new focal deficit, status epilepticus without an obvious explanation, first seizure over a certain age
NeurophysiologyEEGSupports classification and detects non-convulsive status; a normal routine EEG does not exclude epilepsy — sleep-deprived, ambulatory or video-EEG raises the yield when suspicion remains
CSFLumbar punctureNot routine after every seizure — reserved for suspected infection, and image first if there is a focal deficit, papilloedema or reduced consciousness

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: established status epilepticus, cause not yet identified. Continue the six-question framework once seizures are controlled: was it provoked (send glucose, electrolytes, toxicology, consider imaging), and does he meet criteria for epilepsy going forward — this determines whether long-term antiseizure medication is indicated.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate (already begun in step 1): benzodiazepine first line, IV loading antiseizure medication if seizures continue, anaesthetic/ICU involvement for refractory status.

Definitive: treat any identified reversible cause (correct glucose or sodium, treat withdrawal, treat meningitis empirically if suspected). Decide on long-term antiseizure medication based on recurrence risk factors (epileptiform EEG, structural lesion, previous brain insult, abnormal exam, nocturnal seizure), not reflexively after a single event.

Monitoring and failure: continuous EEG if the patient does not return to baseline after apparent cessation of convulsions — treat unresolved altered consciousness as non-convulsive status until proven otherwise.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

ICU/HDU for status epilepticus. Safety advice for any first seizure includes stopping driving per local regulatory requirements, discussing recurrence risk, and arranging outpatient neurology follow-up with EEG and imaging rather than completing everything acutely if the patient is otherwise stable. SBAR handover states seizure duration, drugs given in order, and the current neurological trajectory.

Two common student errors
  • Assuming a seizure has ended once major limb movements stop, without checking that the patient is actually returning to baseline.
  • Labelling every convulsive-looking event epilepsy without a witness account of before, during and after.

Check your understanding

Q1At what point does a seizure become status epilepticus?

Answer. A generalised convulsive seizure lasting 5 minutes, or recurrent seizures without full recovery of consciousness between them, is treated as status epilepticus.

Q2What is the first-line drug for status epilepticus, and what are the alternatives if IV access is not available?

Answer. IV lorazepam if IV access is available. If not, buccal or intramuscular midazolam, or rectal diazepam, are alternatives.

Q3A patient has stopped convulsing but remains unresponsive 20 minutes later. What must you consider, and what test helps?

Answer. Non-convulsive status epilepticus must be considered whenever a patient fails to return to baseline after apparent cessation of convulsions. Urgent EEG is needed to diagnose it.

Q4Why is a witness account often more valuable than any single test in assessing a possible seizure?

Answer. Because seizure is largely a clinical diagnosis: details of what happened before, during and after (aura, evolution, colour change, duration, post-event confusion) distinguish epileptic seizure from syncope or a dissociative attack far more reliably than a single blood test or even EEG — a normal EEG does not exclude epilepsy.

Case scenario

A 19-year-old woman collapses at a bus stop after standing for a long time in the heat. Witnesses describe her going pale, then a brief few seconds of jerking of the limbs while unconscious, followed by rapid full recovery within a minute with no confusion. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what feature most helps distinguish it from an epileptic seizure?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Convulsive syncope is most likely. The pallor, prolonged standing/heat trigger, and — most importantly — the rapid, complete recovery without a period of post-event confusion favour syncope over epileptic seizure; brief myoclonic jerks can occur in syncope and are not on their own diagnostic of epilepsy. Epileptic seizures typically produce a period of post-ictal confusion or sleepiness.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 24-year-old man has a generalised tonic-clonic seizure witnessed to last 7 minutes and continuing on arrival of the ambulance crew. IV access is not yet obtained. What is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

  1. IV phenytoin as first-line
  2. Buccal or intramuscular midazolam
  3. No treatment; seizures usually self-terminate within 10 minutes
  4. Wait for IV access before giving any medication
  5. Oral levetiracetam
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. This is status epilepticus (>5 minutes); if IV access is unavailable, buccal/IM midazolam (or rectal diazepam) is the appropriate first-line route. D: unsafe delay — treatment should not wait for IV access. E: oral route is inappropriate and too slow in a seizing patient who cannot swallow safely. A: IV antiseizure loading medications are second-line, after a benzodiazepine, and require IV access. C: status epilepticus by definition has not self-terminated and requires active treatment.

MCQ 2. A 30-year-old man has his first seizure, fully witnessed, with clear return to baseline after 20 minutes. Glucose, electrolytes and CT head are normal. Routine EEG the next day is normal. What is the most appropriate interpretation?

  1. He must be started on lifelong antiseizure medication regardless of findings
  2. Epilepsy is excluded because the EEG is normal
  3. No further follow-up is needed since all tests are normal
  4. The normal EEG means this was definitely a dissociative attack
  5. A normal EEG does not exclude epilepsy; decisions on antiseizure medication depend on recurrence-risk factors, not the EEG alone
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. A normal routine EEG does not exclude epilepsy; the decision to start long-term antiseizure medication after a single seizure is based on recurrence-risk factors (epileptiform EEG, structural lesion, prior brain insult, abnormal exam, nocturnal seizure), not reflexively. B: incorrect — EEG sensitivity for epilepsy is limited, especially as a single routine trace. A: reflexive treatment after one seizure without risk factors is not indicated. D: a normal EEG does not distinguish epileptic from dissociative events. C: outpatient neurology follow-up with driving advice and safety-netting is still required.

Presentation 4Reduced consciousness

A falling level of consciousness is an emergency first and a diagnostic puzzle second. Whatever the cause, the first job is always the same: protect the airway, treat anything instantly reversible, and only then work out the differential. Delirium — a sudden problem with attention and thinking, not just "being confused" — sits at the milder end of the same spectrum, and its quiet, sleepy form is easy to miss.

Figure 2.4. Approach to reduced consciousness from immediate ABCDE priorities and collateral history through focused examination, early investigations, broad diagnostic pathways, diagnosis and ongoing care.

Clinical vignette

Presentation. A 76-year-old man on the surgical ward, three days post-hip fracture repair, is found unresponsive to voice but responsive to pain, with pinpoint pupils and a respiratory rate of 8. Before reading on, decide: what do you give before you order a single test?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Airway first — a lower conscious level puts the airway at risk. Check the conscious-level score, breathing rate and pattern, oxygen level, pulse, blood pressure, temperature and glucose right away — low blood sugar is instantly reversible and must never be missed. Pinpoint pupils with a slow breathing rate in a post-operative patient means opioid overdose until proven otherwise: give naloxone straight away while you keep assessing.

Step 2. Collateral history — not optional

The patient cannot give a history; the collateral history, drug chart and timeline become the history. Establish the baseline cognitive state, exactly what changed and when, every drug given in the preceding 24–48 hours (opioids, sedatives, anticholinergics started in hospital), bowel and bladder function, sleep, alcohol history, and any preceding trauma, seizure or infective symptoms.

In plain terms: an elderly post-operative man with acute reduction in conscious level, pinpoint pupils and hypoventilation, in the context of recent opioid administration.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Check serial GCS; pupil size, symmetry and reactivity (pinpoint suggests opioid or a pontine lesion; a fixed dilated pupil suggests uncal herniation); brainstem reflexes; focal limb signs; neck stiffness; signs of trauma; injection sites; fetor (ketotic, hepatic, uraemic); signs of sepsis. A structural lesion typically produces asymmetric or focal signs; a toxic-metabolic cause typically produces symmetric findings with preserved pupillary light reflexes (except with opioids and some toxins).

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
StructuralIntracranial haemorrhage, large infarct, mass lesion, hydrocephalus, herniation
Toxic / drugOpioids, benzodiazepines, alcohol, anticholinergics, sedative accumulation in renal/hepatic impairment
MetabolicHypoglycaemia, hyper/hyponatraemia, hypercalcaemia, uraemia, hepatic encephalopathy, hypercapnia, hypothyroidism/myxoedema, Addisonian crisis
InfectiveSepsis, meningitis, encephalitis, urinary or respiratory source in the elderly
Seizure-relatedPost-ictal state, non-convulsive status epilepticus
Hypoxic / vascularRespiratory failure, cardiac arrest, severe hypotension
Must not miss
  • Hypoglycaemia — check glucose before anything else; instantly reversible, and delay causes irreversible harm
  • Opioid or benzodiazepine toxicity — reversible with an antidote; do not wait for a toxicology screen to treat
  • Non-convulsive status epilepticus — failure to return to baseline after any seizure, or unexplained fluctuating responsiveness
  • Herniation — falling GCS with a new dilated pupil, Cushing's triad (hypertension, bradycardia, irregular respiration) — call for immediate senior/neurosurgical help

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideCapillary glucose, ABG, ECG, urinalysisImmediate reversible causes and screening for sepsis or metabolic derangement
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, calcium, LFTs, TFTs, ammonia if hepatic cause suspected, blood cultures, toxicology screen where indicatedCovers the metabolic/toxic/infective branches of the schema systematically rather than piecemeal
ImagingCT headNew focal deficit, trauma, anticoagulation, failure to improve after correcting an obvious cause, or diagnosis remains unclear
NeurophysiologyEEGSuspected non-convulsive status epilepticus, especially if the patient does not wake despite correction of an apparent cause

Routine CT is not necessary for every confused or drowsy inpatient — order it when the clinical picture, not habit, demands it.

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: opioid-induced respiratory depression and reduced consciousness in a post-operative patient. Alternative kept alive: a coexisting cause — check glucose and perform a full septic and metabolic screen even after a clear response to naloxone, because more than one precipitant commonly coexists, especially in the frail elderly (the vulnerable-brain-plus-precipitating-insult model).

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: naloxone titrated to effect (small incremental doses to reverse respiratory depression without precipitating acute severe pain or withdrawal); airway support; oxygen; treat any other reversible cause identified (dextrose for hypoglycaemia, flumazenil only with extreme caution and senior input for benzodiazepine toxicity given seizure risk).

Definitive: review and rationalise the drug chart; treat the underlying surgical pain with a lower-risk regimen; multicomponent supportive care — reorientation, lighting, hydration, mobilisation — once the acute threat is controlled.

Monitoring and failure: naloxone has a shorter half-life than many opioids — re-sedation is expected and requires repeat dosing or an infusion. Monitor respiratory rate and conscious level closely for several hours after apparent reversal, not just at the first response.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Enhanced observation level for several hours given the risk of re-sedation. Document escalation triggers (respiratory rate, GCS thresholds). Complete and communicate medication reconciliation. SBAR handover explicitly flags the risk of recurrence, not just the resolved episode.

Two common student errors
  • Treating reduced consciousness as a single diagnosis to be found, rather than checking glucose and giving naloxone/dextrose empirically while the workup proceeds in parallel.
  • Discharging attention once the patient rouses after naloxone, missing the re-sedation that follows as the antidote wears off before the opioid does.

Check your understanding

Q1What two things must be checked or given before any test result comes back in a patient with reduced consciousness?

Answer. Capillary glucose (and treat hypoglycaemia immediately if low) and, if pinpoint pupils and slow breathing suggest opioid toxicity, naloxone — both are instantly actionable and must not wait for laboratory confirmation.

Q2Why should a patient be monitored for several hours after apparently waking up following naloxone?

Answer. Naloxone has a shorter half-life than most opioids, so its effect can wear off before the opioid does, causing re-sedation. Ongoing monitoring, and repeat dosing or an infusion, may be needed.

Q3What pupillary and vital sign findings suggest brain herniation, and what should you do?

Answer. A falling GCS with a new fixed, dilated pupil, plus Cushing's triad (hypertension, bradycardia, irregular respiration), suggests herniation. Call for immediate senior and neurosurgical help.

Q4Why should you keep looking for other causes even after a patient responds well to naloxone?

Answer. More than one precipitant commonly coexists, particularly in frail elderly patients (the vulnerable-brain-plus-precipitating-insult model) — a good response to naloxone does not exclude, for example, coexisting hypoglycaemia or sepsis, so glucose and a septic/metabolic screen should still be completed.

Case scenario

An 82-year-old woman on the medical ward becomes drowsy and confused overnight. She has a urinary catheter, was started on a sedating antihistamine for itching two days ago, and has not opened her bowels in five days. She is afebrile, glucose is normal, and there is no focal neurological deficit. What is the most likely explanation for her presentation, and what is the most appropriate initial approach?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. This is most likely a multifactorial delirium (a milder point on the same spectrum as reduced consciousness), driven by the anticholinergic sedating antihistamine, constipation, and possibly an occult catheter-associated urinary infection. The initial approach is to identify and correct reversible precipitants: stop or review the sedating/anticholinergic drug, treat constipation, screen for infection (including urine), and provide supportive/reorientation measures — rather than assuming a single structural or "confusion of old age" cause.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 68-year-old man is found unresponsive with pinpoint pupils and a respiratory rate of 6 shortly after receiving IV morphine for postoperative pain. What is the most appropriate immediate action?

  1. Observation only, as opioid effects will wear off
  2. Naloxone, titrated to effect, while continuing assessment
  3. Arterial blood gas before any treatment
  4. CT head to exclude a structural cause
  5. Flumazenil
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Pinpoint pupils, hypoventilation and recent opioid administration make opioid toxicity the presumed diagnosis; naloxone should be given immediately, titrated to reverse respiratory depression without precipitating severe pain or withdrawal. C, D: both delay reversible treatment; tests should run in parallel, not before treatment. E: flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines, not opioids, and carries seizure risk. A: unsafe — respiratory rate of 6 requires immediate action, not passive observation.

MCQ 2. A patient's GCS falls from 14 to 9 over two hours after a head injury. The right pupil becomes fixed and dilated, blood pressure rises, and heart rate falls to 48/min. What is the most likely process, and what is the most appropriate immediate action?

  1. Vasovagal episode — lie the patient flat and observe
  2. Brain herniation — call for immediate senior/neurosurgical review
  3. Opioid toxicity — give naloxone
  4. Hypoglycaemia — give dextrose
  5. Non-convulsive status epilepticus — arrange EEG
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. A falling GCS with a new fixed, dilated pupil and Cushing's triad (hypertension, bradycardia, irregular respiration) after head injury indicates raised intracranial pressure with impending herniation — this needs immediate senior/neurosurgical escalation, not a stepwise workup. C, D: there is no history pointing to opioids or hypoglycaemia, and neither explains the pupil finding or Cushing's triad. E: EEG is not the immediate priority when herniation signs are present. A: this pattern is not consistent with a vasovagal event and delaying escalation risks fatal outcome.

Presentation 5Dizziness and vertigo

Patients use the word "dizziness" for at least four different feelings — true vertigo (a false sense of spinning), presyncope, unsteadiness, and vague light-headedness — and the first job is working out which one this is, because each has a different set of causes. Within true vertigo, the single most useful distinction is central versus peripheral, because one is usually harmless and the other can be a stroke.

Figure 2.5. Approach to dizziness and vertigo, separating presyncope, disequilibrium and true vertigo and using bedside features and HINTS findings to distinguish peripheral from central causes.

Clinical vignette

Presentation. A 61-year-old man with hypertension develops sudden, continuous, severe vertigo with vomiting, unsteady gait, and mild dysarthria, worse over the last two hours and not improving. Before reading on, decide: is this labyrinthitis, or does anything here already worry you?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Check airway, breathing, circulation, vital signs, glucose, and — specifically — whether the patient can walk. A patient who cannot walk unaided or sit up unsupported, with sudden vertigo, is a red flag on its own, whatever else you find, and should be treated as a possible stroke until proven otherwise.

Step 2. History and red flags

First clarify what the patient means: a true spinning or moving sensation (vertigo), a feeling of impending faint (presyncope), unsteadiness on the feet with a clear head (disequilibrium), or a vague non-specific light-headedness. Then establish the timing pattern, which does more diagnostic work than any single symptom:

PatternTypical cause
Seconds, triggered by head/position changeBenign paroxysmal positional vertigo (BPPV)
Single episode, hours to a day or more, continuous, often with hearing loss/tinnitusVestibular neuritis or labyrinthitis
Minutes to hours, recurrent, with hearing loss, tinnitus, aural fullnessMénière's disease
Sudden, continuous, with any additional neurological symptom or inability to walkPosterior circulation stroke or TIA until excluded

Ask specifically about diplopia, dysarthria, dysphagia, facial or limb weakness/numbness, new severe headache, and cardiovascular risk factors. In plain terms: a hypertensive man with acute, continuous, severe vertigo with gait ataxia and dysarthria — a brainstem or cerebellar pattern until proven otherwise.

Step 3. Discriminating examination — the HINTS battery

In acute continuous vertigo with nystagmus, the bedside HINTS examination outperforms early MRI for detecting posterior circulation stroke when performed by a trained examiner:

  • Head Impulse test — a normal result (no corrective saccade) in an acutely vertiginous patient is paradoxically reassuring for a central cause
  • Nystagmus — direction-changing or purely vertical/torsional nystagmus points to a central cause; unidirectional horizontal nystagmus fits a peripheral cause
  • Test of Skew — a vertical ocular misalignment on alternate cover testing points to a central cause

Add a full cranial nerve exam, limb and gait ataxia assessment (finger-nose, heel-shin, tandem gait), and hearing tests (Rinne/Weber) — new unilateral hearing loss favours a peripheral labyrinthine cause, but does not exclude a stroke in the AICA territory.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
Peripheral vestibularBPPV; vestibular neuritis/labyrinthitis; Ménière's disease
CentralPosterior circulation (cerebellar/brainstem) stroke or TIA; vertebral artery dissection; migraine with vertigo; demyelination; posterior fossa tumour
Cardiovascular (presyncope, not true vertigo)Arrhythmia; orthostatic hypotension; vasovagal
OtherDrug/toxin-induced; anxiety-related; multisensory disequilibrium of the elderly
Must not miss
  • Posterior circulation stroke/TIA — acute vertigo with gait ataxia, dysarthria, diplopia, or an abnormal HINTS central pattern
  • Vertebral artery dissection — neck pain, recent trauma or manipulation, younger patient
  • Cardiac arrhythmia presenting as "dizziness" — palpitations, exertional onset, syncope — a different presentation, revisited from the cardiorespiratory topic

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideFull HINTS exam, gait assessment, lying/standing BP, capillary glucose, ECGDiscriminates central from peripheral and screens for a cardiovascular cause of presyncope
LaboratoryFBC, glucose, U&E if indicated by historyRarely diagnostic alone but excludes metabolic mimics
ImagingMRI brain with diffusion-weighted imaging, not CTEarly posterior fossa infarcts are frequently missed on CT; MRI is required if central features are present — do not be falsely reassured by a normal CT
SpecialCT/MR angiographyWhen dissection is suspected

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: suspected posterior circulation stroke, based on the combination of acute continuous vertigo, gait ataxia and dysarthria — a central HINTS pattern would strengthen this further. Alternative kept alive: vestibular neuritis with an incidental mild dysarthria — but the inability to sit or stand unaided argues strongly against a purely peripheral cause and should not be explained away.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: treat as a stroke pathway — urgent MRI, stroke team involvement, standard stroke-unit monitoring and blood pressure management per local protocol pending imaging.

Definitive: if stroke is confirmed, manage per the acute stroke pathway including thrombolysis eligibility assessment within the appropriate time window. If vestibular neuritis is confirmed instead, use short-course vestibular sedatives only for the first 24–48 hours, then move to early mobilisation and vestibular rehabilitation exercises, since prolonged sedative use delays central compensation. For BPPV, the Epley (or equivalent) repositioning manoeuvre is both diagnostic and therapeutic.

Monitoring and failure: any new or progressive focal sign is the trigger to escalate immediately rather than wait for scheduled imaging.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit under stroke/acute medicine for any central pattern or red flag. For clear peripheral vestibular disease, safe discharge with reassurance, vestibular rehabilitation advice, and explicit safety-netting for new weakness, speech change or worsening gait ataxia. SBAR handover states the HINTS findings explicitly, not just "vertigo, likely peripheral".

Two common student errors
  • Labelling any spinning sensation "labyrinthitis" without testing gait — inability to stand unsupported is a red flag in its own right.
  • Relying on a normal CT head to exclude a cerebellar or brainstem stroke — CT is insensitive to early posterior fossa infarction; MRI is required when central features are present.

Check your understanding

Q1Why is it important to clarify exactly what a patient means by "dizziness" before going further?

Answer. "Dizziness" can mean true vertigo, presyncope, disequilibrium, or vague light-headedness, and each has a different set of likely causes and a different workup. Assuming vertigo when the patient actually means presyncope (for example) sends the assessment down the wrong path.

Q2In the HINTS exam, why is a normal head impulse test in an acutely vertiginous patient a worrying sign rather than a reassuring one?

Answer. A normal head impulse test (no corrective saccade) means the peripheral vestibular reflex is intact. In a patient who is acutely and severely vertiginous, an intact peripheral reflex suggests the problem lies centrally instead — so, paradoxically, "normal" here points toward a central (stroke) cause.

Q3Why is a normal CT head not sufficient to exclude a cerebellar or brainstem stroke in acute vertigo?

Answer. CT is insensitive to early posterior fossa infarcts. MRI with diffusion-weighted imaging is required if central features are present; a normal CT should not be used to reassure a patient with red-flag features.

Q4What single bedside finding, on its own, should make you treat acute vertigo as a possible stroke regardless of other findings?

Answer. Inability to walk unaided or sit up unsupported. This is a red flag in its own right, independent of any other clinical feature.

Case scenario

A 34-year-old woman describes recurrent episodes of vertigo lasting a few seconds each, brought on specifically by rolling over in bed or looking up. Between episodes she feels entirely well, with no hearing change and no neurological symptoms. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what is a bedside diagnostic and therapeutic manoeuvre?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (BPPV) is most likely, given brief, position-triggered episodes with no interictal symptoms and no hearing or neurological change. The Dix-Hallpike test can reproduce the vertigo and characteristic nystagmus to confirm the diagnosis, and the Epley (or equivalent) repositioning manoeuvre is both diagnostic and therapeutic.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 55-year-old man presents with sudden vertigo, vomiting, and inability to stand unsupported. HINTS testing shows a normal head impulse test, direction-changing nystagmus, and skew deviation. What is the most likely diagnosis and most appropriate next step?

  1. Posterior circulation stroke — urgent MRI brain and stroke team involvement
  2. Ménière's disease — refer to outpatient ENT
  3. Anxiety-related dizziness — reassure and discharge
  4. Vestibular neuritis — start vestibular sedatives and discharge
  5. BPPV — perform Epley manoeuvre
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. A central HINTS pattern (normal head impulse test, direction-changing nystagmus, skew deviation) plus inability to stand unsupported strongly suggests posterior circulation stroke; urgent MRI and stroke pathway involvement are required. D: vestibular neuritis produces a peripheral HINTS pattern, not this central one. E: BPPV is positional and brief, not sudden and continuous with these findings. B: Ménière's would not present acutely like this nor with a central HINTS pattern. C: unsafe — the clinical picture meets red-flag criteria for stroke.

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old patient recovering from confirmed vestibular neuritis asks about treatment. Which is the most appropriate advice?

  1. Continue vestibular sedatives long-term to control symptoms
  2. Urgent MRI is required before any treatment decision
  3. Use vestibular sedatives only for the first 24–48 hours, then mobilise early and start vestibular rehabilitation
  4. No treatment is available; symptoms will resolve regardless of activity
  5. Strict bed rest for two weeks
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. Short-course vestibular sedatives for the first 24–48 hours followed by early mobilisation and vestibular rehabilitation is correct; prolonged sedative use delays central compensation. A, E: both delay recovery by prolonging sedation or inactivity. D: early mobilisation and rehabilitation measurably improve and speed recovery. B: MRI is not required once the diagnosis is already confirmed and no central features are present.

Presentation 6Acute visual loss

Sudden visual loss is an eye emergency, and often a brain and blood-vessel emergency too, at the same time. Two questions structure everything else: is it painful or painless, and is it one eye or both? Sudden, painless loss of vision in one eye, in an older patient, is giant cell arteritis or a blocked retinal vessel until proven otherwise — and either way, you are racing the clock to prevent permanent damage.

Figure 2.6. Approach to acute visual loss, organised by painful versus painless and monocular versus binocular presentations, with sight-threatening red flags, targeted examination and urgent investigations.

Clinical vignette

Presentation. A 74-year-old woman describes sudden, complete, painless loss of vision in her right eye over a few seconds, an hour ago. She mentions a dull headache and jaw discomfort when chewing over the past week. Before reading on, decide: what is the single most time-critical action, and why can it not wait for an ophthalmology appointment?
