Step 1. Sick or not sick — and is this eye or brain about to be permanently lost?

This is not a resuscitation emergency in the usual sense, but it is a race against time for the eye: sudden, painless loss of vision in one eye, in an older patient with pain on chewing, is giant cell arteritis until ruled out. Vision lost from GCA is often permanent and can spread to the other eye within days if untreated. Do not wait for same-day test results before starting treatment.

Step 2. History and red flags

Establish: one eye or both (test each eye separately — patients often do not notice unilateral loss until the good eye is covered); painful or painless; sudden or progressive; complete or partial (altitudinal, central, peripheral); positive phenomena (flashes, floaters, a curtain descending) versus purely negative loss; associated headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, proximal muscle aching (GCA); associated eye pain with halos (angle-closure glaucoma); trauma; migraine history; risk factors for vascular disease and blood clots.

In plain terms: an elderly woman with sudden, painless, complete monocular visual loss with systemic features suggesting giant cell arteritis.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Check visual acuity in each eye separately; pupils, specifically testing for a relative afferent pupillary defect (RAPD) — present in optic nerve or extensive retinal disease, absent in a purely refractive or media problem; visual fields to confrontation; eye movements; the front of the eye for redness, corneal clarity and blood in the anterior chamber; and fundoscopy for retinal pallor with a cherry-red spot (central retinal artery occlusion), retinal haemorrhages ("blood and thunder" fundus in central retinal vein occlusion), a pale swollen disc (anterior ischaemic optic neuropathy), or a normal-looking fundus with a swollen disc appearing later (optic neuritis). Also feel the temporal artery for tenderness, thickening or reduced pulsation, and check eye pressure if angle-closure glaucoma is suspected.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
Vascular retinalCentral or branch retinal artery occlusion; central or branch retinal vein occlusion; vitreous haemorrhage
Optic nerveArteritic anterior ischaemic optic neuropathy (GCA); non-arteritic AION; optic neuritis; compressive optic neuropathy
Structural / retinalRetinal detachment; macular haemorrhage
Anterior segmentAcute angle-closure glaucoma; corneal disease
Neurological (often bilateral or homonymous)Occipital stroke; migraine with visual aura; raised intracranial pressure with papilloedema
Must not miss
  • Giant cell arteritis — jaw claudication, scalp tenderness, new headache over 50; start corticosteroids immediately on clinical suspicion, before biopsy confirmation
  • Central retinal artery occlusion — a stroke of the eye; time-critical, and, where local protocols allow, the patient should be assessed and treated on the same emergency stroke-style pathway, given the shared vascular risk and the need to exclude a carotid or cardioembolic source
  • Retinal detachment — flashes, floaters, a curtain or shadow — same-day ophthalmology
  • Acute angle-closure glaucoma — painful red eye, halos, hard globe, mid-dilated pupil — same-day ophthalmology, do not delay for a headache workup

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideVisual acuity each eye, RAPD, intraocular pressure, fundoscopy (dilated if possible)Localises to retina, optic nerve or anterior segment before any blood test is sent
LaboratoryESR, CRP, FBC and platelets when GCA is suspectedESR is often markedly raised in GCA, but a normal value does not exclude it — treat on clinical suspicion regardless
SpecialTemporal artery ultrasound and/or biopsyConfirms GCA but must not delay corticosteroid treatment
SpecialSame-day ophthalmology review with dilated fundus examRequired for any acute visual loss where the diagnosis is not immediately obvious and benign
SpecialMRI brain/orbits; carotid imaging; echocardiographyWhen a neurological or embolic cause (occipital stroke, CRAO with embolic source) is suspected

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: arteritic anterior ischaemic optic neuropathy secondary to giant cell arteritis. Alternative kept alive: non-arteritic AION or CRAO from an embolic source — both still need urgent vascular risk-factor assessment, but only GCA changes today's treatment to immediate high-dose steroid.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: high-dose corticosteroid (oral or IV per local protocol and severity) started on clinical suspicion of GCA, the same day, before ESR or biopsy results return. The guiding principle throughout this presentation is that visual loss already present is often permanent, and treatment protects the other eye and any remaining vision.

Definitive: temporal artery biopsy within about one to two weeks of starting steroids (it does not need to precede treatment); long-term steroid-sparing strategy and bone/gastric protection with rheumatology input. For CRAO, urgent same-day ophthalmology and assessment for a cardioembolic or carotid source, as for any arterial ischaemic event. For retinal detachment or angle-closure glaucoma, emergency ophthalmological/surgical management.

Monitoring and failure: new visual symptoms in the fellow eye, or systemic deterioration, is the trigger for immediate re-assessment and escalation of steroid dose or route.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Same-day ophthalmology and, where GCA is suspected, joint rheumatology input. Admit if there is visual threat to the fellow eye or significant systemic illness. Document the time of onset and the time steroids were started, since this is the detail that matters most on review. SBAR handover explicitly flags "steroid started for suspected GCA — protecting the other eye" so the next clinician does not stop it prematurely.

Two common student errors
  • Waiting for ESR or biopsy confirmation before starting steroids in suspected GCA — the treatment decision is clinical and urgent.
  • Treating "flashes and floaters" as reassuring rather than as a possible retinal detachment requiring same-day ophthalmology assessment.

Check your understanding

Q1What two questions structure the initial approach to any patient with sudden visual loss?

Answer. Is the loss painful or painless, and does it affect one eye or both? These two questions quickly narrow the differential and dictate urgency.

Q2Why must corticosteroids be started for suspected GCA before ESR or biopsy results are available?

Answer. Visual loss from GCA is often permanent once established, and untreated GCA can cause loss of vision in the second eye within days. Waiting for confirmatory tests risks irreversible bilateral blindness, so treatment is started on clinical suspicion.

Q3What fundoscopic finding is classic for central retinal artery occlusion, and why is CRAO managed like a stroke?

Answer. Retinal pallor with a cherry-red spot at the macula is classic for CRAO. It is managed as a stroke of the eye because it shares the same vascular risk factors and embolic mechanisms as cerebral stroke, and the same urgent search for a carotid or cardioembolic source is needed.

Q4A patient reports new flashes and floaters with a shadow creeping across their vision. Why is this not reassuring?

Answer. These are classic symptoms of retinal detachment, which requires same-day ophthalmology assessment; treating them as benign risks permanent visual loss if detachment is not repaired promptly.

Case scenario

A 45-year-old man presents with sudden, painful loss of vision in his left eye, with haloes around lights and a red eye. On examination the left pupil is mid-dilated and poorly reactive, and the globe feels firm to palpation. What is the most likely diagnosis and the most appropriate immediate step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Acute angle-closure glaucoma is most likely, given painful red eye, haloes, a mid-dilated poorly reactive pupil, and a firm globe. The most appropriate immediate step is same-day emergency ophthalmology referral for intraocular pressure measurement and urgent pressure-lowering treatment — this should not be delayed for a headache workup.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 70-year-old woman has sudden, painless, complete loss of vision in one eye. Fundoscopy shows retinal pallor with a cherry-red spot at the macula. What is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Optic neuritis
  2. Central retinal artery occlusion
  3. Vitreous haemorrhage
  4. Retinal detachment
  5. Central retinal vein occlusion
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. A cherry-red spot with retinal pallor is the classic finding in central retinal artery occlusion. E: CRVO classically shows a "blood and thunder" fundus with widespread haemorrhages, not a cherry-red spot. D: retinal detachment shows an elevated, folded retina, not a cherry-red spot. A: optic neuritis typically shows a swollen or normal-looking disc, not retinal pallor with a cherry-red spot. C: vitreous haemorrhage obscures the fundus view rather than showing this specific sign.

MCQ 2. A 76-year-old man has sudden painless visual loss in one eye, new headache, scalp tenderness and jaw claudication. ESR result will not be available for several hours. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

  1. Await ESR before treatment
  2. Arrange temporal artery biopsy and treat only if positive
  3. Start topical antiglaucoma drops
  4. Refer routinely to ophthalmology outpatients
  5. Start high-dose corticosteroids now, on clinical suspicion of giant cell arteritis
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. The clinical picture is classic for GCA-related visual loss; corticosteroids must start immediately on clinical suspicion to protect the remaining vision and the other eye, without waiting for ESR or biopsy. A, B: both delay treatment and risk permanent bilateral visual loss. D: routine referral is unsafe in a time-critical diagnosis. C: there are no features here of angle-closure glaucoma, and topical drops would not address GCA.

Block 3 · Week 4 of the module
Gastrointestinal presentations

Presentation 1Acute abdominal pain

Abdominal pain is a symptom, not a diagnosis. Before asking "what's causing this?", ask whether the patient is unstable, bleeding, losing blood supply to the gut, or peritonitic. The first job is spotting who needs resuscitation and urgent surgery, vascular treatment or gynaecology input.

Figure 3.1. Approach to acute abdominal pain from immediate red-flag assessment through focused history, examination, initial investigations, anatomical diagnostic pathways and urgency of disposition.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 74-year-old man presents with sudden severe central abdominal and back pain. He is pale and sweaty, pulse 118/min, BP 82/54 mmHg. His abdomen is mildly tender but not rigid. Before reading on, decide: does a soft abdomen reassure you here?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Look straight away for: low blood pressure; a fast heart rate or altered consciousness; pale, sweaty skin, cold hands and feet or a weak pulse; breathing difficulty; severe dehydration; major gut or abdominal bleeding; sepsis; a pulsatile abdominal mass; or pregnancy with pain, bleeding or collapse. This patient's combination — sudden central pain radiating to the back, low blood pressure and a fast heart rate, with a soft abdomen — is the classic, dangerous picture of a leaking or ruptured aortic aneurysm. A soft abdomen does not reassure you here.

Step 2. History and red flags

Use SOCRATES, but let site and migration, onset and character do the discriminating. Pain migrating from central to the right iliac fossa supports appendicitis. Pain radiating to the back suggests pancreatic, aortic or renal disease. Colicky pain that comes in waves suggests obstruction. Sudden severe pain from the very first second suggests perforation, rupture, torsion, pancreatitis or ischaemia. Establish which came first, pain or vomiting — pain preceding limited vomiting supports appendicitis more than vomiting preceding pain. Ask about a pregnancy possibility in any woman of childbearing age.

In plain terms: an elderly man with sudden severe central abdominal pain radiating to the back with hypotension and tachycardia — a ruptured or leaking aortic aneurysm until excluded.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Look for involuntary guarding, rigidity, rebound or percussion tenderness, and a patient who lies very still (peritonitis) versus one who writhes or paces (colic). Palpate specifically for a pulsatile, expansile mass. Examine hernial orifices in any suspected obstruction. Remember the chest, heart, aorta, urinary tract, pelvis and testes as sources of pain referred to or from the abdomen. Perform a pregnancy test whenever pregnancy is possible.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Think in mechanisms, not organs:

MechanismExamples and typical pattern
InflammationAppendicitis, diverticulitis, cholecystitis, pancreatitis, PID, pyelonephritis — gradual, initially diffuse, later localised, constant, worse with movement
Perforation / rupturePerforated peptic ulcer or diverticulum, ruptured ovarian cyst, ectopic pregnancy, ruptured AAA — abrupt, rapidly severe, generalised peritonitis or shock
ObstructionIntestinal obstruction, biliary colic, ureteric colic — colicky, waxing and waning; becoming constant signals complication
IschaemiaAcute mesenteric ischaemia — pain out of proportion to a deceptively soft abdomen; early lactate is often normal
Must not miss
  • Ruptured or leaking AAA — sudden central/back pain, hypotension, a soft abdomen does not exclude it
  • Mesenteric ischaemia — pain out of proportion to examination; a normal early lactate is falsely reassuring
  • Ectopic pregnancy — any woman of childbearing age with pain and a positive pregnancy test until proven otherwise
  • Perforated viscus — sudden severe pain with peritonism; a normal erect chest film does not exclude it

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideUrinalysis, pregnancy test, capillary glucose, ECG if referred cardiac pain possibleCheap tests that redirect the whole differential in minutes
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, glucose, CRP, lipase/amylase, group and save, lactate if shock or ischaemia suspectedA normal count or a small amylase rise does not exclude serious disease; lactate may be late in mesenteric ischaemia
ImagingErect chest X-ray for free air; bedside ultrasound for gallstones, free fluid, aortic diameterAnswers a defined question; a normal film never excludes perforation on its own
SpecialCT abdomen and pelvisThe most useful single investigation in the undifferentiated acute abdomen; do not let contrast-nephropathy concern delay an urgent diagnosis

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: ruptured/leaking abdominal aortic aneurysm. Alternative kept alive: acute pancreatitis with retroperitoneal haemorrhage — but the hypotension out of proportion to examination and the back radiation both favour the vascular diagnosis. This is a "no test before resuscitation" situation: bedside ultrasound for the aorta, if available immediately, should not delay a call for surgical/vascular help.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: resuscitation and diagnosis occur simultaneously — high-flow oxygen, two large-bore cannulae, permissive hypotension (avoid over-resuscitating a contained rupture), group and cross-match, urgent surgical/vascular team activation.

Definitive: emergency endovascular or open aneurysm repair per vascular surgical assessment; for other causes, treat per mechanism — antibiotics and source control for perforation/inflammation, decompression for obstruction, revascularisation for ischaemia.

Monitoring and failure: serial vital signs and repeated abdominal examination — a single examination is a snapshot of an evolving illness; escalate if pain localises, physiology worsens, or peritonism develops despite initial reassuring findings.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Theatre or interventional radiology directly from the emergency department for confirmed rupture; HDU/ICU-level monitoring throughout; SBAR handover states the working diagnosis, the resuscitation strategy (permissive hypotension), and exactly which team is already mobilised.

Two common student errors
  • Being reassured by a soft, non-rigid abdomen in a haemodynamically unstable patient with sudden central/back pain — rigidity is a late sign in vascular catastrophe.
  • Treating a single normal examination and normal initial bloods as sufficient to exclude serious pathology, rather than reassessing serially as an active diagnostic test.

Check your understanding

Q1Why does a soft, non-tender abdomen not rule out a ruptured or leaking abdominal aortic aneurysm?

Answer. A contained retroperitoneal rupture bleeds behind the peritoneum, so peritonism (rigidity, guarding) can be absent or only appear late. The diagnosis rests on the pattern of sudden central/back pain with shock, not on finding a rigid abdomen.

Q2Why can a normal early lactate be misleading in suspected acute mesenteric ischaemia?

Answer. Lactate only rises once ischaemia has been present long enough to overwhelm clearance, so an early sample can still be normal despite established bowel ischaemia. Pain out of proportion to examination findings is a more reliable early clue.

Q3What single investigation is generally most useful in an undifferentiated acute abdomen, and what should not be allowed to delay it?

Answer. CT of the abdomen and pelvis. Concern about contrast-induced nephropathy should not delay this scan when there is a genuine emergency needing a diagnosis.

Q4Give two "must not miss" causes of acute abdominal pain (other than ruptured AAA) and their key clue.

Answer. Any two of: ectopic pregnancy (positive pregnancy test in a woman of childbearing age with pain), perforated viscus (sudden severe pain with peritonism — a normal erect chest X-ray does not exclude it), or mesenteric ischaemia (pain out of proportion to examination).

Case scenario

A 34-year-old woman presents with sudden severe left iliac fossa pain that started ten minutes ago. She is pale, HR 122/min, BP 88/60 mmHg. Her last menstrual period was seven weeks ago, and a urinary pregnancy test is positive. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what is the immediate next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Ruptured ectopic pregnancy until proven otherwise. Start resuscitation (large-bore IV access, fluids, group and cross-match) and make an urgent gynaecology referral for likely emergency surgery — do not wait for further imaging before making a life-saving referral in a shocked patient with a positive pregnancy test and acute pelvic pain.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 70-year-old man has sudden severe abdominal pain radiating to the back. HR 110/min, BP 90/60 mmHg. His abdomen is soft with mild central tenderness. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate step?

  1. Start oral analgesia and observe for four hours before further action
  2. Reassure the patient because the abdomen is soft
  3. Activate urgent surgical/vascular review while resuscitating, arranging immediate bedside imaging only if it does not delay this
  4. Arrange an outpatient CT abdomen for next week
  5. Discharge with safety-net advice once pain settles with analgesia
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. This is the classic presentation of a leaking/ruptured AAA — sudden pain radiating to the back with shock. A soft abdomen does not exclude this (B is wrong and dangerous). Outpatient imaging (D) and observation (A, E) all risk fatal delay in an unstable patient with a vascular emergency; the priority is simultaneous resuscitation and urgent surgical/vascular activation (C).

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old man with atrial fibrillation and no anticoagulation presents with sudden, severe, diffuse abdominal pain. His abdomen is soft with only mild tenderness. Venous lactate is normal. Which statement is correct?

  1. Pain out of proportion to examination, in a patient with a source of embolism, should raise strong suspicion of acute mesenteric ischaemia despite a normal lactate
  2. CT imaging should be delayed until lactate rises
  3. A normal lactate excludes mesenteric ischaemia
  4. This presentation is typical of simple constipation and can be managed with laxatives
  5. A soft abdomen excludes a surgical emergency
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Acute mesenteric ischaemia classically causes pain out of proportion to examination, and lactate rises late, so neither a normal lactate (C) nor a soft abdomen (E) excludes it. AF is an embolic source. Constipation (D) does not explain sudden severe pain with an embolic risk factor. Waiting for lactate to rise (B) risks fatal delay — urgent CT angiography is needed instead.

Presentation 2Upper gastrointestinal bleeding

Resuscitating the patient, judging the risk, and working out the diagnosis all happen at the same time — but endoscopy only happens once resuscitation is adequate, never before. The first haemoglobin result can look deceptively normal, because the blood hasn't yet diluted down enough to show the true loss.

Figure 3.2. Approach to suspected upper gastrointestinal bleeding from ABCDE stabilisation and focused history through initial investigations, risk stratification, differentiation of variceal and non-variceal causes, endoscopy and definitive diagnosis.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 61-year-old man with known cirrhosis vomits a large volume of fresh blood. He is confused and smells of alcohol. HR 128, BP 86/52, RR 26. Before reading on, decide: what is your very first action, before any drug or scope?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Airway first: is he vomiting large volumes of blood, is he less conscious, does he have liver-related brain dysfunction — can he protect his own airway? Get anaesthetics/critical care involved early and protect the airway before endoscopy whenever there's massive vomiting of blood, severe agitation, reduced consciousness or liver-related brain dysfunction — all present or threatened here. Start continuous monitoring, put in two large IV lines, send bloods as you do it, and activate the major-haemorrhage protocol for ongoing severe bleeding.

Step 2. History and the source clues

Characterise the bleeding (fresh blood, coffee-ground, melaena, single or continuing episode) without over-weighting the patient's own volume estimate, which is notoriously inaccurate. Distinguish the likely lesion: peptic ulcer/erosive disease (previous ulcer, dyspepsia, NSAID/aspirin use, recent critical illness) versus variceal bleeding (known cirrhosis, portal hypertension, alcohol, previous banding) versus Mallory-Weiss tear (retching before haematemesis) versus malignancy (dysphagia, weight loss, early satiety). Review antiplatelet and anticoagulant use in detail — stopping antiplatelet treatment after a recent coronary stent can itself be dangerous and needs urgent joint cardiology/gastroenterology discussion, not automatic cessation.

In plain terms: a man with known cirrhosis and a large-volume haematemesis with shock and encephalopathy — presumed variceal bleeding until endoscopy proves otherwise.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Check circulatory signs: pallor, cool sweaty peripheries, tachycardia, hypotension, a low JVP, oliguria. Look for stigmata of chronic liver disease: jaundice, spider naevi, palmar erythema, ascites, splenomegaly, asterixis. On abdominal examination, marked tenderness or rigidity suggests a different emergency (perforation) and should prompt urgent surgical assessment — mild or absent tenderness is typical of uncomplicated GI bleeding. Digital rectal examination confirms melaena or fresh blood rather than relying on the patient's verbal description.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CauseCharacteristic clues
Peptic ulcer / erosive diseaseNSAID/aspirin use, dyspepsia, critical illness
Oesophageal / gastric varicesKnown cirrhosis, portal hypertension, alcohol
Mallory-Weiss tearRetching or vomiting before haematemesis
MalignancyDysphagia, weight loss, anorexia, previous UGI cancer
Oesophagitis / gastritisReflux history, NSAID use, alcohol
Must not miss
  • Variceal haemorrhage — treat empirically before endoscopy confirms it, given the mortality of delay
  • Posterior duodenal ulcer eroding the gastroduodenal artery — a cause of catastrophic, difficult-to-control bleeding
  • Airway compromise from massive haematemesis or encephalopathy — protect the airway before endoscopy, not during it

Step 5. Tiered investigation and risk scoring

TierTestJustification
BedsideVenous/arterial blood gas for rapid Hb and lactateFaster than a formal FBC while resuscitation continues
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, LFTs and albumin, PT/INR and APTT, group and antibody screen, cross-matchA disproportionately raised urea relative to creatinine supports an upper GI source; repeat Hb after resuscitation, not just once
Risk scoreGlasgow–Blatchford score pre-endoscopy; Rockall score for rebleeding/mortalityGlasgow–Blatchford estimates the need for transfusion or endoscopic treatment; a score must never override active bleeding, shock or clinical concern
DefinitiveUpper GI endoscopyConfirms the source, distinguishes variceal from non-variceal bleeding, grades rebleed risk (Forrest classification), and provides haemostasis — timed urgently for unstable bleeding, within \~12–24 h for most other admissions, and never before adequate resuscitation

Glasgow–Blatchford score (calculated before endoscopy)

VariablePoints
Urea 6.5–7.9 mmol/L2
Urea 8.0–9.9 mmol/L3
Urea 10.0–24.9 mmol/L4
Urea ≥25 mmol/L6
Male Hb 120–129 g/L1
Male Hb 100–119 g/L3
Male Hb <100 g/L6
Female Hb 100–119 g/L1
Female Hb <100 g/L6
Systolic BP 100–109 mmHg1
Systolic BP 90–99 mmHg2
Systolic BP <90 mmHg3
Pulse ≥100/min1
Melaena1
Syncope2
Hepatic disease2
Cardiac failure2

Interpretation: a score of 0 (some local pathways allow up to 1–2) suggests very low risk and possible outpatient management with urgent follow-up; higher scores predict a greater need for transfusion, endoscopic treatment, or both. Exact low-risk cut-offs vary slightly between local protocols, so always follow your institution's pathway — and never let a low score override active bleeding, shock or clinical concern.

Rockall score (predicts rebleeding and mortality)

ComponentHigher-risk features
Age (pre-endoscopy)Risk rises with increasing age, especially ≥60 and ≥80 years
Shock (pre-endoscopy)Hypotension (systolic BP <100 mmHg) scores higher than tachycardia alone
Comorbidity (pre-endoscopy)Renal failure, liver failure or metastatic malignancy score higher than cardiac failure/ischaemic heart disease
Endoscopic diagnosis (full score only)Upper GI malignancy scores highest; no lesion found or Mallory-Weiss tear scores lowest
Stigmata of recent haemorrhage (full score only)Active bleeding, an adherent clot or a visible/spurting vessel score highest; a clean ulcer base scores lowest

Interpretation: the pre-endoscopy score (age, shock, comorbidity) can be calculated immediately; the complete post-endoscopy score adds the endoscopic findings and more accurately predicts rebleeding and death. Higher scores should prompt closer monitoring and earlier senior/gastroenterology input.

Forrest classification of bleeding peptic ulcers

ClassEndoscopic appearanceClinical significance
IaSpurting arterial bleedingVery high risk; requires endoscopic therapy
IbOozing bleedingHigh risk; requires endoscopic therapy
IIaNon-bleeding visible vesselHigh risk; requires endoscopic therapy
IIbAdherent clotIntermediate/high risk; often treated
IIcFlat pigmented spotLow risk
IIIClean ulcer baseLow risk

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: variceal haemorrhage in decompensated cirrhosis. Glasgow–Blatchford score is very high given the shock, melaena/haematemesis and hepatic disease — this patient requires transfusion and urgent endoscopic intervention regardless of the exact score. A restrictive transfusion strategy applies once bleeding is controlled: consider packed red cells below 70–80 g/L, avoiding over-transfusion which can worsen portal pressure in variceal bleeding.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: airway protection as above; fluid/blood resuscitation targeting adequate but not excessive volume expansion (over-resuscitation raises portal pressure and rebleeding risk in varices); empirical vasoactive drug therapy and prophylactic antibiotics started before endoscopy when variceal bleeding is suspected, per local protocol.

Definitive: endoscopic band ligation for confirmed oesophageal varices, or endoscopic clipping, thermal treatment or injection for a bleeding ulcer with Forrest Ia/Ib/IIa stigmata. Balloon tamponade or a covered stent can bridge to further treatment if endoscopic control fails. Interventional radiology or surgery is used when endoscopic and drug treatment cannot control the bleeding.

Monitoring and failure: continuous vital signs, serial Hb, urine output; rebleeding or failure to stabilise despite first-line endoscopic therapy is the trigger for repeat endoscopy, radiological or surgical escalation, not repeated conservative attempts.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

HDU/ICU for any haemodynamically significant or variceal bleed; gastroenterology and, where relevant, hepatology involved before endoscopy, not after; SBAR handover states the working cause, the Glasgow–Blatchford score, resuscitation given, and the endoscopy plan and timing.

Two common student errors
  • Reassured by a normal first haemoglobin — haemodilution takes time, so an early normal Hb does not exclude a life-threatening bleed.
  • Automatically stopping antiplatelet therapy after a recent coronary stent without urgent cardiology discussion — abrupt cessation can precipitate stent thrombosis.

Check your understanding

Q1Why can the first haemoglobin be misleadingly normal in acute upper GI bleeding?

Answer. In acute haemorrhage, red cells and plasma are lost together, so the concentration (Hb) does not fall until fluid shifts and resuscitation cause haemodilution. A normal first Hb does not exclude a major bleed — repeat it after resuscitation.

Q2What should be done about a patient's antiplatelet therapy if they have a recent coronary stent and are now bleeding?

Answer. Do not automatically stop it. Abrupt cessation can precipitate stent thrombosis, so this needs an urgent joint cardiology/gastroenterology discussion rather than a reflex decision.

Q3Which endoscopic stigmata (Forrest classification) are considered high risk and require endoscopic therapy?

Answer. Forrest Ia (spurting vessel), Ib (oozing) and IIa (non-bleeding visible vessel). IIb (adherent clot) is intermediate/high risk and often treated; IIc and III are low risk.

Q4Why should transfusion be restrictive rather than liberal once a variceal bleed is controlled?

Answer. Over-transfusion raises portal pressure and increases the risk of rebleeding in variceal haemorrhage, so packed red cells are generally considered once Hb falls below roughly 70–80 g/L rather than transfusing to a higher target.

Case scenario

A 55-year-old man with no known liver disease takes ibuprofen regularly for back pain. He presents with two episodes of melaena and one episode of fresh haematemesis. HR 105/min, BP 100/70 mmHg. Hb 98 g/L, urea 9.5 mmol/L. What is his approximate Glasgow–Blatchford score contribution from these values, and what does the score tell you?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Urea 9.5 mmol/L scores 3; male Hb 98 g/L (<100) scores 6; pulse ≥100 scores 1; melaena scores 1 — already at least 11 points from these features alone, before adding systolic BP and any syncope/comorbidity points. This is a high-risk score indicating a strong likelihood of needing transfusion or endoscopic treatment, regardless of the exact total — he needs admission, resuscitation and urgent endoscopy, most likely for an NSAID-related peptic ulcer.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 61-year-old man with known cirrhosis has a large-volume haematemesis, is confused, and smells of alcohol. HR 128/min, BP 86/52 mmHg. What is the most appropriate immediate priority, before any drug or endoscopy?

  1. Arrange urgent endoscopy as the very first step
  2. Perform a full liver screen before resuscitation
  3. Give oral proton pump inhibitor before anything else
  4. Wait for a formal Glasgow–Blatchford score before acting
  5. Protect the airway and involve anaesthetics/critical care given reduced consciousness and massive haematemesis, while starting resuscitation
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. With reduced consciousness/encephalopathy and massive haematemesis, airway protection comes first — endoscopy (A) must follow resuscitation and airway safety, not precede it. Oral PPI (C) is irrelevant here and unsafe to give orally in this state. Waiting for a score (D) delays life-saving action; a full liver screen (B) is not urgent priority in an unstable bleeding patient.

MCQ 2. Endoscopy in a patient with a bleeding gastric ulcer shows a non-bleeding visible vessel. What Forrest class is this, and what is the appropriate action?

  1. Forrest Ia — requires emergency surgery only
  2. Forrest III — no treatment needed
  3. Forrest IIa — high risk, requires endoscopic therapy
  4. Forrest IIc — low risk, discharge is reasonable
  5. Forrest IIb — always requires surgery
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. A non-bleeding visible vessel is Forrest IIa, a high-risk stigma requiring endoscopic therapy. Forrest III (B) is a clean base (low risk) and IIc (D) a flat pigmented spot (low risk) — neither matches this description. Forrest Ia (A) is active spurting bleeding, not a visible vessel, and surgery is not the automatic first step for any of these — endoscopic therapy is (E is also wrong, as IIb is adherent clot, usually treated endoscopically, not always surgically).

Presentation 3Lower gastrointestinal bleeding

What decides the first steps is less the colour of the blood and more the patient's stability, whether the bleeding is still going, and their other illnesses. Painless, large-volume bleeding is usually diverticular or from abnormal vessels; cramping pain with bloody diarrhoea points to colitis.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 72-year-old man on apixaban for atrial fibrillation passes several large-volume, painless, maroon-coloured stools over two hours. HR 112, BP 96/60. Initial Hb 118 g/L. Before reading on, decide: does the near-normal haemoglobin reassure you?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Judge severity by how stable the patient is, not by the colour of the stool. A systolic blood pressure under 100, a fast heart rate, a drop in blood pressure on standing, fainting or confusion, cold hands and feet, or ongoing blood loss during your assessment — any of these means significant bleeding. Don't rely on the first haemoglobin result — it can stay normal for 24–72 hours before the blood dilutes down, exactly as in this patient, who has almost certainly lost a life-threatening amount despite an Hb of 118 g/L.

Step 2. History and the pattern that localises the lesion

DescriptionLikely implication
Bright red blood on wiping / coating stoolAnorectal or distal rectal source
Blood streaked through brown stoolRectosigmoid or anorectal source
Bright red blood, large volumeUsually colonic, but brisk upper-GI bleeding remains possible
Maroon stoolRight colon or small bowel
Black, tarry stoolUsually upper GI; occasionally small bowel/right colon

Painless, large-volume bleeding suggests diverticular bleeding or angioectasia; cramping pain with bloody diarrhoea suggests ischaemic, infectious or inflammatory colitis. Review anticoagulant and antiplatelet use in detail, and ask about recent colonoscopy or polypectomy.

In plain terms: an anticoagulated older man with acute, painless, large-volume maroon-coloured rectal bleeding and early haemodynamic compromise despite a near-normal haemoglobin.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Full haemodynamic assessment as for upper GI bleeding; abdominal examination for tenderness or a mass; digital rectal examination for stool character, masses or fissures; inspection for haemorrhoids. Absence of significant pain with large-volume bleeding argues against ischaemic colitis and supports a diverticular or vascular source.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CauseTypical pattern
Diverticular bleedingPainless, large-volume, often self-limiting but can recur
AngioectasiaPainless, often in older patients, may be recurrent and low-grade
Ischaemic colitisCramping pain with bloody diarrhoea, often in vascular-risk patients
Inflammatory bowel diseaseDiarrhoea, urgency, tenesmus, systemic symptoms
Colorectal neoplasiaAltered bowel habit, weight loss, progressive anaemia — consider even when haemorrhoids are also present
Anal fissure / haemorrhoidsBright red blood on wiping, often with pain (fissure) or painless (haemorrhoids)
Must not miss
  • Brisk upper GI bleeding presenting as haematochezia — consider upper endoscopy first if haemodynamically severe and no clear lower source
  • Colorectal cancer masquerading as "just haemorrhoids" — altered bowel habit or weight loss changes the workup
  • Anticoagulant-associated bleeding requiring reversal — individualise reversal against thrombotic risk; do not let correction unnecessarily delay definitive investigation

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideSerial vital signs, serial Hb (not a single value)Localises the trend, which matters more than any single number
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, PT/INR, group and antibody screen, cross-match if significantAnticoagulation status changes the reversal and investigation plan directly
Risk scoreA validated tool such as the Oakland scoreSupports the decision on urgent inpatient versus outpatient pathway
Imaging/endoscopyUrgent CT angiography if bleeding is active/severe; colonoscopy (after preparation, if feasible) for the stable patientCT angiography requires active bleeding to localise a source; colonoscopy needs bowel preparation and is difficult during uncontrolled bleeding — the two are complementary, not interchangeable

Oakland score

Variable includedDirection of effect
AgeOlder age adds points
SexIncluded as a variable
Previous lower GI bleeding admissionA prior admission adds points
Findings on digital rectal examinationBlood seen adds points
Heart rateHigher heart rate adds points
Systolic blood pressureLower systolic BP adds points
HaemoglobinLower haemoglobin adds points

Interpretation: a low total score supports safe outpatient management with planned investigation; a higher score supports admission. Use the validated online calculator for the exact score and the cut-off adopted by your local pathway — this table gives the variables, not a substitute for calculating the real score, and it never overrides clinical judgement or ongoing bleeding.

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: significant lower GI haemorrhage, likely diverticular, in the context of therapeutic anticoagulation. Alternative kept alive: a brisk upper GI source presenting as haematochezia, especially given the haemodynamic severity — upper endoscopy should be considered if no clear lower source is found or the presentation is severe. He is high-risk by any validated score given the haemodynamic compromise despite a near-normal Hb, the anticoagulation, and ongoing bleeding.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: two large-bore cannulae, crystalloid resuscitation, activate the major haemorrhage pathway if appropriate; individualised anticoagulant reversal balancing bleeding severity against thrombotic risk (this is a shared decision with haematology/cardiology, not an automatic full reversal).

Definitive: urgent CT angiography with catheter angiography and embolisation if a bleeding point is localised; colonoscopy with endoscopic haemostasis (clips, thermal treatment, injection) once the patient is stable enough for preparation; surgery reserved for uncontrolled bleeding when endoscopic and radiological options fail.

Monitoring and failure: serial Hb and haemodynamics; restart anticoagulation when clinically appropriate, since prolonged cessation increases thromboembolic risk — this decision is revisited daily, not deferred indefinitely.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit for monitoring, serial Hb and definitive investigation; gastroenterology and interventional radiology informed early given the anticoagulation and haemodynamic compromise; SBAR handover states the anticoagulant, last dose, reversal given (if any), and the investigation plan and timing.

Two common student errors
  • Being reassured by a near-normal initial haemoglobin in acute haemorrhage — haemodilution has not yet occurred.
  • Assuming all haematochezia is a lower GI source — brisk upper GI bleeding can present identically and changes the first investigation to upper endoscopy.

Check your understanding

Q1Why might a patient with major lower GI bleeding still have a near-normal haemoglobin?

Answer. Haemoglobin reflects concentration, not volume lost, and haemodilution can take 24–72 hours to develop. A near-normal Hb soon after acute bleeding does not exclude a life-threatening loss — judge severity by haemodynamics and trend, not a single Hb value.

Q2What does painless, large-volume maroon or bright red bleeding typically suggest, compared with cramping pain and bloody diarrhoea?

Answer. Painless, large-volume bleeding suggests a diverticular or vascular (angioectasia) source. Cramping pain with bloody diarrhoea suggests colitis — ischaemic, infectious or inflammatory.

Q3When should a brisk upper GI source be considered in a patient presenting with haematochezia?

Answer. When the bleeding is haemodynamically severe and no clear lower source is found — brisk upper GI bleeding can transit quickly and present as fresh or maroon rectal bleeding rather than melaena, so upper endoscopy should be considered first in this situation.

Q4How should anticoagulant reversal be approached in significant lower GI bleeding?

Answer. Individualise it — balance bleeding severity against thrombotic risk as a shared decision with haematology/cardiology, rather than reversing automatically; restart anticoagulation once clinically appropriate, since prolonged cessation raises thromboembolic risk.

Case scenario

An 80-year-old woman with no significant past history passes bright red blood mixed through loose stool for two days, with lower abdominal cramping. She has known peripheral vascular disease. HR 96/min, BP 118/76 mmHg. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what examination clue would support it?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Ischaemic colitis — cramping abdominal pain with bloody diarrhoea in a patient with vascular risk factors is the classic pattern, distinguishing it from the painless bleeding typical of diverticular disease or angioectasia. Look for abdominal tenderness over the affected segment (often left-sided) and assess for signs of more severe ischaemia such as peritonism.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 72-year-old man on apixaban passes several large-volume, painless, maroon stools over two hours. HR 112/min, BP 96/60 mmHg. Hb 118 g/L. What is the most appropriate interpretation of this haemoglobin result?

  1. It confirms the bleeding is minor
  2. It means the bleeding has already stopped
  3. It should reassure you that transfusion is not needed
  4. It excludes the need for further investigation
  5. It cannot be relied on to exclude major bleeding, because haemodilution has not yet occurred
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. The haemodynamic picture (tachycardia, borderline hypotension) shows significant bleeding despite a near-normal Hb — haemodilution takes time. Options A, C, B and D all wrongly treat a single early Hb as reassuring or definitive, which is the classic student error in acute GI bleeding.

MCQ 2. Which investigation is most appropriate for a haemodynamically unstable patient with active, large-volume lower GI bleeding and no clear diagnosis yet?

  1. Urgent CT angiography
  2. Elective flexible sigmoidoscopy after bowel preparation
  3. Barium enema
  4. Outpatient colonoscopy in two weeks
  5. Stool culture only
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. CT angiography can localise active bleeding in an unstable patient and does not require bowel preparation. Colonoscopy (D, B) needs preparation and is difficult during active severe bleeding. Barium enema (C) is not used in acute bleeding and can obscure later angiography/endoscopy. Stool culture (E) does not address active haemorrhage.

Presentation 4Vomiting

Vomiting is a coordinated reflex, not just "an upset stomach" — how it started, when it happens, and what's in it tell you more than how much there is. The task is to tell a harmless, self-limiting cause apart from vomiting that signals a blockage, a surgical abdomen, raised pressure in the brain, or a metabolic emergency.

Figure 3.4. Approach to vomiting from confirmation and severity assessment through red-flag recognition, targeted history and examination, focused investigations, broad differential diagnosis, reassessment and a final management plan.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 68-year-old man presents with three days of large-volume vomiting, now containing undigested food eaten the day before. He has central abdominal distension and has not passed flatus for 24 hours. Before reading on, decide: what does vomiting undigested food from yesterday tell you?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Assess hydration, electrolytes, whether the airway is protected (there's a risk of inhaling vomit, especially with reduced consciousness), and any signs of a surgical abdomen (guarding, rigidity). Prolonged vomiting causes low fluid volume, low potassium and a specific blood chemistry pattern — check glucose and electrolytes early, and treat significant dehydration with IV fluids rather than waiting for the patient to drink again.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask about timing relative to meals: vomiting straight after eating suggests gastric outlet or motility disease, while vomiting delayed by hours points the same way but further along the gut. Ask about the content. Bilious vomit excludes obstruction above the point where bile enters the gut. Faeculent vomit suggests a blockage low in the small bowel or colon. Undigested food from a previous meal suggests gastric stasis or outlet obstruction. Ask about associated symptoms: headache and neurological signs suggest raised pressure in the brain; vertigo suggests a vestibular cause; abdominal pain and distension suggest obstruction. Also ask whether pain or vomiting came first — vomiting before pain makes appendicitis less likely and a primary gut or metabolic cause more likely.

In plain terms: an older man with several days of vomiting undigested food, abdominal distension and absolute constipation — a mechanical bowel obstruction until excluded.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Check hydration status: skin turgor, mucous membranes, capillary refill, postural BP. Look for abdominal distension, visible peristalsis, and tinkling or absent bowel sounds. Check for surgical scars. Examine the hernial orifices specifically, because an incarcerated hernia is an easily missed obstructing lesion. Do a full neurological screen — fundoscopy for papilloedema, gait, cranial nerves — when a central cause is suspected.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
Mechanical obstructionAdhesions, hernia, malignancy, volvulus, gastric outlet obstruction (peptic stricture, malignancy)
Gastrointestinal, non-obstructiveGastroenteritis, gastroparesis, pancreatitis, cholecystitis, peptic ulcer disease
Central / neurologicalRaised intracranial pressure, migraine, vestibular disease, labyrinthitis
Metabolic / endocrineDKA, uraemia, hypercalcaemia, Addisonian crisis, pregnancy (hyperemesis)
Drug-inducedOpioids, chemotherapy, digoxin toxicity
Must not miss
  • Mechanical bowel obstruction — especially with a strangulated hernia or closed-loop obstruction, both surgical emergencies
  • Raised intracranial pressure — early-morning vomiting, headache worse lying flat, papilloedema
  • Diabetic ketoacidosis or another metabolic emergency — check glucose and a blood gas in any unexplained vomiting
  • Hyperemesis with dehydration in pregnancy — check a pregnancy test in any woman of childbearing age

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideCapillary glucose, urinalysis/ketones, pregnancy test, postural BPRapidly screens metabolic and pregnancy-related causes
LaboratoryU&E (hypokalaemia, hypochloraemic alkalosis), amylase/lipase, calcium, venous blood gasConfirms the electrolyte consequence of vomiting itself and screens pancreatic/metabolic causes
ImagingErect chest and supine abdominal X-ray, then CT abdomen/pelvisPlain films may show dilated loops and air-fluid levels; CT defines the transition point and cause of obstruction definitively
SpecialCT or MRI brainReserved for a clinical pattern suggesting a central cause — not a reflex test for undifferentiated vomiting

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: mechanical small bowel obstruction, likely due to adhesions or a mass lesion given his age. Alternative kept alive: gastric outlet obstruction — distinguished by the CT transition point and the character of vomiting (gastric outlet obstruction classically causes vomiting of undigested food without bile, whereas small bowel obstruction more distal to the ampulla is typically bilious). Signs that would change this to an emergency: fever, tachycardia, rising lactate or peritonism, suggesting strangulation.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: nil by mouth, nasogastric decompression, IV fluid and electrolyte replacement (correct hypokalaemia and the metabolic alkalosis before it worsens), antiemetics chosen by likely mechanism (avoid prokinetics such as metoclopramide if mechanical obstruction is suspected).

Definitive: surgical review for any suspected strangulation, closed-loop obstruction or failure of conservative management; treat the underlying cause once identified — for example endoscopic or surgical relief of gastric outlet obstruction, or specific management of the metabolic or central cause.

Monitoring and failure: nasogastric output, abdominal examination and vital signs at least every few hours; new fever, tachycardia, rising lactate or peritonism is the trigger for urgent surgical reassessment rather than continued conservative management.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit under surgery for suspected mechanical obstruction; document fluid balance and electrolyte trend explicitly; SBAR handover states the suspected transition point, the trend in nasogastric output, and the explicit trigger for escalation to theatre.

Two common student errors
  • Prescribing a prokinetic antiemetic (such as metoclopramide) before mechanical obstruction has been excluded — this can worsen a closed-loop obstruction.
  • Treating vomiting as a diagnosis in itself rather than working out whether the content and timing point to an obstructive, central or metabolic cause.

Check your understanding

Q1What does vomiting of undigested food eaten more than 12–24 hours earlier suggest?

Answer. Gastric stasis or gastric outlet obstruction — food is retained in the stomach rather than passing through normally.

Q2Why should prokinetic antiemetics such as metoclopramide be avoided if mechanical obstruction is suspected?

Answer. A prokinetic increases gut motility and pressure against a blockage, which can worsen a closed-loop obstruction and increase the risk of perforation.

Q3What electrolyte and acid-base pattern is typical after prolonged vomiting, and why?

Answer. Hypokalaemia and a hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis, because vomiting loses hydrogen and chloride ions (gastric acid) and potassium, and volume depletion drives further renal potassium loss.

Q4Give two features in the history that should raise concern for a central (neurological) cause of vomiting rather than a gut cause.

Answer. Any two of: early-morning vomiting, headache worse on lying flat, papilloedema on fundoscopy, or other neurological signs.

Case scenario

A 24-year-old woman who is 8 weeks pregnant presents with severe vomiting for a week, unable to tolerate oral fluids. She has lost 4% of her pre-pregnancy body weight. Urinalysis shows ketones 3+. What is the most likely diagnosis and the immediate management priority?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Hyperemesis gravidarum. The immediate priority is IV fluid and electrolyte replacement (she cannot tolerate oral intake and has ketonuria and weight loss indicating significant dehydration/starvation), alongside antiemetics safe in pregnancy; a pregnancy test/confirmation and exclusion of other causes of vomiting should also be part of the initial assessment.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 68-year-old man has three days of large-volume vomiting containing food eaten the previous day, abdominal distension, and no flatus for 24 hours. Which of the following is the most appropriate first-line antiemetic approach?

  1. No antiemetic until surgery has been arranged
  2. High-dose opioids to control associated pain and vomiting together
  3. Metoclopramide, since it works quickly
  4. Domperidone as a prokinetic to clear the stomach
  5. An antiemetic that is not prokinetic, while investigating for mechanical obstruction
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. This presentation (undigested food, distension, absolute constipation) suggests mechanical obstruction, so prokinetics such as metoclopramide (C) or domperidone (D) should be avoided as they can worsen a closed-loop obstruction. Withholding all antiemetics (A) is unnecessarily unkind and not required. Opioids (B) do not treat vomiting and can worsen ileus/constipation.

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old man on long-term opioids for chronic pain develops persistent vomiting, constipation and mild confusion. Blood tests show a corrected calcium of 3.2 mmol/L. What is the most likely explanation for his vomiting?

  1. Raised intracranial pressure
  2. Opioid side effect alone
  3. Hypercalcaemia
  4. Gastroenteritis
  5. Mallory-Weiss tear
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. A corrected calcium of 3.2 mmol/L is significantly elevated and explains vomiting, constipation and confusion together. While opioids (B) can cause vomiting and constipation, they do not explain the calcium result or confusion as well. Gastroenteritis (D) does not explain the biochemistry. Raised ICP (A) and Mallory-Weiss tear (E) are not supported by the given findings.

Presentation 5Diarrhoea

Assess the patient before chasing the exact cause. Whatever turns out to be causing severe diarrhoea, the most important first step is always the same: restore fluids and electrolytes.

Figure 3.5. Approach to diarrhoea: stabilisation, focused history, initial tests, classification by duration and phenotype, early management, differential diagnosis and specialist escalation.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 79-year-old woman in a nursing home develops profuse watery diarrhoea with some blood mixed in, ten days after completing a course of antibiotics for a urinary infection. She is mildly dehydrated with lower abdominal tenderness. Before reading on, decide: does the antibiotic history change your first test?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Record temperature, pulse, blood pressure, mental state, hydration and circulation, how often they're passing stool, and roughly how much fluid they've lost. Several features mean the patient needs urgent assessment: severe dehydration or inability to keep fluids down, altered mental state, low blood pressure or organ dysfunction, clearly bloody stool, a fever over 38.5°C, severe or localised pain, signs of peritonitis, six or more loose stools in 24 hours, frailty or living in a care home, a weakened immune system, and — relevant here — recent antibiotics or a hospital/care-facility stay. Start replacing fluids and electrolytes straight away: by mouth if tolerated, by drip if significantly depleted.

Step 2. History and red flags

Confirm this is true diarrhoea and not faecal incontinence, urgency without volume, or overflow around impaction — a rectal examination can prevent an extensive but misdirected workup. Classify by pattern: colonic (frequent, small-volume, blood/mucus, urgency, lower cramping) versus small-bowel (larger volume, less blood, central discomfort); inflammatory (blood, pus, fever, systemic toxicity, usually needs stool microbial testing) versus non-inflammatory (watery, vomiting-predominant, usually self-limiting). Ask specifically and by name about recent antibiotics, recent hospitalisation, travel, food exposures, sick contacts and immunocompromise.

In plain terms: a frail nursing-home resident with inflammatory diarrhoea ten days after antibiotic exposure — Clostridioides difficile until excluded.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Look for distress, fever and dehydration. Check for abdominal tenderness, distension and peritonism — severe or localised pain with peritonism raises concern for toxic megacolon or complicated colitis. Do a digital rectal examination for blood, a mass or impaction. Always do this before extensively investigating "diarrhoea" in an older or immobile patient, since overflow around impaction is easily missed.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
Acute infective, non-inflammatoryViral gastroenteritis, toxin-mediated food poisoning, non-invasive bacteria
Acute infective, inflammatoryInvasive bacteria (Salmonella, Shigella, Campylobacter, EHEC), C. difficile, parasitic (Entamoeba)
Non-infective inflammatoryNew or flaring inflammatory bowel disease, ischaemic colitis, microscopic colitis
Overflow / functionalFaecal impaction, IBS (a diagnosis of exclusion once alarm features are addressed)
Chronic (>4 weeks)Osmotic/secretory watery, malabsorptive/fatty, motility-related, drug-induced
Must not miss
  • Clostridioides difficile colitis, potentially fulminant — recent antibiotics or hospitalisation with bloody or profuse diarrhoea
  • Shiga-toxin-producing/enterohaemorrhagic E. coli — bloody diarrhoea with risk of haemolytic uraemic syndrome; avoid antimotility agents and consider withholding antibiotics pending testing
  • Toxic megacolon or complicated colitis — severe pain, distension, peritonism, systemic toxicity
  • Faecal impaction with overflow — easily missed without a rectal examination, especially in the immobile elderly

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideVital signs, hydration assessment, rectal examinationConfirms severity and excludes impaction before further testing
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, CRP, lactate if unwellQuantifies dehydration, inflammation and systemic compromise
StoolC. difficile testing specifically requested (not assumed as part of a generic panel), stool culture/PCR including Shiga-toxin detection when inflammatoryRecent antibiotic exposure is the specific trigger to request C. difficile testing by name — a generic "stool culture" request may not include it
ImagingAbdominal X-ray or CT if toxic megacolon or complicated colitis is suspectedAssesses colonic dilatation and complications in severe or deteriorating disease
EndoscopyFlexible sigmoidoscopy/colonoscopy with biopsyNot routine in acute diarrhoea — reserved for unexplained severe symptoms, negative stool workup with persistence, or suspected IBD

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: Clostridioides difficile infection. Severity is stratified by fever, white cell count, rising creatinine, albumin, lactate and evidence of colonic dilatation or peritonism — features of severe or fulminant disease change management from oral therapy alone to escalated treatment and surgical involvement.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: isolate/enteric precautions, fluid and electrolyte replacement, stop the precipitating antibiotic if possible, avoid antimotility agents in suspected inflammatory or toxin-mediated diarrhoea.

Definitive: first-line oral antimicrobial therapy per local C. difficile guideline (for example oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin per local protocol) for confirmed infection; escalate to specialist gastroenterology/surgical input for severe, fulminant or relapsing disease, including consideration of faecal microbiota transplantation for recurrence per local pathway.

Monitoring and failure: daily stool frequency, vital signs, white cell count, lactate and abdominal examination; worsening despite therapy, or signs of toxic megacolon, is the trigger for urgent surgical review.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit for isolation, fluid management and monitoring given her frailty and comorbidity; document severity markers explicitly; SBAR handover states the confirmed organism, severity markers, and isolation status.

Two common student errors
  • Ordering a generic stool culture without specifically requesting C. difficile testing in a patient with recent antibiotic exposure.
  • Skipping the rectal examination and missing faecal impaction with overflow as the true cause of "diarrhoea" in an immobile or elderly patient.

Check your understanding

Q1Why should a rectal examination be performed before extensively investigating "diarrhoea" in an elderly, immobile patient?

Answer. Overflow diarrhoea around faecal impaction can look identical to true diarrhoea, and a rectal examination can identify impaction quickly, preventing an unnecessary and misdirected workup.

Q2Why is a generic "stool culture" request not sufficient after recent antibiotic exposure?

Answer. A routine stool culture panel does not automatically include Clostridioides difficile testing — this must be requested specifically by name when recent antibiotic use or hospitalisation is present.

Q3Why should antimotility agents be avoided in suspected Shiga-toxin-producing/enterohaemorrhagic E. coli infection?

Answer. Antimotility agents (and possibly antibiotics, pending testing) may increase the risk of haemolytic uraemic syndrome by prolonging toxin exposure/absorption in the gut.

Q4What clinical and laboratory features suggest severe or fulminant C. difficile colitis requiring escalation beyond standard oral therapy?

Answer. Fever, a high white cell count, rising creatinine, low albumin, rising lactate, and evidence of colonic dilatation or peritonism on examination/imaging — these should trigger specialist gastroenterology/surgical involvement rather than oral therapy alone.

Case scenario

A 30-year-old man returns from a trip to South Asia with three days of profuse watery diarrhoea, mild cramping, and no blood. He is mildly dehydrated but haemodynamically stable and alert. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Oral rehydration therapy, since this is most consistent with acute non-inflammatory (likely viral or toxin-mediated/traveller's) diarrhoea without red-flag features. Routine stool testing and antibiotics are not automatically required in a stable patient with a non-inflammatory picture; safety-net advice and review if red flags (blood, high fever, severe pain, worsening dehydration) develop.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 79-year-old nursing-home resident develops watery diarrhoea with some blood, ten days after completing antibiotics for a UTI. Which single test should be specifically requested?

  1. Routine stool culture only
  2. Ova, cysts and parasites only
  3. Clostridioides difficile toxin/PCR testing, requested by name
  4. Faecal calprotectin
  5. Coeliac serology
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. Recent antibiotic exposure with new diarrhoea in a frail, institutionalised patient is the classic trigger for specifically requesting C. difficile testing, which is not automatically included in a routine culture (A). Faecal calprotectin (D) and coeliac serology (E) are for chronic/inflammatory bowel disease workup, not this acute presentation. Ova/parasites (B) are more relevant with travel or specific exposure history.

MCQ 2. A 6-year-old child develops bloody diarrhoea after eating undercooked beef. Which of the following management steps is most appropriate?

  1. Start loperamide immediately to reduce stool frequency
  2. Avoid antimotility agents and hold antibiotics pending stool testing, given the risk of Shiga-toxin-producing E. coli
  3. Start empirical antibiotics immediately
  4. Reassure and discharge with no follow-up
  5. Arrange elective colonoscopy the same day
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Bloody diarrhoea after undercooked beef raises concern for STEC/EHEC, where antimotility agents (A) and antibiotics (C) may increase the risk of haemolytic uraemic syndrome, so both should be withheld pending stool testing. Discharge with no follow-up (D) is unsafe given the HUS risk. Colonoscopy (E) is not an appropriate first step in acute infective bloody diarrhoea.

Presentation 6Jaundice

Don't start with a huge liver blood-test panel while missing acute liver failure, sepsis, or an infected, blocked bile duct. Four questions structure the whole case: is the patient acutely unwell; is the bilirubin conjugated or unconjugated; does the pattern look like liver-cell damage or blocked drainage; and are the bile ducts dilated?

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 58-year-old woman develops fever with rigors, right upper quadrant pain and jaundice over 24 hours. She is confused and hypotensive. Before reading on, decide: name the two eponymous symptom clusters at play here, and explain why the second one is worse news.

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Assess airway, breathing, circulation; temperature, heart rate, blood pressure, oxygen level; glucose; mental state and flapping tremor; signs of bleeding; hydration and urine output; signs of sepsis. This patient's fever, right upper abdomen pain and jaundice make up Charcot's triad, which defines acute cholangitis. Add low blood pressure and confusion and that's Reynolds' pentad — severe, pus-forming cholangitis, an emergency needing resuscitation, antibiotics and urgent drainage of the bile duct, usually by ERCP.

Step 2. History and red flags

Establish the tempo: sudden, fluctuating jaundice with pain and fever suggests cholangitis, while progressive painless jaundice suggests malignant obstruction or progressive hepatocellular disease. Ask about alcohol, drug and toxin exposure (including paracetamol), viral hepatitis risk factors, autoimmune disease, gallstone history and weight loss. Ask about pale stool and dark urine — both support conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia and an obstructive component — and about pruritus, which supports cholestasis. In a patient without known cirrhosis, actively screen for encephalopathy — subtle poor concentration, disturbed sleep or personality change may precede drowsiness and coma, and its presence alongside coagulopathy (INR ≥1.5) defines acute liver failure.

In plain terms: a woman with fever, right upper quadrant pain and jaundice progressing to hypotension and confusion — acute suppurative cholangitis (Reynolds' pentad) until proven otherwise.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Assess the depth of jaundice. Look for stigmata of chronic liver disease (spider naevi, palmar erythema, ascites, splenomegaly). Check for asterixis and higher mental function to screen for encephalopathy. Examine the abdomen: right upper quadrant tenderness with a positive Murphy's sign supports cholecystitis, while a palpable, non-tender gallbladder with painless jaundice (Courvoisier's sign) suggests malignant obstruction below the cystic duct — classically a pancreatic head tumour — rather than gallstone disease.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryBilirubin / urineCommon causes
Pre-hepaticUnconjugated; urine bilirubin absentHaemolysis, ineffective erythropoiesis, Gilbert syndrome
HepaticConjugated, unconjugated or mixed; urine bilirubin often presentViral hepatitis, drugs, alcohol, autoimmune hepatitis, cirrhosis, sepsis, congestion
Post-hepaticPredominantly conjugated; urine bilirubin presentCholedocholithiasis, pancreatic/biliary malignancy, strictures, chronic pancreatitis
Must not miss
  • Acute suppurative cholangitis — Reynolds' pentad; needs resuscitation, antibiotics and urgent ERCP drainage
  • Acute liver failure — coagulopathy (INR ≥1.5) plus encephalopathy without pre-existing cirrhosis; check a paracetamol level in every case of severe acute hepatocellular injury
  • Malignant biliary obstruction — painless progressive jaundice with a palpable gallbladder (Courvoisier's sign)

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideCapillary glucose, blood gas/lactate if unwellScreens for hypoglycaemia and severity in acute liver failure
LaboratoryFull liver biochemistry, INR/coagulation, FBC, U&E, paracetamol level, blood cultures if septic, viral hepatitis serology, autoimmune and metabolic liver screen as indicatedInterpret the pattern (hepatocellular versus cholestatic) before choosing further tests, not in parallel with everything at once
ImagingUltrasound first-lineRapid, non-invasive, assesses duct dilatation and gallstones — but a normal ultrasound does not exclude early, intermittent or partial obstruction
SpecialMRCP for detailed duct imaging; ERCP when therapeutic drainage is required; endoscopic ultrasound for distal duct/ampullary lesionsERCP is both diagnostic and therapeutic and should not be delayed once suppurative cholangitis is diagnosed

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: acute suppurative cholangitis (Reynolds' pentad) secondary to presumed choledocholithiasis. Alternative kept alive: a biliary stricture or malignant obstruction with superimposed infection — imaging after initial stabilisation clarifies this, but it does not change today's urgent priority of source control.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: sepsis-pathway resuscitation — oxygen, IV fluids, blood cultures before antibiotics, broad-spectrum antibiotics covering biliary organisms per local protocol, correct coagulopathy if intervention is imminent.

Definitive: urgent ERCP for biliary decompression and stone extraction, or percutaneous transhepatic drainage if ERCP is not immediately available or fails; for acute liver failure, transfer to a specialist liver unit. Check a paracetamol level in every case and give N-acetylcysteine for confirmed or probable paracetamol toxicity; some specialist protocols also use N-acetylcysteine in non-paracetamol acute liver failure, but this should follow the liver unit's own guidance, alongside intensive monitoring and transplant assessment.

Monitoring and failure: serial observations, lactate, liver biochemistry and coagulation; failure to improve after decompression, or progression of encephalopathy/coagulopathy, is the trigger for escalation to critical care and liver-unit discussion.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

HDU/ICU-level care pending and after ERCP; gastroenterology/endoscopy and, where relevant, hepatology and critical care informed immediately; SBAR handover explicitly states which triad/pentad criteria are met and the exact time antibiotics and drainage were achieved.

Two common student errors
  • Ordering a broad "liver screen" of every autoimmune and viral test simultaneously instead of first classifying the pattern as hepatocellular or cholestatic to direct testing.
  • Treating a normal ultrasound as excluding obstruction — early, intermittent or partial obstruction can all show a normal scan.

Check your understanding

Q1What are Charcot's triad and Reynolds' pentad, and why does Reynolds' pentad carry a worse prognosis?

Answer. Charcot's triad is fever, right upper quadrant pain and jaundice, and defines acute cholangitis. Reynolds' pentad adds hypotension and confusion, and defines severe, suppurative cholangitis — a more advanced, higher-mortality emergency needing urgent resuscitation, antibiotics and biliary drainage.

Q2What two features define acute liver failure in a patient without pre-existing cirrhosis?

Answer. Coagulopathy (typically INR ≥1.5) and hepatic encephalopathy, occurring together in a patient without known chronic liver disease.

Q3What does Courvoisier's sign suggest, and how reliable is it?

Answer. A palpable, non-tender gallbladder with painless jaundice suggests malignant obstruction below the cystic duct, classically a pancreatic head tumour, rather than gallstone disease. It is neither fully sensitive nor fully specific — its absence does not exclude malignancy.

Q4Why is albumin an unreliable marker of severity in acute liver injury, compared with INR?

Answer. Albumin has a long half-life, so it can remain normal even in severe acute liver injury. INR reflects short-lived clotting factors made by the liver and falls quickly with acute synthetic failure, making it a much more sensitive marker of acute severity.

Case scenario

A 32-year-old woman is brought in by her partner, who reports she took a large paracetamol overdose four days ago and did not seek help at the time. She is now drowsy but rousable, with subtle personality change noted by her partner. INR is 2.1. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what is the immediate priority?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Paracetamol-induced acute liver failure — a rising INR (≥1.5) with early encephalopathy (personality change, drowsiness) in a patient without pre-existing liver disease meets the definition. The immediate priority is N-acetylcysteine (paracetamol-specific treatment, given even late presentations may benefit), urgent transfer to/discussion with a specialist liver unit, and intensive monitoring with early transplant assessment.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 58-year-old woman has fever with rigors, right upper quadrant pain and jaundice over 24 hours, now with hypotension and confusion. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

  1. Delay antibiotics until MRCP confirms the diagnosis
  2. Immediate liver biopsy to confirm the diagnosis
  3. Reassurance, since jaundice with fever is usually self-limiting
  4. Oral antibiotics and outpatient ultrasound in one week
  5. Sepsis-pathway resuscitation, blood cultures before antibiotics, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics, and urgent ERCP for biliary drainage
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. Fever, RUQ pain and jaundice plus hypotension and confusion is Reynolds' pentad — suppurative cholangitis, an emergency needing resuscitation, cultures, prompt antibiotics and urgent biliary decompression (usually ERCP). Outpatient management (D) and reassurance (C) are dangerously inadequate. Liver biopsy (B) is not indicated or urgent here. Delaying antibiotics for imaging (A) risks deterioration and death.

MCQ 2. A 64-year-old man has painless, progressive jaundice, weight loss, and a palpable, non-tender gallbladder. Ultrasound shows a dilated common bile duct with no gallstones. What is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Acute cholecystitis
  2. Pancreatic head carcinoma causing malignant biliary obstruction
  3. Viral hepatitis
  4. Choledocholithiasis
  5. Gilbert syndrome
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Painless progressive jaundice, weight loss and a palpable non-tender gallbladder (Courvoisier's sign) with a dilated duct but no stones strongly suggests malignant obstruction, classically a pancreatic head tumour. Cholecystitis (A) is typically painful with a positive Murphy's sign. Choledocholithiasis (D) usually causes pain and does not classically produce a palpable non-tender gallbladder. Viral hepatitis (C) causes a hepatocellular, not obstructive, pattern. Gilbert syndrome (E) causes mild unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia without duct dilatation or a mass.

Presentation 7Ascites

Never assume new ascites is "just the known cirrhosis" without tapping the fluid to check. Infection, cancer and blood-vessel problems can all exist alongside chronic liver disease — and one fluid sample answers most of the urgent questions at once.

Figure 3.7a. Approach to ascites with emphasis on early diagnostic paracentesis, SAAG-based interpretation, recognition of spontaneous or secondary bacterial peritonitis and investigation of the underlying cause.

Figure 3.7b. Supplementary approach to acute abdominal distension, integrating urgent red flags, examination and initial investigations with obstructive, fluid, gaseous, inflammatory, vascular and other causes.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 55-year-old man with known alcohol-related cirrhosis and stable ascites is admitted with increasing abdominal girth, new fever and mild confusion. Before reading on, decide: what single bedside procedure must happen today, regardless of how "typical" this looks?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Check for fluid on the lungs, sepsis, confusion from liver failure, signs of the kidneys shutting down (rising creatinine, low urine output), and severe electrolyte problems. New fever or confusion in a patient with ascites is spontaneous bacterial peritonitis until ruled out — this changes the plan from "manage known cirrhosis" to "treat a probable abdominal infection today."

Step 2. History and red flags

Establish tempo (rapid accumulation raises malignant, infective or vascular causes over simple decompensation), associated symptoms (fever, abdominal pain, confusion, weight change, leg swelling), and background (known liver disease and its severity, cardiac disease, nephrotic-range proteinuria, malignancy risk). Ascites preceding marked leg oedema points to a hepatic or portal-hypertensive cause rather than a cardiac or renal one.

In plain terms: a man with known cirrhosis and stable ascites now presenting with increasing girth, fever and confusion — new decompensation with suspected spontaneous bacterial peritonitis.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Confirm ascites clinically — look for shifting dullness, and a fluid thrill if the ascites is tense. Look for stigmata of chronic liver disease. Check for asterixis and higher mental function. Check for abdominal tenderness: this is atypical for uncomplicated ascites and raises concern for spontaneous bacterial peritonitis or another intra-abdominal problem. Also examine for signs of right heart failure (raised JVP) or nephrotic syndrome (periorbital oedema, a history of frothy urine).

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Category (by SAAG)Examples
High SAAG (≥11 g/L) — portal hypertensiveCirrhosis, alcoholic hepatitis, cardiac ascites (heart failure, constrictive pericarditis), Budd-Chiari syndrome
Low SAAG (<11 g/L) — non-portal-hypertensivePeritoneal malignancy, tuberculous peritonitis, pancreatic ascites, nephrotic syndrome
Must not miss
  • Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis — ascitic neutrophil count ≥250 cells/mm³ confirms it and mandates prompt antibiotic treatment
  • Hepatorenal syndrome — rising creatinine in a patient with ascites and no other explanation, after excluding hypovolaemia and nephrotoxins
  • Malignant ascites — do not assume all ascites in known cirrhosis is uncomplicated portal-hypertensive disease
  • Budd-Chiari syndrome — ascites with hepatomegaly and abdominal pain, especially with a prothrombotic history

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideDiagnostic ascitic tap: cell count and differential, albumin, total protein, culture (bedside inoculation of blood-culture bottles improves yield)The single highest-yield test — a neutrophil count ≥250 cells/mm³ diagnoses spontaneous bacterial peritonitis immediately
