LaboratorySerum albumin (for the serum-ascites albumin gradient), FBC, U&E, LFTs, coagulationThe SAAG distinguishes portal-hypertensive from non-portal-hypertensive causes more reliably than the old exudate/transudate split
CytologyAscitic fluid cytology when malignancy is possibleConfirms peritoneal malignancy as the low-SAAG cause
ImagingAbdominal ultrasound ± Doppler of the hepatic and portal veinsConfirms ascites, assesses liver architecture, and screens for Budd-Chiari or portal vein thrombosis

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: spontaneous bacterial peritonitis complicating decompensated cirrhosis, pending the ascitic neutrophil count. Alternative kept alive: hepatic encephalopathy from another precipitant (GI bleed, constipation, a new sedative) with coincidental stable ascites — but the new fever specifically shifts the working diagnosis towards infection, and both must be actively sought rather than assumed to be a single cause.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: do the diagnostic tap before antibiotics, as long as this doesn't meaningfully delay treatment. Start empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics per the local spontaneous bacterial peritonitis protocol once the tap is sent. In confirmed SBP, give IV albumin on day 1 and day 3 to reduce the risk of hepatorenal syndrome, per standard practice. Treat any encephalopathy in parallel — identify and correct the precipitant, and give lactulose.

Definitive: tailor antibiotics to culture results; secondary prophylaxis with long-term antibiotics after a first episode of SBP per local protocol; address the underlying decompensation and consider diuretic optimisation once infection is controlled, not before.

Monitoring and failure: repeat ascitic tap at 48 hours if the response is inadequate — failure of the neutrophil count to fall by at least 25% suggests treatment failure or an alternative/secondary cause and should prompt reassessment for a surgical source.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit for treatment of SBP and monitoring of renal function; document the SAAG, neutrophil count and culture result explicitly in the notes; SBAR handover states the tap result, antibiotics started, and the albumin administration plan.

Two common student errors
  • Assuming any ascites in a patient with known cirrhosis is simple decompensation without a diagnostic tap — infection and malignancy are easily missed this way.
  • Withholding albumin in confirmed spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, which measurably increases the risk of hepatorenal syndrome and mortality.

Check your understanding

Q1What single bedside test should be performed in any patient with known cirrhosis and new ascites, fever or confusion, and why?

Answer. A diagnostic ascitic tap. New fever or confusion in a patient with ascites is spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) until it is ruled out, and the tap (cell count, differential, culture, albumin/protein) is the single test that answers most urgent questions.

Q2What ascitic neutrophil count confirms spontaneous bacterial peritonitis?

Answer. An ascitic neutrophil count of 250 cells/mm³ or more.

Q3What does the serum-ascites albumin gradient (SAAG) distinguish, and give one example from each category.

Answer. A high SAAG (≥11 g/L) indicates a portal-hypertensive cause, such as cirrhosis or cardiac ascites. A low SAAG (<11 g/L) indicates a non-portal-hypertensive cause, such as peritoneal malignancy or tuberculous peritonitis.

Q4Why is IV albumin given on day 1 and day 3 in confirmed spontaneous bacterial peritonitis?

Answer. It reduces the risk of hepatorenal syndrome, a serious complication of SBP, and has been shown to improve outcomes including survival — withholding it increases risk.

Case scenario

A 60-year-old woman with cirrhosis and ascites has a diagnostic tap showing an ascitic neutrophil count of 600 cells/mm³. Her creatinine is rising despite fluid resuscitation and no other cause is found. What complication should be suspected, and what is the appropriate step regarding albumin?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Confirmed spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (neutrophils ≥250/mm³) with evolving hepatorenal syndrome (rising creatinine without another explanation, after excluding hypovolaemia and nephrotoxins). IV albumin should be given on day 1 and day 3 of treatment as standard practice in confirmed SBP, since withholding it increases the risk of hepatorenal syndrome and death.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 55-year-old man with known cirrhosis and stable ascites develops new fever and mild confusion. What is the single most important immediate investigation?

  1. CT abdomen and pelvis
  2. Ascitic fluid cytology
  3. Abdominal ultrasound with Doppler
  4. Diagnostic ascitic tap with cell count, differential and culture
  5. Serum ascites albumin gradient calculation only
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. New fever or confusion in ascites means SBP until excluded, and the diagnostic tap with cell count/culture is the single highest-yield test. Ultrasound (C) and CT (A) do not diagnose SBP. SAAG (E) helps classify the cause of ascites but does not detect infection. Cytology (B) looks for malignancy, not infection.

MCQ 2. Ascitic fluid analysis in a patient with cirrhosis shows serum albumin 28 g/L and ascitic albumin 12 g/L (SAAG = 16 g/L), with a neutrophil count of 80 cells/mm³. What is the correct interpretation?

  1. Low SAAG, consistent with peritoneal malignancy
  2. High SAAG and confirmed SBP
  3. The SAAG cannot be interpreted without further testing
  4. High SAAG, consistent with portal hypertension, and no spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (neutrophils below the diagnostic threshold)
  5. Low SAAG and confirmed SBP
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. A SAAG of 16 g/L is ≥11 g/L, so it is high and consistent with portal hypertension (as expected in cirrhosis). The neutrophil count of 80 cells/mm³ is below the 250 cells/mm³ threshold for SBP, so infection is not confirmed on this sample. A is wrong (SAAG is high, not low); B and E wrongly diagnose SBP; C is wrong because the SAAG can be calculated and interpreted directly from the values given.

Block 4 · Week 5 of the module
Renal and electrolyte presentations

Presentation 1Reduced urine output and acute kidney injury

Low urine output is a sign, not a diagnosis. Before you order an autoimmune screen or decide it's tubular injury, first check the low output is real, treat anything immediately dangerous, and only then work out whether the problem sits before the kidney, in the kidney, or after it. The order is always: check it's real → stabilise → localise → investigate → escalate.

Figure 4.1. Approach to reduced urine output, including confirmation of oliguria or anuria, early resuscitation, exclusion of urinary obstruction, focused assessment, initial investigations and classification into pre-renal, intrinsic renal and post-renal causes.

Clinical vignette

A 76-year-old man is 8 hours after hip surgery. The nurse reports he has passed no urine for 3 hours through his catheter. BP 108/65, pulse 92/min. Before reading on, decide: what one bedside action must happen right now?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — and is the oliguria even real?

Sudden zero urine output in a catheterised patient is a blocked catheter until you prove otherwise. Check the tubing for kinks or loops, look for blood clots or sediment, and scan the bladder before assuming kidney failure. "The patient hasn't passed urine" can mean true anuria, retention, incontinence, poor record-keeping, or a blocked catheter — very different problems that need different first actions. Once you've verified it, run ABCDE: look for tachypnoea, hypoxaemia and pulmonary oedema; assess volume status (underfilled — dry mucosae, tachycardia, postural hypotension, cool peripheries, low JVP; or overfilled — raised JVP, oedema, crackles); get an urgent ECG, potassium, urea and creatinine, bicarbonate, glucose and a blood gas. Treat severe hyperkalaemia, acidosis, pulmonary oedema or shock immediately — don't wait for the full renal work-up.

Step 2. History and red flags

Establish the actual volume passed, the time period, and the patient's weight. For a 70 kg patient the oliguria threshold (0.5 mL/kg/hour) is about 35 mL/hour, so 20 mL/hour for several hours is still clinically important even though urine is being produced. Ask about other fluid losses (diarrhoea, vomiting, drains, bleeding), recent contrast exposure, nephrotoxic drugs (NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, aminoglycosides), urinary symptoms suggesting obstruction, and systemic features of glomerulonephritis or vasculitis (haematuria, rash, arthralgia, haemoptysis).

In plain terms: an elderly post-operative man with acute anuria through an indwelling catheter — treat this as a catheter or drainage problem until you've physically excluded it, with pre-renal hypoperfusion from surgical fluid shifts as the leading medical cause after that.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Assess volume status as above. Palpate for a distended bladder even in a catheterised patient — an empty bladder does not exclude obstruction, since the obstruction may be above the bladder. Examine the abdomen and flanks for a palpable kidney, bladder or aortic mass. Do a digital rectal or pelvic examination when prostatic or pelvic obstruction is possible. Check skin and joints for vasculitic or embolic signs.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
Pre-renalTrue volume loss (haemorrhage, GI losses, burns); internal sequestration (pancreatitis, peritonitis); reduced cardiac output; sepsis/vasodilatation; NSAID/ACEi-ARB-impaired autoregulation
Intrinsic renalAcute tubular injury (shock, sepsis, nephrotoxins, pigment from rhabdomyolysis/haemolysis); glomerular disease (rapidly progressive GN); interstitial nephritis; vascular (renal artery/vein occlusion, vasculitis)
Post-renalCatheter/urethral obstruction; bladder outlet obstruction; bilateral ureteric obstruction; obstruction of a solitary functioning kidney
Must not miss
  • Bilateral ureteric obstruction or obstruction of a solitary kidney — treat sudden anuria as an obstruction or vascular emergency until excluded
  • Obstructed, infected urinary system — a urological emergency needing urgent decompression
  • Severe hyperkalaemia, refractory acidosis or pulmonary oedema — these kill before the renal diagnosis is established
  • Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis — haematuria, red-cell casts, hypertension — time-critical for immunosuppression

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideBladder scan, catheter flush/patency check, fluid balance chart review, weightConfirms or excludes a mechanical/measurement problem before the full renal work-up begins
LaboratoryU&E, bicarbonate, potassium, FBC, CK, urinalysis and microscopy for castsMuddy brown granular casts support acute tubular injury; red-cell casts support glomerulonephritis
ImagingRenal tract ultrasoundExcludes obstruction — but a normal scan doesn't always exclude it (e.g. retroperitoneal fibrosis), so keep investigating if clinical suspicion stays high
SpecialRenal biopsyReserved for suspected glomerulonephritis, vasculitis or interstitial nephritis, where the diagnosis changes treatment urgently

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: pre-renal AKI from intraoperative and postoperative volume shifts, on top of a possible catheter drainage problem. Alternative kept alive: evolving acute tubular injury if hypoperfusion has been severe or prolonged — pre-renal AKI reverses quickly once perfusion is restored, but if hypoperfusion persists it progresses to ischaemic tubular injury. Stage using the KDIGO urine-output and creatinine criteria (stage 3: anuria ≥12 hours, or <0.3 mL/kg/hour for ≥24 hours, or creatinine ≥3× baseline) — this patient is already at or approaching stage 3 by urine output alone.

KDIGO AKI staging

StageSerum creatinineUrine output
11.5–1.9× baseline, or a rise of ≥26.5 µmol/L within 48 hours<0.5 mL/kg/hour for 6–12 hours
22.0–2.9× baseline<0.5 mL/kg/hour for ≥12 hours
3≥3.0× baseline, or a rise to ≥353.6 µmol/L, or starting renal replacement therapy<0.3 mL/kg/hour for ≥24 hours, or anuria for ≥12 hours

Interpretation: a patient is staged by whichever of creatinine or urine output gives the higher (worse) stage. Higher stage means higher risk of needing dialysis and of dying, and should prompt earlier nephrology review — it is not just a label for the notes.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: fix any catheter/obstructive problem first; give a cautious fluid challenge if the patient is genuinely underfilled, reassessing after each bolus; stop nephrotoxic drugs (NSAIDs, and hold ACE inhibitors/ARBs during the acute illness); treat hyperkalaemia, acidosis or pulmonary oedema immediately if present, whatever the underlying cause turns out to be.

Definitive: treat the mechanism you've identified — relieve obstruction (urology), give immunosuppression for confirmed glomerulonephritis/vasculitis (nephrology), and give source control and antibiotics for sepsis-related tubular injury. Loop diuretics can help control fluid overload once the patient is volume-replete, but they do not reverse tubular injury and must not delay dialysis when it's indicated.

Monitoring and failure: hourly urine output, daily weight, repeated U&E and bicarbonate, ECG monitoring if hyperkalaemia is significant. Indications for urgent renal replacement therapy (AEIOU): refractory Acidosis, refractory Electrolyte disturbance (hyperkalaemia), dialysable Intoxication, refractory Overload (pulmonary oedema), and severe symptomatic Uraemia (encephalopathy, pericarditis). Dialysis is started for the patient's physiological complications, never for an isolated creatinine number.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Involve nephrology early for stage 3 AKI, persistent oliguria despite initial measures, or any AEIOU feature. Involve urology for confirmed or suspected obstruction. Document the fluid-balance plan and the explicit trigger for escalation to critical care. An SBAR handover states the AKI stage, the presumed mechanism, and what has already been excluded (catheter problem, obstruction).

Two common student errors
  • Ordering an autoimmune and vasculitis screen before checking whether the catheter is simply blocked or kinked — verify before you investigate.
  • Assuming a normal renal tract ultrasound always excludes obstruction, missing causes such as retroperitoneal fibrosis or early partial obstruction.

Check your understanding

Q1In a catheterised patient with sudden anuria, what should you check before assuming kidney failure?

Answer. Check the catheter and drainage system first — look for kinks, loops, clots or displacement, and perform a bladder scan. Sudden anuria in a catheterised patient is a blocked catheter until proven otherwise.

Q2What is the KDIGO urine-output threshold that defines oliguria triggering an AKI diagnosis?

Answer. Urine output <0.5 mL/kg/hour for more than 6 consecutive hours (this is the stage 1 urine-output criterion).

Q3What do the letters AEIOU stand for in the indications for urgent renal replacement therapy?

Answer. Refractory Acidosis; refractory Electrolyte disturbance (usually hyperkalaemia); dialysable Intoxication; refractory Overload (pulmonary oedema); severe symptomatic Uraemia (encephalopathy, pericarditis).

Q4Why might a "normal" renal tract ultrasound not fully exclude obstruction?

Answer. Some causes of obstruction, such as retroperitoneal fibrosis or early partial obstruction, may not show clearly on ultrasound. If clinical suspicion remains high despite a normal scan, further imaging (CT/MRI) should be considered.

Case scenario

An 82-year-old woman with osteoarthritis, taking regular ibuprofen, develops vomiting and diarrhoea for three days. She is now oliguric with BP 95/60, dry mucous membranes and a creatinine of 250 µmol/L (baseline 80 µmol/L). What is the most likely mechanism of her AKI, and what is the first management step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Pre-renal AKI due to volume depletion from vomiting and diarrhoea, worsened by an NSAID impairing renal autoregulation. The first steps are to stop the NSAID and give cautious fluid resuscitation with reassessment, rather than proceeding straight to an autoimmune or renal-specific work-up.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 68-year-old man admitted with sepsis becomes oliguric. Urine microscopy shows muddy brown granular casts, and creatinine has risen from a baseline of 90 to 300 µmol/L over 36 hours. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Acute tubular injury
  2. Pre-renal AKI due to sepsis-induced hypoperfusion
  3. Acute interstitial nephritis
  4. Post-renal obstruction
  5. Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Muddy brown granular casts are the classic finding in acute tubular injury. Pre-renal AKI typically has a bland urine sediment without casts (B is wrong). Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis produces red-cell casts and haematuria, not granular casts (E is wrong). Post-renal obstruction is diagnosed by imaging, not urine microscopy (D is wrong). Interstitial nephritis classically shows white-cell casts and sterile pyuria, sometimes with eosinophiluria (C is wrong).

MCQ 2. A patient with AKI has a potassium of 6.8 mmol/L and ECG showing broad QRS complexes despite two rounds of IV calcium gluconate and insulin-dextrose. Urine output remains <0.3 mL/kg/hour. What is the next best step?

  1. Start a loop diuretic
  2. Start an oral potassium binder and reassess in 24 hours
  3. Repeat insulin-dextrose a third time
  4. Arrange urgent renal replacement therapy
  5. Repeat calcium gluconate only, with no other change
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. Refractory hyperkalaemia with ECG changes despite medical treatment, in a patient who is already oliguric, is an AEIOU indication for urgent dialysis — further medical measures should not delay it. Repeating insulin-dextrose (C) or calcium alone (E) treats symptoms already shown not to be working. An oral binder (B) is far too slow for this emergency. A loop diuretic (A) is unlikely to work in significant oliguric AKI and does not address the immediate arrhythmia risk.

Presentation 2Hyperkalaemia and electrolyte emergencies

A normal ECG does not rule out dangerous hyperkalaemia. Potassium level and ECG changes correlate poorly, and a life-threatening potassium level can exist with an almost-normal trace. Don't delay emergency treatment while you work through a tidy diagnostic checklist.

Figure 4.2. Systematic approach to electrolyte disturbance, covering sodium, potassium, calcium, magnesium and phosphate abnormalities, urgent red flags, investigations and integration of clinical clues.

Clinical vignette

A 68-year-old man with CKD on an ACE inhibitor presents unwell with vomiting. Potassium returns at 7.2 mmol/L. He is asymptomatic and his ECG shows only slightly peaked T waves. Before reading on, decide: does the mild ECG change reassure you enough to wait for a repeat sample before treating?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Look straight away for seizure, coma or severe confusion, an abnormal heart rhythm, syncope or arrest, breathing or muscle weakness, tetany or throat spasm, shock, fluid on the lungs, and severe kidney injury. Start ABCDE, get IV access, and put the patient on cardiac monitoring. Don't wait to confirm the potassium result when the level is very high, rising fast, or the ECG has changed — his mildly peaked T waves at a potassium of 7.2 are not reassuring; they mean treatment starts now, not after a repeat sample.

Step 2. History and red flags

Confirm the result is genuine: was the sample haemolysed, was there prolonged tourniquet use or fist-clenching, is there marked thrombocytosis or leucocytosis, was blood taken near an infusion? Don't wait for confirmation when the patient is symptomatic or the value is severe — repeat the sample in parallel with treatment, not instead of it. Ask about renal disease, potassium-raising drugs (ACE inhibitors, ARBs, spironolactone, NSAIDs, trimethoprim, heparin), diabetes and recent insulin omission, tissue injury (rhabdomyolysis, tumour lysis, massive haemolysis), and symptoms of adrenal insufficiency.

In plain terms: a man with CKD on renin-angiotensin blockade, presenting with severe hyperkalaemia and early ECG change in the context of an acute illness that is reducing his renal excretion further.

Step 3. Discriminating examination and the ECG sequence

Look for muscle weakness or reduced reflexes (severe hyperkalaemia can cause an ascending flaccid paralysis mimicking Guillain-Barré syndrome), signs of the underlying cause (volume status, signs of tissue injury), and get continuous cardiac monitoring. ECG changes typically progress as potassium rises: peaked T waves → PR prolongation and QRS widening → loss of P waves → a sine-wave pattern → ventricular fibrillation or asystole — but this progression is unreliable enough that the absolute potassium value and its trend matter as much as the ECG appearance.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

MechanismExamples
Reduced renal excretionAKI/CKD, hypoaldosteronism, type 4 RTA, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, spironolactone/amiloride/eplerenone, NSAIDs, trimethoprim, calcineurin inhibitors
Movement out of cellsInsulin deficiency/hyperglycaemia, metabolic acidosis, beta-blockade, digoxin toxicity, succinylcholine, tissue injury
Cell destructionRhabdomyolysis, tumour lysis, massive haemolysis, trauma
SpuriousHaemolysed sample, fist-clenching, marked thrombocytosis/leucocytosis
Must not miss
  • Hyperkalaemic cardiac arrest — can occur with minimal ECG warning; treat the number and the trend, not just the tracing
  • Tumour lysis or rhabdomyolysis — hyperkalaemia together with hyperphosphataemia, hypocalcaemia and AKI
  • Adrenal insufficiency — hyperkalaemia with hyponatraemia and metabolic acidosis
  • Severe hypocalcaemia, hypermagnesaemia or profound hypokalaemia — the other electrolyte emergencies that share this same "protect, shift, remove" logic

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideECG and continuous cardiac monitoring, repeat potassiumConfirms the result and tracks the response to treatment in real time
LaboratoryU&E, glucose, venous blood gas and bicarbonate, CK, calcium/phosphate/uric acid if tissue destruction is suspectedIdentifies the mechanism and screens for tumour lysis or rhabdomyolysis
SpecialCortisol, renin/aldosteroneReserved for suspected adrenal insufficiency or unexplained persistent hyperkalaemia — not first-line

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: severe hyperkalaemia due to acute-on-chronic kidney injury, precipitated by vomiting/dehydration in a patient on an ACE inhibitor. Alternative kept alive: superimposed adrenal insufficiency if hyponatraemia and acidosis are also present. Severity is defined by the potassium value, its trajectory, and the presence of ECG change or symptoms — not by any single cut-off in isolation.

Step 7. Management ladder — protect, shift, remove, dialyse

Immediate (protect the myocardium): IV calcium gluconate 10% 10 mL (or calcium chloride 10% 10 mL if central access is available, per local protocol) given over 5–10 minutes when ECG changes or severe toxicity are present. This does not lower potassium — it stabilises the myocardial membrane, its effect lasts roughly 30–60 minutes, and the dose can be repeated after about 15 minutes if ECG changes persist.

Shift potassium into cells: 10 units of soluble insulin with 25 g glucose IV over 15–30 minutes (with capillary glucose monitoring to catch delayed hypoglycaemia), plus nebulised salbutamol at a higher-than-standard dose (typically 10–20 mg, well above the usual bronchodilator dose). IV sodium bicarbonate may help when significant metabolic acidosis coexists, but it is an adjunct, not first-line.

Remove potassium: stop causative drugs (ACE inhibitor/ARB, spironolactone, NSAIDs); use an appropriate potassium-binding agent per local protocol; consider loop diuretics if there is adequate residual renal function and the patient is not volume-depleted.

Dialysis: for severe or refractory hyperkalaemia, particularly with significant kidney failure — don't delay renal replacement therapy while repeating medical measures that have already failed.

Monitoring and failure: repeat potassium and glucose at 1–2 hours; continuous cardiac monitoring until resolved; if potassium fails to fall, or ECG changes recur, that is the trigger for urgent dialysis rather than repeating insulin/glucose doses.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Monitored bed with continuous ECG until potassium is controlled and trending down; early nephrology involvement given the CKD background and the possibility of dialysis; SBAR handover states the peak potassium, the treatment already given and when it was given, and the plan for the repeat level.

Two common student errors
  • Being reassured by a near-normal ECG and delaying treatment for a severely elevated or rapidly rising potassium — the correlation between the two is poor.
  • Giving insulin and glucose once and not rechecking capillary glucose afterwards, missing delayed hypoglycaemia.

Check your understanding

Q1Why can a near-normal ECG not be relied on to exclude severe hyperkalaemia?

Answer. Serum potassium and ECG changes correlate poorly. Life-threatening hyperkalaemia can be present with an almost normal trace, so you treat the potassium value and its trend, not just the ECG appearance.

Q2What is the four-part framework for treating severe hyperkalaemia?

Answer. Protect the myocardium (IV calcium gluconate/chloride); shift potassium into cells (insulin-dextrose, nebulised salbutamol, ± bicarbonate if acidotic); remove potassium (stop causative drugs, potassium binder, loop diuretic if appropriate); dialyse for severe or refractory cases, especially with kidney failure.

Q3Does IV calcium gluconate lower the serum potassium?

Answer. No. It stabilises the myocardial cell membrane and reduces arrhythmia risk, but does not lower potassium. Its effect is temporary (roughly 30–60 minutes), so potassium-lowering treatment must follow.

Q4Give two reasons a high potassium result might be spurious, prompting a repeat sample in an asymptomatic patient with a normal ECG.

Answer. A haemolysed sample, prolonged tourniquet time or repeated fist-clenching during venepuncture, or marked thrombocytosis/leucocytosis (platelets/white cells releasing potassium after the sample is taken).

Case scenario

A 45-year-old woman with type 1 diabetes and diabetic nephropathy misses two insulin doses and presents drowsy with deep, sighing breathing. Potassium is 6.9 mmol/L, glucose 32 mmol/L, and her ECG shows peaked T waves with a widened QRS. What is the next best step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Treat immediately rather than waiting for a repeat sample — she is symptomatic with ECG changes. Give IV calcium gluconate first to protect the myocardium, then start insulin (as part of a fixed-rate insulin infusion for her DKA, which will also shift potassium into cells) with close capillary glucose and cardiac monitoring.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 72-year-old man on spironolactone for heart failure has a potassium of 6.6 mmol/L and peaked T waves on ECG. Which is the most appropriate immediate treatment to protect the heart from arrhythmia right now?

  1. Stop spironolactone and recheck potassium in 24 hours
  2. A loop diuretic
  3. An oral potassium binder
  4. IV calcium gluconate 10% 10 mL over 5–10 minutes
  5. Nebulised salbutamol alone
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. IV calcium acts within minutes to stabilise the myocardium, which is the immediate priority with ECG changes. An oral binder (C) works over hours to days, far too slow for immediate cardiac protection. Salbutamol (E) shifts potassium into cells but doesn't protect the membrane directly and is used alongside, not instead of, calcium. Simply stopping the drug (A) does nothing about the current dangerous level. A loop diuretic (B) is too slow and depends on adequate renal function and volume status.

MCQ 2. Regarding insulin-dextrose therapy for hyperkalaemia, which statement is correct?

  1. It replaces the need for calcium gluconate when ECG changes are present
  2. Capillary glucose must be monitored afterwards because of the risk of delayed hypoglycaemia
  3. It is contraindicated in diabetic patients
  4. It should be given without checking a baseline glucose
  5. It permanently removes potassium from the body
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Delayed hypoglycaemia is a well-recognised complication, so glucose must be rechecked after treatment. It shifts potassium into cells rather than removing it from the body (E is wrong). Baseline glucose should always be checked first (D is wrong). It does not replace calcium's membrane-stabilising effect where ECG changes are present — the two are used together (A is wrong). It can and often must be used in diabetic patients with appropriate glucose monitoring (C is wrong).

Presentation 3Acid–base disturbance

An abnormal blood gas is something to act on, not just a result to file away. Work through it in the same order every time: look at the pH, identify the primary problem, check whether the body's compensation makes sense for that problem, then calculate the anion gap — and ask what the result should tell you to do next.

Figure 4.3. Stepwise approach to acid–base disturbance: stabilisation, blood-gas interpretation, identification of the primary disorder, assessment of compensation, anion-gap analysis and cause-specific investigation.

Clinical vignette

A 34-year-old woman with type 1 diabetes presents with vomiting, abdominal pain and deep sighing respiration. Venous gas: pH 7.02, PaCO₂ 1.8 kPa, HCO₃⁻ 4 mmol/L. Glucose 28 mmol/L, ketones 5.8 mmol/L. Before reading on, decide: is the low PaCO₂ itself part of the problem, or part of the solution?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

A pH this low is an emergency: check airway, breathing, circulation, conscious level and hydration right away. Deep, sighing breathing is the body's own way of compensating for severe metabolic acidosis — the low PaCO₂ here is that compensation working correctly, not a separate breathing problem, and must never be treated by slowing her breathing down. Start fluid resuscitation and continuous monitoring while you confirm the cause.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask about the tempo of illness, vomiting or diarrhoea (loss of acid or bicarbonate), diuretic use, laxative abuse, alcohol and toxin ingestion (methanol, ethylene glycol, salicylate), diabetes and insulin omission, renal disease, and respiratory disease or opioid/sedative use that could point to a primary respiratory disturbance instead. Ask specifically what the patient was doing in the hours before symptoms began — a clear precipitant (missed insulin, a binge, a new drug) often points straight to the mechanism.

In plain terms: a young woman with type 1 diabetes and severe metabolic acidosis with an appropriate respiratory compensation, in keeping with diabetic ketoacidosis.

Step 3. The systematic interpretation sequence

Work through the gas in the same fixed order every time, exactly as taught for the breathless patient in Block 1: oxygenation for the FiO₂, then pH, then the primary disturbance, then whether compensation is appropriate.

StepWhat to check
1. pH<7.35 \= acidaemia; >7.45 \= alkalaemia
2. Primary disturbancePaCO₂ moves with a respiratory process; HCO₃⁻ moves with a metabolic process — identify which one explains the direction of the pH change
3. CompensationRespiratory compensation for a metabolic disturbance is fast (minutes to hours); renal compensation for a respiratory disturbance is slow (12–72 hours) — a "normalising" PaCO₂ in severe metabolic acidosis is a red flag for fatigue, not reassurance, exactly as taught for asthma in Block 1
4. Anion gap(Na⁺ + K⁺) − (Cl⁻ + HCO₃⁻); a raised gap points to an unmeasured acid (ketones, lactate, toxins, uraemia); a normal gap points to bicarbonate loss (GI or renal) or hyperchloraemic acidosis
5. Delta ratio / clinical contextChecks for a second, coexisting disturbance masked by the first — always ask "does the compensation fully explain what I see, or is something else going on too?"

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
High anion gap metabolic acidosisDiabetic ketoacidosis, lactic acidosis, uraemia, salicylate/methanol/ethylene glycol poisoning
Normal anion gap metabolic acidosisGI bicarbonate loss (diarrhoea), renal tubular acidosis, early renal failure, acetazolamide
Metabolic alkalosisVomiting/nasogastric loss (low urine chloride), diuretic use (variable/high urine chloride), Conn syndrome, Bartter/Gitelman syndrome
Respiratory acidosisCOPD/type II respiratory failure, sedative or opioid overdose, neuromuscular weakness
Respiratory alkalosisAnxiety/hyperventilation, pain, pregnancy, salicylate toxicity (early), pulmonary embolism, sepsis
Must not miss
  • A "normalising" PaCO₂ in severe metabolic acidosis — signals respiratory fatigue and impending arrest, exactly as a falling respiratory rate signals exhaustion in acute asthma
  • Salicylate poisoning — a mixed respiratory alkalosis and high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis is a specific, easily missed pattern
  • Severe refractory acidosis as a dialysis indication — the "A" in the AEIOU mnemonic from the AKI presentation
  • A second hidden disturbance — always check whether compensation fully explains the picture before closing the case

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestJustification
BedsideVenous or arterial blood gas, capillary glucose and ketonesConfirms the disturbance at the point of care and screens for DKA immediately
LaboratoryU&E, chloride, lactate, osmolality (with an osmolar gap if toxin ingestion is suspected)Calculates the anion gap and screens for an osmolar-gap toxin such as methanol or ethylene glycol
SpecialSalicylate/paracetamol levels, toxicology screenDirected by the history and the specific acid-base pattern found, not sent reflexively

Predict the anion gap and the expected compensation before calculating them — this turns the blood gas from a number to interpret into a hypothesis to test, exactly as taught for chest pain investigations in Block 1.

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: severe high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis due to diabetic ketoacidosis, with appropriate respiratory compensation. Alternative kept alive: concurrent lactic acidosis from dehydration/hypoperfusion, which would widen the gap further than ketones alone explain — check a lactate to be sure a second process isn't adding to the acidosis.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: treat the underlying cause, not the pH number in isolation — IV fluid resuscitation and a weight-based fixed-rate insulin infusion for DKA per local protocol. Don't give bicarbonate reflexively for metabolic acidosis: correcting the underlying process usually corrects the pH, and bicarbonate has specific narrow indications (severe refractory acidosis with haemodynamic compromise, hyperkalaemia, specific toxin ingestions) rather than general use.

Definitive: replace potassium once urine output and renal function are confirmed adequate, since insulin therapy drives potassium into cells and can unmask hypokalaemia; identify and treat the specific precipitant (infection, missed insulin, new illness).

Monitoring and failure: hourly capillary ketones/glucose and venous gas until resolution, watching specifically for the anion gap closing rather than fixating on glucose alone. If the acidosis fails to improve despite adequate treatment, re-examine for a second process or inadequate resuscitation — refractory severe acidosis is itself a dialysis indication.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

HDU-level monitoring for severe DKA given the risk of cerebral oedema (particularly in younger patients) and the need for hourly gas and electrolyte review; document the anion gap trend explicitly, not just the pH; SBAR handover states the primary and secondary disturbances identified, the treatment plan, and the parameter being followed to confirm resolution.

Two common student errors
  • Treating a "normalising" PaCO₂ in severe metabolic acidosis as reassuring, missing that it signals respiratory fatigue rather than recovery.
  • Giving bicarbonate reflexively to correct a low pH without treating the underlying cause or checking whether it is actually indicated.

Check your understanding

Q1A patient has severe metabolic acidosis and their PaCO₂ is falling. Is this reassuring?

Answer. No — a falling PaCO₂ is the expected respiratory compensation (hyperventilation blowing off CO₂). If the PaCO₂ starts rising back towards normal in this context, that signals respiratory fatigue and impending failure, not recovery.

Q2What does a raised anion gap indicate, and give three causes.

Answer. It indicates the presence of an unmeasured acid. Causes include ketones (DKA), lactate, toxins (methanol, ethylene glycol, salicylate), and uraemia.

Q3How quickly does respiratory compensation for a metabolic disturbance occur, compared with renal compensation for a respiratory disturbance?

Answer. Respiratory compensation is fast — minutes to hours. Renal (metabolic) compensation is slow — roughly 12–72 hours.

Q4Why should you predict the anion gap and expected compensation before calculating them from the numbers?

Answer. It turns the blood gas into a hypothesis to test. If the actual values don't match your prediction, that mismatch is the clue to a second, coexisting disturbance you would otherwise miss.

Case scenario

A 55-year-old man with COPD, on long-term diuretics, presents lethargic. ABG: pH 7.38, PaCO₂ 7.5 kPa (raised), HCO₃⁻ 32 mmol/L (raised). What does this gas most likely represent, and why can't the near-normal pH be taken as reassuring on its own?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. This is most consistent with chronic compensated respiratory acidosis (from COPD) with an additional element of metabolic alkalosis from long-term diuretic use — a mixed picture in which the two disturbances partly offset each other, producing a near-normal pH despite clearly abnormal PaCO₂ and HCO₃⁻. The pH alone cannot be reassuring here: you need the clinical context (COPD plus diuretics) to recognise that more than one process is at work, exactly the "second hidden disturbance" check from step 3.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 30-year-old man is found unconscious. ABG: pH 7.28, PaCO₂ 2.5 kPa, HCO₃⁻ 9 mmol/L, glucose normal, lactate 1.1 mmol/L. The anion gap is markedly raised, and the PaCO₂ is lower than full respiratory compensation for the acidosis would predict. Salicylate level is pending. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Chronic renal failure
  2. Salicylate poisoning
  3. Diabetic ketoacidosis
  4. Lactic acidosis from sepsis
  5. Opioid overdose
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Salicylate poisoning classically produces a mixed picture: a high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis together with a primary respiratory alkalosis from direct stimulation of the respiratory centre, so the PaCO₂ is lower than compensation alone would predict. DKA (C) would show a raised glucose and ketones. Lactic acidosis (D) would show a high lactate, which is normal here. Chronic renal failure (A) doesn't typically cause this severe an acute acidosis or a primary respiratory alkalosis. Opioid overdose (E) causes respiratory acidosis (reduced ventilation), the opposite of what's seen here.

MCQ 2. A patient with severe DKA has a pH of 7.0. Which statement about bicarbonate therapy is correct?

  1. It has no role in metabolic acidosis under any circumstances
  2. It should be given routinely to all patients with a pH below 7.1
  3. It should be given before starting insulin
  4. It is reserved for severe refractory acidosis with haemodynamic compromise or life-threatening hyperkalaemia, not given routinely
  5. It corrects the underlying process causing the acidosis
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. Bicarbonate has narrow, specific indications and is not routine treatment for a low pH. It does not correct the underlying process (E is wrong) — fluids and insulin do that in DKA. Giving it routinely by a pH cut-off alone (B) or before insulin (C) ignores that treating the cause is what actually resolves the acidosis. It does have a role in specific severe/refractory situations, so A is also wrong.

Block 5 · Week 6 of the module
Fever and sepsis presentations

Presentation 1Fever with no obvious source

Fever is a sign, not a diagnosis. The first question is never "what is causing the fever?" It is "is this patient sick right now, and do I need to treat before I find the source?" A normal-looking patient with a fever and no obvious focus is common. The job is to work through a fixed checklist so nothing dangerous is missed, without over-testing everyone.

Figure 5.1. Approach to fever from confirmation and ABCDE assessment through red-flag recognition, focused history, initial investigations, broad differential diagnosis, treatment and reassessment.

Clinical vignette

A 42-year-old woman has had a fever of 38.4°C for two days. She feels generally unwell but has no cough, no urinary symptoms, no rash, and no abdominal pain. Observations are otherwise normal. Before reading on, decide: what three things would you check before deciding this is "probably viral"?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Before hunting for a cause, check whether this patient is physiologically unwell — that matters far more than the actual temperature. Look at breathing rate, oxygen level, heart rate, blood pressure, and mental state. A patient can be seriously ill with a completely normal temperature, and a patient can look well with a high one. Use a simple rule: treat the physiology, not the thermometer.

High-yield pearl

Temperature on its own tells you almost nothing about danger. A young patient with a fever of 39°C who is talking normally and has normal observations is usually safe to work up calmly. An older patient with a completely normal temperature, new confusion and a fast breathing rate may be dying of sepsis. Vital signs beat the thermometer every time.

Step 2. History — hunt for the source systematically

Go through each body system in order so nothing is missed, rather than asking vague open questions: respiratory (cough, sputum, breathlessness), urinary (burning, frequency, back pain), abdominal (pain, diarrhoea, vomiting, jaundice), skin and joints (rash, redness, swelling, new joint pain), central nervous system (headache, neck stiffness, confusion), and anything device- or wound-related (recent surgery, IV lines, catheters). Also ask three questions that are easy to forget: any recent travel, any recent hospital or dental procedure, and any reason the immune system might be weaker than normal (diabetes, steroids, chemotherapy, HIV, spleen removed).

In plain terms: a well-looking woman with an unexplained fever and a completely negative systems review so far — keep going before calling this "probably viral".

Step 3. Examination — look in the places everyone forgets

Examination pearl

A full top-to-toe exam finds sources that a focused exam misses. Always check: the mouth and throat, the skin over the whole body (including the back and between skin folds), any IV line or catheter site, the perineum, pressure areas and the feet (a small foot ulcer can be the whole story in a diabetic patient), the spine (tenderness suggests discitis or an epidural abscess), the joints, and any prosthetic material (a hip replacement, a heart valve). Listen to the heart carefully for a new murmur — it may be the only sign of endocarditis.

Fatal mistake

Forgetting to examine lines, catheters, the perineum, pressure areas and the spine. These are exactly the places where a dangerous source hides in a patient who otherwise looks fine on a quick glance.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Think in four groups — infection, inflammation, neoplasm (cancer), and medication (the "I-I-N-M" pattern):

CategoryExamples
InfectionRespiratory, urinary, abdominal/biliary, skin/soft tissue, endocarditis, tuberculosis, dental/sinus source
Inflammatory / autoimmuneVasculitis, connective tissue disease, inflammatory bowel disease
NeoplasticLymphoma, leukaemia, renal cell carcinoma, other solid tumours
Medication / otherDrug fever, transfusion reaction, thrombosis/pulmonary embolism, thyroid storm
Must not miss
  • Endocarditis — always listen for a new murmur; ask about IV drug use and previous valve disease
  • Spinal infection (discitis / epidural abscess) — new back pain with fever is never "just a strain"
  • A hidden abscess — antibiotics alone will not fix it; it needs to be drained
  • An early presentation of sepsis — re-check vital signs at every review, not just once

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideFull set of vital signs, urine dipstick, capillary glucoseCheap, fast, and changes the plan immediately
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, LFTs, CRP, blood cultures before antibiotics if this doesn't delay treatmentA normal white cell count does not exclude serious infection — severe infection can suppress it, especially in older or immunosuppressed patients
ImagingChest X-ray; further imaging guided by symptomsDirected by whatever the history and exam point to — not ordered as a blanket "fever screen"
SpecialFurther tests only if no source is found after a proper structured look (this becomes "fever of unknown origin" if it persists beyond three weeks)Repeating the history and examination is often more useful than repeating blood tests

Step 6. Risk stratification

Working approach: if vital signs are normal and the full systematic history/exam finds nothing, most patients can be safely observed or discharged with safety-netting — most short-lived undifferentiated fevers are viral and self-limiting. But keep re-checking: a source that isn't there today may appear tomorrow, and physiology can change quickly.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: treat symptoms (fluids, paracetamol) while the workup proceeds; do not start blind antibiotics in a well patient with no identified source and normal observations.

Definitive: treat the specific cause once found, and always try to get a sample (blood, urine, wound swab) before the first antibiotic dose whenever this does not meaningfully delay treatment in an unstable patient.

Monitoring and failure: if the fever persists or the patient deteriorates, repeat the full history and examination rather than just repeating blood tests — most missed diagnoses are found on a second, more careful look, not a fancier test.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Safe discharge with clear return advice for the well patient with normal vital signs and no red flags; admission for observation if any uncertainty remains in a higher-risk patient (elderly, immunosuppressed, comorbid). Handover states exactly what has already been checked and excluded, so the next clinician doesn't have to start from zero.

Two common errors to avoid
  • Assuming a normal white cell count rules out serious infection — it does not, especially in the very young, the very old, and the immunosuppressed.
  • Labelling an unexplained fever "probably viral" without a full systematic history and top-to-toe examination first.

Check your understanding

Q1Why is temperature a poor stand-alone marker of how sick a febrile patient is?

Answer. The height of the temperature does not track severity. A young patient can have a high fever and be physiologically stable, while an elderly or immunosuppressed patient can have life-threatening infection with a normal or even low temperature. Vital signs (respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, heart rate, blood pressure, mental state) are the more reliable severity markers.

Q2Name four examination sites that are easy to miss in a "fever, no source" work-up.

Answer. Any four of: the mouth/throat, IV line or catheter sites, the perineum, pressure areas and feet (especially in diabetics), the spine (for discitis/epidural abscess), the joints, prosthetic material, and auscultation for a new cardiac murmur.

Q3Does a normal white cell count exclude serious infection?

Answer. No. Severe infection can suppress the white cell count, particularly in the very young, the very old and the immunosuppressed. A normal or low count should not be used to talk yourself out of a serious diagnosis.

Q4When does an unexplained fever formally become "fever of unknown origin" (FUO)?

Answer. Classically when fever above 38.3°C has persisted for at least three weeks despite an appropriate structured evaluation finding no cause.

Case scenario

A 68-year-old man on long-term prednisolone for polymyalgia rheumatica presents with five days of low-grade fever (37.9°C) and vague malaise. Observations are normal apart from a heart rate of 96. He has no localising symptoms on systems review. His only recent healthcare contact was a dental filling two weeks ago. What is the most important examination finding to actively seek before labelling this "probably viral"?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. A new cardiac murmur (suggesting infective endocarditis). The recent dental procedure plus long-term immunosuppression raises the pre-test probability of endocarditis, and it can present with nothing more than low-grade fever and malaise. Blood cultures and careful cardiac auscultation (with a low threshold for echocardiography) should be part of the work-up before this is dismissed as viral.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 25-year-old woman has a two-day history of fever (39.1°C) with mild myalgia. She is alert, observations are otherwise normal, and examination is unremarkable. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate management?

  1. Discharge with symptomatic treatment and safety-netting advice, no antibiotics
  2. Arrange CT abdomen/pelvis as a "fever screen"
  3. Label as fever of unknown origin and refer to infectious diseases
  4. Start empirical oral antibiotics pending blood culture results
  5. Admit for IV broad-spectrum antibiotics
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. A well, physiologically normal young patient with an undifferentiated short-lived fever most likely has a self-limiting viral illness; safety-netted discharge with symptomatic treatment is appropriate. E (antibiotics/admission) is unjustified with no evidence of sepsis or a bacterial source. B is a blanket, unguided "fever screen" rather than a directed investigation. D gives antibiotics with no identified bacterial source. C is wrong because FUO requires at least three weeks of fever after a structured evaluation — two days does not qualify.

MCQ 2. A 74-year-old woman with type 2 diabetes presents with three days of fever and malaise. Observations are normal. Systemic review and a focused examination are unremarkable. Which single next step is most likely to reveal the source before ordering further blood tests?

  1. Repeat the full blood count in 24 hours
  2. Empirical antibiotics for presumed urinary source
  3. Reassurance and discharge with no further action
  4. CT chest, abdomen and pelvis
  5. A full top-to-toe examination including feet, perineum, pressure areas, spine and any IV lines
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. In a diabetic patient, sources such as a foot ulcer, perineal infection or spinal infection are easily missed on a cursory exam and should be actively sought before more tests are ordered. A (repeating bloods) does not add new diagnostic information at this stage. B treats an unconfirmed source blindly. D is an unguided, low-yield "fever screen" before basic examination is complete. C is unsafe in a higher-risk (elderly, diabetic) patient with unexplained fever.

Presentation 2Sepsis

Sepsis is infection plus organ damage. It is not simply "a bad infection" or "a high fever" — it is life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by the body's own out-of-control response to infection. The fever itself is not the dangerous part; the organ dysfunction is.

Figure 5.2. Approach to suspected sepsis: early recognition, ABCDE assessment, source identification, organ-dysfunction assessment, the time-critical first-hour care bundle, response assessment, source control, escalation and ongoing management.

Clinical vignette

A 76-year-old woman has had two days of cough and is now more confused than usual. Temp 38.6°C, RR 28, SpO₂ 89% on air, HR 118, BP 86/52. New confusion. Right basal crackles. Before reading on, decide: what is the most dangerous feature in this vignette — and is it the fever?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — recognise sepsis fast

Suspect sepsis whenever infection is accompanied by: low blood pressure, a fast breathing rate, low oxygen, new confusion, reduced urine output, a high lactate, easy bruising/abnormal clotting, jaundice, or mottled/ashen skin. In this vignette the fever is almost the least worrying number — the hypotension, hypoxia, fast breathing and new confusion are what define this as sepsis, and possibly septic shock.

High-yield pearl

New confusion in an older patient with even a mild-looking chest infection may actually be sepsis. Do not write off confusion as "just their age" — it is one of the most reliable warning signs of sepsis in the elderly, who often do not mount a high fever at all.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask about the likely source (cough, urinary symptoms, abdominal pain, skin/wound, recent procedure) and about anything that weakens the immune system (recent chemotherapy, steroids, diabetes, no spleen). Ask specifically about recent antibiotics and known drug allergies, since this changes the choice of emergency antibiotic. Family or nursing-home staff may notice confusion or reduced eating before the patient can tell you anything themselves.

In plain terms: an elderly woman with a chest source of infection and multiple organs already struggling — a working diagnosis of sepsis, quite possibly septic shock, until proven otherwise.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Take a full set of vital signs and calculate a NEWS2 score; look specifically for mottled or ashen skin, a non-blanching rash, cool peripheries and slow capillary refill (signs that the circulation is failing at the tissue level, not just on the monitor); examine the likely source in detail (chest, abdomen, skin, urine, any device). A high NEWS2 score (7 or more, or 5–6 with any worrying additional feature) means this patient needs emergency-level monitoring and a senior review right now.

NEWS2 (National Early Warning Score 2)

Parameter3210123
Respiratory rate (/min)≤89–1112–2021–24≥25
Oxygen saturation (%)≤9192–9394–95≥96
Air or supplemental O₂on O₂air
Systolic BP (mmHg)≤9091–100101–110111–219≥220
Pulse (/min)≤4041–5051–9091–110111–130≥131
Consciousnessalertconfused/voice/pain/unresponsive
Temperature (°C)≤35.035.1–36.036.1–38.038.1–39.0≥39.1

Interpretation: Sum every parameter score. Total 0–4 \= low risk (routine monitoring); a 3 in any single parameter \= low-medium risk (increase monitoring frequency, inform nursing in charge); total 5–6 \= medium risk (urgent review by a clinician competent to assess acutely ill patients); total 7 or more \= high risk (emergency assessment, usually needs critical-care level input).

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

TermWhat it means
Suspected infectionClinical picture fits an infective source, with or without confirmed culture
SepsisSuspected/confirmed infection plus new organ dysfunction (hypotension, hypoxia, confusion, oliguria/AKI, coagulopathy, raised lactate, jaundice)
Septic shockSepsis with persistent low blood pressure needing vasopressors to keep the mean arterial pressure at or above 65 mmHg, plus a lactate above 2 mmol/L, despite adequate fluid resuscitation — the most dangerous category
Must not miss
  • Septic shock — persistent hypotension plus raised lactate despite fluids; needs critical care and vasopressors
  • A source needing surgical drainage — an abscess or infected collection will not respond to antibiotics alone
  • Sepsis with a normal or low temperature — very common in the elderly and the immunosuppressed; do not let a normal thermometer talk you out of the diagnosis

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideFull vital signs, NEWS2, capillary glucose, venous/arterial blood gas with lactateLactate is a key marker of tissue hypoperfusion and drives both diagnosis and the fluid-response plan
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, LFTs, coagulation, blood cultures (from two sites, plus every lumen of any central line)Take cultures before antibiotics whenever this does not meaningfully delay treatment — but never delay treatment in an unstable patient to get them
Source-directedUrine culture, sputum culture, wound swab, imaging as guided by the likely sourceTargets the specific site once suspected from history/exam
RepeatRepeat lactate after initial treatmentA falling lactate is one of the best signs that resuscitation is working

Step 6. Risk stratification

Working diagnosis: sepsis secondary to community-acquired pneumonia, high-risk given the hypotension, hypoxia, confusion and expected raised lactate. Risk is stratified by NEWS2 and by counting how many organ systems are already affected — more organs affected means a sicker patient regardless of the exact numbers.

Step 7. Management ladder — the sepsis bundle, in order

Immediate (first hour): call for senior help now; give oxygen to target saturation; take blood cultures and bloods including lactate without delaying treatment; give IV broad-spectrum antibiotics — high-risk patients need this within one hour, not "soon"; give an IV fluid bolus (a common local rule: 250–500 mL crystalloid over 15 minutes if systolic BP ≤90–100 mmHg or lactate >2 mmol/L), and reassess after every bolus — don't just run fluid in and walk away.

Definitive: identify and control the source — drain any abscess, remove any infected line, operate if a surgical source is found (ideally within about 12 hours once identified); narrow the antibiotic once culture results are back.

Monitoring and failure: reassess blood pressure, heart rate, capillary refill, mental state, urine output and lactate after every intervention; if the patient remains hypotensive after adequate fluid, this is septic shock and needs critical care and vasopressor support — do not keep giving fluid indefinitely, especially in heart failure or kidney disease where over-filling causes harm.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Critical care review for anyone shocked, hypoxic, oliguric or deteriorating despite initial treatment; accurate fluid balance chart and consider a urinary catheter to track output closely; SBAR handover states the suspected source, the time antibiotics were given, and the lactate trend.

Fatal mistakes with sepsis
  • Equating fever with sepsis. Fever without organ dysfunction is not sepsis — and sepsis can exist with a completely normal or low temperature.
  • Excluding sepsis because the temperature is normal. Elderly, immunosuppressed and chemotherapy patients often do not mount a fever even with severe infection.
  • Delaying antibiotics in a shocked patient to wait for test results. In instability, treatment comes first — cultures should not hold up the antibiotic.
  • Treating an abscess with antibiotics alone. Source control (drainage/surgery) is often the definitive treatment, not the antibiotic.

Check your understanding

Q1What is the difference between sepsis and septic shock?

Answer. Sepsis is suspected or confirmed infection plus new organ dysfunction. Septic shock is sepsis with persistent hypotension needing vasopressors to keep the mean arterial pressure at or above 65 mmHg, plus a lactate above 2 mmol/L, despite adequate fluid resuscitation — it is the more dangerous category.

Q2What NEWS2 score defines "high risk", and what should happen at that score?

Answer. A NEWS2 of 7 or more (or 5–6 with a concerning additional feature such as a score of 3 in one parameter, mottling, a non-blanching rash, or a rising lactate) is high risk and needs emergency-level monitoring, senior review, and consideration of critical-care involvement.

Q3Within what time frame should IV antibiotics be given to a high-risk septic patient, and can waiting for blood cultures delay this?

Answer. Within one hour of recognition. Blood cultures should be taken before antibiotics only if this does not meaningfully delay treatment — in an unstable patient, antibiotics must never be delayed to obtain "perfect" cultures.

Q4Why might antibiotics alone fail to treat a septic patient with an intra-abdominal abscess?

Answer. Because a collection of pus needs source control — drainage or surgery — to be cured. Antibiotics cannot adequately penetrate an undrained abscess, so the patient will not improve, and may deteriorate, until the source is controlled.

Case scenario

A 58-year-old man with a urinary catheter develops fever, rigors and confusion over a few hours. Observations: Temp 39.2°C, HR 128, BP 92/58, RR 26, SpO₂ 94% on air. Lactate is 3.1 mmol/L. After 30 mL/kg IV crystalloid over the first hour, his BP is 88/54 and lactate is 3.4 mmol/L. What is the next best step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Escalate for critical-care review and start vasopressor support — this is septic shock (persistent hypotension and a rising lactate despite adequate fluid resuscitation). Continuing to give fluid alone without vasopressor support and senior/critical-care input would not be appropriate at this point, and risks fluid overload without correcting the shock.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 70-year-old woman presents with a UTI. Observations: Temp 38.9°C, HR 104, BP 118/76, RR 18, alert and oriented. NEWS2 is 4. Which is the most appropriate immediate action?

  1. Discharge home with oral antibiotics and no follow-up safety-netting
  2. IV antibiotics within one hour, with routine monitoring and reassessment as per local sepsis pathway
  3. 500 mL crystalloid bolus regardless of blood pressure
  4. Immediate critical-care referral
  5. No antibiotics until urine culture results are available
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. She has suspected infection with a low NEWS2 and stable observations — she still needs prompt antibiotics per local pathway and safety-netted monitoring, but not an emergency escalation. D is excessive for a low-risk NEWS2. E wrongly delays treatment for culture results. C is unnecessary because she is haemodynamically stable with a normal blood pressure. A is unsafe without appropriate safety-netting and follow-up given the diagnosis of sepsis-risk infection.

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old man with sepsis secondary to cholangitis is given IV antibiotics and 30 mL/kg crystalloid. He remains hypotensive (BP 84/50) with a lactate of 4.2 mmol/L despite this. Imaging shows a dilated, obstructed common bile duct with likely pus. What is the most important next step in his definitive management?

  1. Urgent biliary drainage (source control) alongside vasopressor support
  2. Repeat blood cultures before any further action
  3. Continue fluids alone and reassess in 6 hours
  4. Switch to a different empirical antibiotic
  5. Discharge planning once the antibiotic course is confirmed
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. An obstructed, infected biliary system is a source that needs drainage — antibiotics and fluids alone will not resolve it, and he is now in septic shock, which also needs vasopressor support and critical-care involvement. B adds no immediate value and should not delay source control. D is not indicated without evidence the current antibiotic is wrong. C ignores both the persistent shock and the drainable source. E is inappropriate given his current instability.

Presentation 3Fever in the returning traveller

Any feverish patient who has travelled recently needs one extra, very specific question asked before anything else: where exactly did you go, and could this be malaria? Malaria is the single most common cause of fever needing hospital admission after travel to a tropical area, and it can kill within days if missed.

Clinical vignette

A 31-year-old man returned from Ghana eight days ago. He has fever, headache, muscle aches and vomiting. He took malaria tablets but admits he missed several doses. Examination shows no obvious source. Before reading on, decide: does taking malaria tablets, even imperfectly, rule malaria out?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Check vital signs, conscious level and hydration first, as with any febrile patient. Ask specifically whether the patient has been anywhere with malaria risk within the last three months — most malaria presents within a month of return, but it can occasionally present later. A returning traveller with a fever who might have a dangerous, contagious illness (such as a viral haemorrhagic fever) needs isolation precautions considered before routine blood tests, if travel to a high-risk area was within the last 21 days.

Fatal mistake

"He took his malaria tablets" and "he doesn't remember being bitten" are not reassuring. Prophylaxis reduces but does not eliminate the risk, and most people never notice the bite. Malaria must always be actively excluded with a blood test, not talked out of with a good travel story.

Step 2. History — the travel history is the diagnostic test

Take a precise travel history: exactly which countries and regions, urban or rural, exact dates of travel and when symptoms started, malaria prophylaxis and how well it was actually taken, vaccinations received, food and water exposure, freshwater swimming (schistosomiasis), insect and tick bites, animal contact, sexual contact abroad, and any healthcare received overseas (risk of resistant organisms). Symptoms of malaria are non-specific — fever, headache, muscle aches, vomiting — and can easily be mistaken for a viral illness.

In plain terms: a man with fever and non-specific symptoms 8 days after returning from a malaria-endemic country, with imperfect prophylaxis — malaria until proven otherwise.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Look for jaundice, splenomegaly and hepatomegaly (all can occur in malaria), a rash (dengue, typhus, viral haemorrhagic fever), lymphadenopathy, and any localising signs. In severe malaria specifically, watch for confusion, seizures, respiratory distress and evidence of bleeding — these mark a medical emergency, not routine admission.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CauseClues
MalariaAny endemic-area travel; fever, headache, myalgia; must always be actively tested for
Typhoid/paratyphoidGradual fever, headache, abdominal discomfort, relative bradycardia
Dengue / chikungunya / ZikaFever, severe muscle/joint pain, rash, low platelets
Viral hepatitisJaundice, deranged liver tests, risk exposures
LeptospirosisFreshwater exposure, muscle pain, conjunctival redness
Viral haemorrhagic feverRare but high-consequence; specific endemic areas and exposures, bleeding
Must not miss
  • Severe (falciparum) malaria — confusion, seizures, low glucose, kidney injury, jaundice, acidosis, respiratory distress define severe disease and change the whole management plan
  • A high-consequence infectious disease — consider isolation before testing in the right exposure and timing context
  • Sepsis on top of a travel-related infection — the same organ-dysfunction rules from Presentation 2 still apply here

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideThick and thin malaria blood films, rapid malaria antigen testThe films both confirm malaria and tell you which species and how much of the blood is infected (parasitaemia) — repeat if the first test is negative but suspicion remains
LaboratoryFBC (looking for low platelets), U&E, LFTs, glucose, lactate, clotting, blood culturesScreens for severe malaria features and for other travel-related and bacterial causes at the same time
SpecialStool/urine studies, serology for dengue/typhoid, further virology as guided by exposureDirected by the specific exposures uncovered in the travel history

Step 6. Risk stratification

Working diagnosis: presumed malaria pending blood films, given the endemic travel and imperfect prophylaxis. Alternative kept alive: typhoid or another travel-related infection if films are negative — repeat the films rather than abandoning the malaria question after one negative test. Severity is graded by the presence of any severe-malaria feature (confusion, seizure, hypoglycaemia, renal impairment, respiratory distress, high parasitaemia), which changes this from a ward admission to a critical-care one.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: isolate if a high-consequence infection is plausible; start supportive care (fluids, antipyretics) while urgent films are obtained.

Definitive: specific antimalarial treatment per local/species-specific protocol once confirmed, escalated urgently if any severe-malaria feature is present; treat other confirmed travel-related infections with the appropriate specific therapy; involve infectious diseases or tropical medicine early in any uncertain or severe case.

Monitoring and failure: repeat parasite count to track response to treatment; deterioration or any new severe-malaria feature is the trigger for critical-care escalation regardless of how "well" the patient looked on arrival.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit any suspected malaria for monitoring, even if the patient looks reasonably well, since deterioration can be rapid; involve infectious diseases/tropical medicine and, for high-consequence infection concerns, the relevant public health/isolation pathway; SBAR handover states the exact travel history, the prophylaxis taken, and the film results so far.

Two common errors to avoid
  • Not asking a precise travel history at all — a vague "have you travelled recently?" misses the detail that changes the whole differential.
  • Accepting one negative malaria film as final — repeat testing is needed if suspicion remains, since a single film can miss a low parasite count.

Check your understanding

Q1Does taking malaria prophylaxis, even correctly, rule out malaria?

Answer. No. Prophylaxis reduces but does not eliminate the risk of malaria, and can alter the incubation period. Malaria must always be actively excluded with blood films/rapid testing rather than being talked out of on the basis of a good adherence story.

Q2What is the single most important initial investigation in a returning traveller with fever from a malaria-endemic area, and what should you do if it is negative but suspicion remains?

Answer. Thick and thin malaria blood films (plus a rapid antigen test). If the first film is negative but clinical suspicion remains, repeat testing rather than abandoning the diagnosis — a single film can miss a low parasite count.

Q3List three features that indicate severe (falciparum) malaria.

Answer. Any three of: confusion/reduced consciousness, seizures, hypoglycaemia, acute kidney injury, jaundice, acidosis, respiratory distress, or high parasitaemia.

Q4Why might isolation need to be considered before routine blood tests in a returning traveller?

Answer. Because a small number of returning travellers with fever from a high-risk area within the last 21 days could have a high-consequence infectious disease such as a viral haemorrhagic fever, which requires isolation precautions before routine sampling to protect staff and other patients.

Case scenario

A 45-year-old woman returns from a two-week trip to rural Southeast Asia, including freshwater swimming, and presents four days later with fever, severe myalgia and conjunctival redness. Her malaria films are negative. What is the most likely alternative diagnosis, and what specific exposure in the history supports it?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Leptospirosis. Freshwater exposure plus fever, muscle pain and conjunctival suffusion (redness) is a classic pattern; malaria remains possible with a single negative film and should be reconsidered/repeated if she deteriorates, but the specific freshwater exposure and conjunctival findings point toward leptospirosis.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 29-year-old man returns from Nigeria 10 days ago with fever and headache. His first malaria film is negative. He remains febrile and unwell 12 hours later. What is the most appropriate next step?

  1. Start empirical antimalarial treatment without further testing
  2. Discharge, as malaria has been excluded
  3. Arrange outpatient serology only, no repeat film needed
  4. Diagnose a non-specific viral illness and treat symptomatically
  5. Repeat the malaria blood film
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. A single negative film does not exclude malaria, especially with ongoing symptoms; repeat testing is required. B is unsafe — malaria is not excluded by one negative film. A is inappropriate without a confirmed diagnosis unless he develops severe features requiring emergency treatment. C and D both fail to actively pursue the diagnosis that must be excluded first.

MCQ 2. A 38-year-old man returns from a malaria-endemic region and is found to have falciparum malaria with new confusion, a blood glucose of 2.1 mmol/L, and acute kidney injury. Which of the following best describes his current status and management priority?

  1. Discharge once antimalarials are prescribed, as malaria rarely causes rapid deterioration
  2. Uncomplicated malaria — outpatient antimalarial therapy is appropriate
  3. Repeat the blood film in one week to confirm clearance before starting treatment
  4. Severe malaria — requires urgent escalation, likely critical care, and prompt antimalarial treatment
  5. Diagnosis is unlikely to be malaria given the neurological features — investigate for meningitis instead
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. Confusion, hypoglycaemia and acute kidney injury are severe-malaria features requiring urgent escalation and prompt species-specific antimalarial treatment, usually with critical-care input. B understates the severity. E is wrong — these features are recognised complications of severe malaria itself, though other causes of confusion should still be considered in parallel. A is dangerous; severe malaria can deteriorate rapidly. C inappropriately delays treatment.

Presentation 4The febrile immunocompromised patient

Febrile neutropenia is a medical emergency and one of the true "cannot wait" presentations in medicine. A patient with a weakened immune system and a fever may have very few, or no, of the usual signs of infection — no pus, no obvious redness, sometimes not even a raised white cell count — because the cells that cause those signs are the ones that are missing.

Figure 5.4. Approach to the febrile immunocompromised patient, including early recognition of neutropenic sepsis, infection-focused investigation without delaying antibiotics, risk stratification and disposition.

Clinical vignette

A 54-year-old woman received chemotherapy ten days ago. She feels weak and has a sore mouth. Temp 37.8°C, HR 112. Examination shows little else — no clear focus. Before reading on, decide: does the mild-looking temperature and the lack of an obvious source mean this can wait for the morning round?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — treat this as an emergency by definition

Suspect febrile neutropenia in anyone who has had chemotherapy recently, has known bone marrow failure or a blood cancer, or is otherwise significantly immunosuppressed, and who is unwell, feverish, or unexpectedly deteriorating — including with only hypothermia, confusion or diarrhoea and no fever at all. A single temperature of 38°C or higher (or a sustained temperature of at least 38°C), with an absolute neutrophil count below 0.5 × 10⁹/L (or expected to fall below this), is treated as an emergency requiring antibiotics within one hour, not after imaging or a specialist review.

Fatal mistake

Waiting for a source to be found, or for the neutrophil count to come back, before starting antibiotics. In febrile neutropenia, empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics are given within one hour — every hour of delay measurably increases the risk of death. Treat first, investigate in parallel.

Step 2. History and red flags

Confirm the timing of the last chemotherapy cycle (neutrophil count is typically lowest 5–15 days after treatment), ask about mouth/throat soreness (mucositis — a common entry point for infection), line-site symptoms, diarrhoea, and any localising symptom, however mild it seems. Remember that many patients with febrile neutropenia have no identifiable focus of infection at all despite a careful search — this does not mean they are safe.

In plain terms: a woman ten days post-chemotherapy with fever, tachycardia and mucositis but no obvious source — febrile neutropenia until the count proves otherwise, and treated as an emergency regardless.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Look carefully at the mouth (mucositis, oral thrush), every IV line and central-line exit site, the skin (even a mild patch of redness may be all the sign there is), and the perineum for visual inspection only. Avoid a digital rectal examination in neutropenic patients — it can push bacteria from the gut into the bloodstream through fragile, immunosuppressed mucosa.

Examination pearl

Because neutrophils are what cause pus, swelling and redness, a neutropenic patient with a serious abscess may show almost none of the usual signs. A "normal-looking" examination in this context is reassuring about almost nothing — it reflects the missing white cells, not the absence of infection.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryExamples
Bacterial (commonest, treat first)Gram-negative bacteraemia, line infection, mucositis-related translocation, skin/soft tissue
Fungal (consider if prolonged/deep neutropenia)Invasive candidiasis, aspergillosis — especially if fever persists despite antibiotics
ViralReactivation (herpes, CMV) in heavily immunosuppressed patients
PneumocystisConsider in patients on long-term high-dose steroids or with HIV and low CD4 count
Must not miss
  • Febrile neutropenia itself — the diagnosis is an emergency by definition, regardless of how well the patient looks
  • Line-associated bloodstream infection — culture every lumen of any central line, and inspect every exit site
  • Fungal infection in prolonged neutropenia — consider if fever persists despite appropriate antibiotics
  • Rapid deterioration with minimal warning signs — the absence of typical infection signs is expected, not reassuring

Step 5. Tiered investigation — done in parallel with treatment, never before it

TierTestWhy
BedsideFull vital signs, urinalysisScreens for an obvious localising source quickly
LaboratoryFBC with differential (confirms neutrophil count), U&E, LFTs, blood cultures from a peripheral vein and from every lumen of any central lineConfirms the neutropenia and looks for a line source specifically — but blood cultures must never delay the first antibiotic dose
ImagingChest X-rayQuick screen for a respiratory source, even though many patients will have none
SpecialFungal markers, further imagingReserved for fever persisting despite antibiotics or prolonged/deep neutropenia

Step 6. Risk stratification

Working diagnosis: febrile neutropenia, source not yet identified — which is the normal pattern, not a diagnostic failure.
