Risk is stratified by how deep and how long the neutropenia is expected to last, by haemodynamic stability, and by any new organ dysfunction (the same sepsis criteria from Presentation 2 apply here too, on top of the neutropenia itself).

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: ABCDE assessment; the same sepsis bundle as Presentation 2; blood cultures taken quickly but without delaying the antibiotic; immediate broad-spectrum IV antibiotics within one hour per the local febrile-neutropenia protocol — this is the single most time-critical action in this whole document.

Definitive: early haematology or oncology involvement in every case; add antifungal cover if fever persists despite antibiotics or neutropenia is deep/prolonged; remove or replace an infected line once the patient is stable enough.

Monitoring and failure: reassess frequently — deterioration can be rapid and quiet in a neutropenic patient; critical-care discussion for any instability, since these patients can deteriorate with fewer warning signs than an immunocompetent patient would show.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit every case of febrile neutropenia; document the exact time the first antibiotic dose was given (this is often audited, because delay costs lives); haematology/oncology informed immediately; SBAR handover states the neutrophil count, the antibiotic given and when, and any line or mucositis findings.

Fatal mistakes with the immunocompromised febrile patient
  • Waiting for the neutrophil count before treating. If the history fits (recent chemotherapy, known marrow failure) and the patient is feverish or unwell, treat as febrile neutropenia immediately and confirm the count in parallel.
  • Being reassured by a "normal-looking" examination. Missing neutrophils mean missing signs of infection — a quiet exam proves nothing.
  • Performing a digital rectal examination. This can push bacteria into the bloodstream through fragile, immunosuppressed tissue — avoid it.
  • Treating persistent fever with more of the same antibiotic instead of asking "could this now be fungal?" in prolonged or deep neutropenia.

Check your understanding

Q1What defines febrile neutropenia, and within what time frame should empirical antibiotics be given?

Answer. A single temperature of 38°C or higher (or a sustained temperature of at least 38°C) with an absolute neutrophil count below 0.5 × 10⁹/L (or expected to fall below this). Empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics should be given within one hour, without waiting for the count to be confirmed or a source to be found.

Q2Why can a neutropenic patient with a serious infection have an unremarkable examination?

Answer. Pus, swelling and redness are produced by neutrophils. When neutrophils are absent or very low, the usual inflammatory signs of infection cannot form, so a "normal-looking" exam does not exclude a serious infection — it reflects the missing white cells, not the absence of disease.

Q3Why is a digital rectal examination avoided in neutropenic patients?

Answer. It risks pushing gut bacteria through fragile, immunosuppressed rectal mucosa into the bloodstream, potentially causing or worsening bacteraemia.

Q4When should antifungal cover be considered in febrile neutropenia?

Answer. When fever persists despite appropriate broad-spectrum antibiotics, or when neutropenia is deep or prolonged — both raise the likelihood of an invasive fungal infection such as candidiasis or aspergillosis.

Case scenario

A 61-year-old man with acute leukaemia and a central line develops a temperature of 38.2°C. His most recent neutrophil count (checked two days ago) was 0.3 × 10⁹/L. He looks well, observations are otherwise stable, and there is no localising sign on examination. The nursing staff suggest "watching him" until the morning blood results are back. What is the most appropriate immediate action?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Treat as febrile neutropenia now: take peripheral and central-line blood cultures without delay, and give empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics within one hour, regardless of how well he looks or that today's count has not yet been repeated. His known recent neutropenia and current fever meet the definition of an emergency; waiting for morning bloods would inappropriately delay life-saving treatment.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 49-year-old woman is 9 days post-chemotherapy for lymphoma. She has a single temperature of 38.3°C and feels generally unwell, with no localising symptoms. Her most recent neutrophil count (3 days ago) was 0.2 × 10⁹/L. What is the most appropriate next step?

  1. Reassure and review at outpatient clinic in one week
  2. Await a repeat FBC before making any treatment decision
  3. Arrange CT chest/abdomen/pelvis first to look for a source before treating
  4. Admit, take cultures, and start empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics within one hour
  5. Start oral antibiotics only if a source is found on examination
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. This meets the definition of febrile neutropenia (fever plus a very low recent neutrophil count) and is a medical emergency requiring immediate empirical IV antibiotics, with investigations proceeding in parallel. A is unsafe. B and C both delay life-saving treatment. E uses an inadequate route and inappropriately waits for a source that may never be found.

MCQ 2. A 33-year-old man with febrile neutropenia has been on appropriate broad-spectrum IV antibiotics for five days. He remains febrile with deep, prolonged neutropenia, and no bacterial source has been identified despite full cultures. What should be considered next?

  1. Add antifungal cover and investigate for invasive fungal infection
  2. Perform a digital rectal examination to look for a missed source
  3. Change to a different broad-spectrum antibiotic covering the same range of bacteria
  4. Discharge home once he is afebrile for a few hours
  5. Stop antibiotics since cultures are negative
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Persistent fever despite adequate antibiotics in the context of deep/prolonged neutropenia is the classic trigger to consider invasive fungal infection (such as candidiasis or aspergillosis) and to add antifungal cover with directed investigation. C simply repeats the same category of treatment without addressing the likely change in causative organism. E is unsafe given ongoing fever and neutropenia. B is contraindicated in neutropenic patients. D is inappropriate while he remains febrile and neutropenic.

Block 6 · Week 7 of the module
Endocrine emergencies

Presentation 1Hypoglycaemia

About this material. No single departmental source covers hypoglycaemia, hyperglycaemic emergencies, endocrine emergencies and steroid-related problems together. This block is written from standard guidance — NICE, the Joint British Diabetes Societies (JBDS) inpatient care guidelines, and Society for Endocrinology/Endocrine Society guidance.

Low blood glucose is an emergency because the brain runs almost entirely on glucose. The rule that saves lives is simple: check a glucose in any patient who is confused, drowsy, seizing, sweaty or behaving strangely — treat first, ask questions after.

Figure 6.1. Approach to hypoglycaemia from symptom to diagnosis: immediate glucose confirmation, severity assessment, treatment, investigation of the cause, disposition and safety-netting.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 68-year-old man with type 2 diabetes on insulin is found by his wife slumped and sweaty, unable to speak clearly. Capillary glucose is 2.1 mmol/L. Before reading on, decide: what is the very next action, before anything else?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Hypoglycaemia is usually defined as blood glucose below 3.9 mmol/L (70 mg/dL). Below 3.0 mmol/L (54 mg/dL) counts as clinically significant hypoglycaemia. Severity is judged by conscious level and the ability to swallow safely, not by the glucose number alone. Any patient who cannot protect their airway or swallow safely needs IV or IM treatment, not oral carbohydrate.

High-yield pearl

"Check glucose" belongs on the first-minute checklist for every confused, drowsy, seizing or behaviourally odd patient — right alongside airway, breathing and circulation. It costs seconds and can prevent permanent brain injury.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask about insulin or sulfonylurea use and the timing of the last dose, missed or delayed meals, recent exercise, alcohol intake, vomiting or diarrhoea, and any change in kidney function (reduced clearance of insulin or sulfonylureas raises risk). Ask whether hypoglycaemia has happened before and whether the patient still gets warning symptoms — repeated episodes blunt the warning signs ("hypoglycaemia unawareness"), which is itself dangerous.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Look for two symptom groups. Adrenergic symptoms (sweating, tremor, palpitations, hunger, anxiety) usually appear first. Neuroglycopenic symptoms (confusion, slurred speech, incoordination, abnormal behaviour, seizure, coma) mean the brain is now short of fuel. Neuroglycopenic symptoms with no preceding adrenergic warning is exactly the pattern of hypoglycaemia unawareness, common after repeated episodes or long-standing diabetes.

Fatal mistake

Assuming a confused diabetic patient is "just hyperglycaemic" or intoxicated, without checking glucose first. Treating an unconscious hypoglycaemic patient as a psychiatric or alcohol problem delays treatment and risks permanent neurological damage.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CauseClues
Insulin or sulfonylurea excess/timing errorCommonest cause; ask about dose, timing, missed meal
AlcoholAlcohol blocks the liver's glucose release; consider in any hypoglycaemic patient with an alcohol history
SepsisCan cause hypoglycaemia, especially in the very young, elderly or malnourished
Adrenal insufficiencyConsider especially with hyponatraemia, hyperkalaemia, hypotension
Liver failureReduced glucose reserve and production
Insulinoma / factitious insulin or sulfonylurea useRare; consider in recurrent unexplained hypoglycaemia in someone without diabetes
Must not miss
  • Recurrent nocturnal hypoglycaemia — can present only as morning headache or unrefreshing sleep; ask specifically
  • Prolonged sulfonylurea-induced hypoglycaemia — long-acting agents can cause recurrent hypoglycaemia for 24–72 hours and need admission and monitoring, not a single treat-and-discharge
  • Underlying adrenal insufficiency or sepsis in a non-diabetic patient with unexplained hypoglycaemia

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideCapillary glucose, repeat after treatmentConfirms and tracks response; capillary meters can be inaccurate at very low values, so send a formal lab glucose if uncertain
LaboratoryU&E, LFTs, cortisol if adrenal insufficiency suspectedLooks for a contributing cause when the history doesn't make it obvious
SpecialInsulin, C-peptide and sulfonylurea screen taken during the hypoglycaemic episode if the cause is unclear (especially in a non-diabetic patient)Distinguishes exogenous insulin, endogenous overproduction (insulinoma) and other causes — must be drawn while glucose is still low, or the diagnostic value is lost

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: insulin-related hypoglycaemia in a known diabetic patient, most likely a dose or timing error. Risk is stratified by conscious level (can the patient swallow safely?), by whether a long-acting agent is involved (needs longer observation), and by whether the patient lives alone or has hypoglycaemia unawareness (needs more robust safety-netting or admission).

Step 7. Management ladder

Conscious and able to swallow: give 15–20 g of fast-acting oral carbohydrate (glucose tablets, gel, or a sugary drink), recheck glucose in 10–15 minutes, and repeat if still low. Once corrected, give a longer-acting starchy snack to prevent relapse.

Unconscious, seizing, or unable to swallow safely: give nothing orally. Give IV glucose (typically 150–200 mL of 10% glucose over about 10–15 minutes, or per local protocol) via a large vein, or IM glucagon 1 mg if IV access is not immediately available (glucagon works less well in alcohol-related or chronically starved patients, whose glycogen stores are depleted). Recheck glucose after 10–15 minutes and repeat treatment if still low.

Definitive: identify and correct the precipitant (adjust insulin dose/timing, treat sepsis, address alcohol use). If a long-acting sulfonylurea is implicated, admit for extended monitoring, since hypoglycaemia can recur for 24–72 hours.

Monitoring and failure: once glucose is corrected, monitor conscious level and repeat glucose regularly. If hypoglycaemia recurs despite treatment, or the cause is unclear, escalate to diabetes/endocrine review.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Most short-acting insulin-related episodes can be observed briefly and discharged once stable, with safety-net advice and a review of the insulin regimen. Admit anyone with a long-acting sulfonylurea overdose, an unclear cause, recurrent severe episodes, or hypoglycaemia unawareness. Handover should state the glucose trend, the treatment given, and the suspected precipitant.

Two common student errors
  • Giving oral glucose to a drowsy patient who cannot safely swallow — this risks aspiration; use IV or IM treatment instead.
  • Treating and discharging sulfonylurea-induced hypoglycaemia without extended monitoring — these episodes can recur for days.

Check your understanding

Q1At what capillary glucose level is hypoglycaemia usually defined, and at what level does it become "clinically significant"?

Answer. Below 3.9 mmol/L (70 mg/dL) is the general threshold; below 3.0 mmol/L (54 mg/dL) is clinically significant hypoglycaemia requiring prompt action.

Q2Why should oral glucose never be given to a drowsy patient who cannot swallow safely?

Answer. Impaired swallowing risks aspiration into the airway. These patients need IV glucose or IM glucagon instead.

Q3Why is glucagon less effective in a patient with alcohol excess or chronic starvation?

Answer. Glucagon works by mobilising stored liver glycogen. In alcohol excess or starvation, glycogen stores are already depleted, so there is little substrate to release.

Q4Why does hypoglycaemia caused by a long-acting sulfonylurea need admission rather than treat-and-discharge?

Answer. Long-acting sulfonylureas have a prolonged action and can cause recurrent hypoglycaemia for 24–72 hours, so a single correction is not enough — the patient needs extended monitoring.

Case scenario

A 74-year-old woman with type 2 diabetes on gliclazide is brought in confused. Capillary glucose is 2.4 mmol/L. She is treated with IV glucose and becomes fully alert, with a repeat glucose of 7.1 mmol/L. Two hours later she becomes drowsy again and her glucose is 3.0 mmol/L. What is the most appropriate next step in management?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Admit for extended monitoring (typically at least 24–48 hours) with regular glucose checks and IV dextrose as needed. Gliclazide is a long-acting sulfonylurea, and recurrent hypoglycaemia hours after apparent correction is expected — a single treat-and-discharge is unsafe.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 45-year-old man with type 1 diabetes is found unconscious at home. Paramedics cannot get IV access. Capillary glucose is 1.8 mmol/L. What is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

  1. Wait until IV access is obtained before giving any treatment
  2. Oral sugary drink via nasogastric tube
  3. Subcutaneous insulin to "reset" glucose control
  4. IM glucagon 1 mg
  5. Oral glucose gel rubbed on the gums
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. IM glucagon 1 mg is the appropriate treatment when IV access is unavailable in an unconscious hypoglycaemic patient. E is wrong and dangerous — he cannot protect his airway, and oral/gum absorption is unreliable and risks aspiration. A wastes time and risks brain injury — treatment should not be delayed for IV access. C is nonsensical and would worsen hypoglycaemia. B requires placing an NG tube, which takes too long and still relies on unreliable gut absorption in an unconscious patient.

MCQ 2. A 30-year-old man with no known diabetes is investigated for recurrent unexplained hypoglycaemic episodes. Which single investigation strategy is most important to reach a diagnosis?

  1. Random blood glucose measured the next morning
  2. Insulin, C-peptide and sulfonylurea screen taken during a spontaneous hypoglycaemic episode
  3. Fasting lipid profile
  4. HbA1c measured at any convenient time
  5. Oral glucose tolerance test one week later
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Samples for insulin, C-peptide and a sulfonylurea screen must be taken while the glucose is actually low, to distinguish insulinoma (high insulin and B-peptide), exogenous insulin use (high insulin, suppressed C-peptide) and sulfonylurea use. D and A are wrong because they don't capture the diagnostic window during hypoglycaemia — an HbA1c or random glucose taken later tells you nothing about the mechanism. E is wrong because a glucose tolerance test looks for hyperglycaemia/diabetes, not the cause of hypoglycaemia, and doesn't need to be delayed a week. C is irrelevant to the diagnosis of hypoglycaemia.

Presentation 2Hyperglycaemic emergencies (DKA and HHS)

Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) and hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state (HHS) are the two life-threatening extremes of uncontrolled diabetes. DKA is mainly an insulin-deficiency and acidosis problem (classically type 1, but it can occur in type 2). HHS is mainly a severe dehydration and hyperosmolality problem (classically type 2, in older patients). The two can overlap.

Figure 6.2. Approach to hyperglycaemia from symptom to diagnosis, including early assessment, ketone and acid–base evaluation, differentiation of DKA and HHS, initial management, monitoring and escalation.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 22-year-old woman with type 1 diabetes presents with two days of vomiting, abdominal pain and deep, rapid breathing. She is drowsy. Capillary glucose is "HI" (unreadable, very high). Before reading on, decide: what three bedside tests confirm DKA right now?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

DKA is diagnosed by a triad: glucose usually above 11 mmol/L (or known diabetes), ketosis (blood ketones ≥3 mmol/L or significant urine ketones), and acidosis (venous bicarbonate <15 mmol/L and/or venous pH <7.3). Severity is judged by pH, bicarbonate, conscious level, potassium and haemodynamic status — not by the glucose number, which can occasionally be only modestly raised ("euglycaemic DKA", seen especially with SGLT2 inhibitor use).

DKA diagnostic criteria and severity (JBDS)

ComponentThreshold
Glucose (D)>11.0 mmol/L, or known diabetes
Ketones (K)Blood ketones ≥3.0 mmol/L, or significant ketonuria (≥2+ on urine sticks)
Acidosis (A)Venous bicarbonate <15.0 mmol/L and/or venous pH <7.3

Interpretation: all three components must be present to diagnose DKA. Any one of the following marks severe DKA and should prompt consideration of HDU/ICU-level care and senior/critical care review: blood ketones >6 mmol/L, bicarbonate <5 mmol/L, venous/arterial pH <7.0, potassium <3.5 mmol/L on admission, GCS reduced, oxygen saturation <92% on air, systolic BP <90 mmHg, pulse >100 or <60 bpm, or anion gap >16.

High-yield pearl

Deep, sighing (Kussmaul) breathing plus abdominal pain plus vomiting in a young patient is DKA until proven otherwise. It is commonly mistaken for a surgical abdomen or gastroenteritis. Always check glucose and ketones in this presentation.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask about the known diabetes type and usual control, any missed or reduced insulin doses (including pump failure), intercurrent illness or infection (the classic precipitant), new-onset diabetes (DKA can be the first presentation), vomiting or reduced intake, alcohol, and any SGLT2 inhibitor use (associated with euglycaemic DKA, sometimes triggered by reduced intake, surgery or intercurrent illness even with a near-normal glucose).

In plain terms: a known type 1 diabetic with vomiting, abdominal pain and Kussmaul breathing is classic DKA, most likely precipitated by illness or a missed insulin dose.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Assess hydration carefully (dry mucous membranes, reduced skin turgor, tachycardia, hypotension — DKA patients are often significantly fluid-depleted despite looking less unwell than the biochemistry suggests), conscious level (a falling GCS in DKA is an emergency — risk of cerebral oedema, especially in younger patients), and look for the precipitant (chest, urine, skin, abdomen for a source of infection).

Step 4. Schema — DKA versus HHS

FeatureDKAHHS
Typical patientYounger, type 1 (can occur in type 2)Older, type 2
OnsetFast (hours)Slow (days)
GlucoseUsually >11, can be lower with SGLT2iVery high, often >30
Ketones/acidosisSignificant ketosis and acidosisMinimal ketosis, near-normal pH
OsmolalityLess markedly raisedVery high (often >320 mOsm/kg)
Dominant problemInsulin deficiency → ketosis/acidosisSevere dehydration → hyperviscosity, thrombosis risk
Must not miss
  • Cerebral oedema — rare but devastating, mainly in younger patients; suspect with headache, falling GCS, or new neurological signs, especially if fluid/insulin correction has been too fast
  • Hypokalaemia after insulin starts — insulin drives potassium into cells; check potassium before starting insulin and monitor closely
  • The underlying precipitant — infection, missed insulin, new diagnosis, myocardial infarction — treating the glucose without finding why it happened invites relapse
  • Venous thromboembolism in HHS — the severe hyperviscosity of HHS carries a real clotting risk; VTE prophylaxis is part of standard management

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideCapillary and formal glucose, capillary or blood ketones, venous blood gasConfirms the triad and grades severity (pH, bicarbonate) quickly
LaboratoryU&E (especially potassium), FBC, calculated osmolality, blood cultures if infection suspectedPotassium drives the treatment plan; osmolality distinguishes/grades HHS
Source-directedChest X-ray, urinalysis/culture, ECGHunts for the precipitant — infection, MI
RepeatHourly capillary glucose and ketones; gas and potassium at 1–2 hourly intervals initiallyDKA management is protocol-driven and needs frequent reassessment, not one set of bloods

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: DKA, likely precipitated by an intercurrent illness or insulin omission. Severity (and the need for critical-care-level monitoring) is graded by pH/bicarbonate, potassium, conscious level, and haemodynamic stability — severe DKA (see the criteria table above) needs a higher level of care.

Step 7. Management ladder — follow the local DKA/HHS protocol precisely

Immediate (DKA): IV fluid resuscitation first (0.9% saline), then a fixed-rate IV insulin infusion (typically 0.1 units/kg/hour) once potassium is known to be safe (>3.5 mmol/L) — do not give insulin before checking potassium, since insulin can precipitate dangerous hypokalaemia. Continue the patient's long-acting (basal) insulin throughout if already on one. Add potassium replacement to IV fluids once levels are known, titrated to keep potassium in the normal range. Add glucose-containing fluid once glucose falls below roughly 14 mmol/L, while continuing the insulin infusion to switch off ketone production.

Immediate (HHS): cautious, slower IV fluid replacement is the primary treatment (correcting the profound dehydration). Insulin is often started at a lower rate or delayed until fluids alone are not lowering glucose, since HHS patients are very insulin-sensitive and rapid correction risks dangerous osmotic shifts. VTE prophylaxis is routine given the high thrombosis risk.

Definitive: treat the precipitant (antibiotics for infection, review the insulin regimen, address non-adherence); involve the diabetes team early.

Monitoring and failure: hourly glucose and ketones, 2-hourly gas/potassium initially. DKA resolution is defined by ketones falling, pH/bicarbonate normalising and the patient eating and drinking — not simply by glucose normalising. If ketones/acidosis aren't improving as expected, recheck that the insulin infusion is actually running and reassess for a missed precipitant.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Severe DKA/HHS (reduced GCS, severe acidosis, shock, very high osmolality) needs high-dependency or critical-care-level monitoring. Before discharge, ensure the patient (or their diabetes team) has a clear plan to prevent recurrence — sick-day rules, insulin education, and follow-up. SBAR handover states the precipitant found, the potassium trend, and the resolution criteria met (or not yet met).

Fatal mistakes in DKA/HHS
  • Starting insulin before checking potassium. Insulin drives potassium into cells and can precipitate life-threatening hypokalaemia and arrhythmia in a patient who is already potassium-depleted.
  • Stopping the insulin infusion once glucose normalises. The infusion must continue (with added glucose fluid) until ketosis and acidosis have resolved — glucose normalising does not mean the DKA has resolved.
  • Missing euglycaemic DKA in a patient on an SGLT2 inhibitor with a normal-looking glucose who is vomiting, unwell, and has a metabolic acidosis — check ketones regardless of the glucose number.
  • Correcting HHS too fast. Overly rapid fluid or glucose correction in HHS risks dangerous osmotic shifts and cerebral oedema — go slower than the DKA protocol pace.

Check your understanding

Q1What are the three diagnostic components of DKA?

Answer. Glucose >11 mmol/L (or known diabetes), ketosis (blood ketones ≥3 mmol/L or significant ketonuria), and acidosis (venous bicarbonate <15 mmol/L and/or venous pH <7.3).

Q2Why must potassium be checked before starting the insulin infusion in DKA?

Answer. Insulin drives potassium into cells. If potassium is already low (<3.5 mmol/L) and insulin is started without correcting it first, the patient can develop life-threatening hypokalaemia and arrhythmia.

Q3What defines resolution of DKA, and why is glucose normalising not enough on its own?

Answer. Resolution requires ketones to fall and pH/bicarbonate to normalise, with the patient eating and drinking again. Glucose can normalise well before the ketoacidosis has actually resolved, which is why the insulin infusion (with glucose-containing fluid added) must continue until acidosis and ketosis clear.

Q4Why is fluid and glucose correction deliberately slower in HHS than in DKA?

Answer. HHS patients have very high osmolality and are very insulin-sensitive. Rapid correction of fluids or glucose causes large, fast osmotic shifts that can cause cerebral oedema or other neurological injury, so correction is done more cautiously than in DKA.

Case scenario

A 19-year-old man with type 1 diabetes on an insulin pump presents with polyuria, vomiting and abdominal pain after his pump cannula was accidentally dislodged for 12 hours. Glucose is 24 mmol/L, blood ketones 5.2 mmol/L, venous pH 7.18, bicarbonate 9 mmol/L, potassium 4.9 mmol/L. He is diagnosed with DKA and IV fluids are started. What is the most appropriate next step before starting the insulin infusion?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Nothing further needs to delay insulin here — potassium is already known and is 4.9 mmol/L (safe, >3.5 mmol/L), so a fixed-rate IV insulin infusion (about 0.1 units/kg/hour) can be started once fluid resuscitation is underway. The point of the case is that potassium must be checked (not assumed) before insulin starts; here it has already been checked and is safe, so insulin should not be delayed further.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 26-year-old woman on an SGLT2 inhibitor for type 2 diabetes presents with vomiting and malaise after a period of reduced food intake before minor surgery. Capillary glucose is 10.8 mmol/L. Venous gas shows pH 7.22, bicarbonate 12 mmol/L. What is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Gastroenteritis with lactic acidosis
  2. Simple dehydration
  3. Euglycaemic diabetic ketoacidosis
  4. Alcoholic ketoacidosis
  5. Hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. SGLT2 inhibitors can cause DKA with a near-normal or only mildly raised glucose ("euglycaemic DKA"), often triggered by reduced intake or surgery — the acidosis (pH 7.22, bicarbonate 12) confirms this. B is wrong because dehydration alone does not cause a significant metabolic acidosis of this degree. E is wrong because HHS features very high glucose (often >30 mmol/L) and minimal acidosis — the opposite pattern here. A is a distractor; nothing points to gastroenteritis-driven lactic acidosis over the classic SGLT2i-DKA picture, and ketones should be checked to confirm. D is possible in principle but there's no alcohol history given, and the clinical context (SGLT2 inhibitor, reduced intake before surgery) is classic for euglycaemic DKA.

MCQ 2. A 70-year-old man with type 2 diabetes is brought in drowsy and confused after several days of poor fluid intake. Glucose is 42 mmol/L, blood ketones 0.8 mmol/L, venous pH 7.34, calculated osmolality 340 mOsm/kg. Which management step is most appropriate?

  1. Cautious, gradual IV fluid replacement as the primary initial treatment, with VTE prophylaxis
  2. Rapid IV fluid bolus to correct osmolality within 2 hours
  3. Withhold all fluids until osmolality is confirmed by the laboratory
  4. Fixed-rate insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hour as the first step, before any fluids
  5. Oral rehydration only, since he is not in DKA
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. This is HHS (very high glucose, minimal ketosis/acidosis, markedly raised osmolality). Cautious, gradual fluid replacement is the primary treatment, and VTE prophylaxis is routine because of the high clotting risk from hyperviscosity. D is wrong because insulin is not the first priority in HHS and, if used, is started cautiously (often at a lower rate) once fluids alone aren't lowering glucose — rapid correction risks dangerous osmotic shifts. B is wrong and dangerous — rapid correction of osmolality can cause cerebral oedema. E is wrong — a confused, severely hyperosmolar patient needs IV fluids, not oral rehydration. C is wrong — treatment should not be delayed while awaiting formal confirmation in a patient who is already drowsy and confused.

Presentation 3Endocrine emergencies (thyroid storm, myxoedema coma, adrenal crisis)

Three rare but rapidly fatal endocrine emergencies share a pattern: they are easy to miss because they mimic more common conditions (sepsis, heart failure, delirium), and treatment cannot wait for definitive lab confirmation. Suspicion and early empirical treatment save lives.

Figure 6.3. Bedside diagnostic approach to suspected endocrine emergencies, using presenting syndromes and key initial tests to distinguish hypoglycaemia, DKA, HHS, adrenal crisis, thyroid storm, myxoedema coma and pituitary apoplexy.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 34-year-old woman with known Graves' disease presents with fever, agitation, a racing heart (HR 150, irregular), sweating and vomiting after stopping her thyroid medication two weeks ago for a minor procedure. Before reading on, decide: does she need to wait for thyroid function tests before treatment starts?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — three patterns to recognise instantly

EmergencyRecognise by
Thyroid stormKnown/suspected hyperthyroidism + fever + severe tachycardia/arrhythmia + agitation or confusion + GI symptoms (vomiting, diarrhoea) — often triggered by infection, surgery, or stopping antithyroid medication
Myxoedema comaKnown/suspected hypothyroidism + hypothermia + reduced consciousness + bradycardia + hyponatraemia — often in an elderly patient, precipitated by infection, cold exposure, or sedating drugs
Adrenal (Addisonian) crisisKnown adrenal insufficiency or long-term steroid use + hypotension unresponsive to fluids + abdominal pain/vomiting + hyponatraemia + hyperkalaemia
High-yield pearl

All three of these diagnoses are clinical — treatment should never wait for the confirmatory blood test (TFTs, cortisol) to come back. If the pattern fits, treat empirically now and confirm later.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask specifically about known thyroid or adrenal disease, recent medication changes (stopping antithyroid drugs, missed thyroxine, missed or under-dosed steroids), recent illness, surgery or trauma (all classic triggers for storm and crisis), and any long-term steroid use — including inhaled, topical, or intra-articular steroids, which patients often forget to mention but which can still suppress the adrenal axis.

Fatal mistake

Not asking about steroid use because the patient says "I'm not on any tablets" — inhaled steroids for asthma/COPD, long courses of topical steroids, and repeated steroid injections can all suppress the adrenal axis and precipitate a crisis when the patient becomes unwell or the steroid is stopped abruptly.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Thyroid storm: fever (sometimes very high), tachycardia often with atrial fibrillation, tremor, sweating, agitation or frank psychosis, goitre, and eye signs of Graves' disease if present. Myxoedema coma: hypothermia, bradycardia, dry coarse skin, non-pitting oedema, delayed reflexes, reduced GCS. Adrenal crisis: hypotension that responds poorly to fluid alone, hyperpigmentation (in primary adrenal insufficiency), abdominal tenderness, signs of dehydration.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Must not miss
  • Thyroid storm mistaken for sepsis or an anxiety attack — both share fever and tachycardia; the goitre, eye signs, and known Graves' history are the clues
  • Myxoedema coma mistaken for simple hypothermia or sepsis — bradycardia (rather than the tachycardia usually seen with sepsis or a shivering response to hypothermia) plus a history of hypothyroidism are the clues
  • Adrenal crisis mistaken for gastroenteritis or "unexplained" septic shock — especially in a patient on long-term steroids who has just had them stopped, reduced, or forgotten during an acute illness
  • Precipitating a crisis by stopping steroids during an intercurrent illness — the opposite is needed: steroid doses must be increased, not stopped, during illness ("sick day rules")

Step 5. Tiered investigation — never let this delay treatment

TierTestWhy
BedsideVital signs, capillary glucose, ECGScreens for arrhythmia (storm), assesses severity
LaboratoryTFTs (storm/myxoedema), random cortisol ± short Synacthen test if time allows (adrenal crisis), U&E (hyponatraemia in all three; hyperkalaemia specifically in adrenal crisis), glucose (can be low in all three), blood cultures if infection suspected as triggerConfirms the diagnosis and looks for the precipitant, but results should not delay empirical treatment in a patient who fits the clinical pattern

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis: thyroid storm in a known Graves' patient who stopped antithyroid medication, precipitated by the recent procedure. A diagnostic scoring system — the Burch-Wartofsky Point Scale — can support the clinical diagnosis of thyroid storm but should never be required before starting treatment in a clearly compatible presentation.

Burch-Wartofsky Point Scale (thyroid storm)

DomainPoints
Temperature5 points at 37.2–37.7°C, rising in steps to 30 points at ≥40.0°C
CNS effects0 \= none; 10 \= mild agitation; 20 \= delirium/psychosis/extreme lethargy; 30 \= seizure/coma
GI-hepatic dysfunction0 \= none; 10 \= diarrhoea/nausea/vomiting/abdominal pain; 20 \= unexplained jaundice
Tachycardia (bpm)5 points at 99–109, rising in steps to 25 points at ≥140
Congestive heart failure0 \= none; 5 \= mild (pedal oedema); 10 \= moderate (bibasilar crackles); 15 \= severe (pulmonary oedema)
Atrial fibrillation0 \= absent; 10 \= present
Precipitant history0 \= negative; 10 \= positive (infection, surgery, stopped medication, etc.)

Interpretation: total score ≥45 is highly suggestive of thyroid storm; 25–44 is suggestive of impending storm; <25 makes storm unlikely. The score supports but does not replace clinical judgement — a compatible clinical picture should be treated without waiting to calculate or confirm the score.

Step 7. Management ladder

Thyroid storm: supportive care (cooling, fluids, treat any arrhythmia), beta-blockade (usually propranolol) to control heart rate and peripheral effects, an antithyroid drug (e.g. propylthiouracil or carbimazole per local protocol) to block new hormone synthesis, iodine solution given after the antithyroid drug (to avoid fuelling further hormone synthesis), and steroids (hydrocortisone), which block peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 and cover possible relative adrenal insufficiency. Treat the precipitant.

Myxoedema coma: IV thyroid hormone replacement (per specialist/local protocol), IV hydrocortisone before or with the thyroid hormone (giving thyroid hormone alone can precipitate an adrenal crisis in a patient with coexisting adrenal insufficiency), passive rewarming, cautious fluid management, and treatment of the precipitant. Needs critical care.

Adrenal crisis: immediate IV hydrocortisone (typically 100 mg IV, then continued dosing) without waiting for cortisol results, aggressive IV fluid resuscitation (0.9% saline), correction of hypoglycaemia if present, and treatment of the precipitant. This is one of the few true "treat first, test later" emergencies in medicine.

Monitoring and failure: continuous cardiac monitoring in storm and myxoedema coma; frequent reassessment of blood pressure and glucose in adrenal crisis; escalate to critical care for any of the three if not responding to initial treatment.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

All three diagnoses warrant high-dependency or intensive-care-level admission with endocrinology involved early. Before any patient with adrenal insufficiency is discharged, ensure they and their family understand sick-day rules (doubling or tripling steroid dose during illness) and carry an emergency steroid card/injection kit. SBAR handover states the treatment already given and the time hydrocortisone was administered.

Fatal mistakes with endocrine emergencies
  • Waiting for TFTs or cortisol before treating a clinically obvious storm, myxoedema coma, or adrenal crisis. All three are treated on clinical suspicion.
  • Giving thyroid hormone in myxoedema coma without covering with steroids first. Can unmask a fatal adrenal crisis.
  • Stopping a patient's usual steroids because they are "nil by mouth" or unwell. Steroid doses should be increased, and given IV if oral intake is not possible — never simply omitted.
  • Missing adrenal insufficiency in a patient on long-term inhaled, topical, or intra-articular steroids who is not thought of as "on steroids."

Check your understanding

Q1Why should treatment of thyroid storm, myxoedema coma, or adrenal crisis never wait for confirmatory blood tests?

Answer. All three are rapidly fatal and are diagnosed clinically. Waiting for TFTs, cortisol, or a Synacthen test result before treating delays life-saving therapy in a patient who already fits the clinical pattern.

Q2In thyroid storm treatment, why is iodine solution given after the antithyroid drug, not before?

Answer. Iodine given before the antithyroid drug can transiently fuel new thyroid hormone synthesis (the Wolff-Chaikoff effect can be bypassed early on). Giving the antithyroid drug first blocks new hormone synthesis so the iodine cannot make the storm worse.

Q3Why must steroids be given before or with thyroid hormone in myxoedema coma?

Answer. Myxoedema coma patients may have coexisting adrenal insufficiency. Thyroid hormone increases the metabolic clearance of cortisol and increases metabolic demand; giving it alone can precipitate a life-threatening adrenal crisis if the adrenal reserve is inadequate.

Q4What clues distinguish myxoedema coma from sepsis-related hypothermia?

Answer. Sepsis with hypothermia usually still triggers a compensatory tachycardia or shivering response. Myxoedema coma characteristically causes bradycardia, along with a known or suspected history of hypothyroidism, dry coarse skin, and delayed reflexes.

Case scenario

A 45-year-old man on long-term prednisolone for inflammatory bowel disease is brought to hospital with vomiting, diarrhoea and dizziness for two days. He has stopped his prednisolone himself because he "didn't want to take tablets while he had a stomach bug." Observations show BP 78/48 mmHg (not improving after 1 litre of IV fluid), HR 118. Blood tests show sodium 128 mmol/L, potassium 6.1 mmol/L, glucose 3.2 mmol/L. What is the most likely diagnosis and the most important immediate treatment?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Adrenal (Addisonian) crisis, precipitated by abrupt withdrawal of long-term steroids during an intercurrent illness. The most important immediate treatment is IV hydrocortisone (typically 100 mg IV) given without waiting for a cortisol result, alongside aggressive IV fluid resuscitation and correction of the hypoglycaemia.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 40-year-old woman with Graves' disease develops fever, agitation and a heart rate of 160 bpm three days after thyroid surgery. Which of the following is the most appropriate first-line drug to control her heart rate and peripheral symptoms?

  1. Digoxin
  2. Carbimazole alone, with no other agent needed
  3. Amiodarone
  4. Propranolol
  5. Verapamil
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. Propranolol (a non-selective beta-blocker) controls heart rate and peripheral adrenergic symptoms in thyroid storm and is first-line alongside antithyroid drugs. A (digoxin) is not first-line and is less effective at controlling the hyperadrenergic state. B is wrong because carbimazole blocks new hormone synthesis but does not control the immediate heart rate/haemodynamic problem — beta-blockade is still needed. C (amiodarone) contains iodine and can worsen thyrotoxicosis; it is not appropriate here. E (verapamil) is a rate-control option in some contexts but is not the standard first-line choice in thyroid storm; propranolol is preferred and also has some benefit reducing peripheral T4-to-T3 conversion.

MCQ 2. A 30-year-old woman with known primary adrenal insufficiency is admitted with a lower respiratory tract infection. She is alert, tolerating oral intake, with BP 118/76 mmHg. What is the most appropriate management of her steroid replacement during this admission?

  1. Halve her hydrocortisone dose to reduce infection risk
  2. Stop her hydrocortisone until the infection resolves, to avoid immunosuppression
  3. Increase her usual oral hydrocortisone dose (sick-day rules) while she is unwell
  4. Switch her immediately to IV hydrocortisone regardless of how she is tolerating oral intake
  5. Continue her usual hydrocortisone dose unchanged
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. During intercurrent illness, patients with adrenal insufficiency should follow sick-day rules and increase their usual oral steroid dose (commonly doubling it), since illness increases physiological steroid demand. B and A are dangerous and precisely backwards — stopping or reducing the dose risks precipitating an adrenal crisis. E under-treats her increased physiological need during illness. D is unnecessary while she is alert and tolerating oral intake — IV hydrocortisone is reserved for patients who are vomiting, unable to take oral medication, or haemodynamically unstable.

Presentation 4Steroid-related problems

Corticosteroids are prescribed extremely widely, and every prescriber needs a working knowledge of two opposite dangers: the complications of long-term steroid use, and the danger of suddenly stopping them.

Figure 6.4. Approach to a steroid-related problem, highlighting adrenal crisis and insufficiency, steroid-induced hyperglycaemia, DKA or HHS, and chronic iatrogenic Cushingoid complications.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 58-year-old woman has been on prednisolone 15 mg daily for polymyalgia rheumatica for eight months. She is admitted with a chest infection. The junior doctor considers stopping her prednisolone "since she's unwell and it suppresses immunity." Before reading on, decide: is this the right move?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — two opposite emergencies to recognise

Long-term steroid use suppresses the body's own adrenal axis (hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal, or HPA, suppression). Two dangerous situations follow from this. First, abrupt withdrawal or failure to increase the dose during illness can precipitate an adrenal crisis (see Presentation 3). Second, long-term continuation carries a real burden of complications that must be actively screened for, not just accepted as "the cost of treatment."

Fatal mistake

Stopping or withholding a patient's long-term steroid because they are "unwell" or "nil by mouth." This is exactly backwards — illness is when the steroid dose needs to go up (sick-day rules), and interrupting it can precipitate a life-threatening adrenal crisis.

Step 2. History and red flags

For any steroid-treated patient, establish the dose, duration and route (oral, IV, inhaled, topical, intra-articular — all can suppress the axis if high-dose or prolonged), the reason for treatment, whether they have ever been told about sick-day rules, and whether they carry a steroid card. As a rough guide, HPA suppression becomes a clinically relevant concern with doses equivalent to prednisolone ≥5 mg/day for more than four weeks, though individual susceptibility varies — the safest approach is to treat any patient on longer-term steroids as potentially adrenally suppressed.

Step 3. Examination pearl — screen for the complications systematically

Examination pearl

Steroid complications follow a memorable pattern across systems: skin (thinning, easy bruising, striae, poor wound healing), metabolic (weight gain, moon face, buffalo hump, hyperglycaemia/steroid-induced diabetes), bone (osteoporosis, avascular necrosis — ask about hip/joint pain), eyes (cataracts, raised intraocular pressure/glaucoma), mental health (mood change, insomnia, occasionally frank psychosis), cardiovascular (hypertension, fluid retention), and infection risk (reactivation of latent TB, atypical or masked presentations of infection since steroids blunt the usual inflammatory signs and fever).

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

ProblemPractical point
Adrenal suppression / crisis riskAssume suppression with prednisolone-equivalent ≥5 mg/day for >4 weeks; needs sick-day dosing, never abrupt withdrawal
Masked infectionSteroids blunt fever and inflammatory markers — a patient on steroids can have a serious infection with a deceptively unremarkable exam and near-normal CRP/white count
Steroid-induced hyperglycaemia/diabetesCheck glucose regularly in anyone starting moderate-to-high dose steroids, especially if already at risk of diabetes
Osteoporosis and fracture riskConsider bone protection (calcium/vitamin D ± bisphosphonate) with prolonged steroid courses per local/NICE guidance
Peptic ulcerationRisk increased further when combined with NSAIDs; consider gastroprotection
Must not miss
  • Adrenal crisis precipitated by abrupt steroid withdrawal or missed sick-day dosing — see Presentation 3 for management
  • A masked serious infection in a steroid-treated patient who "doesn't look that unwell" — have a lower threshold for investigating
  • Reactivation of latent tuberculosis in a patient starting significant immunosuppression, especially with relevant risk factors or travel history

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideCapillary glucose, blood pressure, weightScreens for the commonest metabolic complications
LaboratoryU&E, glucose/HbA1c, consider bone profileBaseline and ongoing monitoring for long-term steroid courses
SpecialDEXA scan for bone density (per duration/dose and local guidance), short Synacthen test if HPA suppression needs formal assessment before stopping steroidsGuides bone-protection decisions and safe steroid withdrawal planning

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working plan for the vignette patient: continue her usual prednisolone, and actually increase the dose per sick-day rules while she is treated for the chest infection, rather than stopping it. Risk of adrenal crisis is highest in patients who are unwell, vomiting (unable to take oral steroid, requiring IV substitution), or who have had their steroid interrupted without a covering plan.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: never stop a patient's established long-term steroid abruptly. During acute illness, surgery or trauma, increase the dose (sick-day rules — commonly doubling the usual oral dose, or switching to IV hydrocortisone if the patient cannot take oral medication or is significantly unwell) per local protocol.

Long-term/definitive: use the lowest effective steroid dose for the shortest necessary duration; arrange bone protection for anticipated long courses; monitor glucose and blood pressure regularly; screen for and manage complications proactively rather than reactively. When stopping a long course, taper gradually rather than stopping abruptly, guided by dose, duration and clinical response — this may need specialist input for complex or very long courses.

Monitoring and failure: if a steroid-treated patient develops unexplained hypotension, vomiting, abdominal pain or collapse during an intercurrent illness, treat as adrenal crisis (Presentation 3) until proven otherwise.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Ensure every long-term steroid patient has a steroid card and understands sick-day rules before discharge. Handover to any team taking over care must explicitly flag "this patient is on long-term steroids — do not stop without a taper/cover plan," since this is exactly the kind of instruction that gets lost between shifts.

Two common student errors
  • Stopping steroids in an unwell patient "to reduce infection risk" — the opposite is usually needed.
  • Being falsely reassured by a normal-looking exam or near-normal inflammatory markers in a steroid-treated patient — steroids mask the usual signs of serious infection.

Check your understanding

Q1What dose and duration of prednisolone is generally considered enough to cause clinically relevant HPA suppression?

Answer. A prednisolone-equivalent dose of 5 mg/day or more for longer than four weeks is a reasonable threshold to assume clinically relevant HPA suppression, though individual susceptibility varies.

Q2Why can a serious infection be missed in a patient on long-term steroids?

Answer. Steroids blunt the normal inflammatory response, so fever, CRP and white cell count can look deceptively normal even with a significant underlying infection — clinical suspicion needs to stay high regardless of "reassuring" numbers.

Q3What are sick-day rules for a patient on long-term steroids, and when should they switch to IV hydrocortisone instead of increasing the oral dose?

Answer. Sick-day rules mean increasing the usual oral steroid dose (commonly doubling it) during illness, surgery or trauma. Patients should switch to IV hydrocortisone if they cannot tolerate oral medication (e.g. vomiting) or are significantly unwell/haemodynamically unstable.

Q4Which non-oral steroid routes can still cause adrenal suppression, and why does this matter?

Answer. Inhaled, topical, and intra-articular steroids can all suppress the adrenal axis if used at high dose or for a prolonged period. Patients often don't consider these "steroids," so a history of "no tablets" doesn't exclude adrenal suppression — ask specifically about all steroid routes.

Case scenario

A 62-year-old man on long-term inhaled corticosteroids (high dose) for severe asthma, plus intermittent courses of oral prednisolone for exacerbations, is admitted for an elective hip replacement. The anaesthetist asks whether he needs any steroid cover perioperatively, since he is "not on regular oral steroids." What is the most appropriate advice?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Yes — he should be assumed to be potentially HPA-suppressed given high-dose inhaled steroid use plus intermittent oral courses, and should receive perioperative steroid cover (e.g. supplemental hydrocortisone around the time of surgery) per local protocol, rather than being treated as "not on steroids."

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 55-year-old woman on prednisolone 10 mg daily for six months for giant cell arteritis is admitted with a urinary tract infection and is vomiting. Which is the most appropriate step regarding her steroid?

  1. Stop the prednisolone until she recovers from the infection
  2. Continue her usual oral dose unchanged
  3. Halve the dose to reduce infection risk while she is septic
  4. Switch to IV hydrocortisone since she cannot reliably take oral medication, and increase the dose for sick-day cover
  5. Give a single one-off dose of IM depot steroid instead
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. She is vomiting and cannot reliably absorb oral medication, so IV hydrocortisone is needed, and the dose should be increased (sick-day cover) because of the acute illness. A is dangerous — stopping risks adrenal crisis. B is inadequate because oral absorption is unreliable with vomiting, and the dose also needs increasing, not just continuing unchanged. C is backwards — reducing the dose during illness increases adrenal crisis risk. E is not an appropriate substitute for titratable IV dosing during an acute illness.

MCQ 2. A 48-year-old man has been on prednisolone 20 mg daily for three months for a vasculitis and is otherwise well. Which of the following complications should be actively screened for as part of his routine long-term steroid monitoring?

  1. Osteoporosis, hyperglycaemia and hypertension
  2. Hyponatraemia due to adrenal crisis
  3. Hyperkalaemia
  4. Hypoglycaemia
  5. Bradycardia
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Long-term steroid monitoring should actively screen for osteoporosis (bone protection), steroid-induced hyperglycaemia/diabetes, and hypertension, alongside skin, eye and mental health complications. D is wrong — steroids cause hyperglycaemia, not hypoglycaemia. C is wrong — steroids with mineralocorticoid activity tend to cause hypokalaemia, not hyperkalaemia (hyperkalaemia is a feature of adrenal insufficiency/crisis, the opposite problem). B is wrong — he is currently on steroids, not in adrenal crisis; hyponatraemia with hyperkalaemia would suggest adrenal insufficiency, not ongoing steroid therapy. E is wrong — there is no recognised association between steroid therapy and bradycardia.

Block 7 · Week 8 of the module
Haematology and oncology presentations

Presentation 1Anaemia

Anaemia means a low haemoglobin. It is a laboratory finding, not a diagnosis — the low Hb is where the question starts, not where it ends. Two things organise every anaemia work-up: the MCV (what do the red cells look like?) and the reticulocyte count (is the bone marrow responding as it should?). Always use both together.

Figure 7.1. Approach to anaemia from symptoms and initial assessment to confirmation, MCV and reticulocyte classification, targeted investigation, escalation and likely diagnostic endpoints.

Clinical vignette

Case. A 69-year-old man is found on routine blood tests to have Hb 10.1 g/dL, MCV 73 fL, and ferritin 14 ng/mL. He denies any visible bleeding. Before reading on, decide: is giving iron tablets on its own a complete management plan here?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Before you look for the cause, check whether the anaemia itself is causing harm right now: breathlessness at rest, chest pain, syncope, low blood pressure, a persistently fast pulse, heart failure, or confusion. Symptomatic anaemia, or a haemoglobin that is falling fast, needs hospital assessment whatever the exact number is. A patient whose anaemia developed slowly may tolerate a very low haemoglobin; a smaller, faster drop can cause cardiac ischaemia in someone with limited cardiac reserve.

High-yield pearl

The haemoglobin number alone does not tell you who is unstable. How fast it fell, and how much cardiorespiratory reserve the patient has, matter as much as the value itself. Always ask "how fast did this happen?" as well as "how low is it?"

Step 2. History — hunt systematically for blood loss and mechanism

Ask about anaemia symptoms: fatigue, breathlessness on exertion, palpitations, dizziness. Then search deliberately for blood loss: gastrointestinal (black stools, blood in stool, NSAID use, weight loss), gynaecological (heavy or prolonged periods), and other sources (blood in urine, nosebleeds, frequent blood donation). Ask about diet (meat, green vegetables, vegan diet, pica — craving ice or starch strongly suggests iron deficiency), malabsorption (coeliac disease, previous stomach or bowel surgery), chronic disease (CKD, rheumatoid arthritis, cancer), drugs (anticoagulants, chemotherapy), and family or ethnic background (thalassaemia, sickle cell disease, hereditary spherocytosis).

In plain terms: an older man with new microcytic, iron-deficient anaemia and no obvious bleeding source volunteered has iron deficiency due to unexplained blood loss until proven otherwise.

Step 3. Examination pearl

Examination pearl

Look beyond conjunctival pallor. Angular cheilitis, a smooth (atrophic) tongue and spoon-shaped nails point to iron deficiency. Peripheral neuropathy and reduced vibration/joint-position sense point to B12 deficiency — this can occur even with a normal haemoglobin or MCV, so examine for it whenever the history fits. Jaundice and splenomegaly point to haemolysis. Lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly and bone tenderness point to marrow or systemic disease.

Step 4. Schema — organise by MCV, then split by reticulocyte response

MCV categoryMain causes
MicrocyticIron deficiency, thalassaemia, anaemia of inflammation, sideroblastic anaemia
NormocyticAcute blood loss, haemolysis (high reticulocytes); CKD, inflammation, early iron deficiency, marrow infiltration/failure (low reticulocytes)
MacrocyticMegaloblastic: B12/folate deficiency. Non-megaloblastic: alcohol, liver disease, hypothyroidism, reticulocytosis, myelodysplasia
Must not miss
  • Gastrointestinal malignancy as the cause of "unexplained" iron deficiency in an adult without a convincing menstrual or other physiological explanation, especially in older patients
  • Anaemia plus thrombocytopenia plus schistocytes — this is a microangiopathic haemolysis pattern (TTP/HUS/DIC) and an emergency, not a routine outpatient anaemia referral
  • Pancytopenia, blasts, or a leukoerythroblastic film — needs urgent haematology, not repeat outpatient bloods
  • Neurological B12 deficiency — may occur with a normal MCV; the damage is irreversible if replacement is delayed

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
Core triadFBC with indices, reticulocyte count, blood filmMCV classifies the cells; reticulocytes classify marrow behaviour — always use both together
LaboratoryFerritin and iron studies, renal and liver profile, bilirubin/LDH/haptoglobin if haemolysis suspected, B12/folate, CRP/ESR, TFTsA ferritin below roughly 30 ng/mL in an anaemic adult is essentially diagnostic of iron deficiency, but a normal or high ferritin does not exclude it during inflammation
SpecialDirect antiglobulin test, haemoglobin electrophoresis, bone marrow examinationReserved for unexplained anaemia, a poor reticulocyte response without a clear cause, additional cytopenias, or a film showing blasts/dysplasia

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: iron-deficiency anaemia due to occult chronic blood loss, most likely gastrointestinal, until specifically excluded. Giving iron alone is not a diagnosis and not a complete plan — the source of blood loss must be actively pursued, especially in a man or postmenopausal woman without an obvious explanation.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: in unstable or symptomatic anaemia — ABC approach, IV access, urgent bloods including group-and-save/crossmatch, identify and control active bleeding, ECG/troponin if ischaemia is suspected. Transfusion decisions rest on clinical status and rate of fall, not the haemoglobin number alone.

Definitive: treat the mechanism — replace iron/B12/folate as indicated (never give folate alone before excluding B12 deficiency, since this can worsen neurological damage while the blood count improves), investigate and treat the source of blood loss (upper/lower GI investigation as the history guides), and manage the underlying chronic disease.

Monitoring and failure: reassess the response to iron/B12 replacement. If the response is not as expected, reconsider the diagnosis (ongoing blood loss, malabsorption, a coexisting deficiency) rather than simply increasing the dose.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit and involve haematology urgently for symptomatic or rapidly falling haemoglobin, active bleeding, or an emergency pattern on the film (schistocytes, blasts, pancytopenia). Stable, well-characterised chronic anaemia can be worked up as an outpatient with GI/gynaecology referral as indicated. Handover should state the MCV/reticulocyte pattern, the working mechanism, and what has already been excluded.

Fatal mistakes with anaemia
  • Prescribing iron because the MCV is low, without asking why. Treating the number instead of finding the cause can delay a cancer diagnosis.
  • Giving folate before excluding B12 deficiency. The blood count improves while irreversible neurological damage continues.
  • Using serum iron alone to diagnose iron deficiency. It falls in inflammation too — always interpret it together with ferritin and transferrin saturation.
  • Ignoring the platelet and white cell counts. Anaemia plus other cytopenias, or blasts on the film, turns this from routine into urgent.

Check your understanding

Q1What are the two key initial tests that organise every anaemia work-up, and what does each one tell you?

Answer. The MCV and the reticulocyte count. MCV classifies the red cells (microcytic, normocytic, macrocytic); the reticulocyte count tells you whether the bone marrow is responding appropriately to the anaemia. Use both together to build the differential.

Q2A ferritin of 14 ng/mL is essentially diagnostic of iron deficiency, but a ferritin of 280 ng/mL does not exclude it. Why?

Answer. Ferritin below roughly 30 ng/mL in an anaemic adult is a highly specific marker of depleted iron stores. But ferritin is also an acute-phase reactant, so it rises with inflammation, infection, liver disease or malignancy — a normal or high ferritin during inflammation can mask coexisting iron deficiency, so interpret it alongside transferrin saturation and CRP.

Q3Why should you never give folate alone before excluding B12 deficiency?

Answer. Folate can correct the megaloblastic blood picture, but if B12 deficiency is the real cause, its neurological damage (peripheral neuropathy, subacute combined degeneration) will continue or worsen — and this damage can be irreversible if replacement is delayed.

Q4Which combination of findings on FBC and blood film should make you treat an anaemia as an emergency rather than a routine referral?

Answer. Anaemia with thrombocytopenia and schistocytes (fragmented red cells) suggests a microangiopathic haemolytic process — TTP, HUS or DIC — and needs urgent same-day assessment. Pancytopenia, circulating blasts, or a leukoerythroblastic film also need urgent haematology input, not repeat outpatient bloods.

Case scenario

A 45-year-old vegan woman presents with fatigue and tingling in both feet. Hb is 11.8 g/dL, MCV 102 fL, folate normal, and B12 is low-normal. What is the most important next step in her management?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Investigate and treat for B12 deficiency rather than dismissing the result because it is only "low-normal" or because the Hb/MCV changes are mild. Neurological B12 deficiency can occur with a near-normal haemoglobin and MCV, and the compatible neuropathy here makes this the working diagnosis until excluded. Crucially, do not give folic acid first or alone — replace B12 before or alongside folate, because folate can mask the anaemia while neurological damage continues.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 72-year-old man with rheumatoid arthritis has Hb 9.8 g/dL, MCV 78 fL, ferritin 210 ng/mL, CRP markedly elevated, and a low reticulocyte count. What is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Iron-deficiency anaemia — start oral iron
  2. Vitamin B12 deficiency — start B12 replacement
  3. Anaemia of inflammation — treat the underlying disease and reassess
  4. Haemolytic anaemia — check haptoglobin and LDH
  5. Myelodysplastic syndrome — refer for bone marrow biopsy immediately
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. A normocytic anaemia with a high ferritin, raised CRP and a low reticulocyte response in a patient with active rheumatoid disease is classic for anaemia of inflammation (anaemia of chronic disease). A is wrong because a high ferritin argues against iron deficiency (though coexisting deficiency is still possible if ferritin were low). E is premature — bone marrow biopsy is reserved for unexplained cytopenias, additional abnormal cell lines, or film abnormalities, none of which are described. B is wrong as there is no macrocytosis or other clue pointing to B12 deficiency. D is wrong as there is no evidence of haemolysis (no jaundice, no reported rise in LDH/bilirubin, and reticulocytes are low rather than high).

MCQ 2. A 30-year-old woman has Hb 7.5 g/dL, MCV 68 fL, and ferritin 4 ng/mL. She reports very heavy periods and occasional dark stools. She is haemodynamically stable. What is the best next step?

  1. Start iron replacement and investigate both the gynaecological and gastrointestinal sources of blood loss
  2. Transfuse red cells regardless of symptoms because the haemoglobin is very low
  3. Proceed directly to bone marrow examination
  4. Start oral iron only and review in six weeks
  5. Refer to psychiatry for an eating disorder assessment
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. This is severe iron-deficiency anaemia with two plausible bleeding sources reported (menorrhagia and possible GI bleeding) — both must be actively investigated in parallel with starting replacement; treating the number without finding the cause risks missing a GI malignancy or failing to control the gynaecological bleeding. B is wrong because transfusion decisions depend on symptoms and haemodynamic status, not the haemoglobin number alone, and she is stable. D is incomplete — iron alone without investigating the cause is not an adequate plan. E is unsupported — there is no evidence given for an eating disorder, and it would not explain the biochemical picture of iron deficiency. C is premature — bone marrow examination is not indicated for straightforward iron-deficiency anaemia with an identified likely mechanism.

Presentation 2Abnormal bleeding and bruising

Every bleeding patient raises two questions at once: where is the blood coming from, and why has it started or failed to stop? The pattern of bleeding — into skin and mucous membranes, versus into joints and deep tissue — usually tells you which part of the clotting system to check first.

Figure 7.2. Approach to abnormal bleeding and bruising from presentation and stability assessment through screening coagulation tests, further testing and diagnostic classification.

Clinical vignette

Case. A 64-year-old man is bleeding briskly through an abdominal drain after surgery. His platelet count, PT, APTT and fibrinogen are all normal. Before reading on, decide: does a normal coagulation screen rule out a bleeding problem here, and what is the most likely explanation?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — recognise major haemorrhage early

Up to roughly 30% of blood volume can be lost before blood pressure falls, so do not wait for hypotension to recognise major bleeding. Watch for a heart rate ≥120, systolic BP <90, altered mental state, cool peripheries, prolonged capillary refill, or a rapidly rising transfusion requirement. A normal early haemoglobin does not exclude major acute blood loss — it has not had time to fall yet.

Fatal mistake

"The haemoglobin is normal, so the blood loss can't be severe." This is false in early acute haemorrhage — haemoglobin does not fall until fluid shifts and replacement occur. Judge severity from the vital signs and clinical picture, not a single early haemoglobin.

Step 2. History and red flags

Characterise the current bleed: where, continuous or intermittent, how much, and any pain suggesting a deep muscle, retroperitoneal or joint bleed. Then ask about previous haemostatic challenges — a previous surgery, dental extraction or delivery without excessive bleeding tells you more than simply asking "do you bruise easily?" Ask about timing of onset (lifelong suggests an inherited disorder; new in adulthood suggests medication, liver or renal disease, marrow disease, DIC, or an acquired inhibitor), and take a drug history by name: warfarin, DOACs, heparin, aspirin, NSAIDs, and supplements the patient may not think to mention.

In plain terms: a postoperative patient bleeding briskly from a single site with an entirely normal coagulation screen has surgical bleeding until proven otherwise, not a coagulopathy.

Step 3. Discriminating examination — use the bleeding pattern

FeaturePrimary haemostasis (platelets/vWF)Secondary haemostasis (coagulation factors)
Typical sitesSkin, mucous membranesJoints, muscles, deep tissue
ManifestationsPetechiae, purpura, small bruises, epistaxis, menorrhagiaHaemarthrosis, large haematoma, retroperitoneal bleed
Timing after injuryImmediate, persistent oozingOften delayed or recurrent

Petechiae (small, flat, non-blanching spots) strongly suggest thrombocytopenia or a platelet disorder. Palpable purpura suggests vasculitis. Haemarthrosis and large muscle haematomas strongly suggest a coagulation-factor disorder.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Must not miss
  • DIC — suspect it with sepsis, trauma, cancer or an obstetric complication plus bleeding from several sites. Falling platelets and fibrinogen with a rising PT/APTT can appear before any single value crosses the lab's abnormal threshold — serial trends matter more than one result
  • An acquired factor inhibitor — new severe bleeding in an older adult with no personal or family bleeding history, and an isolated prolonged APTT that fails to correct on mixing
  • Surgical/anatomical bleeding masquerading as "coagulopathy" — a single bleeding site with a normal coagulation screen needs a surgeon, not more plasma
  • Pseudothrombocytopenia — always check the film for platelet clumping before diagnosing ITP or transfusing platelets for an unexpectedly low automated count

ISTH overt DIC score

ParameterPoints
Platelet count >100 ×10&sup9/L / 50–100 / <500 / 1 / 2
Fibrin-related marker (e.g. D-dimer): no increase / moderate increase / strong increase0 / 2 / 3
Prolonged PT: <3 s / 3–6 s / >6 s0 / 1 / 2
Fibrinogen >1 g/L / ≤1 g/L0 / 1

Interpretation: a total score ≥5 is compatible with overt DIC; a score <5 is suggestive but not confirmatory, and the test should be repeated on serial samples — a single result never excludes evolving DIC.

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
Core screenFBC, blood film, PT/INR, APTT, fibrinogen, thrombin timeLocalises the defect to platelets, primary or secondary haemostasis, or reveals a consumptive process
Second-linevWF antigen/activity, factor VIII activity, mixing study, individual factor assays, D-dimerA mixing study distinguishes a factor deficiency (corrects on mixing) from an inhibitor/anticoagulant effect (does not correct)
RepeatSerial platelet count, PT, APTT, fibrinogen during active or ongoing bleedingA single result cannot safely characterise a dynamic haemostatic disorder such as evolving DIC

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: surgical/anatomical bleeding, not a haemostatic disorder, given the single site and entirely normal screen. General rule: a persistently bleeding single postoperative site should raise concern for a surgical problem first, not automatically be attributed to coagulopathy. Bleeding from several unrelated sites, or spontaneous bleeding, favours a generalised haemostatic defect.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate (major haemorrhage): A–E assessment, activate the local major haemorrhage protocol, two large-bore IV lines, send bloods before transfusion if this does not delay treatment, continuously reassess perfusion, and get early definitive source control (surgery, endoscopy, interventional radiology or obstetrics as relevant) — imaging must not delay an urgently needed intervention. Avoid excessive crystalloid, which worsens dilutional anaemia and coagulopathy; warm the patient and blood products, and watch for the dangerous triad of hypothermia, acidosis and coagulopathy.

Definitive: treat the specific cause once localised — surgical or interventional control for an anatomical source; specific factor replacement, DDAVP, or antifibrinolytics for an inherited or acquired haemostatic disorder, guided by haematology; treat the underlying trigger in DIC (sepsis, obstetric complication). Blood components are reserved for active bleeding or high bleeding risk, not simply for correcting numbers.

Monitoring and failure: repeat the film and coagulation screen if bleeding continues. Do not accept "the coagulation screen is normal" as the final word if the bleeding history is convincing — request vWF, factor VIII, and platelet-function testing with haematology guidance.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Major or ongoing haemorrhage needs urgent senior, surgical and haematology involvement, and often critical care. Isolated mild mucocutaneous bleeding with a convincing lifelong history can be worked up in a specialist outpatient haemostasis clinic. Handover should state the bleeding pattern (mucocutaneous vs deep), the screening results, and whether a mixing study has been sent.

Two common student errors
  • Assuming a normal PT and APTT excludes a bleeding disorder — von Willebrand disease and platelet dysfunction can have entirely normal screening tests.
  • Diagnosing ITP from a low platelet count alone, without inspecting the film first — ITP is a diagnosis of exclusion.

Check your understanding

Q1How does the bleeding pattern help localise the problem to primary versus secondary haemostasis?

Answer. Primary haemostasis (platelet/vWF) disorders cause mucocutaneous bleeding — petechiae, purpura, epistaxis, menorrhagia — that starts immediately after injury and oozes persistently. Secondary haemostasis (coagulation factor) disorders cause deep bleeding — haemarthrosis, muscle haematoma, retroperitoneal bleeding — that is often delayed or recurrent.

Q2Why doesn't a normal PT and APTT rule out a significant bleeding disorder?

Answer. Von Willebrand disease and platelet function disorders can have entirely normal PT and APTT, since these tests screen the coagulation factor cascade, not platelet number or function. A convincing bleeding history should still prompt vWF and platelet-function testing even with a normal screen.

Q3What does a mixing study distinguish, and why does it matter?

Answer. A mixing study distinguishes a clotting factor deficiency (the prolonged PT/APTT corrects when mixed 1:1 with normal plasma, because the missing factor is replaced) from an inhibitor or anticoagulant effect (it does not correct, because the inhibitor also acts on the added normal plasma). This changes management completely — factor replacement versus treating/removing an inhibitor.

Q4Why do serial coagulation results matter more than a single result when DIC is suspected?

Answer. Early DIC can show platelet and fibrinogen values that are still technically within the normal range but are falling from the patient's own baseline, with the PT/APTT starting to rise. The trend over time reveals the consumptive process before any single value crosses the laboratory's abnormal threshold.

Case scenario

A 35-year-old woman, two weeks postpartum with a retained placental fragment, develops widespread new bruising, oozing from her IV sites, and heavier vaginal bleeding. Serial bloods over 12 hours show a falling platelet count, falling fibrinogen, and a rising PT and APTT. What is the diagnosis, and what is the immediate management priority?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) triggered by an obstetric complication (retained products, likely with associated infection/sepsis). The immediate priority is to treat the underlying trigger — urgent evacuation of retained tissue and treatment of any sepsis — alongside supportive blood component replacement (platelets, fresh frozen plasma, cryoprecipitate/fibrinogen concentrate) guided by ongoing bleeding and repeat coagulation results, with senior obstetric and haematology involvement and activation of the major haemorrhage protocol if bleeding is significant. Treating the trigger, not just the numbers, is what resolves DIC.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A patient is bleeding steadily from a single abdominal drain site after surgery. FBC, PT, APTT and fibrinogen are all normal. What is the best next step?

  1. Transfuse platelets
  2. Give fresh frozen plasma empirically
  3. Start a DOAC reversal agent
  4. Request surgical review to look for an anatomical bleeding source
  5. Simply repeat the coagulation screen and wait
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. A single bleeding site with an entirely normal coagulation screen is surgical/anatomical bleeding until proven otherwise and needs a surgeon, not blood products. B and A treat a coagulopathy that the numbers do not support. E delays necessary action. C is irrelevant with no history of DOAC use given and normal coagulation tests.

MCQ 2. A patient has an isolated prolonged APTT that does not shorten when their plasma is mixed 1:1 with normal plasma. What does this most likely indicate?

  1. Von Willebrand disease
  2. Pseudothrombocytopenia
  3. Vitamin K deficiency
  4. A hereditary factor VIII deficiency (haemophilia A)
  5. An acquired factor inhibitor or the presence of an anticoagulant such as heparin/lupus anticoagulant
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. Failure to correct on mixing means a factor is present but being neutralised — by an inhibitor (e.g. acquired factor VIII inhibitor) or an anticoagulant effect (heparin contamination, lupus anticoagulant) — rather than simply missing. D is wrong because a true factor deficiency corrects on mixing (the missing factor is supplied by normal plasma). C mainly prolongs the PT, not an isolated APTT. A typically causes a normal or only mildly prolonged APTT and a mucocutaneous bleeding pattern, not this mixing-study result. B is a platelet-count artefact, unrelated to APTT mixing studies.

Presentation 3Lymphadenopathy

An enlarged lymph node is a clinical sign, not a diagnosis. Your job is to work out whether it is localised or generalised, what it feels like, what drains into it, and whether it needs watching, blood tests, or biopsy now.

Figure 7.3. Approach to lymphadenopathy, integrating red flags, initial investigations, localised versus generalised patterns, referral thresholds and common diagnostic endpoints.

Clinical vignette
