Case. A 24-year-old presents with a painless left supraclavicular node, generalised itch, fatigue, and drenching night sweats requiring a change of clothes. The node is firm, rubbery and mobile. Before reading on, decide: what makes this presentation especially concerning, and what is the diagnostic priority?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Urgently assess airway and circulation if there is stridor, facial swelling, venous congestion or severe respiratory distress — large mediastinal or neck masses can compress the airway or great vessels. Otherwise, this is rarely an immediate physiological emergency, and the priority shifts to working out how urgently a tissue diagnosis is needed.

High-yield pearl

Supraclavicular lymphadenopathy is a high-risk location and always needs an explanation. It should prompt active consideration of intrathoracic, abdominal, or lymphoproliferative disease, not be dismissed.

Step 2. History — characterise the node and ask about B symptoms

Ask when it was first noticed, whether the onset was sudden or gradual, whether it is stable, shrinking or growing, painful or painless, and whether other areas are now involved. Ask specifically about the three classical "B symptoms" of lymphoma — unexplained fever above 38°C, drenching night sweats requiring a change of clothes or bedding, and weight loss greater than 10% over six months — and be precise: do not accept "I sweat at night" as a B symptom without clarifying the severity. Ask about local infective symptoms in the drainage territory (sore throat, dental pain, ear discharge, skin infection for cervical nodes; breast/limb symptoms for axillary; genital/perineal/limb symptoms for inguinal), and about exposure history (TB contact, cat scratches, sexual history/HIV risk, travel, smoking and alcohol).

In plain terms: a young adult with painless, progressive, rubbery supraclavicular lymphadenopathy plus B symptoms and pruritus is highly concerning for Hodgkin lymphoma, and tissue diagnosis is the priority.

Step 3. Examination pearl

Examination pearl

Feel every nodal region, not just the one the patient points to: pre/post-auricular, submandibular, cervical (anterior and posterior), supraclavicular, axillary, epitrochlear, inguinal, femoral, and popliteal. For every abnormal node, document the site, exact size in centimetres, tenderness, consistency, mobility, matting, and any overlying skin change or sinus. Then examine the drainage territory: mouth/teeth/pharynx/ears/scalp/thyroid for cervical nodes, breast and arm for axillary, genitalia/perineum/leg for inguinal. Finally, check for hepatosplenomegaly — lymphadenopathy plus organomegaly strongly suggests systemic infection or a lymphoproliferative/infiltrative disorder.

Step 4. Schema — use the feel of the node to narrow the differential

FindingInterpretation
Tender, soft, mobileUsually reactive or infective
Rubbery, firm, mobile, non-tenderSuggestive of lymphoma
Hard, irregular, fixed, non-tenderSuggestive of metastatic carcinoma
Matted nodesTuberculosis, other granulomatous disease, or malignancy
Fluctuant node or discharging sinusSuppuration or caseating mycobacterial disease
Must not miss
  • Lymphoma — painless, rubbery, progressive nodes with B symptoms; a normal FBC does not exclude it
  • Metastatic head-and-neck carcinoma — a hard, fixed, painless cervical node in a smoker/drinker, especially with hoarseness or dysphagia, needs full ENT assessment, not repeated antibiotics
  • Tuberculous lymphadenitis — painless, initially mobile, becoming matted; roughly half of patients have no fever or night sweats, so their absence does not exclude TB
  • An unrecognised airway/vascular-compressive mass — stridor, facial swelling or venous congestion needs urgent action

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BasicFBC and film, CRP/ESR, chest X-rayScreens for infection, marrow involvement, mediastinal/hilar disease — but a normal FBC does not exclude Hodgkin lymphoma
Extended baseline (if malignancy suspected)Renal/liver profile, LDH, calcium, urateLDH may rise with cell turnover but is neither sensitive nor specific enough to diagnose lymphoma on its own
TargetedEBV/CMV/HIV serology, TB testing, ANA/autoimmune panel — chosen by the clinical pattern, not ordered as a blanket panelDirected by the specific phenotype (mononucleosis-like illness, TB risk, autoimmune features)
TissueExcision biopsy (preferred for suspected lymphoma — preserves nodal architecture), core biopsy, or fine-needle aspiration (useful for suspected metastatic carcinoma or mycobacterial disease, often insufficient for new lymphoma)Do not delay tissue diagnosis in a progressive, suspicious or unexplained node

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Features that accelerate investigation: supraclavicular location, hard/fixed consistency, painless progressive enlargement, a cervical node >1.5 cm without obvious infection, generalised lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, B symptoms, abnormal blood counts, or a smoking/alcohol/prior-cancer history. In the vignette, the supraclavicular location, B symptoms and pruritus place this patient firmly in the "needs urgent tissue diagnosis" category.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: secure the airway if there is compressive threat; otherwise no emergency intervention is usually needed while diagnosis proceeds.

Definitive: treat confirmed reactive/infective causes appropriately (antibiotics for bacterial lymphadenitis, antimicrobial therapy for confirmed TB); arrange urgent excision biopsy for suspected lymphoma; refer to ENT/head-and-neck oncology for suspected metastatic carcinoma; refer to rheumatology for suspected autoimmune or granulomatous disease.

Monitoring and failure: a persistent or continuously enlarging node that was initially thought reactive should not simply be re-reassured — re-examine and reconsider biopsy if it fails to regress as expected.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Urgent (two-week-wait equivalent) referral for suspected-malignancy features (supraclavicular node, hard/fixed node, B symptoms, persistent unexplained cervical node); routine referral for a reactive-appearing node that fails to settle over several weeks; same-day assessment for any airway or vascular compromise. Handover should state the node's characteristics, drainage-territory findings, and whether B symptoms are present.

Fatal mistakes with lymphadenopathy
  • Repeatedly prescribing antibiotics for a persistent painless adult neck mass without establishing a diagnosis. This delays cancer diagnosis.
  • Being reassured by a normal FBC in suspected lymphoma. The blood count is frequently normal in Hodgkin lymphoma.
  • Sending an FNA alone for a suspected new lymphoma. Nodal architecture is needed — arrange excision or an adequate core biopsy instead.
  • Excluding TB because there is no fever or night sweats. About half of patients with TB lymphadenitis have no constitutional symptoms.

Check your understanding

Q1What are the three classical B symptoms of lymphoma, and how should you clarify "night sweats" with a patient?

Answer. Unexplained fever above 38°C, drenching night sweats requiring a change of clothes or bedding, and weight loss greater than 10% over six months. Do not accept "I sweat at night" at face value — ask specifically whether the sweating is severe enough to require changing clothes or bedding.

Q2How does the physical character of a node help distinguish lymphoma, metastatic carcinoma and TB lymphadenitis?

Answer. Rubbery, firm, mobile, non-tender nodes suggest lymphoma. Hard, irregular, fixed, non-tender nodes suggest metastatic carcinoma. Matted nodes, sometimes progressing to fluctuance or a discharging sinus, suggest tuberculosis or another granulomatous disease.

Q3Why is excision biopsy preferred over fine-needle aspiration when lymphoma is suspected?

Answer. Diagnosing and subtyping lymphoma requires assessment of the nodal architecture, which FNA (a cell sample) cannot provide. Excision biopsy, or at least an adequate core biopsy, preserves the tissue structure needed for accurate diagnosis. FNA is more useful when metastatic carcinoma or mycobacterial disease is suspected.

Q4Why doesn't the absence of B symptoms or a normal FBC exclude serious pathology?

Answer. Roughly half of patients with tuberculous lymphadenitis have no fever or night sweats, so their absence does not rule out TB. Similarly, the FBC is frequently entirely normal in Hodgkin lymphoma, so a normal blood count should not be reassuring in a suspicious node.

Case scenario

A 58-year-old man who smokes and drinks heavily has a hard, fixed, painless 3 cm cervical node that has been present for six weeks, along with persistent hoarseness. He has no fever. What is the most important next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Urgent (two-week-wait) referral to ENT/head-and-neck oncology for suspected metastatic squamous cell carcinoma from an occult upper aerodynamic tract primary. He needs a full ENT assessment, including panendoscopy and node biopsy — not a further course of antibiotics, which would only delay the diagnosis.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 45-year-old otherwise well woman has a painless 1.8 cm cervical node, present for five weeks, with no identifiable infective focus in its drainage territory. What is the best next step?

  1. Prescribe a course of broad-spectrum antibiotics
  2. Order an FNA only and close the case if it is reported as reactive
  3. Repeat clinical review in six months
  4. Arrange further assessment, including consideration of biopsy, given the size and persistence
  5. Reassure and discharge with no follow-up
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. A cervical node above roughly 1.5 cm that persists beyond a few weeks without an obvious infective explanation needs further assessment, including consideration of biopsy — persistence and size are the key red flags here, independent of tenderness. E and C both under-investigate a persistent unexplained node. A assumes an infective cause with no supporting evidence. B is risky because an FNA reported as reactive does not reliably exclude early lymphoma, which needs architecture-preserving tissue for diagnosis.

MCQ 2. Which single feature below should most strongly accelerate the urgency of investigating a lymph node?

  1. Tenderness on palpation
  2. Supraclavicular location
  3. Size of 1 cm in a young, otherwise well adult
  4. Bilateral cervical nodes in a teenager with a sore throat
  5. A soft, mobile, mildly tender submandibular node in a patient with a dental abscess
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Supraclavicular lymphadenopathy is a high-risk location that always warrants an explanation and active consideration of intrathoracic, abdominal or lymphoproliferative disease. A is the opposite pattern — tenderness usually suggests a reactive/infective node. C describes a small node in a low-risk context, which is usually not concerning. D and E both describe classic benign reactive patterns (viral pharyngitis, dental infection) that would be expected to resolve with the underlying cause treated.

Presentation 4Unintentional weight loss

Weight loss is a symptom the patient reports, but it is also a sign you should verify objectively. Clinically significant weight loss is generally defined as losing at least 5% of usual body weight over 6–12 months. Non-malignant causes are collectively more common than cancer, but weight loss is still an alarm feature that deserves a structured work-up, not a reflexive "cancer screen."

Figure 7.4. Approach to unintentional weight loss from confirmation and red-flag triage through focused history, examination, first-line tests, differential diagnosis and targeted second-line investigation.

Clinical vignette

Case. A 79-year-old woman has lost 6 kg over 8 months. Her husband died a year ago; she eats tea and toast most days, has painful dentures, and struggles to shop because of poor mobility. Examination shows a low BMI, temporal wasting, poor dentition and low mood, with no lymphadenopathy or abdominal mass. Before reading on, decide: should the work-up here focus mainly on excluding cancer?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — confirm the loss is real first

Before starting any work-up, confirm the weight loss actually happened: what was the previous weight, when was it measured, has the current weight been objectively measured, are clothes/rings/dentures now loose? Remember that oedema, ascites, effusions, diuretics, dehydration and recent hospitalisation can all mask true weight loss on the scale — a patient with heart failure or cirrhosis may be losing real muscle while the number on the scale looks stable because of fluid retention.

High-yield pearl

Ask one high-yield question early: "has your appetite decreased, stayed the same, or increased?" Weight loss with reduced appetite points toward malignancy, depression, chronic infection, or organ failure. Weight loss despite preserved or increased appetite points toward hyperthyroidism, uncontrolled diabetes, or malabsorption. This single question rapidly reorganises the differential.

Step 2. History — think mechanistically, then search for localising clues

Organise causes into four mechanisms: reduced intake (anorexia of illness, depression, dementia, dental disease, dysphagia, poverty/social isolation), malabsorption (coeliac disease, IBD, pancreatic insufficiency, previous GI surgery), increased expenditure (thyrotoxicosis, chronic infection/inflammation, cancer cachexia, COPD, heart failure), and nutrient/calorie loss (glycosuria in uncontrolled diabetes, protein-losing enteropathy, persistent vomiting/diarrhoea). Take a genuine dietary history — what does a typical day of eating look like, who buys and cooks the food, can they afford it, do they eat alone — and ask specifically about dental pain, dentures, dysphagia, mood, memory, and their functional ability to shop and cook.

In plain terms: an older woman with modest weight loss, bereavement, poor dentition, and functional limitation likely has a multifactorial psychosocial and dental cause, not necessarily malignancy — though this must still be confirmed rather than assumed.

Step 3. Examination pearl

Examination pearl

Look specifically for malnutrition, not just a low BMI: temporal wasting, periscapular and upper-arm muscle wasting, loss of subcutaneous fat, prominent clavicles/ribs, reduced grip strength. Examine the mouth and teeth — dental disease, oral candidiasis and poorly fitting dentures are frequently missed causes that are easily treatable. Then complete a full examination: neck (thyroid, nodes), abdomen (masses, organomegaly, ascites), skin, chest, and a cognitive/mood assessment — do not restrict the exam to the abdomen just because "weight loss" suggests cancer.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Must not miss
  • Malignancy with a localising clue — dysphagia, GI bleeding, haemoptysis, a palpable mass or lymphadenopathy dramatically raises the diagnostic yield of imaging/endoscopy; weight loss alone, without a localising feature, has a surprisingly low yield on a blanket CT or colonoscopy
  • Refeeding syndrome risk — before aggressively feeding a severely malnourished patient (BMI <16, >15% weight loss in 3–6 months, minimal intake for >10 days, or low potassium/phosphate/magnesium before feeding), involve a dietitian and feed cautiously with proactive electrolyte replacement and thiamine
  • Reversible psychosocial and dental causes in the elderly — depression, isolation, food insecurity and dental pain are common, treatable, and often missed when clinicians fixate on excluding cancer
  • New diabetes, thyrotoxicosis or adrenal insufficiency as the explanation for weight loss with preserved appetite

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
Basic (all patients)FBC, U&E, LFTs, glucose/HbA1c, TSH, urinalysis, faecal occult blood or colorectal screening, chest X-ray, HIV testing where appropriate, review of age-appropriate cancer screeningCombined with the history and examination, this basic panel identifies the cause in most patients
Targeted (only with a localising clue or an abnormal basic result)Upper GI endoscopy, colonoscopy, coeliac serology, abdominal imaging, TB work-up, cortisol assessmentEscalate to CT, endoscopy or specialist testing only with a localising symptom, abnormal exam, abnormal labs, or a high-risk clinical context — not for weight loss alone
Fatal mistake

Ordering a "whole-body cancer screen" (blanket CT abdomen/pelvis, colonoscopy) purely because the word "weight loss" appears in the history, with no localising symptom or abnormal finding. Undirected imaging or colonoscopy has a very low malignancy yield when weight loss is the only feature — look for localising clues first, and use structured follow-up when the initial evaluation is negative.

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working plan for the vignette: a likely multifactorial cause (bereavement/depression, social isolation, dental pain, functional limitation) — but still complete the basic panel, since a psychosocial explanation does not exclude a coexisting organic cause in an older patient. Red flags needing urgent escalation regardless of the working impression: dysphagia, GI bleeding, haemoptysis, a palpable mass, significant lymphadenopathy, persistent fever/night sweats, severe anaemia, jaundice, or a very low BMI with rapid ongoing loss.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: assess hydration and refeeding risk before starting nutritional support in anyone significantly malnourished; correct any severe electrolyte disturbance found.

Definitive: treat the identified mechanism — dietitian input, dental review, depression/cognitive screening and social support for psychosocial causes; disease-specific treatment for confirmed endocrine, GI, infective or malignant causes.

Monitoring and failure: when the initial evaluation is negative, arrange objective follow-up — reweigh at a defined interval, reassess symptoms and intake, review medications, repeat examination — rather than abandoning the problem or repeating undirected tests indefinitely. Escalate if weight loss continues despite intervention.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Urgent same-visit escalation for any red-flag feature; outpatient work-up with structured follow-up for the well patient with a negative initial evaluation; multidisciplinary input (dietitian, social work, dental) for the frail older patient with a likely multifactorial cause, as in the vignette. Handover should state the percentage weight loss, the appetite pattern, and what has been screened for so far.

Two common student errors
  • Assuming weight loss in an older patient must be cancer, and missing dental, psychiatric, functional and social causes that are common and reversible.
  • Accepting "I've lost weight" without documenting the previous weight, current weight, and time period — this cannot be properly risk-stratified without the actual numbers.

Check your understanding

Q1What is the definition of clinically significant unintentional weight loss?

Answer. Losing at least 5% of usual body weight over 6–12 months.

Q2Why is asking about appetite direction ("decreased, the same, or increased?") such a high-yield early question?

Answer. Weight loss with reduced appetite points toward malignancy, depression, chronic infection or organ failure, while weight loss despite preserved or increased appetite points toward hyperthyroidism, uncontrolled diabetes, or malabsorption. A single answer rapidly reorganises the differential and directs the rest of the history.

Q3Name at least two criteria that identify a patient at high risk of refeeding syndrome.

Answer. BMI below 16 kg/m², more than 15% unintentional weight loss in 3–6 months, little or no nutritional intake for more than 10 days, or low potassium/phosphate/magnesium before feeding starts. Any of these should prompt dietitian involvement and cautious, monitored feeding with proactive electrolyte and thiamine replacement.

Q4Why is a "whole-body cancer screen" not recommended for weight loss without a localising feature?

Answer. Undirected imaging or colonoscopy performed purely because a patient has lost weight, with no localising symptom, abnormal exam finding, or abnormal basic blood test, has a very low yield for finding malignancy. A localising clue (dysphagia, GI bleeding, haemoptysis, a palpable mass) is what should trigger targeted investigation, not the symptom of weight loss alone.

Case scenario

A 70-year-old man with COPD has lost 7 kg over 4 months. His appetite is preserved, but he mentions occasional night sweats and some difficulty swallowing solid food over the last month. What is the most appropriate next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Urgent referral for upper GI endoscopy (two-week-wait equivalent) for suspected oesophageal malignancy. The dysphagia is a localising clue that substantially raises the pre-test probability of finding a cancer on endoscopy, unlike weight loss alone — this should not simply be attributed to his COPD or treated with an undirected screen.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 68-year-old woman reports "losing weight" over the past few months but cannot say how much. What is the most appropriate immediate step before any further investigation?

  1. Establish objective evidence of weight loss — previous documented weight, current weight, and the time period involved
  2. Refer to oncology for a general assessment
  3. Arrange colonoscopy
  4. Reassure her that weight fluctuation is normal with ageing
  5. Order an urgent CT chest/abdomen/pelvis
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Weight loss must be objectively confirmed and quantified before it can be properly risk-stratified — without knowing the percentage lost and the time frame, you cannot judge whether this meets the threshold for "clinically significant" weight loss or plan an appropriate work-up. E, C and B jump to investigation before the symptom is even confirmed. D risks missing a genuine and important symptom.

MCQ 2. A severely malnourished patient (BMI 15.5 kg/m², minimal oral intake for the past two weeks) is about to start nutritional support. What is the most important immediate consideration?

  1. Give a single bolus of IV glucose before starting feeds
  2. Withhold all nutrition until a full cancer work-up is complete
  3. Delay feeding until a dietitian review, which can wait several weeks
  4. Start full-calorie feeding immediately to correct the deficit as fast as possible
  5. Assess refeeding syndrome risk and feed cautiously with proactive electrolyte and thiamine replacement
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. This patient meets high-risk refeeding criteria (BMI <16, minimal intake for >10 days), so nutritional support must start cautiously, with dietitian input and proactive correction/monitoring of potassium, phosphate, magnesium, and thiamine cover, to avoid precipitating refeeding syndrome. D risks exactly the complication being guarded against. B and C inappropriately delay necessary nutritional care. A does not address the electrolyte and thiamine risks of refeeding and could worsen things.

Presentation 5Oncological emergencies

Cancer patients can deteriorate from the cancer itself, from a treatment complication, or from an unrelated acute illness — telling these apart quickly is often the whole diagnostic problem. A small group of oncological emergencies are common enough, and dangerous enough, that every junior doctor must recognise them instantly.

Figure 7.5 — overview. Overview of the approach to an acutely unwell patient with known or suspected cancer, linking the dominant presenting syndrome to neutropenic sepsis, metastatic spinal cord compression, superior vena cava obstruction, raised intracranial pressure, hypercalcaemia of malignancy and tumour lysis syndrome.

Figure 7.5a. Oncological emergencies, page 1: initial assessment and syndrome recognition, linking dominant presenting features to neutropenic sepsis, metastatic spinal cord compression, superior vena cava obstruction, raised intracranial pressure, hypercalcaemia and tumour lysis syndrome.

Figure 7.5b. Oncological emergencies, page 2: focused pathways for neutropenic sepsis and tumour lysis syndrome after anticancer therapy, with key investigations and time-critical treatment cues.

Figure 7.5c. Oncological emergencies, page 3: compression and thoracic syndromes, including metastatic spinal cord compression, superior vena cava obstruction and cardiac tamponade.

Figure 7.5d. Oncological emergencies, page 4: neurological, metabolic and abdominal syndromes, including raised intracranial pressure, hypercalcaemia of malignancy and malignant bowel obstruction.

Clinical vignette

Case. A 58-year-old woman with known metastatic breast cancer presents with confusion, constipation and generalised abdominal pain. Her calcium comes back at 3.4 mmol/L (corrected). Before reading on, decide: what is the most likely explanation, and what should happen right now?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — recognise the major patterns instantly

EmergencyRecognise by
Malignant spinal cord compressionBack pain (often the earliest symptom, may precede neurology by weeks) + leg weakness + sensory level + bladder/bowel dysfunction, in a patient with known or suspected cancer
Superior vena cava obstructionFacial/neck swelling, distended neck and chest wall veins, breathlessness, worse on bending forward or lying flat, in a patient with a mediastinal mass (commonly lung cancer or lymphoma)
Neutropenic sepsisFever in a patient who has had chemotherapy within the last 3 weeks — treated as an emergency by definition, whatever the patient looks like
Tumour lysis syndromeHigh potassium, high phosphate, high urate, low calcium, and acute kidney injury, typically within days of starting chemotherapy for a high-turnover tumour (leukaemia, lymphoma)
Hypercalcaemia of malignancyConfusion, constipation, polyuria/polydipsia, abdominal pain, "bones, stones, groans and psychiatric moans" — in a patient with known or occult malignancy
Malignant airway obstructionStridor, positional breathlessness, in a patient with head/neck or thoracic malignancy
High-yield pearl

New back pain in a patient with known cancer is spinal cord compression until proven otherwise. Neurological signs (weakness, a sensory level, sphincter disturbance) are a late finding — waiting for them before acting risks permanent paralysis. Act on the pain alone.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask about the cancer type, stage, and treatment history (which chemotherapy, when was the last cycle, any known bone/spinal/mediastinal metastases). Ask specifically about new back pain (site, whether worse lying flat or with coughing/straining, any leg weakness or numbness, any change in bladder or bowel habit — even subtle urinary hesitancy can be an early sign of cord compression), breathlessness and its positional pattern, and any recent chemotherapy timing (relevant to both neutropenic sepsis and tumour lysis risk).

Fatal mistake

Attributing new back pain in a cancer patient to "musculoskeletal strain" or simple bone-metastasis pain without an urgent scan. Cord compression is a race against time — every hour of delay before decompression reduces the chance of neurological recovery. Image urgently; do not wait for a neurological deficit to develop.

Step 3. Discriminating examination

For suspected cord compression: examine for a sensory level, leg weakness, reduced anal tone, and check for a palpable bladder (painless retention). For suspected SVC obstruction: look for facial plethora/swelling, distended non-pulsatile neck veins, and dilated collateral veins on the chest wall. For suspected hypercalcaemia: assess hydration and conscious level. For suspected tumour lysis: assess for signs of acute kidney injury and cardiac arrhythmia risk (hyperkalaemia).

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Must not miss
  • Cord compression before an irreversible neurological deficit develops — the single most time-critical "must not miss" in oncology; the outcome depends entirely on how quickly it is recognised and imaged
  • SVC obstruction causing airway compromise — a mediastinal mass can progress to critical airway narrowing
  • Severe hypercalcaemia (>3.5 mmol/L) or symptomatic hypercalcaemia at any level — can cause coma and cardiac arrhythmia if untreated
  • Tumour lysis syndrome in a patient just starting chemotherapy for a high-turnover tumour — a predictable, preventable complication if anticipated and monitored for
  • Neutropenic sepsis in anyone with recent chemotherapy and fever — treated as an emergency regardless of how well the patient looks

Step 5. Tiered investigation

Suspected emergencyKey investigationNotes
Cord compressionWhole-spine MRI, urgently (same day)Whole spine, not just the symptomatic level — metastases are frequently multifocal
SVC obstructionCT chest with contrastDefines the mass and the extent of venous obstruction/collaterals
HypercalcaemiaCorrected calcium, U&E, ECG (short QT, arrhythmia risk)Also send PTH and PTHrP if the malignant cause is not already obvious, to help guide longer-term management
Tumour lysis syndromeU&E, calcium, phosphate, urate, ECGCheck before and monitor closely after starting chemotherapy in high-risk tumours

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: hypercalcaemia of malignancy causing confusion and constipation, in a patient with known metastatic breast cancer — a common and treatable oncological emergency. Severity and urgency scale with the corrected calcium level and the degree of symptoms; confusion is a marker of significant severity regardless of the exact number.

Step 7. Management ladder

Cord compression: high-dose dexamethasone immediately (per local protocol) while an urgent whole-spine MRI is arranged; urgent oncology/spinal surgery/clinical oncology involvement for definitive treatment (radiotherapy or surgical decompression) — every hour matters.

SVC obstruction: sit the patient upright, give oxygen if hypoxic, and involve oncology urgently; corticosteroids are often given empirically while awaiting definitive treatment (radiotherapy, chemotherapy, or stenting depending on the underlying tumour and local practice) — avoid inserting further lines on the affected/obstructed side.

Hypercalcaemia: aggressive IV rehydration (0.9% saline) first, followed by an IV bisphosphonate once rehydrated (per local protocol) — the bisphosphonate takes 2–4 days to reach full effect, so fluids are the immediate priority. Denosumab is an option when hypercalcaemia is refractory to bisphosphonates or renal function limits their use. Treat the underlying malignancy once the patient is stabilised.

Tumour lysis syndrome: aggressive IV hydration before and during chemotherapy in high-risk patients, urate-lowering therapy (allopurinol or rasburicase per risk stratification and local protocol), and close monitoring and correction of electrolytes; this is largely a preventable emergency through anticipation in high-risk patients.

Monitoring and failure: reassess neurology frequently in cord compression even after steroids are started; recheck calcium/renal function regularly during hypercalcaemia treatment; escalate to critical care for any airway compromise or arrhythmia.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Suspected cord compression and SVC obstruction need same-day imaging and urgent oncology/acute oncology service involvement — do not let these wait for the next routine ward round. Hypercalcaemia and tumour lysis syndrome need admission for IV treatment and monitoring. Handover should explicitly flag the time-critical nature of suspected cord compression, since delay at any handover point directly costs the patient neurological function.

Fatal mistakes in oncological emergencies
  • Waiting for a neurological deficit before imaging suspected cord compression. Back pain alone in a cancer patient is enough to justify urgent whole-spine MRI.
  • Treating hypercalcaemia with a bisphosphonate alone, without aggressive fluids first. Fluids are the immediate priority; the bisphosphonate is slow-acting.
  • Not anticipating tumour lysis syndrome before starting chemotherapy in a high-turnover tumour — this is one of the few oncological emergencies that is largely preventable.
  • Delaying antibiotics in neutropenic sepsis to investigate a source first. The same time-critical rule that applies to all sepsis applies here: give antibiotics immediately, investigate in parallel.

Check your understanding

Q1What is the most important early symptom of malignant spinal cord compression, and why should you act on it before neurological signs appear?

Answer. New back pain in a patient with known or suspected cancer, which can precede neurological signs by weeks. Neurological deficit is a late finding, and outcome depends entirely on how quickly compression is recognised and treated — waiting for weakness or a sensory level before imaging risks permanent paralysis.

Q2What does "bones, stones, groans and psychiatric moans" refer to, and what is the correct order of treatment for hypercalcaemia of malignancy?

Answer. It is a mnemonic for the clinical features of hypercalcaemia: bone pain, renal stones, abdominal ("groans") symptoms such as constipation and pain, and psychiatric/neurological symptoms such as confusion. Treatment starts with aggressive IV fluid rehydration, followed by an IV bisphosphonate once rehydrated — fluids are the immediate priority because the bisphosphonate takes 2–4 days to work fully.

Q3What is the classic biochemical tetrad of tumour lysis syndrome?

Answer. High potassium, high phosphate, high urate, and low calcium, typically with acute kidney injury, occurring within days of starting chemotherapy for a high-turnover tumour such as leukaemia or lymphoma.

Q4Why must antibiotics never be delayed in suspected neutropenic sepsis to look for a source first?

Answer. Neutropenic sepsis is treated as an emergency by definition once fever occurs within roughly three weeks of chemotherapy, regardless of how well the patient looks, because a neutropenic patient can deteriorate extremely fast and the absence of a neutrophil response can mask how severe the underlying infection is. Antibiotics are given immediately, with the source investigated in parallel, not beforehand.

Case scenario

A 63-year-old man with prostate cancer and known vertebral bone metastases develops new urinary retention and bilateral leg weakness over two days, with no preceding trauma. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what is the immediate management?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Malignant spinal cord compression. Immediate management is high-dose dexamethasone (per local protocol) started without waiting for imaging, together with an urgent same-day whole-spine MRI and urgent oncology/spinal surgery referral for definitive treatment (radiotherapy or surgical decompression) — every hour of delay reduces the chance of neurological recovery.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A patient starting induction chemotherapy for high-grade lymphoma develops the following on day 2: potassium 6.2 mmol/L, phosphate 2.4 mmol/L, urate markedly elevated, corrected calcium 1.9 mmol/L, and rising creatinine. What is the diagnosis and the most important preventive measure that should have been in place?

  1. Neutropenic sepsis — antibiotics should have been given prophylactically
  2. SIADH — fluid restriction should have been started before chemotherapy
  3. Hypercalcaemia of malignancy — IV bisphosphonate should have been given before chemotherapy
  4. Tumour lysis syndrome — aggressive IV hydration and urate-lowering therapy should have been started before/during chemotherapy in this high-risk patient
  5. Adrenal crisis — stress-dose steroids should have been given before chemotherapy
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. High potassium, high phosphate, high urate, low calcium and rising creatinine shortly after starting chemotherapy for a high-turnover tumour is the classic tumour lysis syndrome tetrad; it is largely preventable with anticipatory IV hydration and urate-lowering therapy (allopurinol or rasburicase) in high-risk patients. C is wrong — hypercalcaemia causes high, not low, calcium. B and E are not supported by the biochemistry given. A is a separate emergency and would not explain this specific biochemical pattern.

MCQ 2. A patient with a known mediastinal mass develops facial and neck swelling with distended, non-pulsatile neck veins, worse when lying flat. What is the diagnosis and the correct first step in management?

  1. Superior vena cava obstruction — sit the patient upright, give oxygen if hypoxic, and refer urgently to oncology
  2. Hypercalcaemia of malignancy — start IV bisphosphonate
  3. Neutropenic sepsis — give empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics immediately
  4. Malignant spinal cord compression — give high-dose dexamethasone
  5. Tumour lysis syndrome — start aggressive IV hydration
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Facial/neck swelling, distended non-pulsatile neck veins and symptoms worse when lying flat in a patient with a mediastinal mass is superior vena cava obstruction; initial management is positional (sitting upright), oxygen if needed, and urgent oncology involvement, with corticosteroids often given empirically while definitive treatment is arranged. D describes a different emergency (back pain/neurology, not described here). E and B describe different biochemical emergencies with no supporting clinical picture given. C is unsupported — there is no mention of fever or recent chemotherapy.

Block 8 · Week 9 of the module
Rheumatological presentations

Presentation 1Joint pain

"Joint pain" does not automatically mean "arthritis." Before you chase a diagnosis, work out where the pain is really coming from, whether it is inflammatory or mechanical, and how many joints are involved. This simple sequence narrows the differential faster than any blood test.

Figure 8.1. Approach to joint pain from initial stabilisation and focused assessment through problem representation, differential diagnosis, joint aspiration and other investigations, management and reassessment.

Clinical vignette

Case. A 73-year-old man with diabetes and known rheumatoid arthritis presents with 18 hours of severe right-knee pain. He cannot weight-bear. The knee is warm, swollen and extremely painful on passive movement. Temperature is 37.3°C. Before reading on, decide: does the near-normal temperature make septic arthritis less likely?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

An acute hot, swollen, single joint is septic arthritis until proven otherwise. This is the one rule in this presentation that overrides everything else. Older, immunosuppressed, diabetic and already-rheumatoid patients can have septic arthritis with little or no fever and only a modest rise in inflammatory markers. Never use a normal temperature to talk yourself out of the diagnosis.

Fatal mistake

Being reassured by a normal or near-normal temperature in a hot, swollen joint. Older, diabetic and rheumatoid patients often have subtle septic arthritis with minimal fever — the joint findings matter more than the thermometer.

Step 2. History — six questions that organise everything else

Ask: exactly where is the pain (get the patient to point with one finger)? Is it inflammatory (morning stiffness lasting more than 30–60 minutes, better with movement, worse after rest) or mechanical (brief stiffness, worse with activity, better with rest)? How many joints — one, a few, or many? What is the pattern (symmetrical or asymmetrical, small or large joints, axial or peripheral)? How fast did it start (minutes to hours suggests trauma, crystal disease or infection; weeks to months suggests chronic inflammatory or degenerative disease)? And is there a red flag or systemic clue? Also ask about infection risk (recent joint injection, prosthetic joint, IV drug use, skin infection), extra-articular features (psoriasis, eye symptoms, oral or genital ulcers, GI or urinary symptoms in the preceding weeks), and drugs (diuretics can trigger gout; anticoagulants raise the risk of haemarthrosis).

In plain terms: an acute, hot, exquisitely painful monoarthritis in a diabetic, already-rheumatoid patient is septic arthritis until adequately excluded — the modest fever changes nothing.

Step 3. Examination pearl

Examination pearl

Look, feel, move — and always compare with the other side. True articular pain hurts with both active and passive movement, in multiple directions. Periarticular pain (tendon, bursa, enthesis) usually hurts with one specific movement, and passive movement is often relatively preserved. Severe pain on gentle passive movement strongly supports an intra-articular process. Always examine beyond the painful joint: hands, feet, spine, skin, nails and eyes — the diagnosis is often made outside the joint the patient points to.

Step 4. Schema — organise by number of joints and time course

PatternLeading causes
Monoarthritis (1 joint)Septic arthritis, gout/CPPD, trauma/haemarthrosis, early RA or psoriatic arthritis
Oligoarthritis (2–4 joints)Reactive arthritis, psoriatic arthritis, peripheral spondyloarthritis, crystal disease
Polyarthritis (≥5 joints)Rheumatoid arthritis (symmetrical small and large joints), viral arthritis, osteoarthritis, SLE
Must not miss
  • Septic arthritis — the pivotal emergency diagnosis in acute monoarthritis; delayed treatment causes irreversible joint destruction
  • A painful prosthetic joint — treat as infected until excluded via the appropriate specialist pathway
  • "Joint pain" that is not really arthritis — deep bony night pain unrelated to movement, with weight loss, points to malignancy, pathological fracture, osteonecrosis or osteomyelitis, not a routine arthritis work-up

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideVital signs, urgent joint aspiration if acute monoarthritisAcute monoarthritis should generally be aspirated to exclude infection — this is the pivotal test
Synovial fluidAppearance, WBC/differential, Gram stain, culture, polarised-light microscopy for crystalsNo single cell-count threshold proves or excludes infection; crystals do not exclude simultaneous infection, and a negative Gram stain does not exclude it either
LaboratoryFBC, CRP/ESR, renal/liver profile, blood cultures if infection suspectedSupports but never replaces synovial fluid analysis; serum urate may be normal during an acute gout attack
Targeted serologyRF/anti-CCP for a rheumatoid pattern; ANA/dsDNA/complement for a connective-tissue pattern; HLA-B27 only with a compatible spondyloarthritis pictureOrder to test a specific hypothesis — there is no universal "rheumatology screen," and broad testing produces incidental positives and confusion
ImagingPlain X-ray (chronic change), ultrasound (effusion/synovitis, guides aspiration), MRI (early sacroiliitis, osteomyelitis, occult fracture)Plain films are often normal in early inflammatory arthritis and are not sensitive for acute septic arthritis

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: septic arthritis until proven otherwise, given the acute hot monoarthritis in a diabetic, immunosuppressed (chronic RA) patient — the modest temperature does not lower suspicion. Do not inject intra-articular glucocorticoid until infection has been adequately excluded, and do not withhold aspiration or antibiotics while awaiting culture results in a genuinely unwell patient.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: assess for sepsis, take blood cultures, aspirate the joint urgently for cell count, Gram stain, crystals and culture before starting antibiotics — unless this would cause unsafe delay — and look for a primary source (skin, endocarditis).

Definitive: start appropriate empirical antimicrobial treatment promptly after sampling; involve orthopaedics and microbiology; treat confirmed crystal arthritis with NSAIDs, colchicine or steroids per usual protocol once infection is excluded; treat confirmed inflammatory arthritis (RA, PsA, SpA) with early rheumatology referral — do not delay referral while waiting for serology, since seronegative disease is common and early treatment improves outcomes.

Monitoring and failure: reassess the joint and the patient's systemic status regularly; failure to improve on treatment for presumed crystal arthritis should prompt reconsideration of coexisting infection.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit any suspected septic arthritis for IV antibiotics, source control and close monitoring. Persistent objective inflammatory polyarthritis needs early rheumatology referral even with normal serology or radiographs. Handover should state the aspiration findings, culture status, and whether steroid injection has (correctly) been withheld pending infection exclusion.

Two common student errors
  • Diagnosing gout from a normal serum urate alone — urate can fall during an acute attack and does not exclude the diagnosis.
  • Ordering RF, anti-CCP and ANA for every painful joint instead of letting the clinical pattern guide which test to order.

Check your understanding

Q1Why should a normal or near-normal temperature never be used to rule out septic arthritis?

Answer. Older, diabetic, immunosuppressed and already-rheumatoid patients frequently have subtle septic arthritis with little or no fever and only a modest rise in inflammatory markers. The joint findings (hot, swollen, severely painful on passive movement) matter more than the thermometer.

Q2How do you distinguish true articular pain from periarticular pain on examination?

Answer. Articular pain hurts with both active and passive movement, in multiple directions, and is often accompanied by an effusion or synovial swelling. Periarticular pain (tendon, bursa, enthesis) usually hurts with one specific movement, and passive movement is often relatively preserved.

Q3A synovial fluid sample shows monosodium urate crystals. Can you now stop worrying about infection?

Answer. No. Crystals do not exclude simultaneous septic arthritis, and a negative Gram stain does not exclude infection either. The sample should still be cultured, and clinical suspicion of infection should be reassessed if the patient fails to improve.

Q4Why is it wrong to order rheumatoid factor, anti-CCP and ANA for every patient with joint pain?

Answer. These tests should be ordered to test a specific clinical hypothesis (a rheumatoid pattern or a connective-tissue disease pattern), not as a routine screen. There is no universal "rheumatology screen" — broad testing in low pre-test-probability patients produces incidental positives and diagnostic confusion, and a negative result does not exclude the disease.

Case scenario

A 59-year-old man taking a thiazide diuretic wakes overnight with sudden severe pain and swelling of his first metatarsophalangeal joint. He also has a temperature of 37.8°C and mild swelling of the ipsilateral ankle. Serum urate is within the normal reference range. What is the most appropriate next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Aspirate the joint (or the most accessible involved joint) for cell count, Gram stain, culture and polarised-light microscopy for crystals. The presentation is highly suggestive of acute gout, and a normal serum urate does not exclude it since urate can fall during an acute attack — but the presence of fever and involvement beyond a single classic podagra joint means septic arthritis must still be actively excluded before the case is labelled "just gout."

MCQs

MCQ 1. Which of the following statements about synovial fluid analysis in acute monoarthritis is correct?

  1. A normal patient temperature excludes septic arthritis
  2. Identification of monosodium urate crystals excludes coexisting septic arthritis
  3. Monosodium urate crystals do not exclude coexisting septic arthritis
  4. A synovial white-cell count below 2,000/mm³ excludes infection
  5. A negative Gram stain reliably excludes septic arthritis
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. Crystal identification and infection can coexist, so finding crystals never rules out sepsis. E is wrong because Gram-stain sensitivity is limited and a negative result does not exclude infection. B is wrong for the same reason as C (it states the opposite). D is wrong because no single cell-count threshold reliably proves or excludes infection — the probability of sepsis rises with the white-cell count and neutrophil percentage but there is no absolute cut-off. A is wrong because older, diabetic and immunosuppressed patients with septic arthritis may have little or no fever.

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old man has six weeks of asymmetrical swelling of his left knee and right ankle, with heel pain and pitting of several fingernails. What is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Rheumatoid arthritis
  2. Reactive arthritis
  3. Psoriatic arthritis
  4. Osteoarthritis
  5. Acute gout
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. Asymmetrical large-joint oligoarthritis with enthesitis (heel pain) and nail pitting is a classic psoriatic arthritis pattern. A is wrong because rheumatoid arthritis is typically symmetrical and favours small joints of the hands and wrists. B is wrong because reactive arthritis follows a preceding gastrointestinal or genitourinary infection, which is not described here, and does not explain the nail changes. E is wrong because gout is usually monoarticular at onset (classically the first MTP joint), not an asymmetrical oligoarthritis with enthesitis. D is wrong because osteoarthritis does not cause nail pitting, enthesitis or inflammatory joint swelling.

Presentation 2Backache

About 90% of acute back pain is mechanical and improves within six weeks without imaging. The real clinical skill here is finding the small minority who have neurological compromise, infection, fracture, malignancy or inflammatory disease hiding inside a very common complaint.

Clinical vignette

Case. A 56-year-old man has had several days of bilateral leg pain (sciatica). He now reports difficulty starting urination, reduced awareness of bladder filling, and numbness around the perineum. Examination shows bilateral leg weakness, reduced saddle sensation and reduced anal tone. Before reading on, decide: what needs to happen in the next ten minutes?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — cauda equina is the emergency that must never be missed

New urinary retention or altered bladder sensation, saddle sensory disturbance, reduced anal tone, bowel disturbance, or bilateral/progressive leg weakness means cauda equina syndrome until proven otherwise. This is a true surgical emergency: get an urgent MRI and an immediate spinal-team discussion, with no delay for routine outpatient tests.

High-yield pearl

Don't just ask "any bladder problems?" — ask precisely: difficulty starting urination, reduced sensation of bladder filling, retention, overflow incontinence, loss of sensation wiping after the toilet, numbness around the perineum, genitals or inner thighs. "Urinary incontinence" is often a late or imprecise description; early urinary retention or altered bladder sensation is the more important, earlier clue.

Step 2. History and red flags

Ask the patient to point to the pain, clarify onset (sudden vs gradual), what worsens or relieves it (flexion, sitting, walking, coughing), and whether leg pain is worse than back pain and radiates below the knee (suggests nerve-root irritation, not simple referred mechanical pain). Screen deliberately for red flags: age under 20 or over 55 at first presentation, major or minor trauma (especially with osteoporosis or steroid use), constant or progressive pain, night or rest pain, thoracic pain, previous cancer, unexplained weight loss, fever or night sweats, immunosuppression or IV drug use, and any bladder, bowel or sexual dysfunction or saddle disturbance.

In plain terms: bilateral sciatica evolving into bladder disturbance and saddle numbness is cauda equina syndrome, a time-critical surgical emergency, not "worsening sciatica."

Step 3. Discriminating examination

Examine in a practical order: standing (spinal curves, deformity, active flexion/extension), gait (antalgic, forward-flexed "stenotic," foot drop, spastic), palpation (paraspinal tenderness/spasm suggests mechanical pain; focal midline vertebral tenderness raises concern for fracture, infection or malignancy), and full lower-limb neurology (tone, power, sensation, reflexes, plantar responses) mapped to a root, multiple roots, or an upper-motor-neuron pattern. Straight-leg raise is positive only when it reproduces radicular leg pain below the knee, not just back or hamstring discomfort. When cauda equina is suspected, assess saddle/perianal sensation and anal tone — a digital rectal exam is not routine for every back pain patient, but is indicated here.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

CategoryKey clues
Mechanical/nonspecificFollows lifting/twisting, worse with activity, better with rest, no red flags, normal neurology
RadicularLeg pain > back pain, below-knee radiation, positive straight-leg raise, dermatomal deficit
InflammatoryGradual onset, young adult, improves with activity, morning stiffness, alternating buttock pain
Serious spinal pathologyCauda equina, fracture, malignancy/myeloma, discitis/vertebral osteomyelitis/epidural abscess
Referred non-spinalAAA, pancreatitis, renal colic/pyelonephritis, pelvic pathology — often unaffected by spinal movement
Must not miss
  • Cauda equina syndrome — back pain often precedes established paralysis or incontinence; waiting for these to develop sacrifices the chance of recovery
  • Malignant spinal cord compression — progressive pain worse lying flat, followed by weakness and sphincter change, in a patient with known or occult cancer; treat as an emergency and image the whole spine, not just the symptomatic level
  • Spinal infection (discitis/vertebral osteomyelitis/epidural abscess) — absence of high fever does not exclude it, especially in diabetic or immunocompromised patients
  • Ruptured/leaking AAA — sudden severe back/abdominal pain with hypotension in an older smoker is not a spinal problem; always examine the abdomen

Step 5. Tiered investigation

ScenarioInvestigation
Uncomplicated mechanical pain, no red flagsNo routine bloods or imaging in the first 6 weeks
Suspected radiculopathyCareful root-specific exam first; MRI if severe/progressive deficit, cauda equina suspected, symptoms persist, or intervention is planned
Suspected cauda equina/cord compressionUrgent (same-day) MRI, whole spine if malignancy/infection is a concern
Suspected fracturePlain radiographs; MRI if neurological compromise or malignancy/infection uncertainty
Suspected infectionFBC, ESR/CRP, blood cultures before antibiotics if stable, urgent MRI
Suspected malignancy/myelomaFBC, calcium, renal/liver profile, ESR/CRP, serum/urine protein electrophoresis, MRI for neural compression
Suspected inflammatory back painESR/CRP, HLA-B27 in context, sacroiliac imaging/MRI

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: cauda equina syndrome — the combination of bilateral neurology, saddle sensory change, reduced anal tone and bladder disturbance leaves no room for a "wait and reassess" approach. This overrides the usual "most back pain doesn't need imaging" rule entirely.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate (cauda equina/cord compression): urgent MRI, immediate spinal-surgical/neurosurgical discussion, and clear documentation of the exact timing and progression of symptoms — do not delay for routine outpatient tests.

Immediate (suspected infection): blood cultures before antibiotics if the patient is stable enough to allow it, urgent MRI, and empirical antibiotics guided by local protocol and microbiology input.

Definitive: surgical decompression for confirmed cauda equina/cord compression; disease-specific treatment for confirmed infection, malignancy or inflammatory disease; conservative management (analgesia, staying active, physiotherapy) plus clear safety-netting for uncomplicated mechanical pain.

Monitoring and failure: review mechanical back pain that fails to improve as expected, or that develops any new red flag, rather than assuming it will simply keep settling.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Same-hour spinal-team involvement for suspected cauda equina or cord compression; admission for suspected spinal infection or unstable fracture; safe discharge with clear red-flag safety-netting and no imaging for uncomplicated mechanical pain. Handover should explicitly document the exact time of onset and progression of any neurological or sphincter symptoms — this timeline directly affects surgical urgency and prognosis.

Fatal mistakes with backache
  • Waiting for established paralysis or incontinence before acting on suspected cord compression or cauda equina. Back pain often precedes the neurological deficit — act on the pain and the early bladder symptoms.
  • Assuming a lumbar MRI excludes cord compression. It does not assess the thoracic or cervical spine — image the whole spine when malignancy or infection is possible.
  • Excluding infection because there is no fever. Spinal infection can present with pain alone, especially in diabetic or immunocompromised patients.
  • Missing a ruptured AAA by focusing only on the spine in an older patient with sudden severe back pain — always examine the abdomen and pulses.

Check your understanding

Q1List the key red-flag symptoms and signs of cauda equina syndrome.

Answer. New urinary retention or altered bladder sensation, overflow incontinence, saddle (perineal/perianal/inner-thigh) sensory disturbance, reduced anal tone, bowel disturbance, and bilateral or progressive leg weakness. Any of these in a patient with back or leg pain means cauda equina syndrome until proven otherwise.

Q2Why is asking "any bladder problems?" not good enough?

Answer. "Urinary incontinence" is often a late or imprecise description of the problem. The earlier and more important clue is new urinary retention or altered bladder sensation (difficulty starting the stream, reduced awareness of bladder filling). You must ask specifically about these rather than relying on a vague general question.

Q3A patient with known prostate cancer has a normal lumbar MRI. Can you exclude malignant cord compression?

Answer. No. A lumbar MRI covers the cauda equina and conus but not the thoracic or cervical spine. If malignant cord compression is suspected, image the whole spine, since metastatic disease can affect multiple levels.

Q4A 29-year-old develops lumbar pain after lifting a box, worse on bending, better lying down, with no leg pain, fever, weight loss or neurological signs. What investigation is needed now?

Answer. None. This is uncomplicated mechanical back pain with no red flags; routine bloods and imaging are not indicated in the first six weeks. Manage with analgesia, staying active, and safety-netting advice to return if red flags develop.

Case scenario

A 68-year-old woman with a history of breast cancer develops two weeks of worsening thoracic back pain, worse when lying flat. She now has mild bilateral leg weakness with brisk knee reflexes and extensor plantar responses, but no bladder or bowel symptoms yet. What is the most appropriate next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Treat as malignant spinal cord compression and arrange urgent whole-spine MRI with immediate oncology and spinal-team involvement. Back pain frequently precedes weakness and sphincter disturbance in cord compression — waiting for these to appear sacrifices the chance of neurological recovery, so this must be treated as an emergency now, not once bladder symptoms develop.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 32-year-old man has two days of lumbar pain after lifting furniture. It is worse on bending and improves lying down. There is no leg pain, no red flags, and examination shows only paraspinal spasm with normal power, sensation and reflexes. What is the most appropriate management?

  1. Urgent MRI of the lumbar spine
  2. Immediate referral to spinal surgery
  3. Strict bed rest for two weeks
  4. Plain lumbar radiograph
  5. Reassurance, advice to stay active, simple analgesia, and safety-net advice
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. This is uncomplicated mechanical back pain with no red flags and a normal neurological examination; the evidence-based approach is reassurance, staying active and analgesia, with no imaging in the first six weeks. A and D are wrong because imaging is not indicated without red flags and would risk finding incidental degenerative changes. B is wrong because there is no neurological deficit or red flag to justify surgical referral. C is wrong because prolonged bed rest delays recovery and is not recommended.

MCQ 2. A 54-year-old man develops sudden urinary retention, saddle numbness and reduced anal tone on a background of worsening bilateral sciatica. What is the most appropriate immediate step?

  1. Urgent MRI and immediate discussion with the spinal team
  2. Plain lumbar radiograph
  3. Reassurance and discharge with safety-net advice
  4. Routine outpatient MRI within two weeks
  5. Oral analgesia and physiotherapy referral
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. This is cauda equina syndrome until proven otherwise, and it is a surgical emergency requiring urgent MRI and immediate spinal-team discussion without delay. D is wrong because any delay risks permanent neurological deficit. E and C are wrong because they fail to recognise an emergency requiring immediate action. B is wrong because plain radiographs do not show the cauda equina or a compressive lesion.

Presentation 3Skin rash

A rash is a clinical sign, not a diagnosis. Before naming it, answer four questions in order: is the patient systemically unwell, what is the primary lesion, where is it distributed, and what is the time course and likely trigger? This sequence — morphology, distribution, chronology — solves far more cases than blood tests.

Figure 8.2. Approach to skin rash from immediate red-flag assessment through morphology and distribution, differential diagnosis, focused investigations, synthesis, treatment and safety-netting.

Clinical vignette

Case. A 19-year-old student develops fever, vomiting and rapidly progressive purple lesions over the legs. He is confused, tachycardic and hypotensive. The lesions do not blanch. Before reading on, decide: does the absence of neck stiffness make this less likely to be meningococcal disease?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — check this before you look closely at the rash

Before examining the rash in detail, assess airway/breathing, pulse/BP/perfusion, temperature, oxygen saturation and mental state. A febrile, shocked or confused patient with a non-blanching rash is meningococcal sepsis until proven otherwise, and treatment must never wait for confirmatory tests.

Fatal mistake

Waiting for investigations, or being reassured by the absence of neck stiffness, before treating suspected meningococcal sepsis. Meningism may be completely absent in meningococcaemia — give antibiotics immediately on clinical suspicion.

Step 2. History — chronology, drugs, and exposures

Establish exact onset, where the first lesion appeared, direction and speed of spread, and how individual lesions evolve (flat to raised, vesicle to crust, wheal resolving within hours vs a persistent pigmented lesion). Ask about itch, pain or burning (severe pain in a widespread rash should never be dismissed as "allergy"), and mucosal symptoms (mouth, eye or genital involvement is a red flag). Build a drug timeline — when each drug was started relative to rash onset — since the drug history is often the single most important investigation in this presentation. Ask about exposures: household contacts, travel, new cosmetics, detergents or occupational chemicals, and sun exposure.

In plain terms: a young man with fever, shock, confusion and a rapidly progressive non-blanching purpuric rash has meningococcal sepsis, and is treated immediately, not investigated first.

Step 3. Examination pearl — describe before you diagnose

Examination pearl

Examine the whole skin surface, including scalp, mouth, eyes, palms, soles, flexures, nails and genital mucosa when indicated. Build a systematic description: primary lesion (macule, papule, plaque, vesicle, bulla, pustule, purpura, ulcer), secondary change (scale, crust, erosion, lichenification), colour, distribution, configuration, and — critically — whether it blanches. Press a glass or transparent surface against the lesion: non-blanching means extravasated blood (petechiae, purpura, ecchymosis) and, in a febrile patient, must trigger consideration of meningococcal sepsis.

Step 4. Schema — build the differential from morphology

Dominant morphologyKey differentials
Morbilliform/maculopapularDrug eruption, viral exanthem, secondary syphilis
Vesicular/bullousHSV, herpes zoster, varicella, pemphigoid, SJS/TEN, drug eruption
PustularFolliculitis, candidiasis, pustular psoriasis, AGEP
WhealsAcute/chronic urticaria (each lesion resolves <24h); urticarial vasculitis if persistent/painful/purpuric
PurpuricSepsis, platelet disorder, coagulopathy, small-vessel vasculitis
Must not miss
  • Meningococcal sepsis — fever, shock or confusion plus a non-blanching rash; treat immediately
  • Stevens–Johnson syndrome/TEN — skin pain, dusky/targetoid lesions, blistering, or mucosal erosions after a new drug; an emergency even before extensive skin detachment develops
  • DRESS — fever, facial oedema, widespread rash, lymphadenopathy and organ dysfunction (eosinophilia, deranged LFTs/renal function), typically weeks after starting a culprit drug (classically allopurinol, antiepileptics)
  • Erythroderma — generalised erythema and scaling over more than 90% of skin threatens temperature regulation and fluid balance; treat as a medical emergency with an ABCDE approach
  • Angioedema involving the tongue, pharynx or larynx — a threatened airway

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
Febrile/unwellBlood cultures, FBC with differential, renal/hepatic profile, CRP, coagulation, lactate/gas if shockedDo not delay treatment for potentially fatal infection while awaiting confirmation
Purpura/suspected vasculitisFBC/platelets, coagulation, renal/liver function, CRP/ESR, urinalysisUrinalysis is crucial — a "skin rash" may actually be systemic vasculitis with glomerular involvement
Vesicular lesionsVesicle fluid for viral PCR (HSV/VZV)Confirms the specific viral cause
Scaly/annular lesionsSkin scraping from the active edge for KOH microscopy and fungal cultureAvoids inappropriately treating a fungal infection with repeated topical steroids, which can mask and worsen it
Suspected severe drug reactionFBC with eosinophils, renal/liver profile, urinalysis, organ-specific tests as guided by symptomsConfirms multisystem involvement in DRESS/SJS-TEN; stop the suspected culprit drug promptly and document it
Persistent/unclear diagnosisSkin biopsy ± direct immunofluorescence (contact dermatology first if immunofluorescence is being considered — specimen handling differs)Histology must be interpreted alongside the clinical picture, not in isolation

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: meningococcal sepsis — the combination of fever, shock, confusion and a rapidly spreading non-blanching purpuric rash is treated as this diagnosis immediately, with investigations run in parallel, not sequentially before treatment.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate (meningococcal sepsis): ABCDE assessment, sepsis resuscitation bundle, immediate appropriate parenteral antibiotics, blood cultures and other samples taken without delaying treatment, senior and critical-care involvement.

Immediate (SJS/TEN or DRESS): stop the suspected culprit drug, ABCDE assessment, assess body-surface involvement and fluid status, urgent dermatology (plus ophthalmology for mucosal/ocular involvement) and critical-care input, monitor renal function/electrolytes/temperature/infection, avoid unnecessary adhesive trauma to fragile skin.

Definitive: targeted antimicrobial therapy for confirmed infection; supportive/specialist management for autoimmune blistering disease, vasculitis or connective-tissue disease per biopsy and serology results; topical or systemic treatment for confirmed inflammatory dermatoses.

Monitoring and failure: reassess extent of skin involvement and organ function serially in severe drug reactions; a rash failing to respond to appropriate initial treatment warrants dermatology re-review rather than repeated empirical steroid courses.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Immediate resuscitation and critical care for suspected meningococcal sepsis or erythroderma; urgent same-day dermatology/ophthalmology/critical-care referral for suspected SJS/TEN or DRESS; routine dermatology referral for persistent or diagnostically uncertain rashes. Handover should state the rash description (morphology, distribution, blanching), the drug timeline, and any mucosal or systemic involvement found.

Two common student errors
  • Writing "erythematous rash" in the notes without describing morphology, distribution or blanching — this is not a usable clinical description.
  • Calling every non-blanching rash "vasculitis" — thrombocytopenia, coagulopathy and sepsis can all cause purpura and must be actively considered.

Check your understanding

Q1What is the single most important action in a febrile, shocked patient with a non-blanching rash?

Answer. Give immediate parenteral antibiotics as part of a sepsis resuscitation bundle. Meningococcal sepsis must be treated on clinical suspicion; investigations (blood cultures and other samples) are taken alongside treatment but must never delay it.

Q2How do you test whether a rash is blanching or non-blanching, and why does it matter?

Answer. Press a glass or other transparent surface against the lesion. If the colour disappears (blanches), the redness is from blood within vessels. If it does not blanch, this indicates extravasated blood (petechiae, purpura or ecchymosis) and, in a febrile patient, must trigger consideration of meningococcal sepsis, though other causes such as thrombocytopenia, coagulopathy and vasculitis are also possible.

Q3How does urticaria differ from urticarial vasculitis in terms of how long individual lesions last?

Answer. Typical urticarial wheals are pruritic and each individual lesion resolves within less than 24 hours. Lesions that persist longer than this, especially if painful, purpuric, or leaving pigmentation behind, suggest urticarial vasculitis rather than ordinary urticaria.

Q4Why is a drug timeline described as "often the single most important investigation" in a suspected drug eruption?

Answer. Because establishing exactly when each drug (prescribed, recently stopped, over-the-counter, herbal) was started relative to rash onset identifies the likely culprit drug, which must be stopped promptly. This is often more diagnostically useful than any blood test, and it directly guides management in severe reactions such as DRESS and SJS/TEN.

Case scenario

A 34-year-old woman started an antiepileptic drug four weeks ago. She now has fever, facial swelling, a widespread morbilliform rash, cervical lymphadenopathy, blood eosinophilia and deranged liver function tests. What is the most likely diagnosis?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. DRESS (drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms). The combination of fever, facial oedema, widespread rash, lymphadenopathy, eosinophilia and organ dysfunction beginning several weeks after starting a known culprit drug class (antiepileptics) is the classic DRESS picture. Management requires stopping the drug immediately, full organ-function assessment, and urgent dermatology/medical review.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A febrile, confused patient has a rapidly spreading non-blanching purpuric rash but no neck stiffness. What is the most appropriate immediate action?

  1. Give immediate parenteral antibiotics as part of sepsis resuscitation
  2. Reassure the patient that meningococcal disease is unlikely given the absence of neck stiffness
  3. Observe for progression of the rash before treating
  4. Wait for blood culture results before giving antibiotics
  5. Perform a lumbar puncture before starting any treatment
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Meningism may be completely absent in meningococcaemia, so the absence of neck stiffness must not delay treatment; antibiotics should be given immediately on clinical suspicion. E is wrong because lumbar puncture is not required before treatment and should never delay it. D is wrong because treatment must not wait for culture confirmation in suspected meningococcal sepsis. B is wrong because it uses a falsely reassuring sign. C is wrong because observation risks rapid deterioration and death.

MCQ 2. A 30-year-old has an itchy, enlarging annular plaque with a raised, scaly edge and central clearing. It was treated with topical steroids for two weeks with worsening of the lesion. What is the most appropriate next investigation?

  1. Repeat course of a more potent topical steroid
  2. Skin biopsy with direct immunofluorescence
  3. Skin scraping from the active edge for KOH microscopy and fungal culture
  4. Patch testing
  5. Serum autoantibody panel (ANA, complement)
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. An enlarging annular, scaly-edged plaque with central clearing that has worsened on topical steroids is characteristic of dermatophyte (fungal) infection being masked and worsened by steroid treatment; a scraping from the active edge for KOH microscopy and culture confirms this. B is wrong because biopsy is reserved for cases where the diagnosis remains unclear after simpler tests. D is wrong because patch testing investigates allergic contact dermatitis, which does not typically produce this annular scaly appearance. A is wrong because continuing steroids on an undiagnosed possible fungal infection will worsen it. E is wrong because there are no systemic or connective-tissue features here to justify an autoantibody panel.

Block 9 · Week 10 of the module
Acute medicine presentations

Presentation 1The acutely deteriorating patient

Being called about a sick patient is one of the most stressful moments of being a junior doctor. Your job is not to know every answer. Your job is to triage fast, run a systematic A-to-E assessment, start basic stabilising treatment, and escalate early and clearly.

Figure 9.1. Structured approach to the acutely deteriorating patient: A–E stabilisation, focused history and examination, problem representation, danger-ranked differential, investigations, management by domain, escalation and reassessment.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. You are called: "Bed 12, NEWS just jumped from 1 to 6, nurse is worried." Before you leave for the ward, decide: what three things do you need to find out first?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — triage on the phone, before you even move

Ask three things immediately. Is the patient conscious and breathing? (If not, this is a peri-arrest — call for help now.) Is the nurse worried? (A nurse's sense that a patient is "off" is often more reliable than the numbers alone.) What is the current NEWS2 score, and which parameters are driving it? A NEWS2 score of 5 or more, or a sudden jump from a low baseline, is a major red flag regardless of the absolute number. While walking to the ward, find out the admission reason, the patient's normal baseline (a saturation of 90% may be normal for a chronic CO2 retainer but life-threatening for someone previously fit), and their escalation plan/ceiling of care.

NEWS2 (National Early Warning Score 2)

ParameterScores higher with
Respiratory rateVery low (≤8) or high (≥21/min, especially ≥25)
Oxygen saturationLow (using scale 2 if the patient is a known CO2 retainer on target 88–92%)
Air or oxygenAny supplemental oxygen adds 2 points
Systolic BPVery low or very high
Heart rateVery low (≤40) or high (≥111, especially ≥131)
ConsciousnessAny level other than fully alert (voice, pain, unresponsive, or new confusion)
TemperatureVery low or very high

Interpretation: Total score 0–4 is low risk (routine monitoring); 5–6, or any single parameter scoring 3, is medium risk and needs an urgent clinical review; 7 or more is high risk and needs emergency assessment, usually by a critical care team. A score of 5 or more, or any sudden rise, should trigger urgent escalation regardless of the exact banding.

High-yield pearl

"Are you worried about this patient?" is one of the most useful questions you can ask a nurse. A nurse's subjective concern frequently comes before any measurable change in the observations — trust it.

Step 2. The bedside assessment — A to E, in the order things actually kill

Airway: Check patency; listen for gurgling, snoring or stridor. A GCS of 8 or less usually means the patient cannot protect their own airway and needs urgent senior/anaesthetic input. Breathing: Check respiratory rate and saturations, and auscultate. Target sats are 94–98% for most patients, but 88–92% for known CO2 retainers. Give 15L high-flow oxygen via a non-rebreathe mask immediately if the patient is critically unwell — hypoxia kills faster than hypercapnia, so do not withhold oxygen out of concern for CO2 retention. Circulation: Check BP, heart rate, capillary refill and JVP. Get wide-bore IV access (even a small cannula beats none in an emergency). Review fluid balance and electrolytes. Disability: Check GCS/AVPU, and always check capillary glucose — hypoglycaemia is a rapidly reversible cause of altered consciousness and a classic stroke mimic. Check pupils. Exposure: Fully expose the patient to look for rashes (anaphylaxis, sepsis, meningitis), bleeding, or signs of DVT/cellulitis — while preserving dignity and avoiding hypothermia.

Step 3. Reassess after every intervention

Examination pearl

A-to-E is not a one-off checklist — it is a loop. After every intervention (oxygen given, fluid bolus run, nebuliser finished), go back and reassess the same parameter. "I gave a fluid bolus" is not a complete action. "I gave a fluid bolus and the BP rose from 82/50 to 96/60" is.

Step 4. Schema — the scenarios behind most deterioration calls

ScenarioKey recognition points
SepsisNEWS2 ≥5 or organ dysfunction with suspected infection — start the Sepsis Six (see Block 5 for detail)
Acute coronary syndromeCentral crushing pain radiating to arm/jaw, sweating, nausea; get a 12-lead ECG immediately
Pulmonary embolismSudden breathlessness, pleuritic pain, hypoxia with a clear chest and normal CXR, especially post-operative
Upper GI bleedHaematemesis or melaena; Glasgow-Blatchford score guides urgency of endoscopy
HypoglycaemiaGlucose <4 mmol/L — check first in any confused or deteriorating patient
HyperkalaemiaK⁺ >6.5 mmol/L or ECG changes — protect the heart with IV calcium gluconate first, then shift potassium
Seizure >5 minutesStatus epilepticus — IV lorazepam or PR/buccal diazepam/midazolam, call for senior help
AnaphylaxisAn airway emergency — IM adrenaline 500mcg into the anterolateral thigh immediately, lie flat with legs raised
Must not miss
  • A GCS of 8 or less — the airway is at risk regardless of the underlying cause; get anaesthetic help early
  • Hypoglycaemia in any confused or deteriorating patient — check glucose before attributing confusion to anything else
