  • A worried nurse with normal-looking numbers — this still deserves urgent review

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideFull vital signs/NEWS2, capillary glucose, 12-lead ECG, urinalysisFast, cheap, and changes the immediate plan
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, LFTs, troponin (if cardiac), venous or arterial blood gas with lactateA VBG/ABG gives an immediate lactate and often a potassium/glucose while formal bloods are pending
ImagingChest X-ray as indicated by the differentialScreens for pneumonia, pulmonary oedema, pneumothorax

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Form a working impression from the A-to-E findings and the clinical context, then commit to it while staying ready to revise it. Do not just keep "ruling things out" without a working diagnosis in mind. Risk is stratified by the NEWS2 trend, the response to initial treatment, and the ceiling of care already agreed for the patient.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: Treat what you find on the A-to-E as you find it. Do not wait to complete the whole assessment before treating an obvious airway or breathing problem.

Definitive: Scenario-specific management (Sepsis Six, ACS bundle, anaphylaxis protocol, seizure protocol, and so on), escalated according to severity.

Monitoring and failure: Reassess after every intervention. Escalate to a senior immediately if the patient is not responding to initial treatment, the NEWS2 is 5 or more, there is a sudden drop in consciousness, or you are dealing with a specific high-risk emergency (sepsis, chest pain, status epilepticus, anaphylaxis). Put out a crash/MET call (2222) if you are unsure or out of your depth — no one is ever criticised for being cautious.

Step 8. Disposition and handover — use SBAR

Before calling a senior, sit down with the drug chart, observations, most recent bloods and notes in front of you, so you are not scrambling mid-call. Structure the call using SBAR: Situation (your name, role, location, and the headline — state clearly that the patient is unstable and what you suspect), Background (name/age/hospital number, admission reason, relevant history only, and the ceiling of care), Assessment (NEWS2 and vital-sign breakdown, your A-to-E findings, any investigation results, and your clinical impression), and Recommendation (be explicit about what you want — "please come and review this patient now," not "please cast an eye over them"). Document everything: A-to-E findings, impression, management plan, and who you spoke to — if it is not written down, it did not happen.

Fatal mistakes with the deteriorating patient
  • Working through the alphabet in strict order without treating a life-threatening finding as soon as you find it. A-to-E is not a checklist to complete before acting — treat as you go.
  • Not checking glucose in a confused or deteriorating patient. Hypoglycaemia is rapidly reversible and easily missed if you do not actively check for it.
  • Vague escalation calls ("could you just cast an eye over this patient?") that leave the senior unsure what is actually being asked, and delay the specific action needed.
  • Ignoring a worried nurse because the numbers "look fine." Subjective concern often comes before objective deterioration.

Check your understanding

Q1What is the first thing you should establish over the phone before going to see a deteriorating patient?

Answer. Whether the patient is conscious and breathing (a peri-arrest needs help called now), whether the nurse is worried, and the current NEWS2 score and which parameters are driving it.

Q2Why should oxygen never be withheld from a critically hypoxic patient who is also a known CO2 retainer?

Answer. Hypoxia kills faster than hypercapnia. Give high-flow oxygen for critical illness, then titrate down to the patient's target range (88–92% for CO2 retainers) once stable.

Q3A patient's fluid bolus is documented as "given." Why is this an incomplete entry?

Answer. A-to-E is a loop — every intervention must be followed by reassessment of the same parameter. The entry should record the effect, e.g. "BP rose from 82/50 to 96/60 after the bolus."

Q4What NEWS2 score, or what pattern, should trigger urgent senior escalation?

Answer. A total score of 5 or more, a single parameter scoring 3, or any sudden rise from a low baseline — regardless of the absolute number.

Case scenario

You are the on-call FY2. A nurse calls you about a 70-year-old man on the surgical ward, three days post-hip replacement. His NEWS2 has risen from 1 to 4 (RR 24, sats 95% on air, HR 108). He looks slightly breathless but is talking normally and is not distressed. The nurse says "he's probably fine, I just have to tell you because of the score." What is the most appropriate next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Go and assess him yourself with a full A-to-E now — do not defer this because he "looks fine" on a passing report. A rising respiratory rate and tachycardia three days after hip surgery should raise concern for pulmonary embolism until proven otherwise, and this needs bedside review, not a phone-only reassurance.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 58-year-old man on the ward has a NEWS2 of 6. On assessment his GCS is 7, he is snoring loudly, and his oxygen saturation is 88% on air. What is the single most appropriate immediate action?

  1. Send an arterial blood gas
  2. Complete the full A-to-E assessment before intervening
  3. Give IV fluids and repeat observations in 30 minutes
  4. Request an urgent CT head
  5. Manage the airway and call for anaesthetic/senior help immediately
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. A GCS of 8 or less with snoring (airway noise) means the airway is at immediate risk — this must be addressed first, before any test or the rest of the assessment. A: an ABG is useful but does not address the immediate airway threat. D: imaging is not the priority until the airway is secure. C: fluids do not address an airway problem and a 30-minute delay is unsafe. B: A-to-E findings are treated as you find them, not held until the whole assessment is finished.

MCQ 2. You are escalating a deteriorating patient to your registrar by phone using SBAR. Which of the following best fits the "Recommendation" component of the call?

  1. "His NEWS2 is 7, mainly driven by respiratory rate and oxygen saturation."
  2. "Could you maybe just have a quick look when you get a chance?"
  3. "Please come and review him now — I'm concerned about worsening sepsis."
  4. "He was admitted three days ago with community-acquired pneumonia."
  5. "I'm the FY1 covering ward 12, calling about the patient in bed 4."
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. The Recommendation must state explicitly what you want done and when. A is the Assessment component. D is Background. E is the introduction/Situation component. B is a vague, non-specific request that leaves the senior unsure what is actually needed — exactly the kind of escalation call to avoid.

Presentation 2Acute drug poisoning (overdose)

Overdoses account for around 10–15% of acute medical admissions in the UK. About a third involve more than one drug, and around half of patients have also taken alcohol — so never assume a single, simple ingestion. The two skills that matter most are recognising a toxidrome from the pattern of vital signs and pupils, and knowing which few antidotes and interventions genuinely change outcome. Most overdoses need supportive care and time, not a specific antidote.

Figure 9.2. Eight-step approach to acute drug poisoning or overdose, from immediate stabilisation and toxidrome assessment through investigations, treatment, toxicology advice, mental-health assessment, handover and reassessment.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 24-year-old woman is brought in by ambulance, found unresponsive at home with an empty paracetamol packet and a note. GCS 14, HR 88, BP 118/76, RR 14. Before reading on, decide: what is the single most important piece of history to establish right now, and why does the timing matter so much?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Start with ABCDE, as with any deteriorating patient. Airway: a GCS of 8 or less cannot reliably protect the airway. Breathing: respiratory depression is the hallmark of opioid and sedative overdose. Circulation: arrhythmia and hypotension can occur with tricyclics, beta-blockers and calcium-channel blockers. Disability: conscious level, pupils, glucose. Many overdose deaths happen because of airway compromise and aspiration, not the drug itself — protecting the airway is often the single most important early action.

High-yield pearl

Always check capillary glucose in any reduced-consciousness overdose. Insulin, sulfonylureas and alcohol can all cause hypoglycaemia, which is instantly treatable and easily missed if you do not actively look for it.

Step 2. History — the details that change management

Establish exactly what was taken (drug name, formulation, how many/what dose), when (timing is everything for the paracetamol treatment nomogram and activated charcoal window), whether it was a single acute ingestion or staggered over time, and what else was taken (co-ingestion, especially alcohol, is common and changes risk). Get collateral history from the ambulance crew, family, or any note found. Ask about psychiatric history, previous overdoses, and current suicidal intent, since this shapes both medical and psychiatric management. Establish the patient's weight, since many toxicology calculations, including the paracetamol nomogram cut-offs, are weight- or time-based.

Fatal mistake

Not pinning down the exact timing of a paracetamol overdose. Treatment decisions — the charcoal window, when to send the level, whether to start treatment empirically — are entirely time-dependent. "Sometime today" is not good enough.

Step 3. Discriminating examination — reading the signs of poisoning

Use this bedside table linking examination findings to likely poison classes, in the same way you would use a toxidrome:

SignConsider
HypoventilationOpioids, ethanol, benzodiazepines, GHB/GBL
Pinpoint pupilsOpioids, organophosphates
Dilated pupilsAnticholinergics, tricyclic antidepressants, stimulants (amphetamine, cocaine)
BradycardiaBeta-blockers, digoxin, GHB/GBL, calcium channel blockers, organophosphates
TachyarrhythmiaTricyclics, anticholinergics, caffeine, theophylline, digoxin, stimulants, antipsychotics
HyperthermiaMDMA, amphetamines, anticholinergics, SSRIs (serotonin syndrome)
HypertensionCocaine, amphetamines, ecstasy
High-yield pearl

Even when a history of self-poisoning is given, do not assume it explains everything. Patients who feel genuinely unwell sometimes self-medicate with paracetamol or aspirin before presenting — always keep a serious underlying illness on the differential rather than anchoring entirely on the overdose story.

Step 4. Must-not-miss

Must not miss
  • Paracetamol overdose in a patient who looks completely well — the patient can look and feel fine for 24–72 hours while liver injury silently progresses; always check a level regardless of symptoms
  • Tricyclic antidepressant cardiotoxicity — a widened QRS on ECG predicts seizures and arrhythmia; get an ECG on every significant overdose
  • Co-ingestion — ask specifically, since patients often under-report what else was taken, especially alcohol
  • Salicylate (aspirin) overdose — a mixed respiratory alkalosis/metabolic acidosis picture with tinnitus and hyperventilation; easy to mistake for "just anxiety"
  • Ongoing suicide risk — medical stabilisation is only half the job; every deliberate overdose needs a psychiatric risk assessment before discharge

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideCapillary glucose, ECG, vital signsScreens immediately for hypoglycaemia and cardiotoxicity (QRS/QT prolongation)
LaboratoryParacetamol and salicylate levels (send on every deliberate overdose of unknown substance, since patients may under-report or not know what they took), FBC, U&E, LFTs, venous/arterial blood gasThe paracetamol level is plotted against a treatment nomogram using the exact time since ingestion; LFTs and INR track evolving liver injury
SpecialSpecific drug levels where relevant (e.g., lithium, digoxin, iron) guided by historyDirected by what was actually taken
ResourceTOXBASE (UK National Poisons Information Service) for every unusual or high-risk ingestionProvides substance-specific, up-to-date management advice — use it early rather than relying on memory

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working plan for the vignette: presumed paracetamol overdose of uncertain exact timing. Send a paracetamol level (and, if timing allows, plot it against the treatment nomogram); treat empirically with N-acetylcysteine if the timing is uncertain or the ingestion is staggered; arrange psychiatric assessment once medically stable. Risk is stratified by the substance(s) taken, the dose relative to body weight, the time since ingestion, and the presence of any toxidrome or ECG abnormality.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: ABCDE — secure the airway (recovery position; intubate if the patient is not protecting the airway and this is not rapidly reversible with naloxone), monitor breathing/BP/temperature/acid-base/electrolytes, treat seizures or arrhythmias, and check capillary glucose in every drowsy patient. Get IV access and send bloods including paracetamol and salicylate levels — measure these on every patient with a deliberate overdose of an unknown substance, since a mixed ingestion may not be volunteered.

Reducing absorption: Activated charcoal 50g orally adsorbs most drugs if given within 1 hour of ingestion (not iron, lithium, alkalis, acids, alcohols, or organic solvents). Multiple-dose charcoal (50g every 4 hours) increases clearance of certain drugs (e.g. carbamazepine, phenobarbital, theophylline) by interrupting enterohepatic recycling. Whole bowel irrigation with polyethylene glycol is reserved for sustained-release or enteric-coated preparations of drugs like lithium or iron. Gastric lavage has an extremely limited role today.

Paracetamol overdose: Take a paracetamol level, U&E, LFTs and INR at presentation. For a single acute ingestion, plot the timed level on the treatment nomogram (a single treatment line at 100 mg/L; there is no separate high-risk line since the 2012 UK guidance update). There is no need to start acetylcysteine before the 4-hour level is known, as long as treatment is started within 8 hours of ingestion if indicated. For a staggered overdose (tablets taken over more than 1 hour), the level cannot be plotted on the nomogram — start acetylcysteine immediately if more than 75 mg/kg has been taken over 24 hours, regardless of the level. Acetylcysteine is given as three weight-based infusions; if ALT doubles or INR rises above 1.3, further acetylcysteine is needed and specialist/liver-unit advice should be sought.

Opioid toxicity: Give naloxone IV in small titrated boluses, repeated every few minutes as needed, aiming for an adequate respiratory rate and depth (roughly >12/min) — not full wakefulness, since over-reversal in an opioid-dependent patient precipitates acute withdrawal. Naloxone's effect wears off faster than most opioids, so repeated boluses or an infusion are usually needed to prevent re-sedation; long-acting opioids such as methadone may need naloxone cover for 48–72 hours.

Benzodiazepine toxicity: Supportive care is usually sufficient. Flumazenil is not used diagnostically in an unconscious patient of uncertain cause, and is contraindicated in mixed tricyclic/benzodiazepine overdose or in patients with epilepsy, because it can precipitate seizures, arrhythmia or death. It is reserved for confirmed pure benzodiazepine overdose with severe respiratory/CNS depression, given as small titrated IV boluses, ideally after discussion with a poisons centre.

Tricyclic antidepressant toxicity: ECG monitoring for a minimum of 6 hours. For QRS prolongation, metabolic acidosis, hypotension or arrhythmia, give IV sodium bicarbonate boluses aiming for an arterial pH of around 7.45–7.55 — this is the key life-saving intervention. Treat seizures with IV benzodiazepines; avoid antiarrhythmic drugs, which can worsen cardiotoxicity.

Salicylate (aspirin) toxicity: Check salicylate and paracetamol levels at 4 hours (repeat every 3–4 hours if symptomatic, since tablets can form masses in the stomach and continue absorbing); check an ABG for acid-base status. For significant levels with clinical features, urinary alkalinisation with IV sodium bicarbonate helps eliminate salicylate; watch potassium closely, as alkalinisation causes hypokalaemia which limits its own effectiveness. Haemodialysis is indicated for severe poisoning, particularly with a very high level or with progressive acidosis, reduced consciousness, seizures, or pulmonary oedema.

Monitoring and failure: Serial observations and repeat bloods (especially LFTs/INR in paracetamol overdose, potassium during alkalinisation). Escalate to critical care for any airway compromise, arrhythmia, or refractory hypotension. Discuss unusual, severe or uncertain cases with a poisons centre/TOXBASE promptly.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit for monitoring and treatment according to the substance-specific protocol. Every deliberate self-poisoning needs a formal psychiatric/risk assessment before discharge, regardless of how medically trivial the overdose was — this should never be skipped because "it was only a small amount." Handover should state exactly what was taken, the timing, the treatment given, and whether psychiatric review is pending or complete.

Fatal mistakes in acute poisoning
  • Discharging a paracetamol overdose because the patient "looks fine." Liver injury can be silent for 24–36 hours; always check the level regardless of symptoms, and treat staggered overdoses by weight-based threshold, not by nomogram.
  • Giving flumazenil diagnostically in a comatose patient of uncertain cause, or in a mixed/unknown overdose. It can precipitate seizures, arrhythmia or death, particularly with a co-ingested proconvulsant or proarrhythmic drug such as a tricyclic.
  • Trying to fully reverse an opioid overdose to complete wakefulness. The goal of naloxone is adequate respiration, not a fully alert patient — over-reversal precipitates acute, distressing withdrawal in a dependent patient.
  • Skipping the psychiatric risk assessment because the overdose was "medically minor." The medical severity of an overdose does not predict suicidal intent or future risk.

Check your understanding

Q1Why is a "staggered" paracetamol overdose managed differently from a single acute ingestion?

Answer. The level cannot be plotted on the standard treatment nomogram because it assumes a single ingestion time. For a staggered overdose, treatment (acetylcysteine) is started based on a weight-based dose threshold (>75 mg/kg over 24 hours) regardless of the level.

Q2Why is flumazenil dangerous to give diagnostically in an unconscious patient of unknown cause?

Answer. If there is a co-ingested proconvulsant/proarrhythmic drug (especially a tricyclic antidepressant) or the patient has epilepsy, flumazenil can precipitate seizures, arrhythmia or death by unmasking the toxic effects of the other drug.

Q3What is the target of naloxone titration in opioid overdose, and why not full wakefulness?

Answer. The target is an adequate respiratory rate and depth, not full alertness. Over-reversal in an opioid-dependent patient precipitates acute, distressing withdrawal.

Q4Why should paracetamol and salicylate levels be sent on every patient with a deliberate overdose of an unknown substance?

Answer. Co-ingestion is common and often under-reported. Both drugs are common, have serious but treatable toxicity, and paracetamol toxicity in particular can be clinically silent early on, so levels must be actively sought rather than relied on history alone.

Case scenario

A 34-year-old man is brought in after taking an unknown number of his amitriptyline tablets around 2 hours ago. He is drowsy but rousable, HR 128, BP 92/58. ECG shows a QRS of 140ms. What is the most important immediate treatment?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. IV sodium bicarbonate, aiming for an arterial pH of around 7.45–7.55. This is the key life-saving intervention in tricyclic antidepressant cardiotoxicity with QRS widening and hypotension, and takes priority alongside standard ABCDE resuscitation and continuous ECG monitoring.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 19-year-old woman took a single acute overdose of paracetamol tablets exactly 5 hours ago. She feels well. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step?

  1. Wait until 24 hours post-ingestion to send a level
  2. Start acetylcysteine immediately regardless of the level
  3. Give activated charcoal only, with no blood test needed
  4. Reassure her and discharge, since she is asymptomatic
  5. Send a paracetamol level now and plot it on the treatment nomogram
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. For a single acute ingestion, a timed level should be sent (levels are only interpretable from 4 hours post-ingestion) and plotted against the nomogram to decide whether acetylcysteine is needed. D is wrong because symptoms are a poor guide to paracetamol toxicity. B is unnecessary before knowing the level in a single, timed ingestion. A delays care unacceptably. C: charcoal may still be considered within 1 hour of ingestion, but a level is essential regardless.

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old man is found unresponsive with pinpoint pupils and a respiratory rate of 6/min. Naloxone is given and his respiratory rate improves to 14/min, but he remains drowsy. What is the most appropriate next step?

  1. Discharge once respiratory rate normalises
  2. Give flumazenil to reverse any co-ingested benzodiazepine
  3. Give a much larger dose of naloxone to achieve full wakefulness
  4. Continue monitoring, with repeat naloxone doses or an infusion available if respiratory depression recurs
  5. Stop all monitoring since the antidote has worked
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. The goal of naloxone is adequate respiratory effort, not full wakefulness, and naloxone's effect wears off before many opioids', so ongoing monitoring with repeat dosing or an infusion is needed. C risks precipitating acute withdrawal. A is unsafe — re-sedation can occur. B is not indicated without confirmed benzodiazepine co-ingestion and carries its own risks. E ignores the risk of recurrent respiratory depression.

Presentation 3The unconscious patient

Coma is a state of unarousable unresponsiveness: no spontaneous eye opening, no comprehensible speech, no voluntary limb movement, and no purposeful response to external stimuli. Facing an unresponsive patient is one of the most frightening calls a junior doctor receives. Working through a fixed set of "red flag" questions, rather than a vague, unstructured differential, turns panic into a systematic, life-saving assessment.

Figure 9.3. Structured approach to the unconscious patient: immediate A–E stabilisation, collateral history, targeted examination, semantic problem representation, danger-ranked differential, investigations, synthesis, escalation and diagnostic time-out.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. You are called to a 58-year-old man found unresponsive on the ward. GCS 6. Pupils are pinpoint and equal. Respiratory rate is 8. Before ordering a single test, decide: what is your immediate action?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — a GCS of 8 or less means the airway is at risk

Any patient with a GCS of 8 or less is presumed unable to protect their own airway and needs urgent senior/anaesthetic input for airway management, regardless of the underlying cause. Start with ABCDE exactly as with any deteriorating patient: airway (listen for gurgling/snoring/stridor), breathing (rate, saturations — respiratory depression with pinpoint pupils is the classic opioid picture), circulation (BP, heart rate, perfusion), disability (GCS/AVPU, pupils, and glucose immediately), exposure (look for injection marks, medical-alert jewellery, rashes, injury from a fall).

Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)

ComponentScoreResponse
Eye opening (4)4Spontaneous
3To voice
2To pain
1None
Verbal response (5)5Orientated
4Confused
3Inappropriate words
2Incomprehensible sounds
1None
Motor response (6)6Obeys commands
5Localises to pain
4Withdraws from pain
3Abnormal flexion (decorticate)
2Extension (decerebrate)
1None

Interpretation: Total score ranges from 3 (worst) to 15 (normal). A GCS of 8 or less generally means the patient cannot protect their own airway and needs urgent senior/anaesthetic assessment. Always report GCS as the total plus the breakdown (e.g. "GCS 10 \= E3 V3 M4"), since the breakdown carries clinical information the total alone loses.

High-yield pearl

The pattern in the vignette — reduced GCS, respiratory depression, pinpoint pupils — is the opioid toxidrome, and it is rapidly reversible with naloxone. Recognising this pattern immediately, rather than working through a long differential first, can be the difference between a five-minute fix and a prolonged ICU admission.

The standard immediate priorities, once ABC is stabilised: check glucose and give IV glucose immediately if hypoglycaemia is possible (this rarely causes harm even if the diagnosis turns out to be wrong); in a malnourished or alcohol-dependent patient, give IV thiamine before the glucose bolus, since glucose alone can precipitate Wernicke's encephalopathy; give naloxone if opioid toxicity is likely (small pupils, reduced respiratory rate); give flumazenil only if benzodiazepine intoxication is likely and there is no history of epilepsy or mixed overdose (see Presentation 2 for full dosing and cautions).

Step 2. History — collateral information is everything

The patient cannot give a history, so gather it from every other source: nursing staff and drug chart (recent opioid or sedative doses, missed insulin, recent seizure), family/witnesses (preceding symptoms, fall, seizure activity, speed of onset), the medical notes (known epilepsy, diabetes, liver/renal failure, psychiatric history), and the physical environment (empty packets, medical-alert bracelet, injection paraphernalia). Ask specifically whether the onset was sudden (favours a vascular event, such as stroke or basilar artery occlusion) or gradual (favours a metabolic or toxic process), and whether there was a preceding seizure, fever, or flu-like illness.

In plain terms: a reduced GCS with respiratory depression and pinpoint pupils on a hospital ward is presumed opioid toxicity from a recent dose until proven otherwise — and it is immediately treatable.

Step 3. Examination pearl — look for coma mimics, not just coma causes

Examination pearl

Not every unresponsive-looking patient has reduced consciousness. Locked-in syndrome (a pontine lesion, e.g. basilar artery stroke) leaves the patient fully aware but unable to move or speak — look for preserved vertical eye movements or blinking to command. Psychogenic (functional) unresponsiveness has positive signs of non-organic origin — resistance to eye opening, or the patient avoiding their own hand when it is dropped over their face during a hand-drop test. Missing these mimics means either missing a devastating stroke that needs urgent treatment, or inappropriately escalating a functional presentation to intensive, invasive management.

The single most useful bedside discriminator is whether the pupils are reactive. In metabolic or toxic coma, however deep, the pupils almost always remain reactive to light (rare exceptions include atropine, hyoscine or glutethimide poisoning, which depress brainstem function directly). Fixed or asymmetric pupils in a comatose patient point strongly toward a structural lesion — a mass, haemorrhage, or herniation — and change the whole urgency of imaging. Also examine systematically for other clues: breathing pattern (shallow/slow suggests opioid; deep, rapid Kussmaul breathing suggests acidosis), breath odour (alcohol, ketones, hepatic or uraemic fetor), skin (needle tracks; bruising over the scalp/mastoids or blood in the nostrils suggesting a base-of-skull fracture; a purpuric rash suggesting meningitis), and the fundi (papilloedema suggests raised intracranial pressure, though its absence does not exclude it).

Step 4. Schema — eight red-flag questions for reduced consciousness

When faced with a low-GCS patient, work through these eight questions systematically:

#QuestionClue/action
1Major anoxic or ischaemic brain insult?Large stroke, post-cardiac-arrest anoxic injury
2Intoxication?Pinpoint pupils (opioid), collateral history of drug/alcohol use
3CNS infection?Preceding fever, flu-like illness, meningism, seizures; consider if immunosuppressed
4Hypoglycaemia or hyperglycaemia?Check glucose immediately — always, in every case
5Hyponatraemia (or hypercalcaemia)?Rapidly checked on a VBG/ABG
6Non-convulsive status epilepticus?Suspect with a preceding seizure, known epilepsy, or unusually slow recovery from a witnessed seizure
7Basilar artery embolus?Sudden onset with brainstem signs (abnormal pupils, abnormal eye movements)
8Psychogenic?Positive functional signs — resisted eye opening, hand-drop avoidance
Must not miss
  • Hypoglycaemia — the single most common and most rapidly reversible cause; check glucose before anything else
  • Opioid toxicity — reversible with naloxone; look for pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression
  • Non-convulsive status epilepticus — easily missed since there is no outward convulsion; consider with any unexplained prolonged reduced consciousness, especially with a seizure history
  • Basilar artery stroke — a devastating, time-critical diagnosis; look for brainstem signs and treat as a stroke emergency
  • Locked-in syndrome — a fully aware patient misread as deeply unconscious; look for preserved eye movements

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideCapillary glucose (immediate), vital signs, pupil exam, ECGGlucose is checked before anything else — it is the fastest, cheapest, most reversible cause
LaboratoryFBC, U&E (sodium, calcium), LFTs, blood gas, blood cultures if infection suspected, paracetamol/salicylate levels if overdose is possibleScreens for the metabolic and toxic causes in the red-flag list
ImagingUrgent CT head — for new-onset reduced consciousness with a falling GCS, focal neurology, asymmetric/fixed pupils, history of trauma/fall, or no other cause foundIdentifies structural causes (haemorrhage, large stroke, space-occupying lesion) requiring urgent intervention
SpecialEEG (to exclude non-convulsive status epilepticus), lumbar puncture with opening pressure (if CNS infection is suspected, once CT has excluded a mass effect and it is safe to proceed)The practical rule: if the CT is normal and brainstem function is intact, proceed to LP — a normal CSF makes metabolic coma the most likely diagnosis

Give antibiotics immediately, before any imaging or LP, if meningism or a purpuric rash raises suspicion of meningitis. Do not let the imaging pathway delay treatment of a time-critical infection.

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: presumed opioid toxicity, given the classic triad of reduced GCS, respiratory depression and pinpoint pupils, most likely from a recent inpatient opioid dose. Risk is stratified by GCS, respiratory rate/effort, and the response to the reversal agent. A patient who responds promptly to naloxone confirms the diagnosis and immediately lowers acuity, though monitoring must continue since naloxone's effect can wear off before the opioid's.

Step 7. Management ladder

Immediate: Secure the airway (positioning, airway adjunct, call anaesthetics if GCS 8 or less and not rapidly reversible), give oxygen, check and treat glucose if low, get IV access.

Reversible causes — treat as you find them: naloxone for suspected opioid toxicity (titrate to adequate respiratory effort, not necessarily full wakefulness, to avoid precipitating acute opioid withdrawal); IV glucose/glucagon for hypoglycaemia; IV benzodiazepine for ongoing/non-convulsive seizure activity; empirical treatment of suspected CNS infection pending investigation if clinically indicated.

Definitive: Disease-specific management once the cause is identified — stroke pathway for basilar occlusion, antimicrobials for confirmed CNS infection, correction of electrolyte abnormality, critical care for ongoing airway/ventilatory support.

Monitoring and failure: Repeat GCS and vital signs frequently. A patient who responds to naloxone still needs ongoing monitoring, since re-sedation can occur once the naloxone wears off if the opioid has a longer half-life. Escalate to critical care for any patient not improving or requiring ongoing airway support.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Any patient with a GCS of 8 or less, or one requiring airway support or reversal-agent monitoring, needs critical care or high-dependency-level observation. Escalate immediately to a senior/anaesthetist for airway concerns, and to the stroke team for any suspected basilar or other acute stroke. Handover should state the working cause, the response to any reversal agent given, and the specific monitoring plan (e.g., "re-check GCS hourly, naloxone may need repeating").

Fatal mistakes with the unconscious patient
  • Not checking glucose immediately. Hypoglycaemia is the fastest, most reversible, and most easily missed cause of reduced consciousness.
  • Giving naloxone once and assuming the problem is solved. Naloxone's effect can wear off before the opioid's — ongoing monitoring is essential, and repeat doses or an infusion may be needed.
  • Missing non-convulsive status epilepticus in a patient with unexplained prolonged reduced consciousness, especially after a witnessed seizure that "should have" resolved by now.
  • Failing to recognise locked-in syndrome or psychogenic unresponsiveness, and either under- or over-escalating the patient's care as a result.

Check your understanding

Q1What is the single most useful bedside discriminator between metabolic/toxic coma and a structural brain lesion?

Answer. Pupillary reactivity. In metabolic or toxic coma the pupils almost always stay reactive to light; fixed or asymmetric pupils suggest a structural lesion (mass, haemorrhage, herniation), with rare toxic exceptions such as atropine, hyoscine or glutethimide.

Q2Why must IV thiamine be given before glucose in a malnourished or alcohol-dependent unconscious patient?

Answer. Giving glucose without thiamine first can precipitate Wernicke's encephalopathy in a thiamine-deficient patient, so thiamine is given before or alongside the glucose bolus.

Q3What clinical clue should make you suspect non-convulsive status epilepticus in an unconscious patient?

Answer. Unexplained prolonged reduced consciousness, especially unusually slow recovery after a witnessed seizure or in a patient with known epilepsy — there is no outward convulsion, so it is easily missed without a high index of suspicion and EEG.

Q4How is locked-in syndrome distinguished from true coma on examination?

Answer. The patient is fully aware but cannot move or speak due to a pontine lesion (e.g. basilar artery stroke). Look for preserved vertical eye movements or blinking to command, which would not be present in true unresponsiveness.

Case scenario

A 70-year-old woman with known epilepsy is found unresponsive by her family. She had a witnessed generalised seizure 40 minutes ago that self-terminated after 2 minutes, but she has not woken up since. On examination GCS is 9, no focal weakness, pupils equal and reactive, glucose is normal. What is the most likely explanation for her ongoing reduced consciousness, and what investigation would confirm it?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Non-convulsive status epilepticus (or a prolonged post-ictal state evolving into it). The unusually slow recovery after a witnessed seizure in a known epileptic is the key clue. An EEG is the investigation that would confirm ongoing seizure activity; empirical treatment with IV benzodiazepines may be trialled while this is arranged.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 62-year-old man is found unresponsive on the ward. GCS is 5. Pupils are pinpoint and reactive, RR 6/min, sats 88% on air. He received IV morphine for pain 30 minutes earlier. What is the most appropriate immediate step, after securing the airway and giving oxygen?

  1. IV naloxone, titrated to respiratory effort
  2. Lumbar puncture
  3. Urgent CT head
  4. EEG
  5. IV flumazenil
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. The clinical picture (recent opioid, pinpoint pupils, respiratory depression, reduced GCS) is the classic, rapidly reversible opioid toxidrome — naloxone is both diagnostic and therapeutic. C and D are appropriate only if naloxone fails to improve him or another cause is suspected. B is unsafe before imaging if raised intracranial pressure has not been excluded, and not indicated here. E is for benzodiazepine toxicity, not supported by this picture, and risky if there is any co-ingestion or seizure history.

MCQ 2. A 45-year-old woman is brought in unresponsive. On examination she resists attempts to open her eyes, and when her hand is dropped above her face during examination, it consistently avoids hitting her face. Vital signs, glucose and pupils are all normal. What is the most likely diagnosis?

  1. Non-convulsive status epilepticus
  2. Psychogenic (functional) unresponsiveness
  3. Basilar artery stroke
  4. Locked-in syndrome
  5. Opioid toxicity
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Resisted eye opening and hand-drop avoidance are classic positive signs of a functional/non-organic cause, with otherwise normal examination and observations. C and D would be expected to show brainstem or focal signs (e.g. abnormal eye movements, real motor deficits), not preserved protective avoidance. A typically presents with reduced, not resisted, responsiveness and no purposeful avoidance. E would show pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression, not normal findings.

Block 10 · Week 11 of the module
Nutrition presentations

Presentation 1Malnutrition and nutritional risk screening

Malnutrition is common and easy to miss, but it makes outcomes worse. UK hospital audits suggest that around a third of patients, and in some series up to 40%, are malnourished or at risk of malnutrition on acute admission — and the risk rises further in patients over 65. It is rarely the presenting complaint; it usually travels alongside another illness and gets missed unless it is actively screened for.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 78-year-old man is admitted with a chest infection. His clothes look loose. He mentions he has "not been eating much" for the past two months but no one has weighed him or asked further. Before reading on, decide: what is missing from his admission clerking, and why does it matter?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Malnutrition is rarely an emergency on its own, but it is a strong marker of vulnerability. It weakens the immune system (more infections), slows wound healing, and weakens respiratory and skeletal muscle, making it harder to fight off any acute illness. It independently increases length of stay and mortality. An acutely unwell patient with unrecognised malnutrition has a higher risk of a poor outcome from whatever illness brought them in.

High-yield pearl

Malnutrition almost never presents on its own — it hides inside another diagnosis (chest infection, stroke, cancer, dementia). Screen every patient on admission; do not wait for it to become clinically obvious.

Step 2. History — screen systematically, do not rely on appearance alone

Ask about recent unintentional weight loss (how much, over what time), reduced appetite or food intake, difficulty chewing or swallowing, and whether clothes, rings or dentures have become loose. Look for the underlying driver: an illness causing anorexia or higher requirements (sepsis, trauma, cancer, chronic inflammatory disease); a physical problem with shopping, cooking or eating (muscle weakness, arthritis, swallowing difficulty, sensory impairment); or a psychosocial driver (loneliness, depression, bereavement, poverty, alcohol). Check the medication list — polypharmacy can itself be a marker of severe underlying disease, and drugs can cause anorexia, sedation, or GI side effects that reduce intake.

In plain terms: an elderly man with loose-fitting clothes and two months of reduced intake, admitted with an acute infection — a malnutrition risk that was never formally screened for on admission.

Step 3. Examination pearl — the MUST score, calculated at the bedside

Examination pearl

The Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST) takes under two minutes and should be completed for every hospital admission. It has three steps, each scored 0–2, which are added together to give an overall risk category.

MUST — Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool

StepCriterionScore
1. BMI (kg/m²)>200
18.5–201
<18.52
2. Unplanned weight loss (past 3–6 months)<5%0
5–10%1
>10%2
3. Acute disease effectAcutely ill with little/no nutritional intake for >5 days (e.g. prolonged unconsciousness, persistent post-stroke swallowing problems, prolonged post-operative ileus)2 (0 if not applicable)

Interpretation: add the three scores. 0 \= low risk (routine care); 1 \= medium risk (observe and document intake); ≥2 \= high risk (refer to a dietitian or nutrition support team).

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

MechanismExamples
Reduced intakeAnorexia of illness, depression/dementia, dysphagia, dental disease, poverty/isolation
MalabsorptionCoeliac disease, IBD, chronic pancreatitis, short bowel
Increased requirement/catabolismSepsis, trauma, surgery, burns, malignancy, chronic inflammatory disease
Nutrient lossPersistent vomiting/diarrhoea, protein-losing enteropathy, high-output stoma
Must not miss
  • Malnutrition masked by fluid retention — a patient with heart failure, cirrhosis or nephrotic syndrome can be losing real muscle and fat while the scale shows a stable or rising weight, because oedema or ascites hides the loss; BMI can be falsely reassuring here.
  • Severe malnutrition risking refeeding syndrome once feeding starts — see Presentation 2, and always screen for this before starting nutritional support, not after.
  • Reversible, treatable drivers — dental pain, ill-fitting dentures, depression, and social factors are common and easily missed if the assessment stops at "this is just old age".

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideWeight, height/BMI, MUST score, food/fluid chartThe core screening tool — cheap, fast, and directly guides the care plan
LaboratoryFBC, U&E, LFTs, calcium, phosphate, magnesium, glucose, CRPScreens for electrolyte derangement and the "acute disease effect" driving intake down; baseline before any nutritional support starts
SpecialIron studies, B12/folate, vitamin D as clinically indicatedDirected by clinical suspicion of a specific deficiency (see Presentation 3)

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working plan for the vignette: complete a MUST score now and repeat it daily/weekly as an inpatient. Given the weight loss and acute illness, this patient is likely medium-to-high risk and should be referred to dietetics. Risk is stratified directly by the MUST category (low/medium/high), which sets the intensity of monitoring and intervention.

Step 7. Management ladder

Low risk: routine clinical care, repeat screening weekly in hospital.

Medium risk: document dietary intake for 3 days; if adequate, repeat screening; if inadequate, follow local policy to improve intake (fortified food, between-meal snacks, dietitian referral if concern persists).

High risk: refer to a dietitian or nutrition support team; treat the underlying condition in parallel; set goals to improve nutritional intake, starting with oral measures (more frequent meals, fortified food, oral nutritional supplements) before escalating to enteral or parenteral routes if oral intake stays inadequate (see Presentation 2).

All risk categories: treat the underlying condition and address the physical or psychosocial barriers found in the history — nutritional supplementation without fixing the driver (dental pain, depression, swallowing difficulty) is unlikely to succeed.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Every admission gets a MUST score within 24 hours per local policy; high-risk patients are flagged for dietetic review before discharge planning is finalised, since unresolved malnutrition is a preventable cause of readmission. Handover states the MUST score, its trend, and whether dietetics has been involved.

Two common student errors
  • Relying on visual impression ("he doesn't look malnourished") instead of calculating a formal MUST score — malnutrition is often missed on eyeballing alone.
  • Treating a stable or normal weight as reassuring in a patient with heart failure, cirrhosis or nephrotic syndrome, without considering that fluid retention may be masking real tissue loss.

Check your understanding

Q1What are the three components of the MUST score, and what total score defines high risk?

Answer. BMI score, unplanned weight-loss score (past 3–6 months), and acute disease effect score, each scored 0–2. A total of ≥2 defines high risk.

Q2Why can a patient with heart failure or cirrhosis have a falsely reassuring BMI despite significant malnutrition?

Answer. Fluid retention (oedema or ascites) can hide real loss of muscle and fat mass, so weight and BMI stay stable or even rise while true nutritional status worsens.

Q3Name two reversible or treatable drivers of poor intake that are easily missed if malnutrition is attributed to "old age".

Answer. Any two of: dental pain or ill-fitting dentures, depression, social isolation or poverty, swallowing difficulty.

Q4What should happen to a patient who scores "high risk" on MUST?

Answer. Refer to a dietitian or nutrition support team, treat the underlying condition, and escalate nutritional support starting with oral measures before enteral or parenteral routes.

Case scenario

An 82-year-old woman is admitted after a fall. Her BMI is 19.2 kg/m². She reports losing about 12% of her body weight over the last 4 months after her husband's death, but has been eating almost normally for the past week. What is her MUST score, and what should happen next?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. BMI 19.2 (18.5–20) scores 1; weight loss >10% in 3–6 months scores 2; acute disease effect is 0, since she has been eating normally for a week. Total \= 3 → high risk. She should be referred to a dietitian or nutrition support team, and the psychosocial driver (bereavement) should be addressed directly, with regular reassessment.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 68-year-old man with decompensated cirrhosis and ascites is admitted. His weight has been stable over the past 3 months and his BMI is 23 kg/m². His albumin is low and his mid-arm muscle circumference is reduced. Which of the following best explains this discrepancy?

  1. Reduced muscle circumference is a normal ageing change and is not significant
  2. Fluid retention (ascites) is masking true loss of muscle and fat mass
  3. He does not have malnutrition because his BMI is normal
  4. The MUST score cannot be applied to patients with liver disease
  5. Low albumin always indicates malnutrition regardless of other findings
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. Ascites can mask real loss of muscle and fat, making BMI falsely reassuring. C is wrong because a normal BMI does not exclude malnutrition here. E is wrong — albumin is affected by inflammation and illness, not nutrition alone, so it should not be used as a standalone marker of malnutrition. D is wrong — MUST can still be used in liver disease, though clinical judgement about fluid status is needed. A is wrong — reduced muscle bulk is a genuine sign of malnutrition, not simply an ageing change.

MCQ 2. A 74-year-old woman is admitted with pneumonia. Her BMI is 19.5 kg/m² (score 1), her weight loss is 3% over the past 4 months (score 0), and her acute disease effect score is 0 because she was eating normally until this admission. What is her overall MUST risk category and the recommended initial action?

  1. Total score 1, low risk — no action needed
  2. Total score 0, low risk — routine weekly rescreening only
  3. Total score 1, medium risk — document dietary intake for 3 days, then repeat screening if intake is adequate or act on local policy if inadequate
  4. Total score 1, high risk — immediate dietitian referral and enteral feeding
  5. Total score 1, medium risk — parenteral nutrition should be started
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. 1 + 0 + 0 \= 1, which is medium risk; the medium-risk pathway is to document intake for 3 days and act accordingly. D is wrong — that describes the high-risk pathway, which needs a score of ≥2. A is wrong — a score of 1 is medium, not low, risk. B is wrong — the total is 1, not 0. E is wrong — parenteral nutrition is not indicated at medium risk, and oral measures come first even at higher risk.

Presentation 2Nutritional support and refeeding syndrome

Refeeding syndrome is one of the most dangerous, preventable complications in internal medicine: feeding a severely malnourished patient too fast can kill them through electrolyte shifts that are easy to miss unless you are actively looking for them.

Figure 10.1. Approach to refeeding syndrome from clinical context and risk assessment through baseline investigations, biochemical interpretation, diagnosis, red flags and post-diagnosis management.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 54-year-old woman with anorexia nervosa is admitted with a BMI of 14.5, having eaten almost nothing for the past 10 days. The team plans to start full-rate nasogastric feeding immediately "to get her nutrition up quickly." Before reading on, decide: what is dangerously wrong with this plan?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — recognise refeeding risk before you feed, not after

In prolonged starvation, the body shifts from glucose to fat and ketone metabolism, and intracellular phosphate, potassium and magnesium become depleted even though serum levels may look normal. When carbohydrate is reintroduced, insulin surges and drives glucose, phosphate, potassium and magnesium rapidly into cells. This can cause severe hypophosphataemia, hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia and fluid retention, with cardiac arrhythmia, cardiac failure, respiratory failure, seizures and death as possible consequences.

Fatal mistake

Starting full-rate feeding in a severely malnourished patient "to catch up" on lost nutrition quickly. This is exactly the scenario that causes refeeding syndrome — the more starved the patient, the more cautiously feeding must be introduced, not the more aggressively.

Step 2. History and red flags for refeeding risk

Ask about how long and how severely intake has been reduced, recent weight loss and how fast it happened, alcohol use, and any pre-existing low potassium, phosphate or magnesium. Use the NICE refeeding-risk criteria to decide whether a patient is high risk.

NICE refeeding-risk criteria

CategoryCriteria
High risk — any one ofBMI <16 kg/m²; unintentional weight loss >15% in the past 3–6 months; little or no nutritional intake for >10 days; low potassium, phosphate or magnesium before feeding
High risk — two or more ofBMI <18.5 kg/m²; unintentional weight loss >10% in the past 3–6 months; little or no nutritional intake for >5 days; history of alcohol misuse or certain drugs (insulin, chemotherapy, antacids, diuretics)

Interpretation: meeting either threshold places the patient in the high-risk group for refeeding syndrome. Feed cautiously, correct or replace electrolytes proactively, and monitor closely from the outset.

In plain terms: a woman with a BMI of 14.5 and 10 days of minimal intake meets the highest-risk refeeding criteria on two separate counts — this is not a "feed her up quickly" situation, it is a "feed her very cautiously and monitor intensively" situation.

Step 3. Examination pearl

Examination pearl

Look for the signs of established malnutrition described in Presentation 1 (temporal wasting, loss of subcutaneous fat, reduced muscle bulk). Once feeding starts, specifically watch for new peripheral oedema, a rising jugular venous pressure, tachycardia, or breathlessness — early signs of the fluid retention and cardiac strain that accompany refeeding syndrome.

Step 4. Schema and the must-not-miss

Must not miss
  • Hypophosphataemia — the hallmark biochemical finding of refeeding syndrome; can cause muscle weakness, respiratory failure (diaphragmatic weakness), cardiac failure, and haemolysis if severe.
  • Hypokalaemia and hypomagnesaemia — both raise the risk of life-threatening cardiac arrhythmia, especially in combination with hypophosphataemia.
  • Thiamine deficiency unmasked by carbohydrate refeeding — glucose given to a thiamine-deplete patient (alcohol-dependent, chronically malnourished) can precipitate acute Wernicke's encephalopathy; thiamine must be given before carbohydrate/glucose, every time (see Presentation 3).
  • Normal-looking baseline electrolytes that fall sharply once feeding starts — a normal pre-feeding potassium, phosphate or magnesium does not mean the patient is safe; it reflects depleted intracellular stores not yet unmasked.

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
Before feedingU&E, calcium, phosphate, magnesium, glucose, FBC, ECGEstablishes a baseline and identifies pre-existing deficiency that must be corrected proactively
During refeeding (high risk)Daily U&E, phosphate, magnesium, calcium for at least the first week, then less often once stableRefeeding electrolyte shifts typically occur within the first few days but risk continues through the first week
OngoingFluid balance chart, weight, clinical observationsDetects fluid retention and cardiac strain early, before overt heart failure develops

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working plan for the vignette: highest-risk refeeding category. Feeding must start at a low calorie rate with proactive electrolyte and thiamine supplementation and daily monitoring, escalating gradually over days rather than reaching full nutritional targets immediately.

Step 7. Management ladder

Before feeding: correct any pre-existing severe electrolyte abnormality where safely possible, but do not delay cautious feeding indefinitely waiting for perfect numbers. Give IV thiamine (and B-complex vitamins) before starting any feed or glucose-containing fluid, particularly in alcohol-dependent or chronically starved patients.

Route of feeding — choose the least invasive route that works: oral intake with fortified food and supplements if the patient can eat; fine-bore nasogastric tube feeding if oral intake is inadequate but the gut is working; percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy (PEG) for anticipated long-term enteral feeding (typically >30 days), e.g. after stroke with persistent dysphagia; parenteral nutrition only if the gastrointestinal tract cannot be used or is not tolerating enteral feeding. Enteral feeding is preferred whenever the gut works, because it is safer, cheaper, and preserves gut mucosal integrity.

Feeding rate in high-risk patients: start at a low rate, well below full estimated requirements, and increase gradually over about 4–7 days as electrolytes remain stable, rather than aiming for full nutritional targets from day one.

Monitoring and failure: check phosphate, potassium and magnesium daily at first and replace proactively as levels fall, rather than waiting for them to become critically low. If refeeding syndrome develops despite precautions, slow or temporarily reduce the feeding rate while aggressively correcting electrolytes, and involve a dietitian/nutrition support team urgently.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

High-risk refeeding patients should have daily dietitian and medical review, ideally with nutrition support team involvement, for at least the first week of feeding. Handover explicitly states the refeeding risk category, the current feeding rate, and the electrolyte-monitoring plan — this is exactly the kind of instruction that gets lost between shifts and causes preventable harm.

Fatal mistakes with nutritional support and refeeding
  • Feeding a severely malnourished patient at full rate from the outset. Start low, monitor daily, and increase gradually — the more starved the patient, the more cautious the approach must be.
  • Giving glucose-containing fluid before thiamine in a chronically malnourished or alcohol-dependent patient. This can precipitate acute Wernicke's encephalopathy.
  • Being reassured by a normal pre-feeding phosphate, potassium or magnesium. These reflect serum levels, not depleted intracellular stores, and can crash within days of feeding starting.
  • Choosing parenteral nutrition when the gut works. Enteral feeding should be used whenever the gastrointestinal tract is functioning — it is safer and has fewer complications.

Check your understanding

Q1Name the two ways a patient can meet NICE high-risk criteria for refeeding syndrome.

Answer. Either one major criterion alone (BMI <16, weight loss >15% in 3–6 months, minimal intake for >10 days, or low potassium/phosphate/magnesium before feeding) or two or more minor criteria (BMI <18.5, weight loss >10% in 3–6 months, minimal intake for >5 days, or a history of alcohol misuse/certain drugs).

Q2Why can a normal pre-feeding phosphate, potassium or magnesium be misleading in a starved patient?

Answer. Serum levels can look normal despite depleted intracellular stores. Feeding triggers an insulin-driven shift of these electrolytes into cells, unmasking the deficit and causing serum levels to crash.

Q3What must be given before carbohydrate/glucose when starting to refeed a thiamine-deplete patient, and why?

Answer. IV thiamine (as part of a B-complex preparation), because carbohydrate refeeding can precipitate Wernicke's encephalopathy in a thiamine-depleted patient.

Q4What is the preferred feeding route when the gut is functioning, and why?

Answer. Enteral feeding (oral or nasogastric), because it is safer, cheaper, and preserves gut mucosal integrity compared with parenteral nutrition.

Case scenario

A 60-year-old man with chronic alcohol excess and 3 weeks of minimal oral intake is admitted with delirium tremens. His BMI is 15.5 kg/m². Baseline potassium, phosphate and magnesium are all within the normal range. The team wants to start nasogastric feeding today at his full calculated energy requirement. What is the single most important change to make to this plan?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Reduce the starting feeding rate substantially — start well below full requirements and increase gradually over days — and give IV thiamine before feeding, with daily electrolyte monitoring. He is at highest refeeding risk (BMI <16, and minimal intake for >10 days) despite normal baseline electrolytes, which reflect serum, not intracellular, stores.

MCQs

MCQ 1. Two days after starting nasogastric feeding in a high-risk patient, she develops new leg swelling, a raised jugular venous pressure and worsening breathlessness. Her phosphate has fallen from 1.1 to 0.3 mmol/L. What is the most appropriate immediate action?

  1. Continue feeding at the same rate and recheck bloods tomorrow
  2. Stop all nutritional support permanently
  3. Give a stat dose of furosemide and continue feeding unchanged
  4. Slow or temporarily reduce the feeding rate and aggressively correct electrolytes, involving the nutrition team urgently
  5. Increase the feeding rate to replace lost energy quickly
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. This is refeeding syndrome with severe hypophosphataemia and signs of fluid overload/cardiac strain, requiring immediate action. A is wrong — continuing unchanged risks further deterioration. B is wrong — feeding should be resumed cautiously once stabilised, not stopped permanently. E is wrong — increasing the rate will worsen the syndrome. C is wrong — a diuretic does not address the underlying electrolyte crisis and is not primary management.

MCQ 2. Which of the following patients meets NICE high-risk criteria for refeeding syndrome based on a single criterion alone?

  1. BMI 20, alcohol misuse, weight loss 5% in 3 months
  2. BMI 15, no other risk factors
  3. BMI 19, taking antacids, weight loss 6% in 6 months
  4. BMI 17, weight loss 8% in 4 months, no other risk factors
  5. BMI 19, weight loss 8% in 4 months, poor intake for 6 days
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: B. A BMI of 15 is below 16, which alone meets a major high-risk criterion. E, D, A and C each meet at most one minor criterion (poor intake >5 days, BMI <18.5, alcohol misuse, or antacid use respectively) but not two or more, and none meets a major criterion alone, so none of them qualifies as high risk.

Presentation 3Micronutrient deficiencies

Vitamin and mineral deficiencies rarely present with their textbook picture in isolation — they usually turn up in alcohol-dependent patients, the chronically malnourished, those with malabsorption, or after bariatric surgery. The one deficiency that must never be missed is thiamine, because the consequences of missing it are severe and preventable.

Figure 10.2. Approach to nutritional deficiencies from symptoms, dietary and medical history, physical signs and screening through deficiency-pattern interpretation, investigation of the underlying cause and treatment.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 47-year-old man with long-standing alcohol dependence is admitted confused, ataxic, with new-onset diplopia. The team gives IV glucose for his low capillary reading before anything else. Before reading on, decide: what has been done in the wrong order, and why does it matter?

Step 1. Sick or not sick — thiamine first, always

The vignette describes the classic triad of Wernicke's encephalopathy: confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia/nystagmus — though the full triad is present in only a minority of cases, so a low threshold for treatment is essential. Giving IV glucose before thiamine in a thiamine-deplete patient can precipitate or worsen Wernicke's encephalopathy, because glucose metabolism uses up the little remaining thiamine. This is one of the most important sequencing rules in acute medicine.

Fatal mistake

Giving IV glucose before thiamine in any patient who could plausibly be thiamine-deficient (alcohol dependence, chronic malnutrition, hyperemesis, prolonged fasting). Thiamine must always be given first, or at the very least at the same time — never glucose alone first.

Step 2. History — think about who is at risk of which deficiency

Ask about alcohol use, dietary restriction (vegan diet, very restricted intake), malabsorption (coeliac disease, IBD, previous gastric/bariatric surgery, chronic pancreatitis), chronic vomiting or diarrhoea, and any prolonged hospital admission on inadequate nutritional support. Ask specifically about symptoms suggesting a particular deficiency: peripheral neuropathy or a sore tongue (B12), leg weakness and swelling (thiamine/beriberi), bleeding gums and poor wound healing (vitamin C), or bone pain and proximal muscle weakness (vitamin D).

In plain terms: a man with alcohol dependence and the classic Wernicke's triad — thiamine deficiency until proven otherwise, needing immediate treatment before any further glucose is given.

Step 3. Schema — key deficiencies and how they present

DeficiencyWho is at riskKey features
Thiamine (B1)Alcohol dependence, chronic malnutrition, hyperemesis, prolonged parenteral feeding without vitaminsWernicke's encephalopathy (confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia); untreated progresses to Korsakoff's (irreversible amnesia); dry beriberi (peripheral neuropathy) or wet beriberi (high-output heart failure, oedema)
Vitamin B12Pernicious anaemia, vegan diet, previous gastrectomy/ileal resection, metformin useMacrocytic anaemia, peripheral neuropathy, subacute combined degeneration of the cord, cognitive change — can occur with a normal MCV; see Block 7 Presentation 1 for the full anaemia work-up
FolatePoor diet, alcohol excess, pregnancy, malabsorption, certain drugs (methotrexate, phenytoin)Macrocytic anaemia; never treat with folate alone until B12 deficiency has been excluded, as this can worsen neurological damage
Vitamin CVery poor diet, alcohol dependence, social isolationScurvy: perifollicular haemorrhages, "corkscrew" hairs, swollen bleeding gums, poor wound healing, spontaneous bruising
Vitamin DLimited sun exposure, dark skin in low-sunlight climates, malabsorption, chronic kidney disease, obesityBone pain, proximal myopathy, osteomalacia in adults; low 25-hydroxyvitamin D on testing
Must not miss
  • Wernicke's encephalopathy — treat empirically on clinical suspicion; do not wait for a thiamine level, since delay risks irreversible Korsakoff's psychosis.
  • B12 deficiency with neurological symptoms and a normal MCV — a normal blood count does not exclude clinically significant B12 deficiency.
  • Treating macrocytic anaemia with folate before checking B12 — can precipitate or worsen subacute combined degeneration of the cord.

Step 4. Examination pearl

Examination pearl

Do not wait for the full classic triad of any deficiency syndrome before treating — Wernicke's encephalopathy presents with all three of confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia in only a minority of cases. A low threshold for empirical thiamine in any at-risk, unwell patient is safe practice; missing the diagnosis is not.

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideCapillary glucose, clinical examination for the specific deficiency syndromeDirects urgency and initial treatment
LaboratoryFBC and film, B12, folate, 25-hydroxyvitamin D, calcium/phosphate/magnesium, LFTsConfirms the specific deficiency once clinically suspected — but treatment for thiamine deficiency should never wait for a laboratory result
SpecialRed cell transketolase activity or circulating thiamine concentration (rarely needed acutely), coeliac serology if malabsorption is suspectedConfirmatory rather than treatment-guiding in the acute setting

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working diagnosis for the vignette: Wernicke's encephalopathy in an alcohol-dependent patient — treated as a clinical emergency, not deferred pending confirmatory testing.

Step 7. Management ladder

Thiamine deficiency/Wernicke's: for suspected or confirmed Wernicke's encephalopathy, give high-dose IV thiamine as part of a B-complex vitamin preparation (e.g. IV Pabrinex) at treatment dose, several times a day for at least 2–3 days, then reassess and step down — always before any glucose-containing fluid. The lower once-daily dose sometimes quoted is a prophylactic regimen for at-risk patients without signs of Wernicke's, not adequate treatment for established disease. Watch for anaphylaxis with IV administration (rare but recognised). Continue oral thiamine after the acute course in patients who remain at risk (e.g. continued alcohol use).

B12 deficiency: replace via the appropriate route (IM hydroxocobalamin for confirmed deficiency, especially with neurological involvement) — never give folate first if B12 status is unknown and neurological symptoms are present.

Vitamin D deficiency: oral colecalciferol replacement per local protocol; correct calcium and phosphate at the same time if osteomalacia is present.

Vitamin C deficiency (scurvy): oral vitamin C replacement; address the underlying dietary or social driver.

Monitoring and failure: reassess the clinical syndrome (confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia) after treatment. An incomplete or delayed response to thiamine still warrants continued treatment and neurology involvement, since some deficits may be only partially reversible if treatment was delayed.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Admit for IV thiamine and monitoring in suspected Wernicke's encephalopathy; involve the alcohol care team where relevant for ongoing support and relapse prevention. Handover states the treatment given, the sequence (thiamine before glucose), and the response to treatment so far.

Two common student errors
  • Giving glucose before thiamine in an at-risk patient — a simple ordering error with potentially devastating, irreversible consequences.
  • Waiting for the complete classic triad of a deficiency syndrome (e.g. all three features of Wernicke's) before treating — most patients do not present with the full textbook picture.

Check your understanding

Q1What is the classic triad of Wernicke's encephalopathy, and how often is it present in full?

Answer. Confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia/nystagmus. Only a minority of patients show all three features, so clinicians must have a low threshold to treat on clinical suspicion alone.

Q2Why must thiamine be given before or with glucose in a thiamine-deficient patient, not after?

Answer. Glucose metabolism uses up remaining thiamine stores, so giving glucose first can precipitate or worsen Wernicke's encephalopathy.

Q3Why should folate never be given alone for macrocytic anaemia until B12 deficiency is excluded?

Answer. Giving folate without correcting a coexisting B12 deficiency can precipitate or worsen subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord.

Q4Which patient groups are at particular risk of vitamin C deficiency (scurvy), and what are its key features?

Answer. Those with a very poor diet, alcohol dependence, or social isolation. Features include perifollicular haemorrhages, corkscrew hairs, swollen bleeding gums, poor wound healing, and spontaneous bruising.

Case scenario

A 39-year-old woman who had gastric bypass surgery 2 years ago presents with progressive unsteady gait, numbness in her feet, and a sore, smooth tongue. Her blood film shows macrocytosis. What is the most likely underlying deficiency, and what should not be given until it is excluded or corrected?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Vitamin B12 deficiency, from malabsorption after gastric bypass. Folate should not be given alone until B12 deficiency has been excluded or treatment for it started, since folate alone can worsen neurological damage such as subacute combined degeneration of the cord.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 45-year-old man with long-standing alcohol dependence is admitted confused, ataxic and with nystagmus. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate thiamine regimen?

  1. High-dose IV thiamine (as part of a B-complex preparation), at treatment dose several times a day for at least 2–3 days, before any glucose
  2. A single low-dose IM thiamine injection, sufficient for both prophylaxis and treatment
  3. Oral thiamine 100 mg once daily
  4. No thiamine needed unless a low serum thiamine level is confirmed first
  5. IV thiamine only after glucose has been given to correct his low capillary glucose
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: A. Suspected Wernicke's encephalopathy needs high-dose IV thiamine given at treatment frequency, before glucose. C is wrong — oral, low-dose thiamine is inadequate for suspected established disease. E is wrong — giving glucose first risks precipitating or worsening Wernicke's. B is wrong — that dose is a prophylactic regimen, not treatment. D is wrong — treatment should be started empirically on clinical suspicion; it must never wait for laboratory confirmation.

MCQ 2. A 70-year-old vegan man presents with fatigue, macrocytic anaemia and peripheral neuropathy. B12 and folate levels are pending. What is the most appropriate initial management?

  1. Wait for both B12 and folate levels before starting any treatment
  2. Start folic acid alone immediately while awaiting the B12 result
  3. Start a therapeutic trial of oral iron
  4. Transfuse packed red cells regardless of haemoglobin level
  5. Treat empirically for B12 deficiency now (e.g. IM hydroxocobalamin), and avoid giving folate alone until B12 deficiency has been excluded or corrected
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: E. With neurological symptoms and a likely B12-deficient diet, B12 deficiency should be treated empirically without delay, and folate should not be given alone. B is wrong — folate alone risks worsening neurological damage if B12 is also deficient. A is wrong — neurological symptoms mean treatment should not be delayed pending results. D is wrong — there is no stated indication for transfusion. C is wrong — there is no evidence of iron deficiency in this vignette.

Presentation 4Obesity and metabolic syndrome

Obesity is now the most common nutritional problem seen in clinical practice in developed countries. It is not simply a cosmetic issue — it drives measurable increases in diabetes, cardiovascular disease, certain cancers and all-cause mortality, and even modest weight loss produces disproportionately large health benefits.

Clinical vignette

Stage 1. A 44-year-old man attends for a routine check. His BMI is 34 kg/m², waist circumference 108 cm. Blood pressure is 148/92, fasting glucose 6.3 mmol/L, triglycerides 2.1 mmol/L, HDL 0.9 mmol/L. Before reading on, decide: does this patient have metabolic syndrome, and why does that label matter clinically?

Step 1. Sick or not sick?

Obesity itself is rarely an acute emergency, but it is a powerful long-term risk multiplier. Central (visceral) obesity in particular predicts cardiovascular and metabolic complications more strongly than BMI alone. Recognise the obesity-related emergencies when they do arise: obstructive sleep apnoea causing severe hypoxia, obesity hypoventilation syndrome, and markedly increased perioperative and anaesthetic risk in patients with morbid obesity.

High-yield pearl

Waist circumference (or waist-to-hip ratio) adds information beyond BMI alone, because centrally distributed fat is metabolically more active and more strongly linked to cardiovascular risk than peripheral fat. Two patients with the same BMI can have very different risk profiles depending on fat distribution.

Step 2. History

Take a weight history (age of onset, triggers, previous attempts at weight loss and what worked or didn't), a dietary and activity history, and screen for obesity-related complications: snoring/witnessed apnoeas and daytime sleepiness (OSA), joint pain (osteoarthritis), heartburn (reflux/hiatus hernia), menstrual irregularity (PCOS), and mood (depression/anxiety, which are both a cause and a consequence of obesity). Ask about secondary causes where clinically suggested — hypothyroidism, Cushing's syndrome, drug-induced weight gain (e.g. corticosteroids, some antipsychotics) — though most obesity has no identifiable endocrine cause.

In plain terms: a man with central obesity, raised blood pressure, impaired fasting glucose and dyslipidaemia — this meets criteria for metabolic syndrome, which flags a significantly higher cardiovascular and diabetes risk that needs proactive management now, not after diabetes has already developed.

Step 3. Examination pearl

Examination pearl

Always measure and document BMI and waist circumference — do not rely on visual impression. Classify using the WHO system below. Examine specifically for signs of a secondary cause (goitre or myxoedematous features for hypothyroidism; striae, proximal myopathy or moon face for Cushing's) if clinically suggested, though this is uncommon.

WHO BMI classification

CategoryBMI (kg/m²)
Underweight<18.5
Healthy weight18.5–24.9
Overweight25–29.9
Obese class I30–34.9
Obese class II35–39.9
Obese class III (morbid obesity)≥40

Interpretation: risk of type 2 diabetes, cardiovascular disease and other complications rises progressively above a healthy weight. Central (visceral) fat, best estimated by waist circumference, adds further risk information beyond BMI alone.

Step 4. Schema — metabolic syndrome and the complications of obesity

Metabolic syndrome (using the widely taught NCEP ATP III/AHA-NHLBI definition) requires 3 or more of the 5 criteria below. The vignette patient meets all five criteria — waist circumference, blood pressure, triglycerides, HDL, and fasting glucose — a striking clustering of cardiometabolic risk that calls for proactive management now, not once diabetes has already developed.

Metabolic syndrome — NCEP ATP III/AHA criteria (3 of 5 required)

ComponentThreshold
Waist circumference>102 cm (men) / >88 cm (women)
Triglycerides≥1.7 mmol/L
HDL cholesterol<1.03 mmol/L (men) / <1.29 mmol/L (women)
Blood pressure≥130 mmHg systolic and/or ≥85 mmHg diastolic (or treated hypertension)
Fasting glucose≥5.6 mmol/L (revised criteria) or known type 2 diabetes — the original 2001 threshold was ≥6.1 mmol/L

Interpretation: 3 or more of the 5 criteria \= metabolic syndrome. This identifies a cluster of cardiometabolic risk factors that together predict type 2 diabetes and cardiovascular disease more strongly than any single factor alone.

Must not miss
  • Obstructive sleep apnoea and obesity hypoventilation syndrome — ask about snoring, witnessed apnoeas and daytime somnolence; untreated OSA carries real cardiovascular and accident risk.
  • Undiagnosed type 2 diabetes — check fasting glucose/HbA1c proactively rather than waiting for symptoms.
  • Non-alcoholic fatty liver disease — common, usually asymptomatic, and part of the same metabolic cluster.
  • Depression and body-image distress — frequently under-recognised and under-treated in patients with obesity, and can be both a cause and a consequence.

Step 5. Tiered investigation

TierTestWhy
BedsideBMI, waist circumference, blood pressureCore anthropometric and cardiovascular screen
LaboratoryFasting glucose/HbA1c, lipid profile, LFTs, TFTs (if clinically indicated)Screens for metabolic syndrome components, diabetes, and fatty liver disease
SpecialSleep study if OSA is suspected; further endocrine work-up only if a secondary cause is clinically suggestedDirected by symptoms rather than performed routinely in every patient

Step 6. Commitment and risk stratification

Working plan for the vignette: class I obesity with metabolic syndrome — a clear indication for structured weight-management intervention and cardiovascular risk-factor treatment now, aiming to prevent progression to overt type 2 diabetes and cardiovascular disease.

Step 7. Management ladder

Lifestyle (first-line for all patients): dietary calorie reduction, with a safe initial target of roughly 0.5–1 kg/week weight loss through a sustained calorie deficit; increased physical activity, which alone produces limited weight loss but helps prevent weight regain and improves general cardiovascular health; behavioural support (structured programmes, family involvement, cognitive behavioural approaches), which improves adherence. A realistic initial goal is around 10% weight loss, which already produces meaningful reductions in blood pressure, HbA1c, and lipid abnormalities.

Pharmacological: used as an adjunct to lifestyle measures, not a replacement. Orlistat (a pancreatic lipase inhibitor) remains an option; newer agents such as GLP-1 receptor agonists are increasingly used for weight management and also improve glycaemic control in patients with coexisting type 2 diabetes. Use per local/NICE guidance and specialist input.

Surgical (bariatric/metabolic surgery): considered for BMI ≥40, or BMI 35–40 with an obesity-related complication such as type 2 diabetes, after conventional measures have failed; NICE guidance also supports earlier/expedited assessment in some patients with recent-onset type 2 diabetes at lower BMI thresholds.
