Restrictive (e.g. sleeve gastrectomy, gastric banding), malabsorptive, or combined procedures (e.g. Roux-en-Y gastric bypass) are available, with combined procedures generally producing greater weight loss. Requires lifelong monitoring for micronutrient deficiencies (iron, calcium, B12, vitamin D) afterward.

Treat the components directly: manage hypertension, dyslipidaemia and hyperglycaemia according to standard protocols in parallel with weight-management measures — do not wait for weight loss alone to normalise these before starting appropriate treatment.

Monitoring and failure: reassess weight, waist circumference and metabolic parameters at intervals; escalate from lifestyle to pharmacological to surgical management if targets are not being met and risk remains high.

Step 8. Disposition and handover

Most obesity management happens in primary care or structured community weight-management services; refer to specialist/bariatric services for the BMI thresholds above, or when complications (severe OSA, uncontrolled diabetes) need specialist input. Handover states the BMI, waist circumference, metabolic syndrome component count, and the current management step.

Fatal mistakes with obesity and metabolic syndrome
  • Treating obesity as a purely cosmetic issue and not screening for diabetes, dyslipidaemia and hypertension. These conditions are frequently asymptomatic until complications occur.
  • Recommending surgery or drug therapy before a genuine trial of lifestyle intervention, except where obesity-related complications make earlier escalation appropriate.
  • Forgetting micronutrient monitoring after bariatric surgery. Malabsorptive procedures in particular carry a real long-term risk of iron, calcium, B12 and vitamin D deficiency.
  • Missing obstructive sleep apnoea by not asking about snoring, witnessed apnoeas and daytime sleepiness in every patient with significant obesity.

Check your understanding

Q1What are the WHO BMI thresholds for overweight and the three classes of obesity?

Answer. Overweight 25–29.9 kg/m²; obese class I 30–34.9; class II 35–39.9; class III (morbid obesity) ≥40 kg/m².

Q2Why does waist circumference add information beyond BMI?

Answer. It reflects central/visceral fat, which is more metabolically active and more strongly linked to cardiovascular and metabolic risk than BMI alone, so two people with the same BMI can have different risk depending on fat distribution.

Q3How many of the five NCEP/AHA metabolic syndrome criteria must be present to make the diagnosis, and what are they?

Answer. 3 or more of: waist circumference, triglycerides, HDL cholesterol, blood pressure, and fasting glucose (thresholds as in the table above).

Q4What long-term monitoring is required after malabsorptive bariatric surgery, and why?

Answer. Lifelong monitoring for micronutrient deficiencies (iron, calcium, B12, vitamin D), because malabsorptive procedures reduce nutrient absorption.

Case scenario

A 51-year-old woman with a BMI of 42 kg/m² and type 2 diabetes diagnosed 8 months ago has tried structured lifestyle intervention with dietitian support for 6 months, with minimal weight loss. What is the most appropriate next step?

AnswerShow the model answer

Answer. Refer for assessment for bariatric/metabolic surgery. A BMI ≥40 (or ≥35 with an obesity-related complication such as diabetes) after failed conventional measures is a standard indication; her diabetes and other risk factors should continue to be treated in parallel.

MCQs

MCQ 1. A 39-year-old man has a BMI of 37 kg/m² and no diabetes. According to the WHO classification, which category does he fall into, and what BMI-based threshold alone would make him potentially eligible for bariatric surgery assessment even without any complication?

  1. Obese class I; BMI ≥30
  2. Overweight; BMI ≥25 alone
  3. Obese class II; BMI ≥30 with any comorbidity
  4. Obese class II; BMI ≥40 alone, or ≥35 with an obesity-related complication
  5. Obese class III (morbid obesity); BMI ≥35 alone
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: D. A BMI of 37 falls in obese class II (35–39.9), and the general surgical threshold is BMI ≥40 alone, or ≥35 with an obesity-related complication. A and B name the wrong class for this BMI. E names the wrong class (class III is ≥40) and the wrong threshold. C understates the BMI threshold for surgery without a qualifying complication.

MCQ 2. A 55-year-old woman has a waist circumference of 90 cm, blood pressure 132/84 mmHg, triglycerides 1.9 mmol/L, HDL 1.1 mmol/L, and fasting glucose 5.2 mmol/L. How many of the five metabolic syndrome criteria does she meet, and does she have metabolic syndrome?

  1. 2 of 5; no metabolic syndrome
  2. 3 of 5; yes, metabolic syndrome
  3. 4 of 5; yes, metabolic syndrome
  4. 4 of 5; no, because glucose is not elevated
  5. 5 of 5; yes, metabolic syndrome
AnswerShow the correct answer

Correct answer: C. Waist >88 cm (women), systolic BP ≥130, triglycerides ≥1.7, and low HDL (<1.29 for women) are all positive — four criteria. Fasting glucose 5.2 does not reach the 5.6 mmol/L revised threshold, so it is not positive, giving 4 of 5. A and B undercount the positive criteria. D is wrong because the diagnosis only requires 3 or more of the 5 criteria — glucose does not need to be one of them. E overcounts, since glucose is not positive.

Symptom-Based Internal Medicine · Blocks 1–10 · Department of Internal Medicine

Answer key

Model answers to the Check-your-understanding questions, case scenarios and MCQs, listed by presentation.

Foundation · How to approach any clinical condition

Worked case: putting the method to work

SBA 1. Correct answer: A. She is sick — a NEWS2 of 9 is in the high-risk band — and resuscitation precedes diagnosis. Complete an A–E assessment and give controlled oxygen titrated to SpO₂ 94–98%. Stabilise first, then investigate. B — a CTPA may well be needed, but not before the patient is assessed and stabilised, and never as the very first action in a hypoxic, tachypnoeic patient. E — there is no evidence of infection to treat yet (clear chest, no focus); antibiotics here are a reflex, not a reasoned step. C — labelling breathlessness with red-flag risk factors as “anxiety” is premature closure, and potentially fatal. D — thrombolysis is reserved for confirmed high-risk (haemodynamically unstable) PE; she is not in that group and PE is not yet confirmed. Learning point: in anyone who might be unwell, A–E and NEWS2 come before the diagnostic work — treat what kills first. (RCP NEWS2)

SBA 2. Correct answer: B. The PaO₂ is low, which defines type 1 respiratory failure. The pH is high with a low PaCO₂ and a normal bicarbonate — a respiratory alkalosis from hyperventilation. Hypoxia with hypocapnia and a clear chest X-ray is a classic, though not specific, pattern for pulmonary embolism. C — type 2 failure needs a high PaCO₂ with acidaemia, the opposite of this gas. D — a metabolic acidosis would show a low bicarbonate and a low pH. A — a metabolic alkalosis would show a raised bicarbonate. E — the gas is clearly abnormal; she is hypoxic on air. Learning point: read every gas in the same sequence — oxygenation, then pH, then the primary disturbance, then compensation.

SBA 3. Correct answer: C. The problem representation — an older, recently immobile woman with acute pleuritic breathlessness, hypoxia, tachycardia and a tender calf, but a clear chest X-ray — fits pulmonary embolism best. The clear film alongside marked hypoxia is itself a clue. B — pneumonia is possible given the mildly raised CRP, but unlikely with clear lung fields and no focal signs. A — there are no ischaemic ECG changes, and a small troponin rise is common in PE from right-ventricular strain. D — heart failure is against a normal-sized heart and a clear, uncongested film. E — musculoskeletal pain cannot explain the hypoxia and tachycardia. Learning point: a precise problem representation, built from semantic qualifiers and risk factors, usually makes the leading diagnosis clear.

SBA 4. Correct answer: E. Formalise the estimate. The two-level Wells score places her in the “PE likely” group, so the correct pathway is straight to CTPA — a D-dimer is not needed in this group — with interim therapeutic anticoagulation if there is no contraindication and a delay is expected. B — D-dimer is a highly sensitive rule-out test used only in the “PE-unlikely” group; ordering it when PE is already likely risks a falsely reassuring result. C — treating the small troponin rise as an ACS is anchoring and premature closure; right-ventricular strain from PE raises troponin. A — antibiotics treat a diagnosis you have not made, and delay the real one. D — discharging a hypoxic patient with a high probability of PE is unsafe. Learning point: choose tests by pre-test probability. A rule-out test is wasted, and can be dangerous, when the probability is already high. (NICE NG158)

SBA 5. Correct answer: B. When PE is likely and confirmatory imaging will be delayed, give therapeutic-dose anticoagulation now unless contraindicated. You treat on the balance of probability rather than leave a likely PE untreated. C — a prophylactic dose is inadequate to treat an established clot. D — aspirin is not a treatment for venous thromboembolism. A — withholding anticoagulation during a delay, when PE is likely and there is no contraindication, exposes her to avoidable risk. E — thrombolysis is not indicated in a haemodynamically stable patient. Learning point: act on a high pre-test probability while you confirm it. Interim anticoagulation is standard when imaging is delayed. (NICE NG158)

SBA 6. Correct answer: C. She now has confirmed PE with hypotension — this is high-risk (massive) PE. It warrants urgent senior and critical-care involvement and systemic thrombolysis unless contraindicated, with surgical embolectomy or catheter-directed therapy as alternatives. A — increasing the oral anticoagulant does nothing for acute obstructive shock. E — a beta-blocker removes the compensatory tachycardia and can precipitate cardiovascular collapse in right-ventricular failure. D — repeating the CTPA delays life-saving treatment in an already-confirmed PE. B — watchful waiting in obstructive shock is unsafe. Learning point: hypotension in confirmed PE defines high-risk PE and changes the treatment from anticoagulation to reperfusion. Recognise it and escalate. (ESC / NICE NG158)

SBA 7. Correct answer: B. SBAR is the structured handover tool that communicates a deteriorating patient clearly and prompts a specific ask. A — SOCRATES characterises pain, it does not structure a handover. D — the surgical sieve generates a differential; it does not communicate one. E — the Wells score risk-stratifies for PE. C — ACVPU grades conscious level. Learning point: call early, and communicate in a shared structure. SBAR gets the right help fast.

SBA 8. Correct answer: D. Treat for a minimum of 3 months, then reassess whether the event was provoked — here by the flight and immobility — or unprovoked, to decide on extended anticoagulation, balancing recurrence risk against bleeding risk. C — five days is far too short for a PE. B — aspirin is inadequate secondary prevention compared with anticoagulation. A — thrombophilia testing does not guide the acute duration, and is unreliable during acute clot and while anticoagulated. E — stopping at discharge leaves a treated PE dangerously under-treated. Learning point: anticoagulation duration is decided by the provoked/unprovoked distinction, not by thrombophilia tests sent in the acute phase. (NICE NG158)

SBA 9. Correct answer: A. Accepting an early, convenient label and stopping the search is premature closure, driven here by anchoring on the first impression and on the troponin. The unexplained hypoxia should have reopened the differential, not closed it. E — framing contributed, but is not the core error; the search was abandoned. C — base-rate neglect is mis-weighting prevalence, not stopping the work-up. D — hindsight bias is a retrospective distortion, which is not what happened here. B — commission bias is a bias towards action; the error here was stopping. Learning point: a patient who does not fit the label — here, hypoxia unexplained by “anxiety” — is a prompt to re-derive the problem, not to reinforce the diagnosis.

Block 1 · Cardiorespiratory presentations

Chest pain

Q1. Answer. The right ventricle depends on adequate preload to maintain cardiac output. GTN and other nitrates are venodilators that reduce venous return; in RV infarction this can cause a severe, sometimes catastrophic, fall in blood pressure. Give cautious IV fluid instead and check the JVP, lung fields and blood pressure before giving any nitrate to a patient with an inferior MI.

Q2. Answer. No. A normal chest X-ray does not exclude aortic dissection. CT aortogram (or transoesophageal echocardiography) is needed if dissection is genuinely suspected.

Q3. Answer. Primary PCI should be delivered within 120 minutes of first medical contact. If this is not achievable, thrombolysis should be given (generally within 12 hours of symptom onset, per local protocol), with transfer for rescue PCI if thrombolysis fails.

Q4. Answer. Failed thrombolysis is defined by less than 50% ST resolution at 60–90 minutes, persistent pain, haemodynamic instability, or arrhythmia. This should trigger urgent rescue PCI.

Case scenario. Answer. This is most consistent with acute pericarditis, possibly with myocardial involvement (myopericarditis), given the widespread ST elevation with PR depression, positional/pleuritic character in a young, well patient after a viral illness, and a mildly raised troponin. It is not typical ACS, which usually shows territorial rather than widespread ST changes. Because troponin is raised, echocardiography and cardiology assessment are still needed to look for myocardial involvement; uncomplicated pericarditis is treated with an NSAID plus colchicine (with gastroprotection).

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. The combination of a large inter-arm blood pressure difference, a new diastolic murmur, and ST elevation should raise strong suspicion of aortic dissection extending into a coronary ostium (mimicking MI). Thrombolysis (E) in a dissection can be fatal by accelerating bleeding into the false lumen, so it must be excluded first. Primary PCI (D) is inappropriate until dissection is excluded, since the ST elevation may be secondary to the dissection itself. A GTN infusion (C) treats neither problem and risks hypotension. Discharge (A) is clearly wrong given the life-threatening presentation. The correct step is to hold reperfusion therapy and urgently image the aorta.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. A GRACE score above 140 identifies a high-risk NSTE-ACS patient, in whom guidelines recommend angiography within about 24 hours. D is wrong because a score this high is not low risk. B is wrong because this level of risk warrants earlier, in-patient angiography rather than an elective outpatient slot. C is wrong: the GRACE score is specifically used in ACS risk stratification, most relevantly in NSTEMI/unstable angina to decide timing of invasive management. A is wrong because "primary PCI" is the emergency reperfusion strategy for STEMI with ongoing ST elevation, not the pathway implied by a GRACE-based decision in NSTEMI.

Acute breathlessness

Q1. Answer. Patients who chronically retain CO₂ can become dependent on relative hypoxia to drive ventilation and can also develop worsening V/Q mismatch with high-flow oxygen. High-flow oxygen can suppress ventilatory drive and worsen CO₂ retention, leading to respiratory acidosis, drowsiness and, if untreated, respiratory arrest. The correct approach is controlled oxygen via a fixed Venturi mask, titrated to SpO₂ 88–92%.

Q2. Answer. A persistent pH below 7.35 with PaCO₂ above 6.5 kPa, despite optimal medical therapy including controlled oxygen, bronchodilators and steroids.

Q3. Answer. In acute asthma the patient should normally be hyperventilating and blowing off CO₂, so a normal or rising PaCO₂ means the patient is tiring and starting to fail — a sign of a life-threatening attack. This is the opposite of COPD, where a degree of chronic CO₂ retention can be an expected baseline.

Q4. Answer. Pulmonary embolism. PE classically causes hypoxaemia and tachycardia with few or no abnormal chest signs and a near-normal chest X-ray, so it is easily missed if you rely on the film to explain the hypoxia.

Case scenario. Answer. Reassess her urgently and check an ABG/repeat peak flow — a quietening chest and a falling heart rate in severe asthma can mean she is tiring and moving toward respiratory arrest, not genuinely improving. This is one of the classic false-reassurance signs in asthma. She needs senior/critical care review and should not be left on the ward assuming she is getting better on clinical impression alone.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. Drowsiness, a bounding pulse and a flapping tremor (asterixis) in a known type II respiratory failure patient on high-flow oxygen suggest CO₂ narcosis from over-oxygenation. The correct immediate step is controlled oxygen via a fixed Venturi device titrated to 88–92%, plus an urgent ABG to confirm. A delays the needed correction of oxygen delivery. B is wrong; there is no history suggesting opioid toxicity. E is premature without first trying controlled oxygen and reassessing. C is dangerous — the patient should never be left without any oxygen while hypoxic; the oxygen dose should be controlled, not withheld entirely.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. Hypoxia and tachycardia with a clear chest and a near-normal chest X-ray is the classic (and easily missed) presentation of pulmonary embolism. A is unlikely without wheeze or a history of asthma. B is unlikely with a normal chest X-ray and no mention of fever, cough or focal signs. D usually shows crackles, a raised JVP, or radiological signs of oedema. C should show absent breath sounds and hyper-resonance, and often an abnormality on the chest X-ray unless very subtle.

Palpitations

Q1. Answer. Is the patient stable? Is the rate fast or slow? Is it regular or irregular? Is the QRS narrow (under 120 ms) or broad (120 ms or more)?

Q2. Answer. This rhythm may be pre-excited AF conducting via an accessory pathway (as in WPW). Blocking the AV node removes competition for the accessory pathway, allowing it to conduct even faster and potentially precipitating ventricular fibrillation.

Q3. Answer. Yes. Paroxysmal AF carries the same thromboembolic risk as permanent AF, so the CHA₂DS₂-VASc score should still be calculated and documented, and anticoagulation considered accordingly.

Q4. Answer. Atrial flutter has an atrial rate of about 300/min; with 2:1 AV conduction this produces a ventricular rate close to 150/min. This is a recognisable pattern that is easy to mistake for a generic "SVT" if flutter waves are not specifically sought.

Case scenario. Answer. Treat this as ventricular tachycardia until proven otherwise, especially given his known structural/ischaemic heart disease — this is a major risk factor for VT over SVT with aberrant conduction. Adenosine is not the correct default step in a regular broad-complex tachycardia in a patient with known heart disease; the priority is senior/cardiology input and preparation for synchronised cardioversion if he becomes unstable, rather than a diagnostic drug trial that could delay definitive treatment of VT.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. Hypertension (1) + diabetes (1) + female sex (1) \= 3. She is under 65 so gets no age points, and has no heart failure, stroke/TIA or vascular disease. A and B undercount the points; D and E overcount by including points she has not earned (she is not 65 or older and has no vascular disease or prior stroke).

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. Abrupt onset and abrupt offset of a regular narrow-complex tachycardia, often terminating with vagal manoeuvres, is classic for AVNRT. D is wrong because sinus tachycardia has a gradual onset and offset and does not terminate abruptly with a Valsalva manoeuvre. C is wrong because AF is irregular. A and E are wrong because both are broad-complex rhythms, not narrow-complex.

Syncope

Q1. Answer. A brief prodrome of sweating, nausea, feeling hot and visual grey-out; rapid, full recovery afterward. Features pointing towards seizure instead include déjà vu/jamais vu before the event and prolonged drowsiness or confusion afterward. Note that brief jerking and incontinence can occur in syncope too, so these alone do not distinguish the two.

Q2. Answer. Exertional syncope suggests a mechanical or arrhythmic cardiac cause (for example severe aortic stenosis, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, or an exercise-triggered arrhythmia) that can cause sudden cardiac death. It should be treated as a cardiac cause until proven otherwise, not dismissed as a simple faint.

Q3. Answer. Blood pressure measured lying down and then again after standing (not just a single seated reading), looking for a significant drop in systolic and/or diastolic pressure on standing.

Q4. Answer. Only when a neurological cause is specifically suspected, or to look for a traumatic complication of a fall. It is not a routine part of the standard syncope work-up.

Case scenario. Answer. This is a classic vasovagal (reflex) syncope, given the clear situational trigger, the typical prodrome, and rapid full recovery with no red flags (no exertional trigger, no murmur, no abnormal ECG, no injury suggesting a sudden unwarned collapse). She does not meet the usual high-risk criteria for admission and can usually be reassured and safety-netted as an outpatient, with advice on trigger avoidance and counter-pressure manoeuvres.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. Exertional syncope with a murmur suggesting aortic stenosis is a high-risk feature requiring admission and urgent echocardiography, not outpatient work-up. D is wrong and potentially dangerous — the exertional pattern and murmur make vasovagal syncope an unsafe default diagnosis. B and A are appropriate tools for selected lower-risk or arrhythmic presentations, but not as the first step here when structural heart disease is suspected and admission is indicated. C treats nothing specific and could be harmful if severe aortic stenosis with a fixed cardiac output is present.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. Prolonged post-event confusion (a post-ictal state) is a specific pointer to seizure. B and E are non-specific because brief jerking and incontinence both occur in syncope as well as seizure. D (pallor) is typical of syncope, not seizure. C (rapid recovery) also favours syncope, not seizure.

Cough and haemoptysis

Q1. Answer. Patients cannot reliably estimate how much blood they've lost, some blood is swallowed or stays within the lung, and even a small volume of blood can be fatal if it blocks the airway or overwhelms a patient with poor respiratory reserve. Severity should be judged by the threat to airway, gas exchange, breathing and circulation, not by an arbitrary volume cut-off.

Q2. Answer. Haematemesis is usually preceded by nausea or vomiting, the blood is often dark or coffee-ground in appearance, and it may contain food. True haemoptysis is coughed up, often bright red and frothy, and may be mixed with sputum.

Q3. Answer. Haemoptysis occurring together with blood in the urine or impaired kidney function should prompt testing for ANA, ANCA and anti-GBM antibodies, and urinalysis with microscopy looking for red-cell casts, to identify conditions such as granulomatosis with polyangiitis, anti-GBM disease or SLE.

Q4. Answer. Bronchial artery embolisation, once the source has been localised (typically by CT chest ± bronchoscopy).

Case scenario. Answer. Urgent chest X-ray and referral on a suspected-cancer pathway. A new or altered cough with haemoptysis and weight loss in a smoker must be treated as suspicious for bronchogenic carcinoma until proven otherwise, even though he is haemodynamically stable and not acutely unwell.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. Airway protection is the immediate priority in life-threatening haemoptysis; positioning the bleeding lung down (if known) protects the good lung, and senior/anaesthetic input should be sought early. A is wrong because volume estimation is unreliable and delays action. E and D are wrong because imaging should not delay immediate airway and breathing management. B is wrong because tranexamic acid should be given IV, not oral, in this setting, and is not a substitute for airway management.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: D. With a haemoglobin below 70 g/L and ongoing bleeding, transfusion is indicated alongside IV tranexamic acid and correction of any clotting abnormality. B is wrong because tranexamic acid should be given IV, and outpatient referral is inappropriate for a life-threatening bleed. A skips first-line resuscitation and medical measures, which should generally be tried or at least initiated in parallel. E uses an incorrect, unsafely low transfusion threshold. C omits necessary transfusion and clotting correction.

Unilateral and bilateral leg swelling

Q1. Answer. D-dimer has good sensitivity but poor specificity. In a low pre-test probability patient, a negative D-dimer can safely exclude PE/DVT, avoiding unnecessary imaging. In a high pre-test probability patient, D-dimer adds little because imaging is required regardless of the result, and a positive result in this context does not add useful information.

Q2. Answer. They are related but distinct scoring tools. The PE Wells score assesses the probability of pulmonary embolism using criteria that include clinical signs of DVT, heart rate, immobilisation/surgery, previous VTE, haemoptysis and malignancy. The DVT Wells score is a separate tool focused specifically on the leg (localised tenderness, whole-leg swelling, calf circumference difference, pitting oedema, collateral veins, and so on) to estimate the probability of DVT itself.

Q3. Answer. It identifies patients at low risk of early (30-day) death from PE, who may be suitable for outpatient management or early discharge, versus higher-risk patients who need admission and closer monitoring.

Q4. Answer. In a high pre-test probability patient without a contraindication, the risk of a missed or delayed diagnosis of a potentially fatal PE outweighs the small risk of anticoagulating briefly before confirmation. Anticoagulation should be started on clinical grounds while imaging is arranged.

Case scenario. Answer. Bilateral, gradual, painless leg swelling with heavy proteinuria points towards a low-albumin state, most likely nephrotic syndrome, rather than DVT (which is typically unilateral, acute and painful) or a simple drug effect. Next steps include serum albumin, renal function, a full urine protein quantification (protein:creatinine ratio or 24-hour collection), and renal referral; the oral contraceptive pill is a relevant background risk factor for VTE but does not explain bilateral, painless, gradual swelling with heavy proteinuria.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. A Wells score of 7.5 is high probability for PE. In high-probability patients, D-dimer is not useful (E is wrong; it doesn't change management and delays care) — the correct step is to proceed straight to imaging and start anticoagulation empirically. C ignores clear red flags (hypoxia, tachycardia, risk factors). D is far too slow for a high-probability, hypoxic patient. A does not provide therapeutic anticoagulation for VTE.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. An sPESI of 0 identifies a low risk of early death from PE, and such patients (if otherwise clinically stable and with adequate support) are potential candidates for outpatient management or early discharge, per current guidance. D is the opposite of what a score of 0 indicates. E is wrong — PESI/sPESI is specifically used after PE is confirmed, to guide disposition. C is wrong; thrombolysis is reserved for haemodynamically unstable (high-risk) PE, not low-risk PE. B is wrong — anticoagulation is still required for confirmed PE regardless of the sPESI score; the score guides the level of monitoring/setting, not whether to anticoagulate.

Block 2 · Neurological presentations

Headache

Q1. Answer. A headache that reaches maximum intensity within seconds ("thunderclap") points to a small group of dangerous causes — SAH, other intracranial haemorrhage, RCVS, arterial dissection, CVST, pituitary apoplexy — regardless of how severe it feels. A headache can be very severe (e.g. migraine) but build up over minutes to hours and still be benign. Onset speed, not pain intensity, is what triggers the emergency pathway.

Q2. Answer. If clinical suspicion remains high, proceed to lumbar puncture for xanthochromia, ideally at least 12 hours after symptom onset — a CT within 6 hours has very high negative predictive value under good conditions, but it does not reach 100%, and LP detects SAH that CT can miss, particularly if performed after 6 hours or when doubt remains.

Q3. Answer. Papilloedema signals raised intracranial pressure. If present, imaging must be done before any lumbar puncture, because LP in the presence of raised ICP and a mass lesion risks brain herniation.

Q4. Answer. Start corticosteroids immediately on clinical suspicion of giant cell arteritis. Visual loss from GCA is often permanent once it occurs, so treatment must not wait for confirmatory tests.

Case scenario. Answer. A postural headache in the puerperium raises two possibilities that both need active exclusion: cerebral venous sinus thrombosis (pregnancy/puerperium is a major risk factor, though CVST headache is not typically classically postural) and low-CSF-pressure headache (e.g. post-dural-puncture if she had an epidural, or spontaneous intracranial hypotension) — a headache that is clearly worse standing and better lying flat is the hallmark of low-CSF-pressure headache. Given the puerperium and progressive course, first prioritise CT or MR venography to exclude CVST before attributing the headache to a benign postural cause.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. An exertional thunderclap headache meets Ottawa SAH criteria; even with a normal early CT, SAH is not fully excluded, so LP for xanthochromia is indicated, timed appropriately after onset. A: wrong — CT sensitivity is very high but not absolute, especially outside a validated 6-hour high-quality-scan protocol. E: repeating CT adds radiation without excluding SAH as reliably as LP. B: MRI is not the standard next test for suspected SAH after a negative CT. D: unsafe — this presentation meets criteria for urgent SAH workup, not routine follow-up.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. Jaw claudication and new headache with scalp tenderness in an older patient is classic for GCA. Treatment must start on clinical suspicion because visual loss is often irreversible once it happens. B and C: both delay treatment while risking permanent visual loss. A: there is no fever or meningism to suggest meningitis. D: unsafe delay in a time-critical diagnosis.

Acute limb weakness

Q1. Answer. Oxygen saturation is often preserved until respiratory muscle failure is advanced, because the lungs themselves are normal — it is the muscles that fail. FVC directly tracks respiratory muscle strength and falls before oxygen levels drop, giving earlier warning of impending respiratory failure.

Q2. Answer. Clinical localisation of a spinal level can be inaccurate, and a compressive lesion may sit in the thoracic or cervical cord above where symptoms suggest. A lumbar-only scan will miss it, so whole-spine MRI is required when the level is uncertain.

Q3. Answer. No. Early CSF protein is normal in roughly a third of patients, and neurophysiology has higher diagnostic yield after the first week. A normal early test does not rule out GBS if the clinical pattern fits.

Q4. Answer. Saddle sensory loss, new urinary retention, and bilateral leg weakness together indicate acute compression threatening permanent bladder, bowel and sexual function, and lower-limb function — this requires same-day imaging and surgical decompression, not routine outpatient referral.

Case scenario. Answer. Metastatic spinal cord compression is the most likely diagnosis, given known malignancy, back pain, bilateral leg weakness and a sensory level. The next best step is urgent whole-spine MRI (not lumbar-only, since the level is at the umbilicus/thoracic region) to confirm the site and extent of compression, with corticosteroids and urgent oncology/spinal surgery input arranged without waiting for the scan to be reported if compression is strongly suspected clinically.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. FVC below approximately 20 mL/kg with a fall of more than 30% from baseline meets the threshold for urgent critical care review, regardless of normal oxygen saturation — saturation is preserved until late in neuromuscular respiratory failure. A, E: unsafe delay. D: oxygen does not address the underlying muscle weakness or impending need for ventilatory support. B: nerve conduction results take time and do not change the urgency of the current respiratory threat.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: D. Sudden unilateral weakness with facial involvement and a clear time of onset is the classic pattern of a cerebral hemisphere stroke. B: GBS is typically symmetrical and ascending over days, not sudden and unilateral. A: myasthenia causes fatigable, often bulbar/ocular weakness, not sudden hemiparesis. C: cord lesions typically cause bilateral signs below a level, not unilateral facial and limb weakness together. E: myopathy causes proximal, symmetrical, gradual weakness.

Seizure

Q1. Answer. A generalised convulsive seizure lasting 5 minutes, or recurrent seizures without full recovery of consciousness between them, is treated as status epilepticus.

Q2. Answer. IV lorazepam if IV access is available. If not, buccal or intramuscular midazolam, or rectal diazepam, are alternatives.

Q3. Answer. Non-convulsive status epilepticus must be considered whenever a patient fails to return to baseline after apparent cessation of convulsions. Urgent EEG is needed to diagnose it.

Q4. Answer. Because seizure is largely a clinical diagnosis: details of what happened before, during and after (aura, evolution, colour change, duration, post-event confusion) distinguish epileptic seizure from syncope or a dissociative attack far more reliably than a single blood test or even EEG — a normal EEG does not exclude epilepsy.

Case scenario. Answer. Convulsive syncope is most likely. The pallor, prolonged standing/heat trigger, and — most importantly — the rapid, complete recovery without a period of post-event confusion favour syncope over epileptic seizure; brief myoclonic jerks can occur in syncope and are not on their own diagnostic of epilepsy. Epileptic seizures typically produce a period of post-ictal confusion or sleepiness.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. This is status epilepticus (>5 minutes); if IV access is unavailable, buccal/IM midazolam (or rectal diazepam) is the appropriate first-line route. D: unsafe delay — treatment should not wait for IV access. E: oral route is inappropriate and too slow in a seizing patient who cannot swallow safely. A: IV antiseizure loading medications are second-line, after a benzodiazepine, and require IV access. C: status epilepticus by definition has not self-terminated and requires active treatment.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. A normal routine EEG does not exclude epilepsy; the decision to start long-term antiseizure medication after a single seizure is based on recurrence-risk factors (epileptiform EEG, structural lesion, prior brain insult, abnormal exam, nocturnal seizure), not reflexively. B: incorrect — EEG sensitivity for epilepsy is limited, especially as a single routine trace. A: reflexive treatment after one seizure without risk factors is not indicated. D: a normal EEG does not distinguish epileptic from dissociative events. C: outpatient neurology follow-up with driving advice and safety-netting is still required.

Reduced consciousness

Q1. Answer. Capillary glucose (and treat hypoglycaemia immediately if low) and, if pinpoint pupils and slow breathing suggest opioid toxicity, naloxone — both are instantly actionable and must not wait for laboratory confirmation.

Q2. Answer. Naloxone has a shorter half-life than most opioids, so its effect can wear off before the opioid does, causing re-sedation. Ongoing monitoring, and repeat dosing or an infusion, may be needed.

Q3. Answer. A falling GCS with a new fixed, dilated pupil, plus Cushing's triad (hypertension, bradycardia, irregular respiration), suggests herniation. Call for immediate senior and neurosurgical help.

Q4. Answer. More than one precipitant commonly coexists, particularly in frail elderly patients (the vulnerable-brain-plus-precipitating-insult model) — a good response to naloxone does not exclude, for example, coexisting hypoglycaemia or sepsis, so glucose and a septic/metabolic screen should still be completed.

Case scenario. Answer. This is most likely a multifactorial delirium (a milder point on the same spectrum as reduced consciousness), driven by the anticholinergic sedating antihistamine, constipation, and possibly an occult catheter-associated urinary infection. The initial approach is to identify and correct reversible precipitants: stop or review the sedating/anticholinergic drug, treat constipation, screen for infection (including urine), and provide supportive/reorientation measures — rather than assuming a single structural or "confusion of old age" cause.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. Pinpoint pupils, hypoventilation and recent opioid administration make opioid toxicity the presumed diagnosis; naloxone should be given immediately, titrated to reverse respiratory depression without precipitating severe pain or withdrawal. C, D: both delay reversible treatment; tests should run in parallel, not before treatment. E: flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines, not opioids, and carries seizure risk. A: unsafe — respiratory rate of 6 requires immediate action, not passive observation.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. A falling GCS with a new fixed, dilated pupil and Cushing's triad (hypertension, bradycardia, irregular respiration) after head injury indicates raised intracranial pressure with impending herniation — this needs immediate senior/neurosurgical escalation, not a stepwise workup. C, D: there is no history pointing to opioids or hypoglycaemia, and neither explains the pupil finding or Cushing's triad. E: EEG is not the immediate priority when herniation signs are present. A: this pattern is not consistent with a vasovagal event and delaying escalation risks fatal outcome.

Dizziness and vertigo

Q1. Answer. "Dizziness" can mean true vertigo, presyncope, disequilibrium, or vague light-headedness, and each has a different set of likely causes and a different workup. Assuming vertigo when the patient actually means presyncope (for example) sends the assessment down the wrong path.

Q2. Answer. A normal head impulse test (no corrective saccade) means the peripheral vestibular reflex is intact. In a patient who is acutely and severely vertiginous, an intact peripheral reflex suggests the problem lies centrally instead — so, paradoxically, "normal" here points toward a central (stroke) cause.

Q3. Answer. CT is insensitive to early posterior fossa infarcts. MRI with diffusion-weighted imaging is required if central features are present; a normal CT should not be used to reassure a patient with red-flag features.

Q4. Answer. Inability to walk unaided or sit up unsupported. This is a red flag in its own right, independent of any other clinical feature.

Case scenario. Answer. Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (BPPV) is most likely, given brief, position-triggered episodes with no interictal symptoms and no hearing or neurological change. The Dix-Hallpike test can reproduce the vertigo and characteristic nystagmus to confirm the diagnosis, and the Epley (or equivalent) repositioning manoeuvre is both diagnostic and therapeutic.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: A. A central HINTS pattern (normal head impulse test, direction-changing nystagmus, skew deviation) plus inability to stand unsupported strongly suggests posterior circulation stroke; urgent MRI and stroke pathway involvement are required. D: vestibular neuritis produces a peripheral HINTS pattern, not this central one. E: BPPV is positional and brief, not sudden and continuous with these findings. B: Ménière's would not present acutely like this nor with a central HINTS pattern. C: unsafe — the clinical picture meets red-flag criteria for stroke.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. Short-course vestibular sedatives for the first 24–48 hours followed by early mobilisation and vestibular rehabilitation is correct; prolonged sedative use delays central compensation. A, E: both delay recovery by prolonging sedation or inactivity. D: early mobilisation and rehabilitation measurably improve and speed recovery. B: MRI is not required once the diagnosis is already confirmed and no central features are present.

Acute visual loss

Q1. Answer. Is the loss painful or painless, and does it affect one eye or both? These two questions quickly narrow the differential and dictate urgency.

Q2. Answer. Visual loss from GCA is often permanent once established, and untreated GCA can cause loss of vision in the second eye within days. Waiting for confirmatory tests risks irreversible bilateral blindness, so treatment is started on clinical suspicion.

Q3. Answer. Retinal pallor with a cherry-red spot at the macula is classic for CRAO. It is managed as a stroke of the eye because it shares the same vascular risk factors and embolic mechanisms as cerebral stroke, and the same urgent search for a carotid or cardioembolic source is needed.

Q4. Answer. These are classic symptoms of retinal detachment, which requires same-day ophthalmology assessment; treating them as benign risks permanent visual loss if detachment is not repaired promptly.

Case scenario. Answer. Acute angle-closure glaucoma is most likely, given painful red eye, haloes, a mid-dilated poorly reactive pupil, and a firm globe. The most appropriate immediate step is same-day emergency ophthalmology referral for intraocular pressure measurement and urgent pressure-lowering treatment — this should not be delayed for a headache workup.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. A cherry-red spot with retinal pallor is the classic finding in central retinal artery occlusion. E: CRVO classically shows a "blood and thunder" fundus with widespread haemorrhages, not a cherry-red spot. D: retinal detachment shows an elevated, folded retina, not a cherry-red spot. A: optic neuritis typically shows a swollen or normal-looking disc, not retinal pallor with a cherry-red spot. C: vitreous haemorrhage obscures the fundus view rather than showing this specific sign.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. The clinical picture is classic for GCA-related visual loss; corticosteroids must start immediately on clinical suspicion to protect the remaining vision and the other eye, without waiting for ESR or biopsy. A, B: both delay treatment and risk permanent bilateral visual loss. D: routine referral is unsafe in a time-critical diagnosis. C: there are no features here of angle-closure glaucoma, and topical drops would not address GCA.

Block 3 · Gastrointestinal presentations

Acute abdominal pain

Q1. Answer. A contained retroperitoneal rupture bleeds behind the peritoneum, so peritonism (rigidity, guarding) can be absent or only appear late. The diagnosis rests on the pattern of sudden central/back pain with shock, not on finding a rigid abdomen.

Q2. Answer. Lactate only rises once ischaemia has been present long enough to overwhelm clearance, so an early sample can still be normal despite established bowel ischaemia. Pain out of proportion to examination findings is a more reliable early clue.

Q3. Answer. CT of the abdomen and pelvis. Concern about contrast-induced nephropathy should not delay this scan when there is a genuine emergency needing a diagnosis.

Q4. Answer. Any two of: ectopic pregnancy (positive pregnancy test in a woman of childbearing age with pain), perforated viscus (sudden severe pain with peritonism — a normal erect chest X-ray does not exclude it), or mesenteric ischaemia (pain out of proportion to examination).

Case scenario. Answer. Ruptured ectopic pregnancy until proven otherwise. Start resuscitation (large-bore IV access, fluids, group and cross-match) and make an urgent gynaecology referral for likely emergency surgery — do not wait for further imaging before making a life-saving referral in a shocked patient with a positive pregnancy test and acute pelvic pain.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. This is the classic presentation of a leaking/ruptured AAA — sudden pain radiating to the back with shock. A soft abdomen does not exclude this (B is wrong and dangerous). Outpatient imaging (D) and observation (A, E) all risk fatal delay in an unstable patient with a vascular emergency; the priority is simultaneous resuscitation and urgent surgical/vascular activation (C).

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. Acute mesenteric ischaemia classically causes pain out of proportion to examination, and lactate rises late, so neither a normal lactate (C) nor a soft abdomen (E) excludes it. AF is an embolic source. Constipation (D) does not explain sudden severe pain with an embolic risk factor. Waiting for lactate to rise (B) risks fatal delay — urgent CT angiography is needed instead.

Upper gastrointestinal bleeding

Q1. Answer. In acute haemorrhage, red cells and plasma are lost together, so the concentration (Hb) does not fall until fluid shifts and resuscitation cause haemodilution. A normal first Hb does not exclude a major bleed — repeat it after resuscitation.

Q2. Answer. Do not automatically stop it. Abrupt cessation can precipitate stent thrombosis, so this needs an urgent joint cardiology/gastroenterology discussion rather than a reflex decision.

Q3. Answer. Forrest Ia (spurting vessel), Ib (oozing) and IIa (non-bleeding visible vessel). IIb (adherent clot) is intermediate/high risk and often treated; IIc and III are low risk.

Q4. Answer. Over-transfusion raises portal pressure and increases the risk of rebleeding in variceal haemorrhage, so packed red cells are generally considered once Hb falls below roughly 70–80 g/L rather than transfusing to a higher target.

Case scenario. Answer. Urea 9.5 mmol/L scores 3; male Hb 98 g/L (<100) scores 6; pulse ≥100 scores 1; melaena scores 1 — already at least 11 points from these features alone, before adding systolic BP and any syncope/comorbidity points. This is a high-risk score indicating a strong likelihood of needing transfusion or endoscopic treatment, regardless of the exact total — he needs admission, resuscitation and urgent endoscopy, most likely for an NSAID-related peptic ulcer.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. With reduced consciousness/encephalopathy and massive haematemesis, airway protection comes first — endoscopy (A) must follow resuscitation and airway safety, not precede it. Oral PPI (C) is irrelevant here and unsafe to give orally in this state. Waiting for a score (D) delays life-saving action; a full liver screen (B) is not urgent priority in an unstable bleeding patient.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. A non-bleeding visible vessel is Forrest IIa, a high-risk stigma requiring endoscopic therapy. Forrest III (B) is a clean base (low risk) and IIc (D) a flat pigmented spot (low risk) — neither matches this description. Forrest Ia (A) is active spurting bleeding, not a visible vessel, and surgery is not the automatic first step for any of these — endoscopic therapy is (E is also wrong, as IIb is adherent clot, usually treated endoscopically, not always surgically).

Lower gastrointestinal bleeding

Q1. Answer. Haemoglobin reflects concentration, not volume lost, and haemodilution can take 24–72 hours to develop. A near-normal Hb soon after acute bleeding does not exclude a life-threatening loss — judge severity by haemodynamics and trend, not a single Hb value.

Q2. Answer. Painless, large-volume bleeding suggests a diverticular or vascular (angioectasia) source. Cramping pain with bloody diarrhoea suggests colitis — ischaemic, infectious or inflammatory.

Q3. Answer. When the bleeding is haemodynamically severe and no clear lower source is found — brisk upper GI bleeding can transit quickly and present as fresh or maroon rectal bleeding rather than melaena, so upper endoscopy should be considered first in this situation.

Q4. Answer. Individualise it — balance bleeding severity against thrombotic risk as a shared decision with haematology/cardiology, rather than reversing automatically; restart anticoagulation once clinically appropriate, since prolonged cessation raises thromboembolic risk.

Case scenario. Answer. Ischaemic colitis — cramping abdominal pain with bloody diarrhoea in a patient with vascular risk factors is the classic pattern, distinguishing it from the painless bleeding typical of diverticular disease or angioectasia. Look for abdominal tenderness over the affected segment (often left-sided) and assess for signs of more severe ischaemia such as peritonism.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. The haemodynamic picture (tachycardia, borderline hypotension) shows significant bleeding despite a near-normal Hb — haemodilution takes time. Options A, C, B and D all wrongly treat a single early Hb as reassuring or definitive, which is the classic student error in acute GI bleeding.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. CT angiography can localise active bleeding in an unstable patient and does not require bowel preparation. Colonoscopy (D, B) needs preparation and is difficult during active severe bleeding. Barium enema (C) is not used in acute bleeding and can obscure later angiography/endoscopy. Stool culture (E) does not address active haemorrhage.

Vomiting

Q1. Answer. Gastric stasis or gastric outlet obstruction — food is retained in the stomach rather than passing through normally.

Q2. Answer. A prokinetic increases gut motility and pressure against a blockage, which can worsen a closed-loop obstruction and increase the risk of perforation.

Q3. Answer. Hypokalaemia and a hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis, because vomiting loses hydrogen and chloride ions (gastric acid) and potassium, and volume depletion drives further renal potassium loss.

Q4. Answer. Any two of: early-morning vomiting, headache worse on lying flat, papilloedema on fundoscopy, or other neurological signs.

Case scenario. Answer. Hyperemesis gravidarum. The immediate priority is IV fluid and electrolyte replacement (she cannot tolerate oral intake and has ketonuria and weight loss indicating significant dehydration/starvation), alongside antiemetics safe in pregnancy; a pregnancy test/confirmation and exclusion of other causes of vomiting should also be part of the initial assessment.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. This presentation (undigested food, distension, absolute constipation) suggests mechanical obstruction, so prokinetics such as metoclopramide (C) or domperidone (D) should be avoided as they can worsen a closed-loop obstruction. Withholding all antiemetics (A) is unnecessarily unkind and not required. Opioids (B) do not treat vomiting and can worsen ileus/constipation.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. A corrected calcium of 3.2 mmol/L is significantly elevated and explains vomiting, constipation and confusion together. While opioids (B) can cause vomiting and constipation, they do not explain the calcium result or confusion as well. Gastroenteritis (D) does not explain the biochemistry. Raised ICP (A) and Mallory-Weiss tear (E) are not supported by the given findings.

Diarrhoea

Q1. Answer. Overflow diarrhoea around faecal impaction can look identical to true diarrhoea, and a rectal examination can identify impaction quickly, preventing an unnecessary and misdirected workup.

Q2. Answer. A routine stool culture panel does not automatically include Clostridioides difficile testing — this must be requested specifically by name when recent antibiotic use or hospitalisation is present.

Q3. Answer. Antimotility agents (and possibly antibiotics, pending testing) may increase the risk of haemolytic uraemic syndrome by prolonging toxin exposure/absorption in the gut.

Q4. Answer. Fever, a high white cell count, rising creatinine, low albumin, rising lactate, and evidence of colonic dilatation or peritonism on examination/imaging — these should trigger specialist gastroenterology/surgical involvement rather than oral therapy alone.

Case scenario. Answer. Oral rehydration therapy, since this is most consistent with acute non-inflammatory (likely viral or toxin-mediated/traveller's) diarrhoea without red-flag features. Routine stool testing and antibiotics are not automatically required in a stable patient with a non-inflammatory picture; safety-net advice and review if red flags (blood, high fever, severe pain, worsening dehydration) develop.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. Recent antibiotic exposure with new diarrhoea in a frail, institutionalised patient is the classic trigger for specifically requesting C. difficile testing, which is not automatically included in a routine culture (A). Faecal calprotectin (D) and coeliac serology (E) are for chronic/inflammatory bowel disease workup, not this acute presentation. Ova/parasites (B) are more relevant with travel or specific exposure history.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. Bloody diarrhoea after undercooked beef raises concern for STEC/EHEC, where antimotility agents (A) and antibiotics (C) may increase the risk of haemolytic uraemic syndrome, so both should be withheld pending stool testing. Discharge with no follow-up (D) is unsafe given the HUS risk. Colonoscopy (E) is not an appropriate first step in acute infective bloody diarrhoea.

Jaundice

Q1. Answer. Charcot's triad is fever, right upper quadrant pain and jaundice, and defines acute cholangitis. Reynolds' pentad adds hypotension and confusion, and defines severe, suppurative cholangitis — a more advanced, higher-mortality emergency needing urgent resuscitation, antibiotics and biliary drainage.

Q2. Answer. Coagulopathy (typically INR ≥1.5) and hepatic encephalopathy, occurring together in a patient without known chronic liver disease.

Q3. Answer. A palpable, non-tender gallbladder with painless jaundice suggests malignant obstruction below the cystic duct, classically a pancreatic head tumour, rather than gallstone disease. It is neither fully sensitive nor fully specific — its absence does not exclude malignancy.

Q4. Answer. Albumin has a long half-life, so it can remain normal even in severe acute liver injury. INR reflects short-lived clotting factors made by the liver and falls quickly with acute synthetic failure, making it a much more sensitive marker of acute severity.

Case scenario. Answer. Paracetamol-induced acute liver failure — a rising INR (≥1.5) with early encephalopathy (personality change, drowsiness) in a patient without pre-existing liver disease meets the definition. The immediate priority is N-acetylcysteine (paracetamol-specific treatment, given even late presentations may benefit), urgent transfer to/discussion with a specialist liver unit, and intensive monitoring with early transplant assessment.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. Fever, RUQ pain and jaundice plus hypotension and confusion is Reynolds' pentad — suppurative cholangitis, an emergency needing resuscitation, cultures, prompt antibiotics and urgent biliary decompression (usually ERCP). Outpatient management (D) and reassurance (C) are dangerously inadequate. Liver biopsy (B) is not indicated or urgent here. Delaying antibiotics for imaging (A) risks deterioration and death.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. Painless progressive jaundice, weight loss and a palpable non-tender gallbladder (Courvoisier's sign) with a dilated duct but no stones strongly suggests malignant obstruction, classically a pancreatic head tumour. Cholecystitis (A) is typically painful with a positive Murphy's sign. Choledocholithiasis (D) usually causes pain and does not classically produce a palpable non-tender gallbladder. Viral hepatitis (C) causes a hepatocellular, not obstructive, pattern. Gilbert syndrome (E) causes mild unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia without duct dilatation or a mass.

Ascites

Q1. Answer. A diagnostic ascitic tap. New fever or confusion in a patient with ascites is spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) until it is ruled out, and the tap (cell count, differential, culture, albumin/protein) is the single test that answers most urgent questions.

Q2. Answer. An ascitic neutrophil count of 250 cells/mm³ or more.

Q3. Answer. A high SAAG (≥11 g/L) indicates a portal-hypertensive cause, such as cirrhosis or cardiac ascites. A low SAAG (<11 g/L) indicates a non-portal-hypertensive cause, such as peritoneal malignancy or tuberculous peritonitis.

Q4. Answer. It reduces the risk of hepatorenal syndrome, a serious complication of SBP, and has been shown to improve outcomes including survival — withholding it increases risk.

Case scenario. Answer. Confirmed spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (neutrophils ≥250/mm³) with evolving hepatorenal syndrome (rising creatinine without another explanation, after excluding hypovolaemia and nephrotoxins). IV albumin should be given on day 1 and day 3 of treatment as standard practice in confirmed SBP, since withholding it increases the risk of hepatorenal syndrome and death.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. New fever or confusion in ascites means SBP until excluded, and the diagnostic tap with cell count/culture is the single highest-yield test. Ultrasound (C) and CT (A) do not diagnose SBP. SAAG (E) helps classify the cause of ascites but does not detect infection. Cytology (B) looks for malignancy, not infection.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: D. A SAAG of 16 g/L is ≥11 g/L, so it is high and consistent with portal hypertension (as expected in cirrhosis). The neutrophil count of 80 cells/mm³ is below the 250 cells/mm³ threshold for SBP, so infection is not confirmed on this sample. A is wrong (SAAG is high, not low); B and E wrongly diagnose SBP; C is wrong because the SAAG can be calculated and interpreted directly from the values given.

Block 4 · Renal and electrolyte presentations

Reduced urine output and acute kidney injury

Q1. Answer. Check the catheter and drainage system first — look for kinks, loops, clots or displacement, and perform a bladder scan. Sudden anuria in a catheterised patient is a blocked catheter until proven otherwise.

Q2. Answer. Urine output <0.5 mL/kg/hour for more than 6 consecutive hours (this is the stage 1 urine-output criterion).

Q3. Answer. Refractory Acidosis; refractory Electrolyte disturbance (usually hyperkalaemia); dialysable Intoxication; refractory Overload (pulmonary oedema); severe symptomatic Uraemia (encephalopathy, pericarditis).

Q4. Answer. Some causes of obstruction, such as retroperitoneal fibrosis or early partial obstruction, may not show clearly on ultrasound. If clinical suspicion remains high despite a normal scan, further imaging (CT/MRI) should be considered.

Case scenario. Answer. Pre-renal AKI due to volume depletion from vomiting and diarrhoea, worsened by an NSAID impairing renal autoregulation. The first steps are to stop the NSAID and give cautious fluid resuscitation with reassessment, rather than proceeding straight to an autoimmune or renal-specific work-up.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: A. Muddy brown granular casts are the classic finding in acute tubular injury. Pre-renal AKI typically has a bland urine sediment without casts (B is wrong). Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis produces red-cell casts and haematuria, not granular casts (E is wrong). Post-renal obstruction is diagnosed by imaging, not urine microscopy (D is wrong). Interstitial nephritis classically shows white-cell casts and sterile pyuria, sometimes with eosinophiluria (C is wrong).

MCQ 2. Correct answer: D. Refractory hyperkalaemia with ECG changes despite medical treatment, in a patient who is already oliguric, is an AEIOU indication for urgent dialysis — further medical measures should not delay it. Repeating insulin-dextrose (C) or calcium alone (E) treats symptoms already shown not to be working. An oral binder (B) is far too slow for this emergency. A loop diuretic (A) is unlikely to work in significant oliguric AKI and does not address the immediate arrhythmia risk.

Hyperkalaemia and electrolyte emergencies

Q1. Answer. Serum potassium and ECG changes correlate poorly. Life-threatening hyperkalaemia can be present with an almost normal trace, so you treat the potassium value and its trend, not just the ECG appearance.

Q2. Answer. Protect the myocardium (IV calcium gluconate/chloride); shift potassium into cells (insulin-dextrose, nebulised salbutamol, ± bicarbonate if acidotic); remove potassium (stop causative drugs, potassium binder, loop diuretic if appropriate); dialyse for severe or refractory cases, especially with kidney failure.

Q3. Answer. No. It stabilises the myocardial cell membrane and reduces arrhythmia risk, but does not lower potassium. Its effect is temporary (roughly 30–60 minutes), so potassium-lowering treatment must follow.

Q4. Answer. A haemolysed sample, prolonged tourniquet time or repeated fist-clenching during venepuncture, or marked thrombocytosis/leucocytosis (platelets/white cells releasing potassium after the sample is taken).

Case scenario. Answer. Treat immediately rather than waiting for a repeat sample — she is symptomatic with ECG changes. Give IV calcium gluconate first to protect the myocardium, then start insulin (as part of a fixed-rate insulin infusion for her DKA, which will also shift potassium into cells) with close capillary glucose and cardiac monitoring.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. IV calcium acts within minutes to stabilise the myocardium, which is the immediate priority with ECG changes. An oral binder (C) works over hours to days, far too slow for immediate cardiac protection. Salbutamol (E) shifts potassium into cells but doesn't protect the membrane directly and is used alongside, not instead of, calcium. Simply stopping the drug (A) does nothing about the current dangerous level. A loop diuretic (B) is too slow and depends on adequate renal function and volume status.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. Delayed hypoglycaemia is a well-recognised complication, so glucose must be rechecked after treatment. It shifts potassium into cells rather than removing it from the body (E is wrong). Baseline glucose should always be checked first (D is wrong). It does not replace calcium's membrane-stabilising effect where ECG changes are present — the two are used together (A is wrong). It can and often must be used in diabetic patients with appropriate glucose monitoring (C is wrong).

Acid–base disturbance

Q1. Answer. No — a falling PaCO₂ is the expected respiratory compensation (hyperventilation blowing off CO₂). If the PaCO₂ starts rising back towards normal in this context, that signals respiratory fatigue and impending failure, not recovery.

Q2. Answer. It indicates the presence of an unmeasured acid. Causes include ketones (DKA), lactate, toxins (methanol, ethylene glycol, salicylate), and uraemia.

Q3. Answer. Respiratory compensation is fast — minutes to hours. Renal (metabolic) compensation is slow — roughly 12–72 hours.

Q4. Answer. It turns the blood gas into a hypothesis to test. If the actual values don't match your prediction, that mismatch is the clue to a second, coexisting disturbance you would otherwise miss.

Case scenario. Answer. This is most consistent with chronic compensated respiratory acidosis (from COPD) with an additional element of metabolic alkalosis from long-term diuretic use — a mixed picture in which the two disturbances partly offset each other, producing a near-normal pH despite clearly abnormal PaCO₂ and HCO₃⁻. The pH alone cannot be reassuring here: you need the clinical context (COPD plus diuretics) to recognise that more than one process is at work, exactly the "second hidden disturbance" check from step 3.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. Salicylate poisoning classically produces a mixed picture: a high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis together with a primary respiratory alkalosis from direct stimulation of the respiratory centre, so the PaCO₂ is lower than compensation alone would predict. DKA (C) would show a raised glucose and ketones. Lactic acidosis (D) would show a high lactate, which is normal here. Chronic renal failure (A) doesn't typically cause this severe an acute acidosis or a primary respiratory alkalosis. Opioid overdose (E) causes respiratory acidosis (reduced ventilation), the opposite of what's seen here.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: D. Bicarbonate has narrow, specific indications and is not routine treatment for a low pH. It does not correct the underlying process (E is wrong) — fluids and insulin do that in DKA. Giving it routinely by a pH cut-off alone (B) or before insulin (C) ignores that treating the cause is what actually resolves the acidosis. It does have a role in specific severe/refractory situations, so A is also wrong.

Block 5 · Fever and sepsis presentations

Fever with no obvious source

Q1. Answer. The height of the temperature does not track severity. A young patient can have a high fever and be physiologically stable, while an elderly or immunosuppressed patient can have life-threatening infection with a normal or even low temperature. Vital signs (respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, heart rate, blood pressure, mental state) are the more reliable severity markers.

Q2. Answer. Any four of: the mouth/throat, IV line or catheter sites, the perineum, pressure areas and feet (especially in diabetics), the spine (for discitis/epidural abscess), the joints, prosthetic material, and auscultation for a new cardiac murmur.

Q3. Answer. No. Severe infection can suppress the white cell count, particularly in the very young, the very old and the immunosuppressed. A normal or low count should not be used to talk yourself out of a serious diagnosis.

Q4. Answer. Classically when fever above 38.3°C has persisted for at least three weeks despite an appropriate structured evaluation finding no cause.

Case scenario. Answer. A new cardiac murmur (suggesting infective endocarditis). The recent dental procedure plus long-term immunosuppression raises the pre-test probability of endocarditis, and it can present with nothing more than low-grade fever and malaise. Blood cultures and careful cardiac auscultation (with a low threshold for echocardiography) should be part of the work-up before this is dismissed as viral.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: A. A well, physiologically normal young patient with an undifferentiated short-lived fever most likely has a self-limiting viral illness; safety-netted discharge with symptomatic treatment is appropriate. E (antibiotics/admission) is unjustified with no evidence of sepsis or a bacterial source. B is a blanket, unguided "fever screen" rather than a directed investigation. D gives antibiotics with no identified bacterial source. C is wrong because FUO requires at least three weeks of fever after a structured evaluation — two days does not qualify.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. In a diabetic patient, sources such as a foot ulcer, perineal infection or spinal infection are easily missed on a cursory exam and should be actively sought before more tests are ordered. A (repeating bloods) does not add new diagnostic information at this stage. B treats an unconfirmed source blindly. D is an unguided, low-yield "fever screen" before basic examination is complete. C is unsafe in a higher-risk (elderly, diabetic) patient with unexplained fever.

Sepsis

Q1. Answer. Sepsis is suspected or confirmed infection plus new organ dysfunction. Septic shock is sepsis with persistent hypotension needing vasopressors to keep the mean arterial pressure at or above 65 mmHg, plus a lactate above 2 mmol/L, despite adequate fluid resuscitation — it is the more dangerous category.

Q2. Answer. A NEWS2 of 7 or more (or 5–6 with a concerning additional feature such as a score of 3 in one parameter, mottling, a non-blanching rash, or a rising lactate) is high risk and needs emergency-level monitoring, senior review, and consideration of critical-care involvement.

Q3. Answer. Within one hour of recognition. Blood cultures should be taken before antibiotics only if this does not meaningfully delay treatment — in an unstable patient, antibiotics must never be delayed to obtain "perfect" cultures.

Q4. Answer. Because a collection of pus needs source control — drainage or surgery — to be cured. Antibiotics cannot adequately penetrate an undrained abscess, so the patient will not improve, and may deteriorate, until the source is controlled.

Case scenario. Answer. Escalate for critical-care review and start vasopressor support — this is septic shock (persistent hypotension and a rising lactate despite adequate fluid resuscitation). Continuing to give fluid alone without vasopressor support and senior/critical-care input would not be appropriate at this point, and risks fluid overload without correcting the shock.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. She has suspected infection with a low NEWS2 and stable observations — she still needs prompt antibiotics per local pathway and safety-netted monitoring, but not an emergency escalation. D is excessive for a low-risk NEWS2. E wrongly delays treatment for culture results. C is unnecessary because she is haemodynamically stable with a normal blood pressure. A is unsafe without appropriate safety-netting and follow-up given the diagnosis of sepsis-risk infection.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. An obstructed, infected biliary system is a source that needs drainage — antibiotics and fluids alone will not resolve it, and he is now in septic shock, which also needs vasopressor support and critical-care involvement. B adds no immediate value and should not delay source control. D is not indicated without evidence the current antibiotic is wrong. C ignores both the persistent shock and the drainable source. E is inappropriate given his current instability.

Fever in the returning traveller

Q1. Answer. No. Prophylaxis reduces but does not eliminate the risk of malaria, and can alter the incubation period. Malaria must always be actively excluded with blood films/rapid testing rather than being talked out of on the basis of a good adherence story.

Q2. Answer. Thick and thin malaria blood films (plus a rapid antigen test). If the first film is negative but clinical suspicion remains, repeat testing rather than abandoning the diagnosis — a single film can miss a low parasite count.

Q3. Answer. Any three of: confusion/reduced consciousness, seizures, hypoglycaemia, acute kidney injury, jaundice, acidosis, respiratory distress, or high parasitaemia.

Q4. Answer. Because a small number of returning travellers with fever from a high-risk area within the last 21 days could have a high-consequence infectious disease such as a viral haemorrhagic fever, which requires isolation precautions before routine sampling to protect staff and other patients.

Case scenario. Answer. Leptospirosis. Freshwater exposure plus fever, muscle pain and conjunctival suffusion (redness) is a classic pattern; malaria remains possible with a single negative film and should be reconsidered/repeated if she deteriorates, but the specific freshwater exposure and conjunctival findings point toward leptospirosis.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. A single negative film does not exclude malaria, especially with ongoing symptoms; repeat testing is required. B is unsafe — malaria is not excluded by one negative film. A is inappropriate without a confirmed diagnosis unless he develops severe features requiring emergency treatment. C and D both fail to actively pursue the diagnosis that must be excluded first.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: D. Confusion, hypoglycaemia and acute kidney injury are severe-malaria features requiring urgent escalation and prompt species-specific antimalarial treatment, usually with critical-care input. B understates the severity. E is wrong — these features are recognised complications of severe malaria itself, though other causes of confusion should still be considered in parallel. A is dangerous; severe malaria can deteriorate rapidly. C inappropriately delays treatment.

The febrile immunocompromised patient

Q1. Answer. A single temperature of 38°C or higher (or a sustained temperature of at least 38°C) with an absolute neutrophil count below 0.5 × 10⁹/L (or expected to fall below this). Empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics should be given within one hour, without waiting for the count to be confirmed or a source to be found.

Q2. Answer. Pus, swelling and redness are produced by neutrophils. When neutrophils are absent or very low, the usual inflammatory signs of infection cannot form, so a "normal-looking" exam does not exclude a serious infection — it reflects the missing white cells, not the absence of disease.

Q3. Answer. It risks pushing gut bacteria through fragile, immunosuppressed rectal mucosa into the bloodstream, potentially causing or worsening bacteraemia.

Q4. Answer. When fever persists despite appropriate broad-spectrum antibiotics, or when neutropenia is deep or prolonged — both raise the likelihood of an invasive fungal infection such as candidiasis or aspergillosis.

Case scenario. Answer. Treat as febrile neutropenia now: take peripheral and central-line blood cultures without delay, and give empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics within one hour, regardless of how well he looks or that today's count has not yet been repeated. His known recent neutropenia and current fever meet the definition of an emergency; waiting for morning bloods would inappropriately delay life-saving treatment.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. This meets the definition of febrile neutropenia (fever plus a very low recent neutrophil count) and is a medical emergency requiring immediate empirical IV antibiotics, with investigations proceeding in parallel. A is unsafe. B and C both delay life-saving treatment. E uses an inadequate route and inappropriately waits for a source that may never be found.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. Persistent fever despite adequate antibiotics in the context of deep/prolonged neutropenia is the classic trigger to consider invasive fungal infection (such as candidiasis or aspergillosis) and to add antifungal cover with directed investigation. C simply repeats the same category of treatment without addressing the likely change in causative organism. E is unsafe given ongoing fever and neutropenia. B is contraindicated in neutropenic patients. D is inappropriate while he remains febrile and neutropenic.

Block 6 · Endocrine emergencies

Hypoglycaemia

Q1. Answer. Below 3.9 mmol/L (70 mg/dL) is the general threshold; below 3.0 mmol/L (54 mg/dL) is clinically significant hypoglycaemia requiring prompt action.

Q2. Answer. Impaired swallowing risks aspiration into the airway. These patients need IV glucose or IM glucagon instead.

Q3. Answer. Glucagon works by mobilising stored liver glycogen. In alcohol excess or starvation, glycogen stores are already depleted, so there is little substrate to release.

Q4. Answer. Long-acting sulfonylureas have a prolonged action and can cause recurrent hypoglycaemia for 24–72 hours, so a single correction is not enough — the patient needs extended monitoring.

Case scenario. Answer. Admit for extended monitoring (typically at least 24–48 hours) with regular glucose checks and IV dextrose as needed. Gliclazide is a long-acting sulfonylurea, and recurrent hypoglycaemia hours after apparent correction is expected — a single treat-and-discharge is unsafe.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. IM glucagon 1 mg is the appropriate treatment when IV access is unavailable in an unconscious hypoglycaemic patient. E is wrong and dangerous — he cannot protect his airway, and oral/gum absorption is unreliable and risks aspiration. A wastes time and risks brain injury — treatment should not be delayed for IV access. C is nonsensical and would worsen hypoglycaemia. B requires placing an NG tube, which takes too long and still relies on unreliable gut absorption in an unconscious patient.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. Samples for insulin, C-peptide and a sulfonylurea screen must be taken while the glucose is actually low, to distinguish insulinoma (high insulin and B-peptide), exogenous insulin use (high insulin, suppressed C-peptide) and sulfonylurea use. D and A are wrong because they don't capture the diagnostic window during hypoglycaemia — an HbA1c or random glucose taken later tells you nothing about the mechanism. E is wrong because a glucose tolerance test looks for hyperglycaemia/diabetes, not the cause of hypoglycaemia, and doesn't need to be delayed a week. C is irrelevant to the diagnosis of hypoglycaemia.

Hyperglycaemic emergencies (DKA and HHS)

Q1. Answer. Glucose >11 mmol/L (or known diabetes), ketosis (blood ketones ≥3 mmol/L or significant ketonuria), and acidosis (venous bicarbonate <15 mmol/L and/or venous pH <7.3).

Q2. Answer. Insulin drives potassium into cells. If potassium is already low (<3.5 mmol/L) and insulin is started without correcting it first, the patient can develop life-threatening hypokalaemia and arrhythmia.

Q3. Answer. Resolution requires ketones to fall and pH/bicarbonate to normalise, with the patient eating and drinking again. Glucose can normalise well before the ketoacidosis has actually resolved, which is why the insulin infusion (with glucose-containing fluid added) must continue until acidosis and ketosis clear.

Q4. Answer. HHS patients have very high osmolality and are very insulin-sensitive. Rapid correction of fluids or glucose causes large, fast osmotic shifts that can cause cerebral oedema or other neurological injury, so correction is done more cautiously than in DKA.

Case scenario. Answer. Nothing further needs to delay insulin here — potassium is already known and is 4.9 mmol/L (safe, >3.5 mmol/L), so a fixed-rate IV insulin infusion (about 0.1 units/kg/hour) can be started once fluid resuscitation is underway. The point of the case is that potassium must be checked (not assumed) before insulin starts; here it has already been checked and is safe, so insulin should not be delayed further.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. SGLT2 inhibitors can cause DKA with a near-normal or only mildly raised glucose ("euglycaemic DKA"), often triggered by reduced intake or surgery — the acidosis (pH 7.22, bicarbonate 12) confirms this. B is wrong because dehydration alone does not cause a significant metabolic acidosis of this degree. E is wrong because HHS features very high glucose (often >30 mmol/L) and minimal acidosis — the opposite pattern here. A is a distractor; nothing points to gastroenteritis-driven lactic acidosis over the classic SGLT2i-DKA picture, and ketones should be checked to confirm. D is possible in principle but there's no alcohol history given, and the clinical context (SGLT2 inhibitor, reduced intake before surgery) is classic for euglycaemic DKA.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. This is HHS (very high glucose, minimal ketosis/acidosis, markedly raised osmolality). Cautious, gradual fluid replacement is the primary treatment, and VTE prophylaxis is routine because of the high clotting risk from hyperviscosity.
