D is wrong because insulin is not the first priority in HHS and, if used, is started cautiously (often at a lower rate) once fluids alone aren't lowering glucose — rapid correction risks dangerous osmotic shifts. B is wrong and dangerous — rapid correction of osmolality can cause cerebral oedema. E is wrong — a confused, severely hyperosmolar patient needs IV fluids, not oral rehydration. C is wrong — treatment should not be delayed while awaiting formal confirmation in a patient who is already drowsy and confused.

Endocrine emergencies (thyroid storm, myxoedema coma, adrenal crisis)

Q1. Answer. All three are rapidly fatal and are diagnosed clinically. Waiting for TFTs, cortisol, or a Synacthen test result before treating delays life-saving therapy in a patient who already fits the clinical pattern.

Q2. Answer. Iodine given before the antithyroid drug can transiently fuel new thyroid hormone synthesis (the Wolff-Chaikoff effect can be bypassed early on). Giving the antithyroid drug first blocks new hormone synthesis so the iodine cannot make the storm worse.

Q3. Answer. Myxoedema coma patients may have coexisting adrenal insufficiency. Thyroid hormone increases the metabolic clearance of cortisol and increases metabolic demand; giving it alone can precipitate a life-threatening adrenal crisis if the adrenal reserve is inadequate.

Q4. Answer. Sepsis with hypothermia usually still triggers a compensatory tachycardia or shivering response. Myxoedema coma characteristically causes bradycardia, along with a known or suspected history of hypothyroidism, dry coarse skin, and delayed reflexes.

Case scenario. Answer. Adrenal (Addisonian) crisis, precipitated by abrupt withdrawal of long-term steroids during an intercurrent illness. The most important immediate treatment is IV hydrocortisone (typically 100 mg IV) given without waiting for a cortisol result, alongside aggressive IV fluid resuscitation and correction of the hypoglycaemia.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. Propranolol (a non-selective beta-blocker) controls heart rate and peripheral adrenergic symptoms in thyroid storm and is first-line alongside antithyroid drugs. A (digoxin) is not first-line and is less effective at controlling the hyperadrenergic state. B is wrong because carbimazole blocks new hormone synthesis but does not control the immediate heart rate/haemodynamic problem — beta-blockade is still needed. C (amiodarone) contains iodine and can worsen thyrotoxicosis; it is not appropriate here. E (verapamil) is a rate-control option in some contexts but is not the standard first-line choice in thyroid storm; propranolol is preferred and also has some benefit reducing peripheral T4-to-T3 conversion.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. During intercurrent illness, patients with adrenal insufficiency should follow sick-day rules and increase their usual oral steroid dose (commonly doubling it), since illness increases physiological steroid demand. B and A are dangerous and precisely backwards — stopping or reducing the dose risks precipitating an adrenal crisis. E under-treats her increased physiological need during illness. D is unnecessary while she is alert and tolerating oral intake — IV hydrocortisone is reserved for patients who are vomiting, unable to take oral medication, or haemodynamically unstable.

Steroid-related problems

Q1. Answer. A prednisolone-equivalent dose of 5 mg/day or more for longer than four weeks is a reasonable threshold to assume clinically relevant HPA suppression, though individual susceptibility varies.

Q2. Answer. Steroids blunt the normal inflammatory response, so fever, CRP and white cell count can look deceptively normal even with a significant underlying infection — clinical suspicion needs to stay high regardless of "reassuring" numbers.

Q3. Answer. Sick-day rules mean increasing the usual oral steroid dose (commonly doubling it) during illness, surgery or trauma. Patients should switch to IV hydrocortisone if they cannot tolerate oral medication (e.g. vomiting) or are significantly unwell/haemodynamically unstable.

Q4. Answer. Inhaled, topical, and intra-articular steroids can all suppress the adrenal axis if used at high dose or for a prolonged period. Patients often don't consider these "steroids," so a history of "no tablets" doesn't exclude adrenal suppression — ask specifically about all steroid routes.

Case scenario. Answer. Yes — he should be assumed to be potentially HPA-suppressed given high-dose inhaled steroid use plus intermittent oral courses, and should receive perioperative steroid cover (e.g. supplemental hydrocortisone around the time of surgery) per local protocol, rather than being treated as "not on steroids."

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. She is vomiting and cannot reliably absorb oral medication, so IV hydrocortisone is needed, and the dose should be increased (sick-day cover) because of the acute illness. A is dangerous — stopping risks adrenal crisis. B is inadequate because oral absorption is unreliable with vomiting, and the dose also needs increasing, not just continuing unchanged. C is backwards — reducing the dose during illness increases adrenal crisis risk. E is not an appropriate substitute for titratable IV dosing during an acute illness.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. Long-term steroid monitoring should actively screen for osteoporosis (bone protection), steroid-induced hyperglycaemia/diabetes, and hypertension, alongside skin, eye and mental health complications. D is wrong — steroids cause hyperglycaemia, not hypoglycaemia. C is wrong — steroids with mineralocorticoid activity tend to cause hypokalaemia, not hyperkalaemia (hyperkalaemia is a feature of adrenal insufficiency/crisis, the opposite problem). B is wrong — he is currently on steroids, not in adrenal crisis; hyponatraemia with hyperkalaemia would suggest adrenal insufficiency, not ongoing steroid therapy. E is wrong — there is no recognised association between steroid therapy and bradycardia.

Block 7 · Haematology and oncology presentations

Anaemia

Q1. Answer. The MCV and the reticulocyte count. MCV classifies the red cells (microcytic, normocytic, macrocytic); the reticulocyte count tells you whether the bone marrow is responding appropriately to the anaemia. Use both together to build the differential.

Q2. Answer. Ferritin below roughly 30 ng/mL in an anaemic adult is a highly specific marker of depleted iron stores. But ferritin is also an acute-phase reactant, so it rises with inflammation, infection, liver disease or malignancy — a normal or high ferritin during inflammation can mask coexisting iron deficiency, so interpret it alongside transferrin saturation and CRP.

Q3. Answer. Folate can correct the megaloblastic blood picture, but if B12 deficiency is the real cause, its neurological damage (peripheral neuropathy, subacute combined degeneration) will continue or worsen — and this damage can be irreversible if replacement is delayed.

Q4. Answer. Anaemia with thrombocytopenia and schistocytes (fragmented red cells) suggests a microangiopathic haemolytic process — TTP, HUS or DIC — and needs urgent same-day assessment. Pancytopenia, circulating blasts, or a leukoerythroblastic film also need urgent haematology input, not repeat outpatient bloods.

Case scenario. Answer. Investigate and treat for B12 deficiency rather than dismissing the result because it is only "low-normal" or because the Hb/MCV changes are mild. Neurological B12 deficiency can occur with a near-normal haemoglobin and MCV, and the compatible neuropathy here makes this the working diagnosis until excluded. Crucially, do not give folic acid first or alone — replace B12 before or alongside folate, because folate can mask the anaemia while neurological damage continues.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. A normocytic anaemia with a high ferritin, raised CRP and a low reticulocyte response in a patient with active rheumatoid disease is classic for anaemia of inflammation (anaemia of chronic disease). A is wrong because a high ferritin argues against iron deficiency (though coexisting deficiency is still possible if ferritin were low). E is premature — bone marrow biopsy is reserved for unexplained cytopenias, additional abnormal cell lines, or film abnormalities, none of which are described. B is wrong as there is no macrocytosis or other clue pointing to B12 deficiency. D is wrong as there is no evidence of haemolysis (no jaundice, no reported rise in LDH/bilirubin, and reticulocytes are low rather than high).

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. This is severe iron-deficiency anaemia with two plausible bleeding sources reported (menorrhagia and possible GI bleeding) — both must be actively investigated in parallel with starting replacement; treating the number without finding the cause risks missing a GI malignancy or failing to control the gynaecological bleeding. B is wrong because transfusion decisions depend on symptoms and haemodynamic status, not the haemoglobin number alone, and she is stable. D is incomplete — iron alone without investigating the cause is not an adequate plan. E is unsupported — there is no evidence given for an eating disorder, and it would not explain the biochemical picture of iron deficiency. C is premature — bone marrow examination is not indicated for straightforward iron-deficiency anaemia with an identified likely mechanism.

Abnormal bleeding and bruising

Q1. Answer. Primary haemostasis (platelet/vWF) disorders cause mucocutaneous bleeding — petechiae, purpura, epistaxis, menorrhagia — that starts immediately after injury and oozes persistently. Secondary haemostasis (coagulation factor) disorders cause deep bleeding — haemarthrosis, muscle haematoma, retroperitoneal bleeding — that is often delayed or recurrent.

Q2. Answer. Von Willebrand disease and platelet function disorders can have entirely normal PT and APTT, since these tests screen the coagulation factor cascade, not platelet number or function. A convincing bleeding history should still prompt vWF and platelet-function testing even with a normal screen.

Q3. Answer. A mixing study distinguishes a clotting factor deficiency (the prolonged PT/APTT corrects when mixed 1:1 with normal plasma, because the missing factor is replaced) from an inhibitor or anticoagulant effect (it does not correct, because the inhibitor also acts on the added normal plasma). This changes management completely — factor replacement versus treating/removing an inhibitor.

Q4. Answer. Early DIC can show platelet and fibrinogen values that are still technically within the normal range but are falling from the patient's own baseline, with the PT/APTT starting to rise. The trend over time reveals the consumptive process before any single value crosses the laboratory's abnormal threshold.

Case scenario. Answer. Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) triggered by an obstetric complication (retained products, likely with associated infection/sepsis). The immediate priority is to treat the underlying trigger — urgent evacuation of retained tissue and treatment of any sepsis — alongside supportive blood component replacement (platelets, fresh frozen plasma, cryoprecipitate/fibrinogen concentrate) guided by ongoing bleeding and repeat coagulation results, with senior obstetric and haematology involvement and activation of the major haemorrhage protocol if bleeding is significant. Treating the trigger, not just the numbers, is what resolves DIC.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. A single bleeding site with an entirely normal coagulation screen is surgical/anatomical bleeding until proven otherwise and needs a surgeon, not blood products. B and A treat a coagulopathy that the numbers do not support. E delays necessary action. C is irrelevant with no history of DOAC use given and normal coagulation tests.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. Failure to correct on mixing means a factor is present but being neutralised — by an inhibitor (e.g. acquired factor VIII inhibitor) or an anticoagulant effect (heparin contamination, lupus anticoagulant) — rather than simply missing. D is wrong because a true factor deficiency corrects on mixing (the missing factor is supplied by normal plasma). C mainly prolongs the PT, not an isolated APTT. A typically causes a normal or only mildly prolonged APTT and a mucocutaneous bleeding pattern, not this mixing-study result. B is a platelet-count artefact, unrelated to APTT mixing studies.

Lymphadenopathy

Q1. Answer. Unexplained fever above 38°C, drenching night sweats requiring a change of clothes or bedding, and weight loss greater than 10% over six months. Do not accept "I sweat at night" at face value — ask specifically whether the sweating is severe enough to require changing clothes or bedding.

Q2. Answer. Rubbery, firm, mobile, non-tender nodes suggest lymphoma. Hard, irregular, fixed, non-tender nodes suggest metastatic carcinoma. Matted nodes, sometimes progressing to fluctuance or a discharging sinus, suggest tuberculosis or another granulomatous disease.

Q3. Answer. Diagnosing and subtyping lymphoma requires assessment of the nodal architecture, which FNA (a cell sample) cannot provide. Excision biopsy, or at least an adequate core biopsy, preserves the tissue structure needed for accurate diagnosis. FNA is more useful when metastatic carcinoma or mycobacterial disease is suspected.

Q4. Answer. Roughly half of patients with tuberculous lymphadenitis have no fever or night sweats, so their absence does not rule out TB. Similarly, the FBC is frequently entirely normal in Hodgkin lymphoma, so a normal blood count should not be reassuring in a suspicious node.

Case scenario. Answer. Urgent (two-week-wait) referral to ENT/head-and-neck oncology for suspected metastatic squamous cell carcinoma from an occult upper aerodynamic tract primary. He needs a full ENT assessment, including panendoscopy and node biopsy — not a further course of antibiotics, which would only delay the diagnosis.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. A cervical node above roughly 1.5 cm that persists beyond a few weeks without an obvious infective explanation needs further assessment, including consideration of biopsy — persistence and size are the key red flags here, independent of tenderness. E and C both under-investigate a persistent unexplained node. A assumes an infective cause with no supporting evidence. B is risky because an FNA reported as reactive does not reliably exclude early lymphoma, which needs architecture-preserving tissue for diagnosis.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. Supraclavicular lymphadenopathy is a high-risk location that always warrants an explanation and active consideration of intrathoracic, abdominal or lymphoproliferative disease. A is the opposite pattern — tenderness usually suggests a reactive/infective node. C describes a small node in a low-risk context, which is usually not concerning. D and E both describe classic benign reactive patterns (viral pharyngitis, dental infection) that would be expected to resolve with the underlying cause treated.

Unintentional weight loss

Q1. Answer. Losing at least 5% of usual body weight over 6–12 months.

Q2. Answer. Weight loss with reduced appetite points toward malignancy, depression, chronic infection or organ failure, while weight loss despite preserved or increased appetite points toward hyperthyroidism, uncontrolled diabetes, or malabsorption. A single answer rapidly reorganises the differential and directs the rest of the history.

Q3. Answer. BMI below 16 kg/m², more than 15% unintentional weight loss in 3–6 months, little or no nutritional intake for more than 10 days, or low potassium/phosphate/magnesium before feeding starts. Any of these should prompt dietitian involvement and cautious, monitored feeding with proactive electrolyte and thiamine replacement.

Q4. Answer. Undirected imaging or colonoscopy performed purely because a patient has lost weight, with no localising symptom, abnormal exam finding, or abnormal basic blood test, has a very low yield for finding malignancy. A localising clue (dysphagia, GI bleeding, haemoptysis, a palpable mass) is what should trigger targeted investigation, not the symptom of weight loss alone.

Case scenario. Answer. Urgent referral for upper GI endoscopy (two-week-wait equivalent) for suspected oesophageal malignancy. The dysphagia is a localising clue that substantially raises the pre-test probability of finding a cancer on endoscopy, unlike weight loss alone — this should not simply be attributed to his COPD or treated with an undirected screen.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: A. Weight loss must be objectively confirmed and quantified before it can be properly risk-stratified — without knowing the percentage lost and the time frame, you cannot judge whether this meets the threshold for "clinically significant" weight loss or plan an appropriate work-up. E, C and B jump to investigation before the symptom is even confirmed. D risks missing a genuine and important symptom.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. This patient meets high-risk refeeding criteria (BMI <16, minimal intake for >10 days), so nutritional support must start cautiously, with dietitian input and proactive correction/monitoring of potassium, phosphate, magnesium, and thiamine cover, to avoid precipitating refeeding syndrome. D risks exactly the complication being guarded against. B and C inappropriately delay necessary nutritional care. A does not address the electrolyte and thiamine risks of refeeding and could worsen things.

Oncological emergencies

Q1. Answer. New back pain in a patient with known or suspected cancer, which can precede neurological signs by weeks. Neurological deficit is a late finding, and outcome depends entirely on how quickly compression is recognised and treated — waiting for weakness or a sensory level before imaging risks permanent paralysis.

Q2. Answer. It is a mnemonic for the clinical features of hypercalcaemia: bone pain, renal stones, abdominal ("groans") symptoms such as constipation and pain, and psychiatric/neurological symptoms such as confusion. Treatment starts with aggressive IV fluid rehydration, followed by an IV bisphosphonate once rehydrated — fluids are the immediate priority because the bisphosphonate takes 2–4 days to work fully.

Q3. Answer. High potassium, high phosphate, high urate, and low calcium, typically with acute kidney injury, occurring within days of starting chemotherapy for a high-turnover tumour such as leukaemia or lymphoma.

Q4. Answer. Neutropenic sepsis is treated as an emergency by definition once fever occurs within roughly three weeks of chemotherapy, regardless of how well the patient looks, because a neutropenic patient can deteriorate extremely fast and the absence of a neutrophil response can mask how severe the underlying infection is. Antibiotics are given immediately, with the source investigated in parallel, not beforehand.

Case scenario. Answer. Malignant spinal cord compression. Immediate management is high-dose dexamethasone (per local protocol) started without waiting for imaging, together with an urgent same-day whole-spine MRI and urgent oncology/spinal surgery referral for definitive treatment (radiotherapy or surgical decompression) — every hour of delay reduces the chance of neurological recovery.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. High potassium, high phosphate, high urate, low calcium and rising creatinine shortly after starting chemotherapy for a high-turnover tumour is the classic tumour lysis syndrome tetrad; it is largely preventable with anticipatory IV hydration and urate-lowering therapy (allopurinol or rasburicase) in high-risk patients. C is wrong — hypercalcaemia causes high, not low, calcium. B and E are not supported by the biochemistry given. A is a separate emergency and would not explain this specific biochemical pattern.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. Facial/neck swelling, distended non-pulsatile neck veins and symptoms worse when lying flat in a patient with a mediastinal mass is superior vena cava obstruction; initial management is positional (sitting upright), oxygen if needed, and urgent oncology involvement, with corticosteroids often given empirically while definitive treatment is arranged. D describes a different emergency (back pain/neurology, not described here). E and B describe different biochemical emergencies with no supporting clinical picture given. C is unsupported — there is no mention of fever or recent chemotherapy.

Block 8 · Rheumatological presentations

Joint pain

Q1. Answer. Older, diabetic, immunosuppressed and already-rheumatoid patients frequently have subtle septic arthritis with little or no fever and only a modest rise in inflammatory markers. The joint findings (hot, swollen, severely painful on passive movement) matter more than the thermometer.

Q2. Answer. Articular pain hurts with both active and passive movement, in multiple directions, and is often accompanied by an effusion or synovial swelling. Periarticular pain (tendon, bursa, enthesis) usually hurts with one specific movement, and passive movement is often relatively preserved.

Q3. Answer. No. Crystals do not exclude simultaneous septic arthritis, and a negative Gram stain does not exclude infection either. The sample should still be cultured, and clinical suspicion of infection should be reassessed if the patient fails to improve.

Q4. Answer. These tests should be ordered to test a specific clinical hypothesis (a rheumatoid pattern or a connective-tissue disease pattern), not as a routine screen. There is no universal "rheumatology screen" — broad testing in low pre-test-probability patients produces incidental positives and diagnostic confusion, and a negative result does not exclude the disease.

Case scenario. Answer. Aspirate the joint (or the most accessible involved joint) for cell count, Gram stain, culture and polarised-light microscopy for crystals. The presentation is highly suggestive of acute gout, and a normal serum urate does not exclude it since urate can fall during an acute attack — but the presence of fever and involvement beyond a single classic podagra joint means septic arthritis must still be actively excluded before the case is labelled "just gout."

MCQ 1. Correct answer: C. Crystal identification and infection can coexist, so finding crystals never rules out sepsis. E is wrong because Gram-stain sensitivity is limited and a negative result does not exclude infection. B is wrong for the same reason as C (it states the opposite). D is wrong because no single cell-count threshold reliably proves or excludes infection — the probability of sepsis rises with the white-cell count and neutrophil percentage but there is no absolute cut-off. A is wrong because older, diabetic and immunosuppressed patients with septic arthritis may have little or no fever.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. Asymmetrical large-joint oligoarthritis with enthesitis (heel pain) and nail pitting is a classic psoriatic arthritis pattern. A is wrong because rheumatoid arthritis is typically symmetrical and favours small joints of the hands and wrists. B is wrong because reactive arthritis follows a preceding gastrointestinal or genitourinary infection, which is not described here, and does not explain the nail changes. E is wrong because gout is usually monoarticular at onset (classically the first MTP joint), not an asymmetrical oligoarthritis with enthesitis. D is wrong because osteoarthritis does not cause nail pitting, enthesitis or inflammatory joint swelling.

Backache

Q1. Answer. New urinary retention or altered bladder sensation, overflow incontinence, saddle (perineal/perianal/inner-thigh) sensory disturbance, reduced anal tone, bowel disturbance, and bilateral or progressive leg weakness. Any of these in a patient with back or leg pain means cauda equina syndrome until proven otherwise.

Q2. Answer. "Urinary incontinence" is often a late or imprecise description of the problem. The earlier and more important clue is new urinary retention or altered bladder sensation (difficulty starting the stream, reduced awareness of bladder filling). You must ask specifically about these rather than relying on a vague general question.

Q3. Answer. No. A lumbar MRI covers the cauda equina and conus but not the thoracic or cervical spine. If malignant cord compression is suspected, image the whole spine, since metastatic disease can affect multiple levels.

Q4. Answer. None. This is uncomplicated mechanical back pain with no red flags; routine bloods and imaging are not indicated in the first six weeks. Manage with analgesia, staying active, and safety-netting advice to return if red flags develop.

Case scenario. Answer. Treat as malignant spinal cord compression and arrange urgent whole-spine MRI with immediate oncology and spinal-team involvement. Back pain frequently precedes weakness and sphincter disturbance in cord compression — waiting for these to appear sacrifices the chance of neurological recovery, so this must be treated as an emergency now, not once bladder symptoms develop.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. This is uncomplicated mechanical back pain with no red flags and a normal neurological examination; the evidence-based approach is reassurance, staying active and analgesia, with no imaging in the first six weeks. A and D are wrong because imaging is not indicated without red flags and would risk finding incidental degenerative changes. B is wrong because there is no neurological deficit or red flag to justify surgical referral. C is wrong because prolonged bed rest delays recovery and is not recommended.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: A. This is cauda equina syndrome until proven otherwise, and it is a surgical emergency requiring urgent MRI and immediate spinal-team discussion without delay. D is wrong because any delay risks permanent neurological deficit. E and C are wrong because they fail to recognise an emergency requiring immediate action. B is wrong because plain radiographs do not show the cauda equina or a compressive lesion.

Skin rash

Q1. Answer. Give immediate parenteral antibiotics as part of a sepsis resuscitation bundle. Meningococcal sepsis must be treated on clinical suspicion; investigations (blood cultures and other samples) are taken alongside treatment but must never delay it.

Q2. Answer. Press a glass or other transparent surface against the lesion. If the colour disappears (blanches), the redness is from blood within vessels. If it does not blanch, this indicates extravasated blood (petechiae, purpura or ecchymosis) and, in a febrile patient, must trigger consideration of meningococcal sepsis, though other causes such as thrombocytopenia, coagulopathy and vasculitis are also possible.

Q3. Answer. Typical urticarial wheals are pruritic and each individual lesion resolves within less than 24 hours. Lesions that persist longer than this, especially if painful, purpuric, or leaving pigmentation behind, suggest urticarial vasculitis rather than ordinary urticaria.

Q4. Answer. Because establishing exactly when each drug (prescribed, recently stopped, over-the-counter, herbal) was started relative to rash onset identifies the likely culprit drug, which must be stopped promptly. This is often more diagnostically useful than any blood test, and it directly guides management in severe reactions such as DRESS and SJS/TEN.

Case scenario. Answer. DRESS (drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms). The combination of fever, facial oedema, widespread rash, lymphadenopathy, eosinophilia and organ dysfunction beginning several weeks after starting a known culprit drug class (antiepileptics) is the classic DRESS picture. Management requires stopping the drug immediately, full organ-function assessment, and urgent dermatology/medical review.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: A. Meningism may be completely absent in meningococcaemia, so the absence of neck stiffness must not delay treatment; antibiotics should be given immediately on clinical suspicion. E is wrong because lumbar puncture is not required before treatment and should never delay it. D is wrong because treatment must not wait for culture confirmation in suspected meningococcal sepsis. B is wrong because it uses a falsely reassuring sign. C is wrong because observation risks rapid deterioration and death.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. An enlarging annular, scaly-edged plaque with central clearing that has worsened on topical steroids is characteristic of dermatophyte (fungal) infection being masked and worsened by steroid treatment; a scraping from the active edge for KOH microscopy and culture confirms this. B is wrong because biopsy is reserved for cases where the diagnosis remains unclear after simpler tests. D is wrong because patch testing investigates allergic contact dermatitis, which does not typically produce this annular scaly appearance. A is wrong because continuing steroids on an undiagnosed possible fungal infection will worsen it. E is wrong because there are no systemic or connective-tissue features here to justify an autoantibody panel.

Block 9 · Acute medicine presentations

The acutely deteriorating patient

Q1. Answer. Whether the patient is conscious and breathing (a peri-arrest needs help called now), whether the nurse is worried, and the current NEWS2 score and which parameters are driving it.

Q2. Answer. Hypoxia kills faster than hypercapnia. Give high-flow oxygen for critical illness, then titrate down to the patient's target range (88–92% for CO2 retainers) once stable.

Q3. Answer. A-to-E is a loop — every intervention must be followed by reassessment of the same parameter. The entry should record the effect, e.g. "BP rose from 82/50 to 96/60 after the bolus."

Q4. Answer. A total score of 5 or more, a single parameter scoring 3, or any sudden rise from a low baseline — regardless of the absolute number.

Case scenario. Answer. Go and assess him yourself with a full A-to-E now — do not defer this because he "looks fine" on a passing report. A rising respiratory rate and tachycardia three days after hip surgery should raise concern for pulmonary embolism until proven otherwise, and this needs bedside review, not a phone-only reassurance.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. A GCS of 8 or less with snoring (airway noise) means the airway is at immediate risk — this must be addressed first, before any test or the rest of the assessment. A: an ABG is useful but does not address the immediate airway threat. D: imaging is not the priority until the airway is secure. C: fluids do not address an airway problem and a 30-minute delay is unsafe. B: A-to-E findings are treated as you find them, not held until the whole assessment is finished.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. The Recommendation must state explicitly what you want done and when. A is the Assessment component. D is Background. E is the introduction/Situation component. B is a vague, non-specific request that leaves the senior unsure what is actually needed — exactly the kind of escalation call to avoid.

Acute drug poisoning (overdose)

Q1. Answer. The level cannot be plotted on the standard treatment nomogram because it assumes a single ingestion time. For a staggered overdose, treatment (acetylcysteine) is started based on a weight-based dose threshold (>75 mg/kg over 24 hours) regardless of the level.

Q2. Answer. If there is a co-ingested proconvulsant/proarrhythmic drug (especially a tricyclic antidepressant) or the patient has epilepsy, flumazenil can precipitate seizures, arrhythmia or death by unmasking the toxic effects of the other drug.

Q3. Answer. The target is an adequate respiratory rate and depth, not full alertness. Over-reversal in an opioid-dependent patient precipitates acute, distressing withdrawal.

Q4. Answer. Co-ingestion is common and often under-reported. Both drugs are common, have serious but treatable toxicity, and paracetamol toxicity in particular can be clinically silent early on, so levels must be actively sought rather than relied on history alone.

Case scenario. Answer. IV sodium bicarbonate, aiming for an arterial pH of around 7.45–7.55. This is the key life-saving intervention in tricyclic antidepressant cardiotoxicity with QRS widening and hypotension, and takes priority alongside standard ABCDE resuscitation and continuous ECG monitoring.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: E. For a single acute ingestion, a timed level should be sent (levels are only interpretable from 4 hours post-ingestion) and plotted against the nomogram to decide whether acetylcysteine is needed. D is wrong because symptoms are a poor guide to paracetamol toxicity. B is unnecessary before knowing the level in a single, timed ingestion. A delays care unacceptably. C: charcoal may still be considered within 1 hour of ingestion, but a level is essential regardless.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: D. The goal of naloxone is adequate respiratory effort, not full wakefulness, and naloxone's effect wears off before many opioids', so ongoing monitoring with repeat dosing or an infusion is needed. C risks precipitating acute withdrawal. A is unsafe — re-sedation can occur. B is not indicated without confirmed benzodiazepine co-ingestion and carries its own risks. E ignores the risk of recurrent respiratory depression.

The unconscious patient

Q1. Answer. Pupillary reactivity. In metabolic or toxic coma the pupils almost always stay reactive to light; fixed or asymmetric pupils suggest a structural lesion (mass, haemorrhage, herniation), with rare toxic exceptions such as atropine, hyoscine or glutethimide.

Q2. Answer. Giving glucose without thiamine first can precipitate Wernicke's encephalopathy in a thiamine-deficient patient, so thiamine is given before or alongside the glucose bolus.

Q3. Answer. Unexplained prolonged reduced consciousness, especially unusually slow recovery after a witnessed seizure or in a patient with known epilepsy — there is no outward convulsion, so it is easily missed without a high index of suspicion and EEG.

Q4. Answer. The patient is fully aware but cannot move or speak due to a pontine lesion (e.g. basilar artery stroke). Look for preserved vertical eye movements or blinking to command, which would not be present in true unresponsiveness.

Case scenario. Answer. Non-convulsive status epilepticus (or a prolonged post-ictal state evolving into it). The unusually slow recovery after a witnessed seizure in a known epileptic is the key clue. An EEG is the investigation that would confirm ongoing seizure activity; empirical treatment with IV benzodiazepines may be trialled while this is arranged.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: A. The clinical picture (recent opioid, pinpoint pupils, respiratory depression, reduced GCS) is the classic, rapidly reversible opioid toxidrome — naloxone is both diagnostic and therapeutic. C and D are appropriate only if naloxone fails to improve him or another cause is suspected. B is unsafe before imaging if raised intracranial pressure has not been excluded, and not indicated here. E is for benzodiazepine toxicity, not supported by this picture, and risky if there is any co-ingestion or seizure history.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. Resisted eye opening and hand-drop avoidance are classic positive signs of a functional/non-organic cause, with otherwise normal examination and observations. C and D would be expected to show brainstem or focal signs (e.g. abnormal eye movements, real motor deficits), not preserved protective avoidance. A typically presents with reduced, not resisted, responsiveness and no purposeful avoidance. E would show pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression, not normal findings.

Block 10 · Nutrition presentations

Malnutrition and nutritional risk screening

Q1. Answer. BMI score, unplanned weight-loss score (past 3–6 months), and acute disease effect score, each scored 0–2. A total of ≥2 defines high risk.

Q2. Answer. Fluid retention (oedema or ascites) can hide real loss of muscle and fat mass, so weight and BMI stay stable or even rise while true nutritional status worsens.

Q3. Answer. Any two of: dental pain or ill-fitting dentures, depression, social isolation or poverty, swallowing difficulty.

Q4. Answer. Refer to a dietitian or nutrition support team, treat the underlying condition, and escalate nutritional support starting with oral measures before enteral or parenteral routes.

Case scenario. Answer. BMI 19.2 (18.5–20) scores 1; weight loss >10% in 3–6 months scores 2; acute disease effect is 0, since she has been eating normally for a week. Total \= 3 → high risk. She should be referred to a dietitian or nutrition support team, and the psychosocial driver (bereavement) should be addressed directly, with regular reassessment.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: B. Ascites can mask real loss of muscle and fat, making BMI falsely reassuring. C is wrong because a normal BMI does not exclude malnutrition here. E is wrong — albumin is affected by inflammation and illness, not nutrition alone, so it should not be used as a standalone marker of malnutrition. D is wrong — MUST can still be used in liver disease, though clinical judgement about fluid status is needed. A is wrong — reduced muscle bulk is a genuine sign of malnutrition, not simply an ageing change.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. 1 + 0 + 0 \= 1, which is medium risk; the medium-risk pathway is to document intake for 3 days and act accordingly. D is wrong — that describes the high-risk pathway, which needs a score of ≥2. A is wrong — a score of 1 is medium, not low, risk. B is wrong — the total is 1, not 0. E is wrong — parenteral nutrition is not indicated at medium risk, and oral measures come first even at higher risk.

Nutritional support and refeeding syndrome

Q1. Answer. Either one major criterion alone (BMI <16, weight loss >15% in 3–6 months, minimal intake for >10 days, or low potassium/phosphate/magnesium before feeding) or two or more minor criteria (BMI <18.5, weight loss >10% in 3–6 months, minimal intake for >5 days, or a history of alcohol misuse/certain drugs).

Q2. Answer. Serum levels can look normal despite depleted intracellular stores. Feeding triggers an insulin-driven shift of these electrolytes into cells, unmasking the deficit and causing serum levels to crash.

Q3. Answer. IV thiamine (as part of a B-complex preparation), because carbohydrate refeeding can precipitate Wernicke's encephalopathy in a thiamine-depleted patient.

Q4. Answer. Enteral feeding (oral or nasogastric), because it is safer, cheaper, and preserves gut mucosal integrity compared with parenteral nutrition.

Case scenario. Answer. Reduce the starting feeding rate substantially — start well below full requirements and increase gradually over days — and give IV thiamine before feeding, with daily electrolyte monitoring. He is at highest refeeding risk (BMI <16, and minimal intake for >10 days) despite normal baseline electrolytes, which reflect serum, not intracellular, stores.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. This is refeeding syndrome with severe hypophosphataemia and signs of fluid overload/cardiac strain, requiring immediate action. A is wrong — continuing unchanged risks further deterioration. B is wrong — feeding should be resumed cautiously once stabilised, not stopped permanently. E is wrong — increasing the rate will worsen the syndrome. C is wrong — a diuretic does not address the underlying electrolyte crisis and is not primary management.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: B. A BMI of 15 is below 16, which alone meets a major high-risk criterion. E, D, A and C each meet at most one minor criterion (poor intake >5 days, BMI <18.5, alcohol misuse, or antacid use respectively) but not two or more, and none meets a major criterion alone, so none of them qualifies as high risk.

Micronutrient deficiencies

Q1. Answer. Confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia/nystagmus. Only a minority of patients show all three features, so clinicians must have a low threshold to treat on clinical suspicion alone.

Q2. Answer. Glucose metabolism uses up remaining thiamine stores, so giving glucose first can precipitate or worsen Wernicke's encephalopathy.

Q3. Answer. Giving folate without correcting a coexisting B12 deficiency can precipitate or worsen subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord.

Q4. Answer. Those with a very poor diet, alcohol dependence, or social isolation. Features include perifollicular haemorrhages, corkscrew hairs, swollen bleeding gums, poor wound healing, and spontaneous bruising.

Case scenario. Answer. Vitamin B12 deficiency, from malabsorption after gastric bypass. Folate should not be given alone until B12 deficiency has been excluded or treatment for it started, since folate alone can worsen neurological damage such as subacute combined degeneration of the cord.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: A. Suspected Wernicke's encephalopathy needs high-dose IV thiamine given at treatment frequency, before glucose. C is wrong — oral, low-dose thiamine is inadequate for suspected established disease. E is wrong — giving glucose first risks precipitating or worsening Wernicke's. B is wrong — that dose is a prophylactic regimen, not treatment. D is wrong — treatment should be started empirically on clinical suspicion; it must never wait for laboratory confirmation.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: E. With neurological symptoms and a likely B12-deficient diet, B12 deficiency should be treated empirically without delay, and folate should not be given alone. B is wrong — folate alone risks worsening neurological damage if B12 is also deficient. A is wrong — neurological symptoms mean treatment should not be delayed pending results. D is wrong — there is no stated indication for transfusion. C is wrong — there is no evidence of iron deficiency in this vignette.

Obesity and metabolic syndrome

Q1. Answer. Overweight 25–29.9 kg/m²; obese class I 30–34.9; class II 35–39.9; class III (morbid obesity) ≥40 kg/m².

Q2. Answer. It reflects central/visceral fat, which is more metabolically active and more strongly linked to cardiovascular and metabolic risk than BMI alone, so two people with the same BMI can have different risk depending on fat distribution.

Q3. Answer. 3 or more of: waist circumference, triglycerides, HDL cholesterol, blood pressure, and fasting glucose (thresholds as in the table above).

Q4. Answer. Lifelong monitoring for micronutrient deficiencies (iron, calcium, B12, vitamin D), because malabsorptive procedures reduce nutrient absorption.

Case scenario. Answer. Refer for assessment for bariatric/metabolic surgery. A BMI ≥40 (or ≥35 with an obesity-related complication such as diabetes) after failed conventional measures is a standard indication; her diabetes and other risk factors should continue to be treated in parallel.

MCQ 1. Correct answer: D. A BMI of 37 falls in obese class II (35–39.9), and the general surgical threshold is BMI ≥40 alone, or ≥35 with an obesity-related complication. A and B name the wrong class for this BMI. E names the wrong class (class III is ≥40) and the wrong threshold. C understates the BMI threshold for surgery without a qualifying complication.

MCQ 2. Correct answer: C. Waist >88 cm (women), systolic BP ≥130, triglycerides ≥1.7, and low HDL (<1.29 for women) are all positive — four criteria. Fasting glucose 5.2 does not reach the 5.6 mmol/L revised threshold, so it is not positive, giving 4 of 5. A and B undercount the positive criteria. D is wrong because the diagnosis only requires 3 or more of the 5 criteria — glucose does not need to be one of them. E overcounts, since glucose is not positive.
